# Four Courtesans Skilled in Poetic Recitation

<note>This title, "Four Courtesans Skilled in Poetic Recitation" (Siji Jieyin), introduces a scene of "Pure Conversation," a traditional Chinese pastime involving witty, philosophical, and poetic dialogue. The women depicted here are <term>courtesans (ji): professional entertainers of the Ming Dynasty who were highly educated in music, literature, and the arts, often serving as intellectual companions to the scholar-official class</term>.</note>

->Amidst the brilliant bloom of a hundred flowers, I freely appraise their beauty to fuel my zest for wine.<-

->With four enchanting courtesans, who else could inspire such songs and provide the perfect themes for poetry?<-

<note>The couplet above captures the "literati" ideal of the Ming period: the harmony between the appreciation of nature (flowers), the indulgence in social drinking (wine), and the collaborative creation of art (poetry) with skilled female companions.</note>

<summary>This page features a woodblock illustration and poetic couplet celebrating a social gathering where scholars find creative inspiration through the company of four highly cultured courtesans. It highlights the intersection of academic pursuits, such as poetry and "pure conversation," with the refined pleasures of wine and beauty.</summary>

<keywords>Ming Dynasty, courtesans, literati culture, poetry, wine, elegant conversation, social gatherings, woodblock print</keywords>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Elegant Conversations" series, moving from the depiction of social gatherings to a narrative account involving the Prefect of Xinghua and a renowned sorcerer. It explores the blurred lines between literati culture, local folklore, and supernatural occurrences.</meta>

# The Bewitching Blossoms of Xinghua

<column-break/>

<margin>This rustic style is not only deeply personal but possesses a grand and formal atmosphere, naturally extending the resonance of his poetry.</margin>
"...Let the cold colors reach the silk robe." <gloss>The Prefect said: "The poetry is very refined, but I fear the man himself might not be able to live up to its weight."</gloss>
Immediately, Shao felt the poetic impulse stir, but having no specific theme, he said, "People usually call my humble self a 'wild and crazy man of the fields.' I dare to ask for a fine line." The Prefect replied, "You may proceed." Shao then recited a regulated poem:
->Looking in four directions at the vast, boundless grass,<-
->Where there is neither green water nor mountains.<-
->The nine-fold red sun of the capital is far away,<-
->While ten miles of clear shade rest among the mulberry and elm.<-
->Within the wind and moon, there are no obstacles;<-
->Where in the universe is there a barrier?<-
->The grass rises from the hand of the plowman,<-
->But the career of an A-heng <term>A-heng: a title for a high minister, specifically referring to the legendary sage-minister Yi Yin</term> is difficult to achieve.<- <gloss>Refers to the story of Yi Yin</gloss>
Shao thanked him, saying, "This is enough to show I have not forgotten my roots." As the wine went around again and they became half-drunk, Shao playfully said to the Prefect: "On this flower festival, there is nothing for our amusement. Would it be possible to summon a few courtesans to accompany our wine?" The Prefect laughed and said, "I have the same wish, but I doubt we can fetch them here." Shao said, "This method is most easy; just watch." Shao made a show of whispering secret words, then finally shouted in a loud voice: "Come quickly! Come quickly! Do not delay!" Suddenly, four courtesans appeared, each carrying a musical instrument, their graceful figures slowly approaching.

<column-break/>

<margin>Shao is a mere practitioner of arts, yet he can command the respect of the scholar-officials and wield the influence of a Prefect. This is an example of what is called 'using the arts of the North to move the high nobility.'</margin>
In the prefecture of Zhangzhou in Fujian, the local customs favor shamans and incantations; there is much that is illusory and strange. There was a practitioner named Xiao Shao, whose skills were particularly refined. Summoning grass to turn into swords or pointing a staff to transform it into a dragon were easy tasks for him. The scholar-officials of Zhangzhou generally treated him with courtesy. At that time, the Prefect of Xinghua, surnamed Shi, given name Dian and style name Dianzhi, was an intimate friend of his; Shao would enter the government offices as if no one else were there. One day in the middle of spring, Shao came from afar to visit the Prefect. The Prefect set out a grand banquet for him beneath the flower terrace in the rear garden. After several rounds of wine, Shao said, "Yesterday, while seeing off a friend on a long journey, I happened to compose a regulated poem to record our parting. I venture to ask you, sir, to correct it." <note>original: "Yingzheng" (郢正) — a polite literati term asking for a superior's correction of one's writing.</note> The Prefect said, "I would be pleased to hear it." Shao then recited the poem:
->The heroic spirit of the universe resides in our kind;<-
->When shall my clear eyes meet the turbid wine?<-
->Leaning against a pillar, I do not tap the sword of the Qi guest,<- <note>Refers to Feng Xuan, a guest of Lord Mengchang who tapped his sword and sang about having no fish or carriage.</note>
->Acting as a fisherman, I inquire after the boat of the man of Chu.<- <gloss>A small boat.</gloss> <note>Refers to Qu Yuan, the tragic poet-statesman of Chu.</note>
->Amidst the plum blossoms on the river, I dream of the Three Mountains;<-
->Amidst the cinnamon trees of Huainan, I face the August waves.<-
->Looking back at a different time, we should both cherish these moments...<-

<summary>This page introduces Xiao Shao, a master of illusions in Zhangzhou, and his close relationship with Prefect Shi Dian. It details a garden gathering where Shao displays his poetic talent and uses supernatural arts to summon courtesans for entertainment.</summary>

<keywords>Xiao Shao, Shi Dian, Zhangzhou, Xinghua, sorcery, literati poetry, Yi Yin, Ming Dynasty folklore, supernatural arts, social drinking</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative following Shao’s offer to summon courtesans to entertain the Prefect during their drinking session. Having just finished his own poem about his humble origins, Shao now uses his supernatural arts to bring forth personifications of flowers to provide music, poetry, and dance.</meta>

### Volume Three
#### Page Fifty-Two

<margin>
That flower spirits can take human form is strange enough. But who provided them with their various musical instruments? This was surely a dream. If a person obtains such an experience, is it a gift from Heaven, or the work of a meddlesome person? I cannot say for certain.
</margin>

From the back door, they slowly came into view. Once the formal greetings were completed, Shao invited them to sit and asked their names. One courtesan replied, "We are named after flowers. My name is Peony. My three younger sisters are Pear Blossom, Azalea, and Rose." Shao then asked, "Can you compose poetry?" The courtesan replied, "Just enough to provide a rhyme." Shao then said to the Prefect, "Let them each speak of their namesake flower."

Peony recited:

> When the crowd of flowers has withered, I begin my fresh beauty,
> As if I share a special bond with the God of Spring.
> My clear essence is not yet tempered in the golden tripod <note>The "golden tripod" refers to the vessels used by high ministers; the Peony is often called the "Prime Minister of Flowers."</note>,
> But my red light first reflects before the jade steps.
> My fragrant form gathers dew, delicate as if speaking,
> My elegant posture leans on the wind, appearing drunk and ready for sleep.
> I remember the poet Letian <note>The pen name of the famous Tang poet Bai Juyi (772–846 CE).</note> in the Secretariat,
> Having finished the purple-mud seals, he composed poems in succession.

Pear Blossom recited:

> Red and purple blossoms fall in the closed spring,
> How can they compare to my white purity, free of a speck of dust?
> A single branch drenched in rain, its icy soul is cold,
> Several trees catching the wind, their snowy color is new.
> When the oriole enters the golden shuttle <note>A metaphor for the weaving of spring scenery.</note>, it is easily distinguished,
> But when the butterfly passes the jade screen, it is hard to tell the truth.
> Beneath the tree, my face washed of makeup is my true appearance;
> The Grand Historian recorded the one who drank wine during the Tang.

Azalea recited:

> The Emperor of Shu flew 

<column-break/>

<margin>
Shao observed that the four courtesans were in high spirits. Though they could write poetry, sing, and dance, when he ordered them to leave, they all transformed back into flowers. This is truly a marvelous art.
</margin>

> Down from the nine-fold heavens, his resentment at yielding the kingdom is endless <note>Refers to the legend of Du Yu, an ancient King of Shu who lost his throne and transformed into a cuckoo (Dujuan), whose mournful cries caused him to spit blood, turning the azaleas red.</note>.
> Crying through the third month, his tears turned to blood,
> Dyeing a thousand mountains until the flowers were all red.
> Brilliant as spreading clouds amidst the spring rain,
> Glowing like iron-forged fire in the sunset.
> Where are the two daughters of the Zen Grove now?
> Looking back, the affairs of the human world are already empty.

Rose <note>Specifically the *Tumi* or Blackberry Rose, which blooms at the very end of spring.</note> recited:

<gloss>Shao observed that the four courtesans were in high spirits. Though they could write poetry, sing, and dance, when he ordered them to leave, they all transformed back into flowers. This is truly a marvelous art.</gloss>

> As spring departs, the red and purple blossoms all drift away,
> How can the "Friend of the King" <note>A literary nickname for the Rose.</note> compare to the one who leads the host of flowers?
> My name was originally derived from wine,
> My refined air can truly aid a poet’s madness.
> Like green dragons, the dense vines spread a thousand strips wide,
> Like purple musk, the thick fragrance scents the entire trellis.
> How many times, on nights of the east wind and bright moon,
> Has one seemed to see Master He <note>Refers to He Xun, a poet famous for his blossoms-inspired verse.</note> before their eyes?

The four courtesans sang as they recited, and they leapt as they danced. Their voices were as delicate as orioles, and their postures as graceful as willows. The Prefect broke into great laughter, becoming extremely joyful and playful. It was truly a case of "a single moment of a spring evening is worth a thousand pieces of gold" <note>A famous idiom from Su Shi, implying that time spent in pleasure is incredibly precious.</note>. He became stirred by passion and was about to make a move to touch them. Shao suddenly shouted, "You may leave!" The four courtesans immediately transformed back into flowers—they were nothing more than the four blossoms: the Peony, the Pear Blossom, the Azalea, and the Rose.

<summary>Shao summons the spirits of four flowers—Peony, Pear Blossom, Azalea, and Rose—who appear as courtesans to entertain the Prefect with sophisticated poetry and dance. The scene ends abruptly when Shao dispels the illusion just as the Prefect attempts to act on his physical attraction to them.</summary>
<keywords>flower spirits, shapeshifting, Peony, Pear Blossom, Azalea, Rose, Bai Juyi, Emperor of Shu, literati entertainment, supernatural arts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Han Tuozhou, a powerful and controversial figure of the Southern Song Dynasty, focusing on his indulgence in luxury and his relationship with four women gifted to him as political bribes.</meta>

### The Poetic Recitations of the Flower Ladies

During the Jiading era <note>The reign period of Emperor Ningzong (1208–1224) during the Southern Song Dynasty.</note>, the Grand Councilor <term>Pingzhang: A high-ranking administrative post, often translated as Grand Councilor or Manager of Governmental Affairs.</term> Han Tuozhou wielded such power that it overshadowed both the inner court and the outer world, causing the people to look upon him with resentment and fear. Those seeking his favor filled his residence with rare products and precious treasures from across the sea, yet Tuozhou looked upon these things with utter indifference. His only true natures <gloss>This section describes his character</gloss> were an insatiable appetite for fine wine and a fondness for feminine beauty, of which he never tired. It became known that those who presented aged spirits and peerless beauties to Chancellor Han <gloss>This is a truly extraordinary matter</gloss> would immediately receive prestigious official posts. For example, Cheng Song, the Military Commissioner of Sichuan, presented a beauty named Songshou <note>Literally "Pine Longevity."</note> to serve Han. She became his favorite, and as a result, Cheng was repeatedly promoted to prominent positions. Hearing of this, people competed to say, "The gateway to his heart <gloss>The nature of his desires is subtle</gloss> is right there; that is where one must drill to get in." Consequently, a Regional Commander of the Jiang-Huai region named Ming Zhu <note>Literally "Bright Pearl."</note>, wishing to bond with the Chancellor, purchased four peerless beauties to present to him. He named them after flowers: Hibiscus, Golden Phoenix, Golden Coin, and Banana Leaf. These four beauties were naturally extraordinary, and Ming Zhu further adorned them with gold-inlaid hairpins, fine cosmetics, and robes of embroidered silk. They became like the companions of the Goddess of the Moon. When they were presented to the Han residence, Tuozhou saw them...

<column-break/>

...and was filled with great joy. He sighed, saying, "I never knew such peerless beauty existed in the human world!" That very day, he promoted Ming Zhu to be the Grand Envoy for Regional Pacification of Jiang-Huai. The four beauties remained in the Chancellor’s residence, attending to him day and night, satisfying his every whim. 

One day, on Tuozhou’s birthday, all the civil and military officials came to offer their congratulations. Ritual silks were arranged in abundance, and a banquet was set with music that stirred the very air with the sound of pipes and strings. Han, acting the part of the boisterous drinker, became nearly intoxicated and retired to the inner chambers for a private feast. There, the four beauties held up wine cups to offer him toasts for his long life. 

<margin>
A birthday banquet was hastily prepared. 
While hundreds of officials offered their congratulations outside, 
the four beauties offered their toasts within. 
A wise man once said that for every three thousand guests in "pearl-adorned shoes" <note>A reference to Lord Mengchang of the Warring States period, known for his many high-ranking guests.</note>, there follows a "golden hairpin" <note>A metaphor for a beautiful woman.</note>; such was the elegance of this scene.
</margin>

Each lady held the flower of her namesake and requested, "On your Lordship's birthday, may we your humble servants offer flowers and recite poems as a prayer for your longevity, like that of the Southern Mountain? Is this permissible?" Tuozhou granted them leave. The first lady, holding a hibiscus flower, recited:

<gloss>Red</gloss> and white loveliness winds around the bamboo fence; their flourishing beauty dazzles the eyes, but for all too short a time. Moistened by dew, they bloom with the morning light, but why must the flowers wither as the evening shadows fall? Girls are loath to pluck them to pin in their hair, while the butterflies and bees are the first to know their hidden fragrance. Among all flowers, this one truly possesses long life, for as the old ones fall, new ones replace them as the days and months pass slowly by.

The second lady, holding a golden phoenix flower <note>Also known as the Garden Balsam or Impatiens.</note>, recited: "By whose hand were you planted in this sprawling pavilion? You have captured the very craftsmanship of Heaven and Earth's creation. Your cinnabar..."

<summary>This page describes how the powerful official Han Tuozhou was bribed with four beauties named after flowers, leading to a lavish birthday celebration where the women recited poetry in his honor.</summary>
<keywords>Han Tuozhou, Southern Song Dynasty, bribery, flower ladies, poetry, Jiading era, Ming Zhu, court corruption</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Han Tuozhou, a powerful Southern Song official. On the previous page, he was presented with four beauties named after flowers by a Regional Commander named Ming Zhu. We now join the narrative as these ladies display their poetic talents before their master.</meta>

<margin>
Poetry is only for elegant thoughts, regardless of status. Every word and phrase possesses its own sound.
</margin>

Their dignified presence was solemn and radiant as the sun, their colors seemed ready to flutter in the wind, and their branches reflected the moon like a pile of glistening gems. Their makeup was like the glowing clouds or exquisite silk. Among all flower varieties, these were hailed as auspicious beauties, most suitable to be praised in poetic verses. 

One lady, holding a **Golden Coin flower** <note>original: 金錢花. This flower's round shape and yellow center resemble ancient copper coins.</note>, recited:

> The forces of nature bestowed form upon the Flower Spirit, 
> Casting golden coins in perfect, even measure. 
> Radiant in the sun and nourished by mist, its shape never fades; 
> Polished by wind and washed by rain, its pattern remains true. 
> Its rich hue has already purchased the beauty of late autumn, 
> Its great value could provide for ten thousand poor homes. 
> If these were permitted for use across the whole earth, 
> Every common soul would become a man of great wealth.

Another lady, holding a **Banana Leaf**, recited:

> Concentrated shades of new green reflect across the courtyard; 
> This spiritual sprout possesses a nature quite unique. 
> The tender green leaves are like ruled paper for writing; 
> The fragrant heart is tightly wound, like an unread scroll. 
> When the flag-like leaves flutter, the autumn wind is fierce; 
> They sound like tinkling jade flutes in the sparse night rain. 
> If plucked to serve as a small fan for one's palm, 
> Even the craft of Lady Ban <note>Ban Jieyu, a Han Dynasty consort famous for her "Poem of the Fan," symbolizing a woman's fear of being discarded.</note> could not compare.

Tuozhou was delighted and became deeply intoxicated. The following day, Tuozhou took the four ladies to West Lake to enjoy the scenery, accompanied by two young attendants <gloss>pageboys</gloss> who were of a beautiful age and 

<column-break/>

<margin>
According to notes from the late Ming Dynasty and later, the common writing <unclear>𦞦</unclear>... [This note appears to be a later bibliographical commentary regarding textual variants in the manuscript].
</margin>

appearance. The four ladies felt an affection for them in their hearts, and the two boys also returned their feelings. Soon after, Tuozhou was heavily drunk and lying in the West Pavilion; his snoring was loud and deep. The four ladies, deceiving themselves into thinking he was fast asleep, proceeded to have illicit relations with the two boys, indulging in reckless banter without the slightest restraint. 

When Han awoke, he seemed to have heard something of the matter. He wished to punish them, but because he lacked concrete evidence, he instead held a banquet in the residence one day and ordered the four ladies <term>To serve wine: *Youshang*, an elegant term for a hostess or consort pouring wine for guests.</term>. Tuozhou feigned drunkenness, acting as if he were in a deep slumber. The two boys and the four ladies then met in a small side-chamber. 

Tuozhou rose and secretly went to observe them. He saw the four ladies and the two boys lying naked together, laughing and whispering in extreme intimacy. Tuozhou was filled with great rage. He immediately ordered his warriors to bind the two boys and the four ladies at the main thoroughfare. Tuozhou personally went there to oversee the execution. 

The corpses of the two boys remained, but as for the four ladies, there were only four stalks of **Hibiscus**, **Golden Phoenix**, **Golden Coin**, and **Banana Leaf**, along with their flowers and leaves. Tuozhou was greatly shocked, and everyone far and near thought it a strange wonder. He summoned Ming Zhu to question him, but Ming Zhu also claimed he did not know where they had truly come from.

<summary>This page recounts how Han Tuozhou's four concubines, after reciting poems about their flower namesakes, are caught in an affair with his attendants and executed, only to transform back into their original botanical forms. This reveals them to be supernatural flower spirits rather than human women, highlighting the theme of illusory beauty and the corruption of the era.</summary>

<meta>This page appears to contain both the conclusion of a dream sequence (left column) and the beginning of the narrative (right column), which is typical for Chinese woodblock prints where text is read from right to left. The right column introduces the protagonist, Fan Zheng, and his visit to the Hundred Flowers Garden in 1432 AD.</meta>

<header>Five Beauties of Exceptional Color, Volume 4, Page 12</header>
<page-num>Two</page-num>
<sig>Five Beauties... 2</sig>

<margin>
As man lives in this worldly realm,
Time passes in quick succession.
Old and young are hurried along,
Everything is just like a dream.
</margin>

<margin>
Meaning that Scholar Fan,
In the Hundred Flowers Garden,
Had but a single dream
Under the flower trellis.
</margin>

Behind the screens and shutters, the man is not seen; the birds finding their path through the flowers are the first to know. The cherries loosen their knots <note>Original: "解結" (untying knots), likely referring to the ripening or opening of the fruit</note> as they hang from the eaves; the willows are able to bend low and enter the door-branches. After getting drunk on "mountain cocoon" wine <note>A term for a rustic or potent local wine</note>, he sings a song; his uneven rhythm makes the boys of Hezhou die laughing. <gloss>Zheng is in Hezhou</gloss> Having finished his poem, his desire for wine grew even more wild; he drank freely and unrestrained, until he was profoundly drunk. Pillowing his head on his arm, he lay down beneath the flower trellis. His fragrant soul drifted along with the scents of the flowers; his wandering spirit followed the wings of butterflies and fluttered about. In a daze, as if in a misty darkness, he suddenly dreamed of five beauties approaching hand-in-hand, laughing merrily. Their beauty was peerless, and their fragrance overwhelmed him. Zheng saw them and was amazed; he bowed and asked where they had come from, and earnestly requested their names. The five beauties each introduced themselves: the first was of the Tao (Peach) family, the second of the Li (Plum) family, the third of the Xing (Apricot) family, the fourth of the Tang (Crabapple) family, and the fifth of the Mudan (Peony) family. They said they had heard a man of talent was here and had come specifically to seek him out. Zheng was overjoyed; he began to flirt with and make advances toward the five beauties, and they did not refuse him. Thus, they united there beneath the trellis. His "spring heart" <note>A poetic euphemism for sexual desire</note> overflowed and his spirits soared...

<column-break/>

# Five Beauties of Exceptional Color
<sig>Volume 4</sig>

In Chengji County of Hezhou, there was a wealthy man who opened a garden and planted rare flowers of the four seasons within it. It was named the "Hundred Flowers Garden." It spanned about a league in circumference. The noble officials and distinguished guests of the region all came to enjoy themselves there. In the middle of spring during the seventh year of the Xuande era <note>The Xuande Emperor reigned from 1425–1435 AD; the seventh year corresponds to 1432 AD</note>, there was a scholar named Fan Zheng, who was a poet. <gloss>Scholar Fan visits the Hundred Flowers Garden, intending to "stroll among the flowers and follow the willows" on a spring day.</gloss> Having heard of the Hundred Flowers Garden's fame, he arrived to view and appreciate it. He saw the hundred flowers competing in elegance and the myriad plants vying for splendor. Zheng's heart was filled with joy, and he composed two regulated poems. The first goes:

->The ninety days of spring are as heady as strong wine<-

Cutting the reds and trimming the greens exhausts the skill of Heaven. A subtle scent quietly breaks through the "Land of Rouge"; beautiful colors are applied to the "Brocade Thicket." Wealth and status seem to return to the <term>Golden Valley Garden</term> <gloss>The Golden Valley Garden</gloss> <note>A legendary 3rd-century garden of the billionaire Shi Chong, symbolizing ultimate luxury</note>, and this prosperity surpasses even the city of Luoyang <note>An ancient capital famous for its peonies</note>. Once a year, the gentle breeze returns; looking back, how can one bear it—it is all like a dream. The second goes:

The spring garden and its spring colors are perfectly suited; the small wishes and the great desires follow one another <gloss>Wishing for the spring outing to heighten his spirits</gloss>...

<summary>This page introduces the poet Fan Zheng visiting the luxurious Hundred Flowers Garden in 1432, where he drinks heavily and dreams of an erotic encounter with five women who personify different flowering trees.</summary>
<keywords>Fan Zheng, Hundred Flowers Garden, Xuande Era, flower spirits, dream narrative, Hezhou, Golden Valley Garden, Luoyang</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dream sequence of Scholar Fan (Fan Zheng) in the "Kingdom of a Hundred Flowers." Having met the personifications of five flowers, he now listens to each of them recite a poem representing their essence before he awakens to the reality of his solitude.</meta>

### Five Beauties of Exceptional Color, Volume 4, Page 13

<margin>Scholar Fan met the five beauties, and after the wine was finished, he immediately insisted they speak in poetry. This was his error; it is a pity his journey only took shape within the realm of dreams.</margin>

Among those of extraordinary beauty, at the height of their joy, each recited a poem to express her nature. **Lady Peach** <note>Original: "陶氏" (Tao), a play on the word for peach</note> recited:

> "With transcendent grace, free from a speck of dust,
> These were pruned after Master Liu had departed. <note>Refers to the legend of Liu Chen, who found immortal maidens among peach blossoms.</note>
> A kind of heavenly craft that I alone love,
> For whom do these ten parts of spring color bloom?
> Above the Jade Emperor’s hall, red clouds gather,
> In the Golden Valley Garden, crimson brocades pile high.
> Watch as they transform into the three-tiered waves,
> Where dragons ride the wind and thunder."

**Lady Plum** <note>Original: "李氏" (Li), the surname for the plum blossom</note> recited:

> "Jade scales and silver blossoms hold a faint scent,
> Not following the reds and purples to compete in fragrance.
> Bones of ice and frost enshroud the colors of spring,
> A spirit of water and moon clad in white night-light.
> Before the platform of the Great Weaver, shedding powdered tears,
> In the Han Emperor’s palace, learning the plum-blossom makeup. <note>Refers to Princess Shouyang, who fell asleep under a plum tree and started a makeup trend when a petal left a mark on her forehead.</note>
> A recluse’s elegant nature is truly pure and simple;
> Chanting before the jeweled forest, her lofty spirit lingers."

**Lady Apricot** <note>Original: "杏氏" (Xing)</note> recited:

> "In the second month, the God of Spring is drunk on the balmy sun,
> Exquisitely adorned, leaning all over the Noon Bridge Villa.
> Red light shines fully upon the coral trees,
> Purple splendor perfumes the embroidered robes.
> How many times has the evening scent reached the wine shop?
> A single branch of spring color reaches out over the neighbor's wall.
> Faced with this, the scholar's spirits are high,
> Composing new poems to place in his brocade bag."

<column-break/>

<margin>A journey to the Kingdom of a Hundred Flowers, told in verse rather than prose. Like the stories of old, it ends with a sudden awakening. Was Scholar Fan's divine encounter a trip to the immortal Tiantai, the mythical Huaxu, or the dream-kingdom of Nanke?</margin>

**Lady Crabapple** <note>Original: "唐氏" (Tang), often associated with the Haitang or Crabapple/Begonia</note> recited:

> "Jiangnan’s second month has such fine spring light,
> A kind of fragrance that is exceptionally rare.
> Brilliant colors in the spring breeze flush the drunken face,
> Tears congeal in the morning dew, wetting the weeping makeup.
> One must pity Xishi <note>A legendary beauty</note>, who is so fond of sleep,
> Yet resent the God of Spring for not granting her fragrance.
> Why was it that in those years, the Great Poet Du <note>Original: "杜工部" (Du Fu). A literary trope notes that Du Fu never wrote poems about crabapples.</note>
> Was too lazy to chant verses and put them in his travel bag?"

**Lady Peony** recited:

> "Only after the faded reds have fallen does the true fragrance emerge,
> Her fine name is known as the King of a Hundred Flowers.
> Competing to boast of a peerless beauty under heaven,
> She alone claims the premier fragrance of the human realm.
> In a drunken state she meets the wind, lovely and wanting to speak,
> Her wondrous form holds the dew, wetting her weeping makeup.
> Sir, do not look at the common, drifting blossoms;
> This one is worthy of being cultivated in the Hall of Broad Daylight."

When the five beauties finished their chanting, they gathered together within the silken curtains, pulling and tugging at one another as if walking hand-in-hand. As they playfully pursued one another, the dream suddenly broke. Scholar Fan opened his eyes and looked around, only to find himself still lying alone beneath the flower trellis. Where were the so-called beauties now? Therefore, he recorded the events in full.

<summary>This page concludes a dream sequence where five personified flowers—Peach, Plum, Apricot, Crabapple, and Peony—recite poems reflecting their legendary qualities. The protagonist, Scholar Fan, awakens to find it was all an illusion, prompting him to record the vision.</summary>
<keywords>Flower personification, flower poetry, Scholar Fan, dream literature, Peach Blossom, Plum Blossom, Peony, King of Flowers, Du Fu, Liu Chen, Nanke dream</keywords>

<meta>This page begins a narrative account of a supernatural encounter involving the scholar Cui Xuanwei. It follows the tradition of "records of the strange" (zhiguai), specifically focusing on the intersection of Daoist practice and the spirits of the natural world.</meta>

<header>Volume 4, Page 14</header>

<margin>This refers to his "dwelling in truth" and devotion to the Way, as well as his diet of medicinal herbs; [Cui] Xuanwei's name likely alludes to his pursuit of the subtle and profound.</margin>

# Strange Discernments from the Garden

In the city of Luoyang, there lived a man named Cui Xuanwei, a <term>scholar-hermit<note>A "chu shi," or a scholar who chooses to live in seclusion rather than seeking government office.</note></term>. He established his residence to the east of the Luoyang gardens, where he "dwelt in truth" and devoted himself to the Way. For thirty years, he sustained himself on a diet of pine resin and <term>Poria cocos<note>An edible fungus found on pine roots, highly valued in Daoist longevity practices.</note></term>. Eventually, his medicinal supplies were exhausted, and he led his young servants into the deep reaches of Mount Song to gather more. When the task was finished and he returned home, he found his house had long been unoccupied; wild weeds and mugwort filled the courtyard. It was a spring night; the wind and moon were clear and bright, and the fragrance of flowers filled the air. Xuanwei sat alone in his courtyard. For some reason, his family members did not come to him, and as a result, he could not fall asleep. While tossing and turning, he suddenly heard the watchman’s drum reporting the arrival of the <term>third watch<note>Approximately midnight.</note></term>. Suddenly, a person dressed in blue knocked at his door. Xuanwei opened the door and questioned the visitor. The person in blue said, "I live here in the Garden. Since the Garden and Luoyang are so close, I wish to take one or two female companions to visit my cousins at the Upper East Gate. May we borrow this place to rest for a short while?" Xuanwei granted permission. Within a few moments, more than ten people followed the person in blue inside. One dressed in a green skirt stepped forward and said, "My surname is Yang." She then pointed to another and said, "This is Lady Li," and another, "This is Lady Tao." Finally, she pointed to a young girl in a scarlet dress and said, "Her surname is Shi, and her name is Acuo." Each was accompanied by their own maidservants.

<column-break/>

<margin>It is fitting that each flower takes its own botanical name as a surname. Referring to the Wind as "Auntie Feng Eighteen" is a particularly clever use of hidden metaphor.</margin>

Once the introductions with Xuanwei were completed, he invited them to sit under the moonlight and asked the reason for their journey. They replied, "We wish to visit Auntie Feng Eighteen. For several days, Auntie has said she wanted to come see us, but she could not. Tonight, the whole group is going to see her." Before they had even settled in their seats, it was announced from the gate that Auntie Feng had arrived. The group was startled and delighted, and they went out to welcome her. Lady Yang said, "Our host is very virtuous; these few words of hospitality alone are superior to anything we might find elsewhere, for I fear other places might not be as fine as this." Xuanwei then came out to meet Lady Feng <gloss>Feng is a pun for Wind</gloss>. Her speech was crisp and cool, and she possessed a refined <term>air of the forest<note>A phrase used to describe the elegant and lofty manner of a person of high character.</note></term>. He bowed and invited them all to sit. Their beauty was peerless, and the entire courtyard was filled with a fragrance so thick it was palpable. The scholar-hermit ordered wine to be served, and each of the ladies sang a song to show off her talent. Xuanwei recorded two of them. A person in red clothing and one in white served the wine; the one in red sang:

> "My jade-like face is bright and pure, surpassing the white snow,
> Especially now, as I face the wind and the moon.
> Lost in thought, I dare not resent the East Wind,
> I only sigh to myself as my beauty secretly fades away."

Then, the person in white served the wine and sang:

This spread captures the climax of their interaction, where the women reveal their true nature as flower spirits and seek Cui’s protection from the destructive forces of nature.</meta>

<margin>Wine stained the crimson lady's dress, and she stood up and left; she had been under protection, but then the other [the wind] failed to protect her.</margin>

Her crimson robe flutters, overflowing with dew,
A single blossom lightly dyed with cinnabar.
I resent that my youthful face cannot remain;
Do not blame the spring wind for its fickle heart.
<note>original: "絳衣披拂露盈盈... 莫怨春風道薄情。" This poem captures the fragility of flowers and their resentment toward the wind.</note>

When Lady Feng <note>Eighteenth Aunt, the wind goddess.</note> held up her cup, her nature was revealed as flirtatious and reckless. She spilled her wine, staining the crimson-clad lady's dress.

The crimson lady <note>Lady Cuo, the pomegranate spirit.</note> grew angry and said, "The others may seek your help, but I shall not!" <gloss>She then brushed off her sleeves.</gloss> She brushed off her sleeves and stood up.

The Eighteenth Aunt said, "The young lady is acting out because of the wine." They all rose and went out the gate. After bidding farewell to the Eighteenth Aunt, who departed to the south, the other women entered the garden to the west and vanished. Xuanwei did not find this particularly strange.

The following night they returned, saying they wished to visit the Eighteenth Aunt’s residence. The crimson lady said angrily, "Why go there again? To Lady Feng’s house? If there is trouble, we should simply ask the <term>scholar-hermit<note>Chu shi, a title for a gentleman living in scholarly retirement.</note></term> for help."

Xuanwei asked them for the reason. The crimson lady replied, "We companions live here in the garden. Every year, we are harassed by malicious winds and cannot live in peace. Therefore, we used to seek the protection of the Eighteenth Aunt. Yesterday, I could no longer endure her, and in the end, we returned to face our hardships alone." <gloss>Meaning they would be shaken down and fall due to the wind.</gloss> "Now we wish to borrow your strength, Scholar. If you do not refuse us, we shall offer a small reward."

Xuanwei said, "What strength could I possibly have to help you?"

<column-break/>

<margin>Xuanwei did not initially attain eternal life, nor did he grasp the heart of the Way; however, through his care for the spirits and deep self-cultivation, his spirit was transformed. His journey to Mount Song is like the legend of the Divine Pearl, lasting for tens of thousands of years.</margin>

The crimson lady said, "All you must do, Scholar, is to prepare a red banner <note>A "Zhu Fan," traditionally used in rituals to ward off evil or command spirits.</note> every year. On it, paint the symbols of the sun, the moon, and the five stars. Erect it in the eastern part of the garden, and we shall be spared from calamity. This year’s dangerous period has already passed, but we ask that on the twenty-first day of this month, at dawn, if there is a slight eastern breeze, you set it up. Then we might escape disaster."

Xuanwei granted his promise. The women all bowed to him in succession and departed.

When the appointed day arrived, Xuanwei did exactly as she had said and erected a banner. Suddenly, a great wind began to rake the earth, coming from the south of Luoyang. It snapped trees and sent sand flying, yet the many blossoms within the garden remained perfectly still and unmoved.

Xuanwei then realized that the different surnames of the women and the various colors of their clothing corresponded to the essences of the many flowers. The one in the crimson dress, named Cuo, was the Pomegranate. Lady Feng was the Goddess of the Wind.

Later, after several seasonal changes, the women of the Yang <note>Likely the Willow spirits.</note> family and the others returned to thank him. Each brought several <term>dou<note>A traditional Chinese unit of dry measure, roughly equivalent to a decaliter.</note></term> of peach and plum blossoms. They said to Cui, "By consuming these, you may extend your years and ward off old age. We wish for you to remain the master of this land forever, so that we too may obtain long life."

Cui consumed the blossoms, and it was said that the effects were indeed miraculous.

One should only say "Ying" and then "Ying" again; as soon as the mouth opens, it is no longer its own stage. This is a surname, yet specifically not a mere word; it is the spoken name of the seed itself.
</margin>
The Stone Recluse said: "I was an old friend of your late father. I hold this wine for you; have you not heard of the *Instruction of the Courtyard*? <note>original: 趨庭之訓 (qūtíng zhī xùn). A reference to the Analects, where Confucius's son hurried through the courtyard to receive his father's teachings; it refers here to the education and discipline of a son by his father.</note>" Thereupon, they enjoyed each other's company with great joy. When the wine had brought them to a state of pleasant intoxication, the Stone Recluse said: "In my house, I have four singing girls. Not only are they skilled at folk songs, but their poetic recitations are also quite noteworthy. I shall have them come out to serve the wine." 

He then called for the four girls. Upon hearing the command, they came slowly and gracefully, carrying their musical instruments. Each introduced herself by surname: one was Miss Ying, another Miss Pi, another Miss Yang, and another Miss Tao. Upon looking at them, they were all of extraordinary beauty. From that point on, the music of their pipes and strings was so powerful it *stopped the drifting clouds* <note>A classic idiom for music so beautiful it arrests nature itself.</note>, and their songs and dances made the dust fly from the rafters, with the sound *circling the beams* <gloss>lingering melody</gloss>. 

The central hall was filled with the greatest joys of the era. As the wine cups were passed and the emotions became boundless, the Stone Recluse said: "Plucking strings and singing are common matters, not enough to truly delight an old friend. You girls must each recite a regulated poem to please our guest's ears. Is that understood?" The four girls bowed in obedience. The Stone Recluse first commanded Miss Ying. Miss Ying recited: "The color is dyed like the tip of a cinnabar grain; its quality is known to be supreme, its flavor better than a sudden awakening—"

<column-break/>

<margin>
In response to the discussion, truth and falsehood naturally cannot be hidden. But if it is not so, why would a scholar act without beauty, yet his actions must inevitably lead back to this?
</margin>
In Haozhou, there was a man named Cheng Qi, whose courtesy name was Tingyong. He was a retired scholar. He was naturally straightforward and held himself to strict standards, never allowing himself the slightest indulgence. In his dealings with others, he was as warm and sincere as a spring breeze. His family circumstances were comfortable and prosperous. He lived alone in a private residence, where even his family members would not lightly enter without a reason. 

One day, as evening fell and he was sitting in leisure, a gray-haired servant suddenly bowed and entered, saying: "The Stone Recluse requests your presence." Qi was an upright man; without even discerning who this "Stone Recluse" was, he immediately accompanied the servant. They walked for less than a mile when they saw a tall forest in the distance and several magnificent houses, appearing just like a scenic village. At the gate, the Stone Recluse, dressed in plain white silk and holding a bamboo staff, came out to welcome him. He led him into the central hall, where they performed the formal greeting rites between host and guest. 

The Recluse said to Qi: "I hope you will not take offense at my sudden invitation. I have prepared some *humble wine* <gloss>literally 'Lu wine,' a modest term for thin or poor-quality wine</gloss> specifically to honor your arrival." He then led him to the front of the hall, where he saw that the meats and fruits were already laid out in abundance. Qi said to the Recluse: "Why have you prepared such a feast?" <gloss>It is suspected the word "wine" is missing after "feast."</gloss>

"...with chrysanthemums for her clothing. The branches are heavy, saturated by rain and mist. The round fruits hang low like red agates, their color matching a face newly touched with purple rouge. Referring to Xiwangmu, the Goddess of the West who possesses the peaches of immortality.</note>, while three are stolen, bringing to mind that youth of the Han <note>original: 漢兒 (Hàn ér). A reference to Dongfang Shuo, a legendary advisor to Emperor Wu of Han who was said to have stolen immortal peaches from the goddess's garden.</note>. May we shine together with the immortals of long life; when shall we walk hand-in-hand by the Jade Pond?" 

The <term>Stone Recluse <note>original: 石處士 (Shí Chǔshì). A personification of the "scholar stones" or decorative rocks found in Chinese gardens.</note></term> also chanted his own verse:

"Several fathoms high and majestic, I sit atop the rockery; through the four seasons, my rugged beauty never changes. Birds pass by this emerald screen, while people gaze as if looking into a landscape painting. In the shade of the 'Azure Snow' dragon, the winding paths are lost; white clouds cast their shadows upon the poet’s altar. When should I build a home beneath these layered cliffs? I pledge to join the pines and bamboo in defying the winter cold." 

Having finished his song, he laughed heartily, and after much merriment, they parted. By then, the sky was growing bright. The next day, Qi led his servants, carrying wine and delicacies, to visit the Stone Recluse, intending to return the favor of the previous night's feast. When he reached the Recluse’s former residence, he saw nothing at all. There was only a steep rockery, surrounded by the brilliant colors of cherry, loquat, bayberry, and grape fruits. Only then did Qi realize they were <term>spirits <note>original: 妖 (yāo). Refers to supernatural beings or manifestations, often derived from plants or objects.</note></term>.

"Many trees were planted within the Kingdom of Shu <note>modern-day Sichuan, famous for its cherries</note>, and several grew before the Queen Mother's fingers. When wild birds peck at them, they carry 'fire-fruits' in their beaks; where mountain apes steal them, 'red pearls' fall. "Cinnabar forms her bones..."

<meta>This page continues the "Selections of Pure Talk," a collection of literary anecdotes and supernatural tales. # Selections of Pure Talk
## Volume Four of the Collected Works

<margin>
Seeking out a female companion and sending her a poem is a deed no less elegant than Mei Shu <note>original: "枚叔" (Mei Shu), the courtesy name of the Han dynasty poet Mei Cheng, famous for his literary gatherings.</note> carrying wine to a banquet.
</margin>
<margin>
Huizhi dreaming of lychees is an instance of his soul departing his body. Meeting the Flower Spirit was a fated encounter; it was not a demonic haunting.
</margin>

# Lychees Entering a Dream

The ancient region of Minyue <note>An area encompassing modern Fujian and parts of Guangdong.</note> has long produced lychees of an extraordinary variety. By the sixth month of the lunar calendar, the fruit ripens; its flavor is delicious and praiseworthy, and its red color is lovely to behold. The world treasures them as rare gems. 

At the end of the Yuanfu era <note>Approximately 1100 CE, during the reign of Emperor Zhezong of the Song Dynasty.</note>, there lived a scholar in Jianning named Tan Huizhi. One day, he invited a friend to travel with him to the famous mountains surrounding the city outskirts. They climbed ladders and followed stone steps, venturing deep into the rocky crags. Reaching a certain valley, they saw a stone bed, flat and steep, and a stream that meandered in circles. His friend remarked, “Is this not like the legendary Shang Mountain?” <note>A mountain famous as a retreat for the "Four White-haired Recluses" who fled the chaos of the Qin Dynasty.</note> Huizhi, moved by the scene, composed a poem in the disciplined octave style:

> Heading south into the Shang Mountains, the pine paths are deep,
> A stone bed and stream water lie in the daytime shade.
> Amidst the clouds, I gather herbs, following the traveler’s staff,
> While inside the cave, tilled fields reflect the green forest.
> Reaching toward the mists and rosy clouds, I raise my hand in vain,
> But returning through the mounds and hills, my heart is grieved.
> The flowers and trees of Wuling <note>The site of the fabled Peach Blossom Spring.</note> should remain forever;
> I shall search once more with my disciples for the <gloss>Taoyuan County</gloss> gates.

When the poem was finished, he said to his friend, “Do you have nothing to say?” His friend likewise composed a poem:

> On a towering peak of a hundred feet, the trees stand thick,
> Though there is mountain light, there is no shade.
> A pair of cranes is well-suited to lead one to this fragrant scene,
> Why should a person of jade <note>A "jade person" (yuren) is a literary term for a refined scholar or a handsome man.</note> be drowned in mundane paperwork?
> The mountains hold an auspicious air, especially suited to the sun,
> The birds chase a light breeze, no longer staying in the forest.
> Here is the very place where Ruan Lang <gloss>the story of Ruan Zhao</gloss> <note>Ruan Zhao was a legendary figure who got lost in the mountains and encountered immortal women.</note> lost his way,
> Where the red lychee trees grow beside a deep stream.

After the poems were completed, the two men walked back hand-in-hand, singing and laughing, truly enjoying themselves. The friend was the first to depart, leaving Huizhi to walk on slowly by himself. Near the outskirts of the city, he saw a garden of lychees. The fruit hung ripe, a brilliant and dazzling purple-red, beautiful enough to be loved. Huizhi plucked and ate some. Feeling weary, he took a short rest under a tree. 

In a daze, he dreamed he entered a room. A beautiful woman in formal dress came out to welcome him, saying, “I am deeply moved that a great gentleman such as yourself has deigned to look upon me with favor. I have felt this gratitude most keenly. I dare to invite you for a brief conversation.” She then took his hand and led him inside, where they performed the rites of husband and wife. Huizhi asked for her name, and the beautiful woman sang:

> My life began in the wilds of the Yue region,
> In the sixth month, I am first presented on a tray in the Southern Provinces.
> My flesh is tender, a husk the color of red phoenix marrow,
> My skin is dry, with rough ridges like a purple rooster’s comb.
> Swallowing the lingering flavor quenches the heart’s thirst,
> Chewing through the celestial center splashes the teeth with cold.
> I yet remember the Consort <note>Referring to Yang Guifei, the famous consort of Emperor Xuanzong, who was notoriously fond of lychees.</note> in those years...

<summary>This page tells the story of the scholar Tan Huizhi, who, after composing poetry about legendary recluses with a friend, falls asleep under a lychee tree and encounters the personified spirit of the fruit in a romantic dream. The narrative blends classical allusions to hidden paradises with the sensory appreciation of the lychee, a prized fruit of Southern China.</summary>
<keywords>lychee, Tan Huizhi, Song Dynasty, Minyue, Jianning, plant spirits, flower goddess, Peach Blossom Spring, Ruan Zhao, poetry, Yang Guifei</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the scholar Tan Huizhi. Having arrived at a scenic mountain retreat, he has encountered a mysterious beauty who is the personification of the lychee tree's spirit. The previous page established the setting in the Minyue region during the Song Dynasty.</meta>

<margin>
The "Six Intentions upon the Pillow" are intimate words; however, the excellence of their composition is limited by their context. I dare not elaborate on them fully.
</margin>

Huizhi laughed as he recalled the line, "A single rider gallops through the red dust to Chang’an," <note>original: "紅塵一騎過長安" (hóng chén yī jì guò cháng'ān). This is a famous line from the Tang poet Du Mu, referring to the high-speed delivery of fresh lychees to the imperial consort Yang Guifei, which exhausted many horses.</note> and after reciting it, he drifted off to sleep in a state of profound tenderness. Upon the pillow, the beauty then recited two poems in the **Ancient Style**. The first said:

> You admire the grace of the peach and plum,
> While I admire the endurance of the pine and cypress.
> The peach and plum sway in the spring breeze,
> Only to wither and fall among the fragrant weeds.
> They are not like the branches of the pine and cypress,
> Which remain green and fair through the long years.

The second said:

> You admire the red conch-shell cup,
> While I admire the mirror of the green phoenix.
> The conch cup overflows with fragrant ale—
> Drinking it brings disorder to the human spirit.
> It is not like the mirror’s growing light,
> Which can reflect what is crooked and what is upright.

The following day, Huizhi asked to take his leave. The beauty wept and said, "Our affections are easily obstructed, and a future meeting is difficult to guarantee. Why do you speak of leaving so soon?" Huizhi replied, "I know well that our feelings are thick and our intentions are intimate, but I only fear that *extreme joy gives birth to sorrow* <note>A common Chinese idiom (lè jí bēi shēng) suggesting that all highs must inevitably lead to lows.</note>. This is why I wish to depart." Seeing she had no choice, the beauty prepared wine for a farewell toast. The side dishes were not rare delicacies; there was only a single tray set upon the table. The fruit on it was red, each piece like a pearl. Huizhi was so preoccupied with his thoughts that he did not even have time to eat; he simply sighed in contemplation. To comfort him, the beauty wiped away her tears and recited another poem:

<column-break/>

<margin>
A unique melody of longing. Every single word is a masterpiece of craft.
</margin>

She said:

> On what day shall we meet again?
> Why must longing be so solitary and sad?
> My rosy cheeks once served your towel and comb, <note>A humble way of saying she acted as his wife or servant.</note>
> But now white hairs appear where our paths diverge.
> How much time has passed since we parted?
> Suddenly, the frost and dew feel cold and desolate.
> I look up to see the bright moonlight,
> While the many stars are scattered in disorder.
> The light in my eyes has already dimmed;
> The flying gnats and crickets cry in the courtyard.
> Moved by emotion, I cannot find sleep;
> How can I brush away the tears that fall?
> The west wind blows against the silk curtains;
> I worry that you will be cold without your robes.
> Is this not a time of great love and tenderness?
> But who is there to truly know it?
> I receive you, admiring your noble virtue;
> Please strive to cherish your own health and radiant spirit.
> Do not speak again of being cast aside;
> Deep sorrow only makes a person grow old.

When she finished her recitation, she saw Huizhi off. Outside the gate, she wept uncontrollably. Huizhi was also deeply moved, and they parted in a lingering embrace, both covered in tears. He had only moved a few steps away when he hurriedly turned his head; suddenly, he tripped and fell, waking with a start. Opening his eyes to look around, he found himself lying flat beneath a **lychee tree**. Only then did his heart realize he had been enchanted by a spirit. He was greatly startled and sighed in wonder at the experience.

The text belongs to a "chuanqi" style short story, likely from a collection similar to the "Sanyan" (Three Words) series, as indicated by the marginalia.</meta>

<margin>
The sixteenth day of the eighth month is no ordinary moonlit night. It is what the ancients called the "Three-Five Beautiful Evening" <note>A reference to the 15th day of the lunar month (3x5=15), specifically the Mid-Autumn Festival, though this takes place the night after.</note>, a time still recognized by those of spiritual understanding.
</margin>

# The Manifestation of the Old Laurel Tree

Di Mingshan of Renhe, Zhejiang, was a man of ample means and a witty, jovial disposition. Though he enjoyed drinking, he never reached the point of stupor. Having received a classical education, he was recognized as a poet by his peers, though some dismissed him as a "profligate scholar." During the **Hung-hsi era** <note>The short reign of the Ming Emperor Renzong, 1425 AD.</note>, he had business in Haiyan. His boat reached Ganpu, about seven *li* to the east, just as twilight fell. It was the sixteenth day of the eighth lunar month; the crimson laurels <term>dan gui; a variety of Osmanthus fragrans</term> offered their fragrance, and the "golden wind" of autumn blew refreshingly. Mingshan felt a surge of inspiration and boldly recited a poem:

> The vast sky for ten thousand miles is a single shade of autumn,
> To whom do the light clouds and fading dew belong?
> The moon shines upon the Silver River <note>The Milky Way</note> across three thousand worlds,
> While a man sits drunken in the Golden Peaks of the twelve-story tower.
> The color of "bamboo leaf" wine stirs the hero's spirit,
> As laurel fragrance fills the head of the "fire chariot" <note>Likely a metaphor for the rapid passage of time or a specific poetic vessel</note>.
> Who will convey my words to **Chang’e** <note>The Goddess of the Moon</note>?
> Grant me a carefree journey through her Toad Palace.

Having finished his poem, he left his boat and walked a short distance. In the distance, he saw the faint glimmer of candlelight in a village. He walked a few paces toward it and found, to his surprise, a wine shop. Mingshan entered the doorway and saw only a single woman of extraordinary beauty.

<column-break/>

<margin>
The woman introduces her family lineage and name—she is herself a solitary, towering Laurel tree. Mingshan, in his lack of spiritual awakening, is blinded by lust and fails to realize this.
</margin>

Upon seeing Mingshan, she rejoiced and said, "Have you come here to drink? Or is your 'intent not in the wine,' like the Old Drunkard of legend?" <note>A reference to Ouyang Xiu’s famous essay "The Pavilion of the Old Drunkard," suggesting his interest might be in her rather than the alcohol.</note>

Mingshan simply replied, "I am here for the wine."

The woman then led Mingshan to a small pavilion behind the main room. A plaque hung there reading: *Heavenly Fragrance Nurturing Excellence*. The room was impeccably clean. The woman asked, "May I ask your honorable surname and which prefecture you hail from?"

Mingshan said, "My name is **Di Mingshan**, a resident of Renhe in Hangzhou. Might I ask your name, fair lady?"

The woman replied, "My surname is **Gui** <note>The character for "Laurel" or "Cinnamon"</note>, and my given name is **Shufang**. My family were of the noble line of the <gloss>Five Laurels of Mount Yan</gloss> <note>original: "Yan shan wu gui" — an allusion to the five sons of Dou Yujun who all passed the civil service exams</note>. My father passed away early, and my kin have scattered. Thus, I live here as a guest, selling wine to make my living."

She then ordered her servant girl, named **Lichun** (Beautiful Spring), to set the table with wine, fruit, and nuts. As the woman and Mingshan drank together and the wine began to take effect, Mingshan composed a poem in the "regulated verse" style regarding the Laurel:

> The Jade Palace is free of dust, the wind and dew are cool,
> Old emerald greens touch the clouds,
> Spitting out new yellow blossoms.
> The seeds were sown in the Toad’s Cave <note>The Moon</note>, their roots must be divine;
> Their name comes from Mount Yan, their beauty surpassing the ordinary.
> Adorning the trees like grains of gold,
> The scent is as piercing and pure as burning agarwood.
> Tonight, I wish to pluck a high branch—
> I beg you, grant me this...

<summary>A scholar named Di Mingshan encounters a beautiful woman named Gui Shufang in a mysterious wine shop during the Mid-Autumn season. The text uses heavy floral symbolism to hint that the woman is actually the spirit of an ancient laurel tree.</summary>
<keywords>Di Mingshan, Gui Shufang, Laurel Tree Spirit, Mid-Autumn Festival, Ming Dynasty fiction, Chinese folklore, Ganpu, wine shop encounter</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Di Mingshan, a scholar who has encountered a mysterious woman by the Ganpu shore on the night after the Mid-Autumn Festival. The previous page established the setting and Mingshan’s initial poetic advances.</meta>

<margin>Mingshan uses his poetry to convey his hidden desires, just as Sima Xiangru once wooed a widow with his zither music.</margin>

Chang'e <note>The Goddess of the Moon; here the woman is being compared to her</note> took her own initiative. Sensing his intentions, she laughed and said, "Did your poem come drifting from the Imperial Moat?" <gloss>She is teasing him by referencing the legend of a scholar and a palace lady who exchanged poems on red leaves sent down the palace stream</gloss>. 

As they drew closer to one another, Mingshan could not restrain himself and recited another poem:

> "The dew is like light rain, the moon like frost,
> I see not the Silver River, but the rows of geese in flight. <gloss>The original pronunciation of 'row' here is *hang*</gloss>
> Faint halos sink into the pond's waves as the full moon turns,
> In the garden, the laurels are exceptionally fragrant.
> On this spring-like night, I gaze toward the Yellow Dragon Gate,
> The cloudy path surely separates us from the White Jade Palace.
> How can I get Chang'e to leave her lunar halls,
> To share this clear light with me in tender affection?"

Hearing the poem, the woman was even more deeply moved. Without waiting for a further invitation, they retired to bed together. The intimacy of their secret union was beyond words.

They remained together in joyful company for several days, until Mingshan suddenly thought of home and offered his farewells. The woman would not allow it. After several more days, he insisted on leaving. Realizing she could not keep him, she wept and said, "Once you leave, we shall not meet again. However, if business ever brings you back to this place, if you would not begrudge me a single visit, it would be my greatest wish."

Mingshan felt a mixture of regret and affection. The woman then composed the "Song of Heartbreak" to see him off, saying:

"The knight-errant of Wuling, <note>Wuling was a district for the wealthy elite in the Han dynasty, often used in poetry to describe stylish young men.</note>

<column-break/>

<margin>This "Song of Heartbreak" also cries out with a sorrow like the wailing of a mourning ape.</margin>

A youth in the days of the spring wind, in the markets of Chang'an. With a brocade saddle and jade bridle on a fine blue-black horse, <gloss>these are the grand trappings of a sturdy steed</gloss>. With a 'snake-shaped' bow and feathered arrows, wearing furs worth a thousand gold pieces, he seeks flowers and breaks willow branches along the Zhangtai Road <note>A famous district for courtesans and entertainment</note>. Surrounded by beauty and greenery, he boasts of his great wealth. He returns home to lie drunk behind tasseled curtains, and in his stupor, still remembers the 'Morning Cloud' song. The brothels only say that the spring light is beautiful, but by the orchid window, the rain beats down and the pear blossoms grow old." <gloss>This section is set to the *Zheng* melody</gloss>.

"By the river, the white waves and mist are woven like cloth; the boat departs and there is no more news. Last year there was a letter from Luoyang, but this year the letters only reach the Imperial Forest. I looked at the lamp last night and saw the 'gold millet' sparks <note>A superstition that sparks on a lamp wick foretold a visitor or good luck</note>; today in Chang'an, I ask for a divination by tortoise shell. I step out the door and cannot tell East from West; wandering silks and falling catkins are lost in the wind." <gloss>This section is set to the *Southern* melody</gloss>.

Mingshan and the woman wept bitterly and parted. After arriving home, there was not a night that he did not dream of her. In the autumn of the following year, Mingshan visited the spot again. When he arrived, he found only lush grass and tall forests; the landscape was a single shade of green as far as the eye could see. Only the old laurel trees lined the path, in full flower and nothing more. Where was the beauty of yesterday?

<summary>Mingshan and the woman (the spirit of a laurel tree) share a brief romance before he departs; returning a year later, he finds only the trees remaining. This page highlights the "scholar and beauty" trope infused with supernatural elements and the transience of love.</summary>

<keywords>Chang'e, laurel spirit, Di Mingshan, Heartbreak Song, Chang'an, Ganpu, botanical spirits, Ming dynasty fiction</keywords>
