<meta>This page begins a narrative concerning a supernatural encounter in the Kuaiji region during the Song Dynasty. It introduces the protagonist, Tao Xikan, and his meeting with a mysterious woman who is later revealed to be the spirit of a willow tree.</meta>

<margin>
The tale of the Demon Willow is truly extraordinary. Those who recorded it did so with great detail and elegant prose. I have therefore transcribed it here.
</margin>

# The Demon Willow of Kuaiji I

During the Xining era <note>The reign of Emperor Shenzong of the Song Dynasty, 1068–1077 AD, a period of significant political reform.</note>, there was a man named Tao Tuan, a native of Fuzhou. He was traveling to Xiuzhou to take up an official post and brought along his son, Tao Xikan, to pursue his studies. Xikan was handsome in appearance and possessed an eccentric, unconventional spirit. He delighted in wandering through mountains and rivers, finding joy in the company of flowers and wine. Often, he would chant poetry alone, his heart moved by the scenery; he possessed a grand ambition that transcended the world, viewing the pursuit of fame and official rank as beneath him. One day, his path took him through Kuaiji <gloss>The name of a mountain and historical region</gloss>. He moored his boat at the foot of the mountain. At that time, a gentle breeze stirred the woods and a pale moon shimmered upon the water. Xikan could not sleep. He rose and had walked only a few paces when a delightful fragrance wafted toward him. In the blink of an eye, a beautiful lady appeared before him. Startled, Tao asked, "Is this a dream? Or a haunting?" The spirit replied, "I admire your noble spirit, sir, and have come specifically to keep you company in your solitude." He asked how far her home was. She replied, "The rocky banks of the shore are but a few steps away; the green mountains are my master, and the water chestnuts and duckweed are my neighbors." Tao said, "Living alone in such a desolate place, do you not find it a rare treat to have a visitor?" She replied, "Not so. I see the moon off and welcome the wind—

<column-break/>

<margin>
The prose here is composed of parallel couplets, and the phrasing is quite excellent, leading the reader into a beautiful state of mind.
</margin>

—How can my dwelling be called solitary when orioles and swallows chatter? How can it be called desolate when the moon drifts across the misty waters? There is no place for gloom here. Why should I need to rely on a 'rare visitor'?" Tao smiled and took the lady's sleeve. They sat together in the moonlight, and he grew intimate with her. She did not refuse him. She then asked, "You travel by sail and trek on foot, busying yourself for what end? If you could stay here long, we could make a lasting vow." Tao said, "This place is indeed wonderful, but my father is going to his official post. He is strict and urges me to discipline myself; it would be difficult to abandon my duties." The lady sighed and said, "Do you not yet know? The Green Sprouts Law <term>The Green Sprouts Law (Qingmiao fa) was a controversial economic reform of the Song Dynasty intended to provide low-interest loans to farmers, but in practice, it often led to crushing debt and corruption.</term> is at its most extreme, and thorns and brambles block the path <gloss>A reference to the treacherous state of politics at the time</gloss>. Those who govern now long to flee into the forest. <margin>The following section consists of parallel phrases, frequently using rhymes and tones from the same phonetic category.</margin> Do you, then, wish to be a tree standing in the wind?" Tao said, "How clumsy of you to say so! Would you have me bury my light in the hills and valleys?" She replied, "Is he who moved the wood at the South Gate <note>original: "徙木南門"; a reference to the legalist reformer Shang Yang, symbolizing a career in government and law.</note> as leisurely as he who planted plum trees on Solitary Mountain <note>original: "種梅孤山"; a reference to the hermit poet Lin Bu, who lived a life of seclusion.</note>? Is he who gazes at flowers in Chang'an <note>original: "看花長安"; a metaphor for succeeding in the imperial examinations and achieving worldly fame.</note> as noble as he who plucks chrysanthemums beneath the fence <note>original: "摘菊籬下"; a reference to Tao Yuanming, the famous poet who abandoned his official post for a simple life.</note>? <gloss>A comparison between a life of reclusive "hiding" and public "revealing."</gloss> How can you insist that living among hills and valleys is not the work of a worthy man?" Tao said, "That may be true, but a gentleman fears his name will perish into obscurity." The lady...

<summary>Tao Xikan, a young man disinterested in official fame, encounters a beautiful female spirit in the mountains of Kuaiji. The spirit critiques the harsh political climate of the Song Dynasty and argues that a life of reclusion in nature is superior to the pursuit of political power.</summary>
<keywords>Tao Xikan, Demon Willow, Kuaiji, Song Dynasty, Green Sprouts Law, Hermit culture, Tao Yuanming, Lin Bu, Reclusion vs. Officialdom</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue between Tao Xikan and the Willow Spirit. They are discussing the fleeting nature of worldly success (fame and official rank) versus the enduring integrity of the recluse, using various historical and botanical metaphors.</meta>

->## Record of Affectionate Exchanges<-
->### Scroll IV<-

<margin>Their debate is inexhaustible, and their sentiments grow ever more poignant. None but those with the ethereal air of an immortal or the character of a Daoist sage could engage in such a conversation.</margin>

“...returning to beneath a rotting cliff,” she continued. 
Tao <note>Tao Xikan, the protagonist</note> said, “If it is as you say, how do you distinguish yourself from others?”
The spirit <note>original: 妖 (yāo), here referring to the Willow Spirit</note> replied, “How could there be any difference? One may attend the banquet in the Apricot Garden <note>A celebratory feast for those who passed the imperial examinations.</note> and join the ranks of the 'Peaches and Plums' <note>A metaphor for talented students or officials.</note> in the Ministry of Rites. This seems different from the life of one living in a humble hut amidst weeds and mugwort. Yet, once the 'Dream of the Southern Branch' <note>A famous literary metaphor for the fleeting nature of worldly success and fame.</note> ends, all that remains are withered grass, desolate brambles, morning mist, and evening rain. All eventually become the same earthen mound. Who then can distinguish the fine wood <gloss>Jia, a high-quality catalpa</gloss> from the common thorns? 

Tao said, “The world is currently scrambling for fame and merit; why do you look upon me with such lofty, detached expectations?” 
The spirit replied, “I simply wish to awaken you. Those who salivate after wealth and rank are no different from those who ‘quench their thirst by looking at plums’ <note>A reference to a story where soldiers were tricked into forgetting their thirst by being told of sour plums ahead; it signifies chasing illusions.</note>. Among those who recklessly pursue fame, who does not have the 'ambition of the pine and vine' <note>The desire for a long-lasting legacy or to cling to power.</note>? They believe their roots are deep and their foundations firm. How can they know that the ‘scenery of the mulberry and elm’ <note>A metaphor for the evening of life or old age.</note> is easily exhausted, or that the dew upon the grass dries quickly? Even as they feast and laugh in the central hall, the ‘chamber of the long night’ <gloss>the grave</gloss> is already being prepared for them. I fear for you.” 
Tao said, “Will I then be bound high above death?” 
The spirit replied, “Death is indeed unavoidable, but one must consider how one meets it. To spend one's mornings seeking plums by the well and evenings picking mallows in the fields—this is a life of ten thousand forms of labor, toil, alarm, and doubt. Even the temple cypress...”

<column-break/>

<margin>The Willow Spirit does not waste words; her arguments are multifaceted and her meaning is profound. Master Tao awakens, his dream of fame vanishes, and he finds contentment. Though his path may not align with the standard classics, he has reached the heart of one who fulfills his true will.</margin>

“...she continued, “...even if the 'Three Locusts' of the royal court and the 'Five Cassias' of the guests <note>Metaphors for high-ranking officials and successful family lineages.</note> are said to be fragrant and flourishing, before long, the wind and dew turn cold and desolate, and one by one they wither. In extreme cases, even the 'Big Tree' of the General <note>A reference to General Feng Yi of the Han Dynasty, known as the 'Big Tree General' for his humility; here the spirit notes that even the great eventually face the woodcutter’s axe.</note> meets the blade. How brief is the splendor! How complete the disappearance!” 
Tao said, “These are but matters encountered after death; why are they worth mentioning?” 
The spirit replied, “Consider the Four Elders who gathered mushrooms <note>Recluses who fled the chaos of the Qin Dynasty to Mount Shang.</note> and the two starving men who picked ferns <note>Bo Yi and Shu Qi, who chose starvation on Mount Shouyang rather than serve a new dynasty.</note>. I do not know how much time has passed since they lived, yet the excellence of Mount Shang and Mount Shouyang remains clear to this day, like the frosted pine and the snow-clad bamboo. Why is this?” 
Tao said, “These were sages. If an ordinary man meets no such fate, is his life not lived in vain?” 
The spirit replied, “This cannot be forced. Among our kind, there are those whose footsteps produce golden lotuses <note>A reference to the beauty of a consort's walk.</note> and those whose makeup resembles flying plum petals. Below them, there are those as common as 'steamed pears' or those who 'chase dates' only to be discarded. If even the relationship between husband and wife is thus, how much more so for a man’s career? Therefore, if Heaven grants me an encounter, I may become a pillar or a beam in a great temple. If Heaven does not grant it, I remain a useless tree <term>Ailanthus or Oak <note>References to the 'useless' trees in the Daoist text Zhuangzi, which survive because they are of no use to carpenters.</note></term> in the mud. Whether one is 'met' with opportunity is a matter of fate. Is it up to man? If it were not so, then how many strategies did the old man fishing on the Wei River <note>Jiang Ziya, who waited until age 80 to be discovered by King Wen of Zhou.</note> possess, and yet he was but one among the plants and <meta>catchword: trees</meta>

<summary>The Willow Spirit delivers a poignant sermon on the vanity of political ambition, comparing the fleeting nature of official success to the enduring spiritual purity of historical recluses. She argues that worldly status is a temporary "dream," while true character is like the "frosted pine" that withstands time.</summary>
<keywords>Willow Spirit, Tao Xikan, Nanke Dream, Mount Shang, Bo Yi and Shu Qi, fate vs. effort, Daoist philosophy, botanical metaphors</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue between Tao Xikan and the Willow Spirit. They have transitioned from discussing the vanity of fame to the specific emotional experience of reclusion versus worldly involvement, using deep botanical and historical metaphors.</meta>

-># Record of the Reincarnation in Qingzhou<-

<margin>
The narrative flows in three distinct stages, the spirit growing ever more intense, the talent and thought ever more expansive. It depicts emotions and circumstances to the utmost degree, yet there is a sense of helplessness in the underlying nature of things.
</margin>

“Who indeed provides for the cattle and sheep of Wang and Lü? <note>References to Wang Yan and Lü Shang, historical figures often associated with retreats or humble beginnings before being called to greatness.</note> While you reside here, is it out of sorrow or out of joy?”

The Spirit <note>original: 妖 (yāo); though often translated as demon, in this context it refers to the ethereal Willow Spirit</note> replied: “When the wind is biting and the rain cold, when the apricot blossoms are hurried and the peach blossoms fade, the mountain paths are desolate and sorrowful clouds stretch for ten miles. Amidst wild moss and crumbling bricks, the spirit is dampened and the soul fades—one cannot say there is no sorrow. Yet when the fragrant isle is sunny and warm, a cluster of emerald mist arises, and painted boats and jade-white steeds pass by while wine-flags flutter in reflection; or perhaps when seeing off the setting sun or greeting the new moon, while the evening cicadas let out a few cries and a wild bird sings, amidst ten thousand strands of spring light, the heart is delighted and the mind at ease. In such moments, one forgets that life must end. Who then could say there is no joy?”

The Scholar <note>Tao Xikan</note> laughed and said, “Joy is indeed joy, yet I lack a single soulmate who truly knows my heart.”

The Spirit also laughed and said, “For a long time I have held you in high regard <gloss>Original: 'blue eyes,' a literary term for showing favor or friendship</gloss>. I am not like other gentlemen. Thus, I did not avoid the suspicion of 'adjusting my hat under the plum tree' <note>Refers to the proverb 'Do not adjust your hat under a plum tree,' meaning to avoid even the appearance of impropriety.</note> and have come to this secret tryst in the mulberry grove.”

The Scholar took her hand and said, “My worldly heart is already shattered. I only regret that I cannot find a place to settle this body.”

The Spirit said, “That is not difficult. We should hide our names in the mountain ravines, look down and bind ourselves to the pines and vines, leaving our traces among the clouds and mists. Let our names be joined by the green waters. We shall live as woodsmen of the mountains and drinkers of the springs, companions to cranes and guests to gulls, following the high path of the fields of Shen <note>Shenye, the place where the legendary sage I Yin farmed before being called to serve.</note>.”

<column-break/>

<margin>
Every section follows the rules of composition, every sentence the rules of phrasing, and every word the rules of diction. The style is classical and elegant, as if the Master of Lake Dongting were competing with the greatest hands.
</margin>

“...becoming a dragon or waiting for the bamboo of Leiyang to move is ultimately useless. <note>References to legends of transformation and resonance that the author deems ultimately futile compared to true reclusion.</note> Moreover, such things may not even be attainable. If we instead take refuge like the Master of the Red Pine <note>Chisongzi, a legendary Daoist immortal who achieved transcendence.</note> or hide ourselves within the green oranges <note>A reference to a story of four immortals who lived inside a giant orange to play chess.</note>, eating the blossoms of chrysanthemums and wearing pendants of orchids, sharing dreams with monkeys and cranes, and finding common feeling with wood and stone—enjoying the ultimate joy between heaven and earth every day—could we truly compare our time to that of ordinary men? If we weigh a smile against death, which is a gain and which is a loss?”

The Scholar rejoiced and said, “I am enlightened! But how is it that you are so profoundly wise?”

The Spirit replied, “The willows of Zhangtai Terrace <note>A famous area for entertainment in ancient Chang'an, often symbolizing beautiful women or fleeting fame.</note> once lined the Han forbidden zones and the Sui embankments. I am not like the willows at the Gate of Baling <note>A place where people traditionally parted ways, often associated with the sorrow of separation.</note>. Those who ask after me are few, while those who follow the flames of the world are many. Therefore, I have made this place my temporary lodging.”

The Scholar asked, “Do you have brothers?”

The Spirit replied, “After the purple thorn tree was cut down, the beans were burned to cook the beans <note>A reference to Cao Zhi's famous 'Seven Steps Verse,' a metaphor for siblings harming one another.</note>. Who is there to cherish the roots or pity the branches?”

The Scholar said, “Why not seek a friend?”

The Spirit replied, “The 'Golden Orchid' bond <note>A metaphor for a deep, unbreakable friendship.</note> has been severed, and the pursuit of power and profit has become the trend. When those who carry thorns to apologize <note>A reference to General Lian Po, who carried thorns to show his sincere apology to a friend.</note> meet the green pines, even they lose their color. Where else should I seek?”

The Scholar said, “If that is so, then one might as well cut oneself off from humanity.”

The Spirit said, “The imperial court lacks fine pillars <term>良幹 (liáng gàn): capable officials who support the state like the pillars of a building</term> capable of their tasks, and the prefectures lack the 'Sweet Pear' trees <note>A reference to Duke Shao of Zhou, whose benevolent rule was so loved that the people protected the sweet pear tree he rested under.</note> that spread grace. They flock toward the 'Tall Trees' <note>喬木 (qiáomù): a metaphor for people of high status or ancient families.</note> that stand as tall as mediocre men, only to be trapped under their robes...”

<summary>This page records a philosophical exchange where the Willow Spirit persuades the scholar to abandon the corrupt and fragmented world of men for a life of reclusion. She uses various botanical metaphors to critique the lack of true friendship, family loyalty, and effective governance in the human realm.</summary>
<keywords>reclusion, Willow Spirit, Tao Xikan, sisterhood/brotherhood, political critique, nature, Daoism, friendship</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Tao Xikan and the Willow Spirit. It transitions from the Spirit’s argument for a life of reclusion to the Scholar’s failed attempt to bring her into his professional life, ending with their final parting and the Scholar’s eventual retirement.</meta>

<columns>
<column-break/>

<margin>
Reading this passage, one feels the lofty spirit of the pines and firs. It stands in stark contrast to those who serve in the courts of the Huai River, merely seeking the rewards of office.
</margin>

...a lone plow below, continuing the singular thread of the Tong River <note>The Tong River is famously associated with the hermit Yan Ziling, who refused to serve the Emperor.</note>; a landscape of flowers and mountains, where frosted grass celebrates its season. One’s spirit escapes into the pines and firs, while daily the heart flies toward the orchids and cassia <note>Symbols of noble character and official success, respectively</note>. This life not only surpasses the famous Bamboo Grove <note>The Seven Sages of the Bamboo Grove, famous 3rd-century recluses</note>, but rivals the fragrance of the Peach Blossom Spring itself. Why must one pant with longing for the pavilions of the Purple Myrtle <note>A reference to the Secretariat or high government office</note>, or demean oneself by leaning against the gates of the "Yellow Brambles"? <note>A metaphor for the constraints and indignities of official life.</note> Why allow the reins and shackles <term>韁鎖 (jiāng suǒ): a common metaphor for the burdens of a career in the imperial bureaucracy</term> of emotion and the fetters of the world to shame and entangle one’s hands and feet?"

The Scholar, seeing that she was so widely learned and possessed such a delicate, alluring beauty, felt certain she was of immortal stock. He sought her affection, and they harmonized together in joy until the rooster crowed three times at dawn. As he prepared to depart, the Scholar asked for her name. The Spirit replied:

"No need to pull my sleeves to ask for my name;
My hidden feelings have long belonged to these slender branches.
At the deserted peaks of King Wu’s Mountain,
How many times have I danced in the wind with a slender waist?"

The Scholar was so drowned in his desire that he did not even say goodbye to his former life, but departed. The next night she returned, and the Scholar hid her within his boat, intending to take her with him to his official post. The Spirit was displeased and said, "The reason I humbly offered my 'rush-like' beauty <note>A self-deprecating term for a fragile woman</note> to you was because I truly wished to help you open the 'Green Field Hall,' to establish a 'White Lotus Society,' to gather the herbs of Wu’an, and to plant the melons of Shao Ping." <note>These are all allusions to famous retreats and the humble, self-sufficient lives of retired officials.</note>

<column-break/>

<margin>
The hidden evil is covered, yet the true emotion is revealed. Born for this moment, the spirit stands revealed. It is a great pity in the end. The white hair and black silk shine like jade. Looking back at the Spirit and Master Tao, how do they compare?
</margin>

"If you are like the clouds reflected in the lake water, how could you willfully step upon a dangerous branch and shake it down for the sake of others?" <note>She warns him that by pursuing his career, he is risking his life and her presence.</note> The Scholar could not contain his grief and pleaded with her earnestly, even weeping.

When they reached Xiufu, the Scholar fell so ill that he nearly did not survive. Fearing there was a malevolent haunting, Master Fan <note>A Buddhist priest</note> was called to set up an altar and perform a healing ritual. At first, the Spirit paid no mind. However, once the Master hung an image of the Bodhisattva Guanyin and transcribed the Secret Divine Mantra of the Shurangama Sutra <note>A powerful Buddhist text used for protection and exorcism</note>, the Spirit prepared to leave. Weeping, she said to the Scholar:

"I have traveled with you for a long time in order to increase your lifespan; I was never haunting you. Now, we part forever." She composed a poem and wept:

"The twenty-first day of mid-winter is the fated hour;
Upon the river, our bond was destined for this time.
Today at the parting path, we share a cup of wine;
With you a thousand miles away, my longing will remain."

Then she vanished and was never seen again. The Scholar’s illness soon recovered. Only then did he realize she was the Spirit of a Willow. Reflecting on her words, he changed his name to Xijing <gloss>Seeking Tranquility</gloss>. He no longer sought official advancement but returned home, where it is said he enjoyed a very long life.

</columns>

<summary>The Willow Spirit attempts to persuade Scholar Tao to choose a life of reclusion over his career, but he tries to force her into his worldly life. After a Buddhist exorcism, the Spirit reveals she was actually a guardian of his health before departing forever, leading Tao to finally retire in peace.</summary>
<keywords>Willow Spirit, reclusion vs. officialdom, Shurangama Mantra, Peach Blossom Spring, retirement, exorcism, Tao Xikan, Guanyin</keywords>

### Wood Spirits Seeking Life

<columns>

**The Four Elders seek peace; let the imperial halls have no need for them as beams and pillars.** <term>棟宇 (dòng yǔ): literally "ridgepoles and roofs," referring to large timbers required for monumental architecture like palaces.</term>

<column-break/>

<margin>

</margin>

**With a single word, they found protection; since then, Mount Chang has weathered the wind and frost.** <note>Mount Chang (Changshan) is a site often associated with spirits and ancient forests. "Wind and frost" refers to the natural aging process of a tree in the wild, which is preferable to being cut down for lumber.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates a legendary encounter where four ancient tree spirits, appearing as "Four Elders," petition an official to spare them from being harvested for palace construction. It emphasizes the Ming Dynasty's fascination with the hidden spiritual lives of nature and the virtue of the official who grants them mercy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the spiritual essence of nature, specifically trees. While the previous page touched upon the romantic encounter of Tao Xikan with a willow spirit, this entry shifts to a more formal, literary encounter where ancient trees take the form of "Retired Scholars" to plead for their survival against human encroachment.</meta>

<header>Speeches from the Qian River, Volume 5</header>

<margin>Poetry is sometimes chanted with a specific purpose or feeling; these words are for when a gentleman knows when to stop and is content.</margin>

# Strange Trees of Mount Chang

-><-

In the fifth year of the Yongle reign <note>1407 AD</note>, there lived a man named Shao Shipeng in Mount Chang. He was a man of great wealth. He and a fellow villager named Wang Ruoxu had formed a bond as relatives through the marriage of their children, and both were renowned for their immense fortunes. One evening, Shipeng was sitting at a banquet; as the night reached its midpoint, he became relaxed and spirited with wine. The waning moon hung slanted in the sky, and the sound of his wandering songs echoed through the air, so startling that the dragons in the pools might have risen. Finding no end to his mood, he loudly recited a poem in the regulated style:

> My life’s ambitions have already gone astray,
> Yet with a jug of wine, I pass through this place once more.
> <gloss>Near the flat thicket</gloss> <note>original: "ping chu," referring to low-lying woods</note> I first notice yellow leaves falling,
> Looking south, I see only the vast white clouds. <gloss>On Mount Chang</gloss>
> Man is about to grow old,
> While by the cold shoals, the water ripples year after year.
> Meeting like this with gray hair,
> What will it be like when the autumn grass returns?

After he finished reciting, he poured himself another drink. His nature felt increasingly bold and unconstrained, and he could not contain his excitement. Suddenly, a head servant reported: "There is someone knocking at the gate." Shipeng ordered him to ask for their names. The servant <term>Cangtou (蒼頭): literally "green-head," a common term for a domestic servant in old texts</term> returned and reported: "Four elders say they seek an audience." Shipeng granted it. The servant opened the gate and led them in. He saw only four 

<column-break/>

<margin>The narration is so lifelike it possesses a strange sentiment, following the stylistic tradition of the Grand Historian. <note>The "Grand Historian" refers to Sima Qian, the father of Chinese historiography known for his vivid character sketches.</note></margin>

old men. Their forms were lean, their long beards were elegant and refined, their complexions were dark, and their clothing was green. They sat down without waiting to be asked. Their speech was hesitant and slow, yet their gait was steady and dignified, even without the use of walking sticks. They bowed to Shipeng and said: "We are the four retired scholars: Pine, Juniper, Cypress, and Scholastic Tree. Unforeseen danger has arisen within our own walls <term>Xiaoqiang (蕭墻): an idiom for internal trouble or danger close at hand</term>, and we have come to seek your protection. Thus, we did not decline to come in the darkness of night." Shipeng sat with them and inquired further. The four elders said: "We are neighbors, and your relative Wang Ruoxu’s family has already lived there for many generations. But recently, he listened to the words of petty men and intends to bring felling axes against us. We have heard that to restore what is ruined and continue what is broken is the fundamental heart of a gentleman; to support the falling and hold up the endangered is the lifelong ambition of a person of humanity. We humbly hope you will use your influence—powerful enough to move the heavens—to allow the four of us to live out our natural spans. We shall certainly think of how to repay this debt of gratitude, even from beyond the grave <note>original: "xianjie," a reference to historical legends where spirits return to "tether the horses" or "knit grass" to help their benefactors</note>." Shipeng said: "In that case, what would you have me do?" The four elders said: "If we can obtain but a single word from you to mediate, he will surely comply. If he complies, we shall be free from worry." Shipeng said: "I agree." The four elders leaped for joy. Each recited a poem in turn before departing. The Retired Scholar Pine recited first:

<vocab>Mount Chang (常山), Shao Shipeng (邵十朋), Wang Ruoxu (王若虚), Speeches from the Qian River (汧言), Restoring the ruined and continuing the broken (興滅継絶), Internal danger (蕭墻), Retired Scholar (處士), Repaying gratitude from the grave (銜結報謝)</vocab>

<summary>A wealthy man named Shao Shipeng is visited by the spirits of four ancient trees (Pine, Juniper, Cypress, and Scholastic Tree) who appear as elderly scholars. They plead for his intervention to stop his relative from cutting them down, illustrating the Ming Dynasty literary theme of nature possessing a moral and spiritual voice.</summary>
<keywords>tree spirits, Mount Chang, Ming Dynasty folklore, environmental ethics, Shao Shipeng, Four Elders, Pine spirit, supernatural encounter</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the story of Shao Shipeng’s encounter with four mysterious elderly men who are the spiritual manifestations of trees. Following the previous page's introduction of the banquet, this section presents the poems recited by the four "Retired Scholars" (representing the Pine, Juniper, Cypress, and Scholar Tree) as they plead for their lives, ending with the revelation that Wang Ruoxu intended to cut them down for timber.</meta>

<margin>
Every poem is marvelous and full of flavor.
</margin>

Towering high, reaching a hundred rods toward the heavens. Through the four seasons, they remain a deep, ancient green. Their needles are thick and lush like hair; their bark is gnarled like dragon scales, proudly braving the snow and frost. Planted in the earth, their fragrant resin congeals into amber. The wind carries their clear rhythm, playing like the pipes and strings of an orchestra. Their great timber should not be felled by a common craftsman, but rather reserved to serve as the pillars and beams for a royal palace. <gloss>Poem on the Pine</gloss> The Retired Scholar of the Juniper chanted: Massive and gnarled, it leans high against the edge of the five-colored clouds. Its dense leaves dance in the breeze, forming a canopy of emerald green. Its great stature is content to age alongside heaven and earth; its deep color does not fear the cold of snow or frost. In its shade, white cranes lodge beside the clouds; in the moonlight, its shadow moves like a coiled blue dragon. It carries the misty atmosphere of the mountains and rivers, and a sense of autumn chill presses upon the poet’s terrace. <gloss>Poem on the Juniper</gloss> The Retired Scholar of the Cypress chanted: Its roots have been coiled in the deep earth since primordial times. Reaching for the clouds and grasping at the mist, its branches are twisted like young dragons. It does not die in the snow or frost, for its "true heart" <note>A pun on "xin," which means both the center of the wood and the spirit/intent of the tree.</note> remains. In the rain and dew, it always holds a wondrous, dark green hue. Though it has not yet been sought as timber for the halls of government, its dragon-and-tiger-like stature grows old in the empty forests. It stands solemn and still as if before the Censorate <note>The Censorate was historically called the "Cypress Terrace" (Baitai) because these trees were planted there to symbolize integrity.</note>, while at night it lies beneath the full, icy wheel of the moon. <gloss>Poem on the Cypress</gloss> The Retired Scholar of the Pagoda Tree <note>Also known as the Chinese Scholar Tree (Huai).</note> chanted: Since ancient times, the royal favor toward us

<column-break/>

<margin>
The Pine, Juniper, Cypress, and Pagoda Tree are but four types of wood, yet they know to seek life. This desire for life and hatred of death is such that humans should feel ashamed by comparison.
</margin>

has been long-lasting. Grown into old trees, their color is a deep, vast green. Their verdant branches densely cover the market of the Confucian officials; their yellow blossoms busily urge the scholars to prepare their luggage <note>The blooming of the Pagoda tree coincided with the civil service examinations.</note>. Ants dream of an empire within the holes of the trunk during the long days, and the sound of cicadas beneath the leaves bids farewell to the setting sun. When shall I gather your seeds to eat? I wish to learn the divine immortals’ recipe for eternal youth. When the chanting ended, the four elders bowed deeply to express their thanks and then departed. The following day, Shipeng kept his appointment and went to Ruoxu’s house. He related in detail the words the four elders had entrusted to him, along with their poems. Deep down, he intended to offer comfort and mediation, hoping Ruoxu would comply. Ruoxu said in shock: "I have counted my neighbors <gloss>The word 'count' (shu) is read in the rising tone</gloss> and I do not know of any four 'Retired Scholars' named Pine, Juniper, Cypress, or Pagoda Tree." After contemplating for a long time, he finally slapped his thigh and sighed: "Alas! So that is it. I was planning to construct a small hall and intended to fell the four trees—the Pine, Juniper, Cypress, and Pagoda Tree—in my back garden to use as beams. I have not yet carried it out, but the spirits of these things have already sensed it. Truly, this is extraordinary! I cannot bear to cut them now." The matter was thereafter laid to rest.

<summary>Four ancient trees, appearing as elderly scholars, recite poems to Shao Shipeng to express their spiritual value and desire to live. Upon hearing this, Wang Ruoxu realizes the "scholars" are the trees in his own garden and cancels his plans to cut them down.</summary>

# The Old Spirits of Chuyang's Woods

In the region of Chuzhou <note>modern-day Anhui province</note>, there lived a scholar named Tuizheng. Since his youth, he had followed his family’s teachings and was well-versed in poetry and the classical rites. As an adult, he devoted himself to the "garden of letters" <term>藝壜 (yì tán): literally an altar of art, referring to the community of scholars and literature</term>. During the long summer days, he would study until exhausted; when the weather was mild, he would often stroll through the fragrant meadows. One day, while wandering near a villa in the West Village, he became so captivated by the flowers and scenery that he forgot his fatigue. Just as he prepared to turn back, he saw the sun sinking behind the mountain ridges and heard the long cries of apes. He realized he had lost his way. As the sky grew dim and dark, he could no longer distinguish the path, and his heart began to feel deeply unsettled. Soon after, five old men appeared, leaning on walking staffs. Seeing Tuizheng’s panicked expression, they laughed and asked, "Are you imitating the one who wept at the end of the road? <gloss>Referring to the poet Ruan Ji, who famously wept when his carriage reached a dead end.</gloss> Or are you mimicking the joy of walking under the moon? <gloss>Referring to the scholar Zhang Sheng from the 'Romance of the Western Chamber'.</gloss>" Tuizheng replied, "The path ahead is lost, and I am stranded in this desolate wilderness. Owls screech above and apes wail below. I have only wood and stone for companions, and ghosts for neighbors. How do I know I will not end up dead in a ditch? This is why I say I must weep."

<column-break/>

<margin>
Tuizheng describes his own unease and lack of joy, yet he possesses a grand spirit that rises far above the common sort.
</margin>

"And yet," Tuizheng continued, "as the mist clears and the bright moon shines for a thousand miles, a cool breeze brings refreshment. The tall trees cast protective shadows. In this solitude, my singing echoes through the cold valley, and the mountain bells and wild flutes offer me their beautiful sounds. I feel a sense of transcendence, as if both my body and the world are forgotten. In such a state, how could I know the road is at an end? This is why one cannot say I lack joy." The elders laughed and said, "Joy is indeed a fine thing, but to cast a precious life into such a dangerous place at night is not the choice of a wise man." Tuizheng asked, "What then should I do?" The elders replied, "Our humble farm is but a short distance from here. We invite you to join us for a night of conversation." Tuizheng thanked them and followed. After walking less than half a mile, they saw a thatched hut nestled within a thicket; this was the elders' dwelling. The five elders invited Tuizheng to sit upon the ground. When he asked for their names, they replied, "We are called the Five Recluses of the Mountain Villa. We have no surnames to speak of. We are nurtured by the cultivation of Heaven and Earth and sustained by the nourishment of creation. We can provide a perch for phoenixes <note>original: 棲鸞 (qī luán) - to host the mythical simurgh or phoenix, a metaphor for harboring great talent</note> and we can raise silkworms; yet we think only of..."

<summary>A scholar named Tuizheng becomes lost in the mountains at twilight and encounters five mysterious elders who challenge his fear and invite him to their secluded hut. The story blends classical literary allusions with a supernatural encounter, suggesting the elders are personifications of the forest itself.</summary>
<keywords>Tuizheng, Chuzhou, West Village, Ruan Ji, Five Recluses, nature spirits, mountain journey, scholar-beauty genre</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page where the scholar Gu Zheng (referred to as Tuizheng) met five mysterious elders in the mountains of Chuzhou after losing his way at dusk. The elders have just begun to engage him in conversation.</meta>

"I am old and can no longer be of use," one said. 
Another elder replied, "Even when fine timber <note>original: 'Qi and Zi' (杞梓), referring to the high-quality wood of the willow and catalpa trees often used as a metaphor for people of great talent</note> is so large that several men must link arms to encircle it, there may still be a few feet of rot. A master craftsman does not discard us because of that. Since we are not truly useless wood, why should we be discarded?" 
Yet another elder said, "Why must we chatter on about the past? Why not each of us recite a poem for the pleasure of this man of letters?" Thereupon, the first elder began to chant:

<margin>The five elders do not reveal their surnames, yet their intentions are already contained within their poems; this is revealed in the words that follow.</margin>

### The Paulownia Tree
My straight trunk towers over the old misty forest; 
It must be that I have been nurtured through many long years.
In the bright moonlight, a phoenix comes to roost upon my branches; <gloss>Theme: Paulownia</gloss> 
In the clear breeze, a single cicada sings beneath my leaves. 
Yellow leaves flutter by the golden well, hastening the autumn colors; <note>The 'golden well' (金井) is a poetic term for a well decorated with a railing, often used to evoke autumn imagery.</note> 
Emerald shade covers the silver garden bed as the noon shadows fall. 
Do not let the woodsman come with his axe to fell me; 
Save this fine material to be crafted into a precious jade zither <term>瑤琴 (yáoqín): a legendary type of zither, often associated with immortals</term>.

The next elder chanted:

### The Maple Tree
The Heavens reveal a beautiful scene that no painting could match; 
As the jade-like dew fades, the leaves turn entirely red. <gloss>Theme: Maple</gloss> 
A few trees stand desolate amidst the autumn rain; 
A thousand groves glow brilliantly in the setting sun. 
The sky of Wu seems as if filled with drifting rosy clouds; <note>Wu and Chu refer to ancient regions of southern and central China.</note> 
The peaks of Chu appear as if scorched by a wildfire. 
Looking back, one hardly notices the light and scenery changing, 
Until the leaves idly wither and scatter, chasing the west wind.

<margin>One elder spoke of the Plum</margin> <note>While the margin mentions the Plum, the poem that follows describes the Willow.</note>
An elder chanted:
The secrets of spring's beauty leak out just as the winter ends; 
Mist and fog entwine ten thousand 

<column-break/>

<margin>These poems are elegant and refined; if one ponders them, their deeper meaning will naturally emerge.</margin>

hanging branches. Thread-like strands are woven where birds sing of spring; 
The trees seem to sob as the dark cicadas buzz on evening boughs. <gloss>Theme: Willow</gloss> 
Before the district of Pengze, the blossoms are like snow; <note>A reference to the poet Tao Yuanming, who was the magistrate of Pengze and famously loved willows.</note> 
Inside the Weiyang Palace, the leaves are shaped like slender eyebrows. 
A message for those holding wine on the road to Yang Pass: 
Do not break my tender branches to give as parting gifts. <note>In ancient China, breaking a willow branch was a traditional gesture when seeing off a friend.</note>

Another elder said:
Around a five-acre homestead, I am planted along the walls; 
My supple branches and lush leaves are a deep, verdant green. <gloss>Theme: Mulberry</gloss> 
Dense shade lines the paths, lost in a blue mist; 
Emerald shadows stretch across the fields in the evening sun. 
The colorful pheasants are not startled, thanks to the ruler’s virtue; 
The silkworms have just matured, feasting on my fragrance. 
Deep in the hollow, golden rings were found; <note>A reference to a legend where a boy saved a bird and was rewarded with golden rings, representing high office.</note> 
The children of the neighboring house know our surname is Yang <term>羊 (Yáng): a surname that sounds identical to the word for 'poplar' or 'willow' (楊)</term>.

An elder chanted:
My straight trunk reaches the clouds, a heap of emerald green; 
The old families never tire of planting me throughout their courtyards. <gloss>Theme: Bamboo</gloss> 
A single stalk, elegant and free, dances with the kites; 
Ten thousand leaves sway gracefully, inviting the phoenix to come. 
My sturdy joints do not compete with the bright beauty of spring; 
With a humble, hollow heart, I am already the leader of the cold season. <note>The 'hollow heart' refers to the bamboo's hollow center, a metaphor for modesty and open-mindedness.</note> 
When shall I be cut as a long rod 
To pull the Golden Ao from the surface of the sea? <note>The Golden Ao is a giant mythical turtle from Chinese mythology.</note>

When the poems were finished, dawn broke and the five elders scattered and vanished. Gu Zheng found himself sitting alone in the middle of a thicket; he returned home, filled with wonder and amazement.

<summary>Five elderly spirits, each personifying a different tree (Paulownia, Maple, Willow, Mulberry, and Bamboo), recite poems to a lost scholar before vanishing at dawn. The encounter serves as an allegory for the hidden talents and enduring virtues of nature and the elderly.</summary>

# Cultivating Truth by the Si River

In the district of Sizhou, there lived a man named Bei Shitai. His ancestors belonged to a great and wealthy household, with assets totaling millions. However, due to his addiction to gambling and his spreading debauchery, his family finances dwindled day by day. In less than ten years, his money pouches were as empty as if they had been washed clean. Only when he reached the end of his resources did he begin to reflect, and a heart for goodness was born. Yet, by then, it was too late. He gave himself a sharp warning and, refusing to lower himself to beg at the "vermilion gates" <note>original: 'zhùmén' (朱門), a term for the wealthy/aristocracy</note> of the rich, he entered the Temple of the Heavenly Canopy to cultivate the Way. After several years, he had achieved nothing, and his heart felt heavy with frustration.

He once composed a poem to express his feelings:
*The time before my eyes flees; who can chase it back?*
*For half a life, the dust of the world has stained my traveler’s robes.*
*I should grow old with the ferns of my native mountains in the autumn wind,*
*Yet I think deeply: where is the iron rod I meant to grind into a needle?* <note>A reference to the proverb about persistence: "Grinding an iron pillar into a needle."</note>
*Mistakenly, I head toward the Canglang River to ask the fishing pier.*

Whenever he felt overcome by emotion, he would express it through verse in this manner. One day, a Realized Person <term>真人 (zhēnrén): a Daoist title for an enlightened being or "True Man"</term> suddenly arrived at the temple. He called himself "Master Non-Action" <note>original: 'Wúwéi Zǐ' (無為子), referencing the Daoist principle of effortless action</note>. His speech and movements were exceptionally heroic and refined. Shitai held him in high regard.

<column-break/>

<margin>
Those who die for profit often use clever words to form alliances. How can the foolish be warned? What is the so-called Way? What is the so-called "Cultivating the Way"? If it is merely like those fellows in blue turbans and tattered robes, then that is just another name for them. The loss of shame and the grief in the heart are even more extreme here.
</margin>

### Volume Four, Page Fifty

At this, Shitai invited him to sit and asked, "I have the ambition to reach the higher realms. My pure cultivation and bitter austerities have been many, yet I am ultimately blocked from the gate of entering the Way. What does the Master have to say about this?"

The Realized Person replied, "Alas! That which is called the Way is like refined gold or beautiful jade, without a single flaw or blemish. Now, though you practice 'pure cultivation,' your heart is truly not yet at peace. Though you practice 'bitter austerities,' your will is truly not yet sincere. How can you succeed? Furthermore, you rely on yourself—can you truly know the harm of profit and desire? Can you truly forbid yourself from pursuing them?" <gloss>An instruction to examine the rising thoughts of the mind.</gloss>

Thereupon, from behind the inkstone, the Master plucked a hibiscus, a cockscomb flower, a scholar-tree flower, and an orchid. He placed them on the pillow and told Shitai to rest for a moment. Shitai did as he was told. In a moment, he fell into a deep sleep and dreamed he had come to a place with vermilion doors and emerald curtains—a magnificent estate. An old man came out and welcomed Shitai into the house. They reached the back hall to drink tea and converse. Following this, the old man ordered four daughters to come out and meet him. They were all of peerless beauty. Each introduced herself: the eldest was named Hibiscus, the second was Cockscomb, the third was Scholar-Tree Blossom, and the youngest was Orchid Fragrance. That night, Shitai...

<summary>Scholar Bei Shitai, having lost his fortune to vice, attempts to seek Daoist enlightenment but struggles with his lingering worldly attachments. A mysterious Master named Wuwei Zi initiates a magical test by placing four flowers on Bei's pillow, sending him into a vivid dream of a wealthy estate and four beautiful women.</summary>

<keywords>Bei Shitai, Master Non-Action, Daoist cultivation, repentance, dream-vision, floral symbolism, Sizhou, spiritual test</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Bei Shitai. Having entered a temple to seek the Way but finding no success, he is met by a "Real Man" (a Daoist immortal or perfected master) who subjects him to a dream-test to reveal the emptiness of his worldly desires.</meta>

# Collection of Instructional Words: Volume 4

<margin>
Even poems composed within a dream can offer insight to those who appreciate such things.
</margin>

Shitai stayed in a small study, but before he could even settle down for sleep, four young women secretly came to visit him. In the dream, he felt an immediate and profound intimacy with them. 

The **Hibiscus** <note>original: 'Fúróng' (芙蓉). In this context, it refers to the Woody Hibiscus which blooms in autumn.</note> recited a poem:
*Verdant clouds of hair and a face of cinnabar, she has the grace of a water nymph;*
*She ranks alongside the Peony, the King of Flowers.*
*Wealth and status cannot corrupt her; though many flowers bloom in the third month,*
*She chooses to reveal her splendor in the depths of late autumn.*
*Her roots willingly endure the seasons of wind and frost,*
*For her colors are all derived from the craftsmanship of Nature itself.*
*One might suspect she comes from a royal banquet at the Qujiang River,*
*Where the King’s attendants lie intoxicated amidst a thicket of silks.*

The **Cockscomb** recited:
*My stem rises several feet high beside the eaves and pillars;*
*I have long been famous for mimicking the crest of a rooster.*
*Heavy with rain, I droop as if drinking or pecking at the ground;*
*Lifted high by the wind, I seem ready to take flight.*
*I do not wither or fall, an old spirit of cinnabar;*
*Neither trimmed nor planted, yet bright as purple brocade.*
*Even if the Goddess of the Moon should pity my transcendent beauty,*
*In the vast, cold Palace of the Moon, there is no soil for me to take root.*

The **Sophora** <note>original: 'Huáiyīng' (槐英). The Sophora or "Scholar Tree" is traditionally associated with the imperial examinations.</note> recited:
*A hundred feet tall and upright, clothed in a dark, shimmering green;*
*Where the west wind blows, the entire forest turns a golden yellow.*
*My high and low branches cast dense shadows, keeping the palace courtyards silent,*
*But my falling blossoms urge the scholars to their busy tasks.* <note>The blooming of the Sophora coincided with the autumn imperial examinations.</note>
*The lingering song of the cicadas cries out in the evening light,*
*While the bees’ affections turn and linger as the day fades—when will I be plucked and taken into the Imperial Palace?*

<column-break/>

<margin>
Lust for beauty and a craving for wealth are common human emotions; so it was for Shitai, even in the midst of his dreams.
</margin>

The **Orchid** <note>original: 'Lánxiāng' (蘭香)</note> recited:
*Cut from the elegant rocks to adorn a fragrant cluster;*
*How would I dare to rank myself among the common weeds?*
*My beauty spills forth like crimson cinnabar condensed from auspicious dew;*
*My fragrance pulls at the dark green leaves, floating on the sunlit breeze.*
*My refined nature displays a beautiful character beneath the courtyard of the Great Teacher;* <note>original: 'Xiètíng' (謝庭). A reference to the Xie family, synonymous with talented youth, or more broadly to a refined household.</note>
*I secretly send my hidden scent through the fields of Chu.* <note>The "fields of Chu" (Chǔ wǎn) refers to the classic imagery of orchids in the poetry of Qu Yuan.</note>
*If the Great Master <note>original: 'Fūzǐ' (夫子), referring to Confucius.</note> were not present in these times,*
*Who would pass down my elegant melody to be played on the ancient lute?*

Shitai was delighted by their talent and beauty. A heart of greed suddenly flared within him. He enticed the four women to steal the family’s treasures, and at the fifth watch <note>The time just before dawn, roughly 3:00 AM to 5:00 AM.</note>, they fled. They had traveled only a few miles when a crowd of pursuers arrived. They bound Shitai and took him before the Magistrate. Upon interrogation, the truth was revealed. The judge sentenced him to be executed in the public market for the crimes of adultery and theft. 

As Shitai faced the executioner’s blade, he was overcome with immense grief. His whole body broke out in a heavy sweat. Suddenly, he woke up. He found himself still lying on his pillow. 

The **Perfected Master** <note>original: 'Zhēnrén' (真人), a term for a Daoist who has achieved spiritual perfection.</note> smiled and asked, "These matters of profit and desire—are they joyful, or are they harmful?"

Shitai replied, "In my heart, I now know their harm."

The Perfected Master then instructed him in the Sacred Law. After a year of practice, Shitai achieved the Way and departed.

<summary>In a vivid dream-vision, Bei Shitai is tempted by four personified flowers into theft and adultery, leading to his "execution." Upon waking, he realizes the emptiness of worldly desires and successfully commits himself to Daoist cultivation.</summary>
<keywords>Bei Shitai, dream-vision, flower personification, poetry, Daoism, moral trial, transcendence, temptation, repentance</keywords>

<margin>
The verses here serve as a dialogue between the dreamer and the apparitions, reflecting the seductive yet illusory nature of the world.
</margin>

<image-desc>A woodblock illustration depicting a man and a woman in an interior setting. The man, on the left, wears a scholar’s cap and long robes, holding a folding fan. Leaving her traces at Mount Wu, drenched in rain; for whom does she trail her dark-green hues? <note>original: 'Wū Shān' (巫山). Mount Wu is a famous literary site associated with the "Goddess of Mount Wu," who met a king in a dream, making it a classic metaphor for fleeting romantic or supernatural encounters. The 'dark-green hues' (dài sè) refer to the pigment used for painting eyebrows, symbolizing a woman's desire to please.</note>

## Seeking Refuge at Mount Wu

In the year 1334 <note>The *Yuantong* era of the Yuan Dynasty</note>, in Guizhou, there was a <term>Defense Commissioner</term><note>A military official rank (*fangyu*)</note> named Mi, whose personal name was Jimin. His family was exceedingly wealthy, and though he was over forty years old, he found it difficult to produce an heir. Although he had several concubines, none had "joined his dreams" <note>An allusion to conceiving a child</note> to provide him with the "blessings of the crickets" <note>A classical reference to having many children, from the *Classic of Poetry*</note>. The Commissioner was deeply troubled by this. One day, while sitting quietly in his hall,

<margin>The old man leads the maiden through the dark of night to seek lodging; one wonders if he came because he knew the master's character was upright.</margin>

a subordinate reported that an old man had arrived with a young lady and requested an audience. Surprised, the Commissioner went to meet them personally. He saw that the maiden’s appearance was as translucent as polished jade, without a single flaw. Her manner was graceful and swaying, like a weeping willow dancing in a drunken breeze. Her eyebrows were curved like the new moon hanging in the sky, and her eyes shimmered like ripples on an autumn lake. She was a peerless beauty. Feeling great delight in his heart, he invited them to sit and asked, "From which family do you come, elder?" The old man replied, "On the sunny south side of Mount Wu, where ancient trees grow lush and green—that is where my house stands." The Commissioner asked, "What brings you here?" The old man said, "Clashing weapons are everywhere, and the people suffer slaughter. We have fled and hope only for a place to stay." The Commissioner granted his request. After sitting for a long time, he spoke to the old man in a relaxed manner: "My house has many

<column-break/>

concubines, but few children. Would you be willing, elder, to entrust your daughter to me? If, by some chance, she should continue my lineage, she would enjoy all the wealth and honor of this house. What could be more fortunate?" The old man rejoiced and said, "I am poor and have no sons; I cannot provide for myself and have only this girl. She is not yet <gloss>betrothed</gloss>. Since you look upon her with such favor, I am willing for her to take up the 'basket and broom' <note>A humble term for becoming a wife or concubine</note>." The Commissioner shouted with joy and ordered a banquet to be prepared to welcome them. The old man said, "My daughter’s name is Ruixiang <note>Literally "Auspicious Fragrance"</note>. She is skilled at singing and poetry. Might I ask you to set a theme, so that her composition may serve as a toast?" 

<margin>Looking toward the height of Mount Wu, it is boundless; later literary allusions are generally of this sort.</margin>

The Commissioner said, "Since your home is at Mount Wu, let 'The Height of Mount Wu' be the theme." Ruixiang then sang:

> Mount Wu is high, so high its peak is beyond sight,
> Tiered summits and continuous cliffs lean against the celestial blue.
> Within the clouds, the hibiscus of the twelve peaks,
> Appear just like the Goddess after removing her heavy makeup.
> King Xiang has been gone these thousand years,
> Yet the traces of 'drifting rain and passing clouds' remain. <note>An allusion to the Goddess of Mount Wu, who famously told King Huai of Chu she was "the clouds in the morning and the rain in the evening"—a metaphor for a romantic encounter.</note>
> Morning after morning, evening after evening, beneath the Terrace of Chu,
> How often are travelers seen pausing their horses?
> The jade pendant’s divine fragrance can no longer be heard,
> In vain, the spirit-mediums perform their songs and dances.
> The path enters the maple forest, where the tree colors turn green,
> At the ancient shrine, the flying rain...

<summary>This page recounts the beginning of a supernatural encounter where Commissioner Mi, a childless official, takes in a beautiful maiden named Ruixiang and her father. The story utilizes the famous literary "Goddess of Mount Wu" motif to establish a sense of romantic mystery.</summary>

<keywords>Mount Wu, Mi Jimin, Ruixiang, Goddess of Mount Wu, fertility, Yuan Dynasty, poetry, King Xiang of Chu</keywords>

# Classified Compendium of the Trial of Passion, Volume 4

<margin>The old man refusing the gold corresponds to the previous chapter; perhaps it is because he had already obtained his prize eight years prior that he uses this pretext?</margin>

> Where the moss grows, we share our favor; where do the sorrowful spirits break?
> The leaves rustle as monkeys cry through the night.

The Commissioner was greatly pleased. He served wine and they drank until they were heavily intoxicated. The next day, the old man took his leave to return home. The Commissioner offered him five ingots of silver as a reward. The old man laughed and said, "I am a simple man with no heirs; I would have nowhere to put such silver. To be able to repay you with my heart is quite enough." The Commissioner thanked him deeply, and the old man departed. One day, the Commissioner was feasting with Ruixiang <note>Her name, Ruixiang, refers to the Winter Daphne flower</note> in the rear garden. He playfully said to her, "Your name is Ruixiang; you should compose a poem on this theme." Ruixiang smiled and chanted:

> Exquisite and clever,
> Adorned in a gown of purple silk;
> Rare petals and strange blossoms grow near the small hall.
> Bearing dew, she wishes to open for the night moon;
> Facing the breeze, she shrinks slightly from the spring frost.
> Many times has her name been tossed to the poetry society,
> Overpowering all other fragrances in the land of the drunk.
> Within the quarters of the old Zen master,
> There is no need for agarwood to scent one's robes.
<note>This poem alludes to the legend of the Ruixiang flower, which was said to have been discovered by a monk who smelled its fragrance in a dream.</note>

The Commissioner was amazed by her talent. On another day, he took Ruixiang on a boat for a pleasure excursion. He said to her, "Traveling by boat is also a joy. You should compose a verse about what you see here." Ruixiang immediately chanted:

> The orchid oars turn ten thousand times, gazing at the islet...

<column-break/>

<margin>Commissioner Zhu acquired Ruixiang and lost his life; Ruixiang remained high-spirited because of this and did not die.</margin>

> It seems to connect the cloud-capped peaks to the old dock of Yue;
> Coming from afar, the ferry crossing is moved.
> Deep within the weeping willows, there are houses;
> The spring colors of Luoyang remain for a thousand years,
> While the homesick heart for the land of Shu <note>Historical region in modern Sichuan</note> stretches ten thousand miles away.
> <gloss>After returning from the land of Shu and Mount Wu</gloss>
> Time is like a mountain pass; from a small boat with slow oars,
> One counts the lotus flowers.

The Commissioner laughed and said, "Your longing for your home is truly expressed in this melody." He consoled her with kind words, and they enjoyed themselves to the fullest before stopping. From then on, his love for her within the bedchamber exceeded that of all his other concubines, and her reputation spread far and wide. At that time, the Chancellor Bayan <note>A powerful and often-criticized Mongol official of the Yuan Dynasty</note> held absolute power. He held the power of life and death, and people on the roads dared only to exchange looks rather than speak <note>A classical idiom describing a climate of extreme fear and oppression</note>. Hearing that Commissioner Zhu had a concubine named Ruixiang, whose beauty rivaled Xi Shi <note>One of the legendary Four Beauties of ancient China</note> and whose talent surpassed the "Snow-Chanter" <note>Xie Daoyun, a famous female poet known for her wit</note>, he desired to see her but had no strategy to do so. It happened that someone falsely accused the Commissioner of plotting rebellion. Bayan issued an order to the local prefecture to confiscate his household, arresting the Commissioner, his wife, and his concubines to be brought to the capital. While they were binding Ruixiang, she suddenly vanished, and no one knew where she had gone.

<summary>This page depicts the brief domestic happiness of Commissioner Zhu and the talented Ruixiang before they are struck by political tragedy involving the Chancellor Bayan. It highlights Ruixiang's poetic brilliance and her mysterious disappearance during her husband's arrest.</summary>
<keywords>Ruixiang, Commissioner Zhu, Bayan, poetry, Mount Wu, Yuan Dynasty, betrayal, supernatural disappearance</keywords>

# Golden Carriage

*A woodblock illustration on the left page: A woman in a traditional long robe stands under a tree with large, five-pointed leaves (possibly a parasol or maple tree). She holds a closed folding fan in her right hand and gazes toward the center of the spread. The ground is depicted with stylized rolling hills and simple vegetation.*

Where the golden carriage departs, tear-stains mark the face and the soft rouge falls. <note>The "soft red" (qinghong) refers to the rouge on her cheeks, which is physically washed away by her tears as the carriage of her lover moves away.</note>

<column-break/>

One

# Jade Pendant

*A woodblock illustration on the right page: A woman with an elaborate high coiffure and long traditional dress stands on a path. In the middle ground, a man is seen beside a carriage with a curved, decorated roof, which is partially obscured by a rocky hill. Branches of a weeping willow tree hang down from the top right corner.*

When the jade pendant arrives, a look of joy reaches the brows, gathered in a faint green. <note>In classical Chinese beauty standards, women used a green pigment to paint "moth-eyebrows." The "faint green" (qianlü) gathering here suggests she is grooming herself and smiling in anticipation of the visitor signaled by the sound of his jade ornaments.</note>

**Key Vocabulary**

<term>Jade Pendant (Yupei)</term>: A decorative stone ornament worn at the waist, which chimed as a person walked; often used in poetry to signal the arrival of a gentleman.
<term>Golden Carriage (Jinche)</term>: An ornate vehicle used by the wealthy or high-ranking officials, symbolizing a journey or a departure.
<term>Smiling expression (Xiaotai)</term>: A person's charming or joyful demeanor.
<term>Brows (Mei)</term>: In Chinese literature, the eyebrows are considered the most expressive part of the face for showing emotion.
<term>Settling/Condensing (Ning)</term>: To gather or freeze in place; here it describes the fixed expression of joy and the application of makeup.
<term>Faint Green (Qianlü)</term>: A poetic term for the color of eyebrow pigment.
<term>Tear-stains (Leihen)</term>: The tracks left by crying.
<term>Face (Lian)</term>: The countenance or cheeks.
<term>Soft Red (Qinghong)</term>: A delicate shade of rouge or makeup.

<summary>This page presents a parallel poetic couplet illustrated by woodblock prints, depicting the emotional contrast between a lover's departure in a carriage and his joyful return signaled by his jade ornaments.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the abstract poetic themes of the previous entry into a specific narrative account. It tells the story of Xie Ao, a scholar whose encounter with a mysterious lady in a "Golden Carriage" provides a literal context for the symbols of arrival and departure discussed previously.</meta>

<margin>
Regarding the Beauty of the Golden Carriage: She was certainly not of the mortal world, nor was she a tree spirit or a flower demon. Arriving as she did by carriage, she must surely have been a celestial guest from the Western realms.
</margin>

# The Golden Carriage from the West

Xie Ao of Chen County was a scholar who had been a candidate for the imperial examinations <note>Specifically the "Jinshi" degree, the highest achievement in the civil service exam system.</note> and was particularly fond of composing poetry in the seven-syllable style. He lived in the Shengdao neighborhood of the capital, Chang'an. In the courtyard of his residence, he grew many peonies. 

One evening, after the rain had cleared, he walked about a hundred paces to the south to gaze upon Zhongnan Peak <gloss>A famous mountain range near the capital</gloss>. He stood there for a long time until he saw a rider galloping from the direction of the west. As the rider drew near, he saw it was a young woman with her hair styled in high, double-loop buns; she wore elegant, understated clothing and possessed a beauty that was truly extraordinary. 

She stopped before Xie Ao and asked, "Sir, are you not waiting for someone?"

Xie Ao replied, "I am merely here to admire the mountain view."

The young woman smiled and bowed, saying, "I wish for us to return to your residence at once." 

Xie Ao was bewildered, but as he looked back toward his home, he saw three or four servants in green robes standing outside his gate. Startled by this strange sight, he entered his house. The servants all stepped forward and bowed to him. Upon entering the hall, he saw it had been decorated with thick rugs and hung with embroidered curtains <gloss>finely stitched single-layer hangings</gloss>. The entire room glowed with the reflection of the silks, and a rare, wonderful fragrance filled the air. Xie Ao was both astonished and frightened, not daring to ask any questions. 

One of the servants stepped forward and said, "There is no need for fear; no harm shall come to you." Shortly thereafter, a golden carriage arrived at the gate, carrying a beautiful...

<column-break/>

<margin>
She departed as the night grew late. In the end, he never questioned her origins. There were no improper words or indecent actions between them. Her arrival may have been prompted simply by a shared love of poetry; what else could it have been?
</margin>

...woman, aged perhaps sixteen or seventeen. Her grace and beauty were of a kind unknown to the mortal world. She stepped down from the carriage, entered the house, and met with Xie Ao. Sitting in the western veranda, she said to him, "This place is known for its famous flowers, which is why I have come to make this appointment with you."

Xie Ao’s fear began to fade. The beauty then prepared a feast for them to share; the utensils and the food were all of the most exquisite and pristine quality. She brought out a jade cup and poured wine for him. Xie Ao took the opportunity to ask, "Who are you, my lady? Are you perhaps a supernatural spirit?"

The beauty smiled but did not answer. When he pressed her further, she said, "You need only know that I am not a mortal. Why ask for more?"

As the night grew late, she said to him, "My home is very far away, and I must now return. I have heard you are skilled in seven-syllable poetry; I would be honored to receive a poem from you."

Xie Ao took up his brush and composed these lines:

> Since our meeting at the Terrace of the Sun, there is no hope for another; <note>The "Terrace of the Sun" (Yangtai) is a classical literary allusion to the site where the King of Chu met a goddess; it signifies a fleeting, divine romance.</note>
> Deep among the green trees, the mist thickens as the jade water-clock slows.
> At midnight, a fragrant breeze fills the moonlit courtyard,
> Before the flowers, I can only recite the poems of the King of Chu.

The beauty asked for her brush to be readied <gloss>the term here refers to the preparation of ink</gloss> and, on a sheet of blue-green paper, she wrote the following:

> There is no path for our longing, so let us not long for one another;
> The flowers among the maples bloom for but a fleeting moment.
> With a heart full of melancholy, the golden carriage returns,
> As the dawn oriole begins to cry.

**Key Vocabulary**

<term>Xie Ao (謝翱)</term>: A scholar and poet of the Tang Dynasty era.
<term>The Golden Carriage from the West (西顧金車)</term>: A title referring to a celestial visitation.
<term>Peony (牡丹)</term>: A flower symbolizing wealth, beauty, and often supernatural attraction in Chinese literature.
<term>Zhongnan Peak (終南峰)</term>: A mountain significant in Daoist and hermit traditions, often associated with the dwelling of immortals.
<term>Terrace of the Sun (陽臺)</term>: A poetic metaphor for a divine or dream-like romantic encounter.
<term>Ink-dipping (掭筆)</term>: The act of smoothing the brush tip in ink before writing.
<term>Blue-green Paper (碧箋)</term>: A decorative, tinted stationery used for writing refined poetry.
<term>Seven-Syllable Poetry (七言詩)</term>: A classic poetic form where each line consists of seven characters.

<summary>This page recounts a supernatural encounter between the scholar Xie Ao and a mysterious celestial woman who arrives at his home in a golden carriage, drawn by his flowers and his poetry. The story concludes with a bittersweet poetic exchange that emphasizes the fleeting nature of their meeting.</summary>
<keywords>Xie Ao, Golden Carriage, supernatural encounter, Tang dynasty poetry, Chang'an, peonies, Zhongnan Mountain, Yangtai, celestial beauty, Seven-syllable verse</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the scholar Xie Ao and his mysterious visitor. It transitions from their initial meeting to their parting, Xie Ao's subsequent failure in the imperial examinations, and a supernatural second encounter at a roadside inn in Xinfeng.</meta>

<page-num>Thirty</page-num>

The calligraphy of the "New Green Willow Branches" was exceptionally refined. Ao admired and praised it for a long time. Then, the beautiful lady boarded her carriage; Ao escorted her to the gate and wept as they bid each other farewell. Before they had even traveled several dozen paces, the carriage and the people suddenly vanished from sight. Ao was struck by the strangeness of the event and recorded the lady's poem, keeping it inside his book-chest. 

In the spring of the following year, having failed the imperial examinations <note>The term "xiadi" refers to failing to place on the list of successful candidates for the metropolitan exam.</note>, Ao headed back east. Upon reaching Xinfeng <gloss>a famous stop for travelers near the capital</gloss>, he stayed at an inn for some time. One evening, as he walked under the moon and gazed into the distance, he was overcome by memories of the previous year. He composed another poem, which said:

> A single sheet of kingfisher-green stationery <note>Original: "cuijian." This was a high-quality, tinted paper used by the elite and for romantic correspondence.</note>, elegant and fine,
> The lingering fragrance of the clouds remains.
> The ink is still fresh, yet it only adds to the bleakness of the view;
> I no longer see that ethereal person from the three immortal isles <note>A reference to the mythical peaks of Penglai, Fangzhang, and Yingzhou, where immortals were said to dwell.</note>.
> The slanting moon shines on my robes in tonight's dream,
> Just as falling flowers and weeping rain marked last spring.
> Within the deep chambers, there is a place even more tragic:
> A clod of earth, base insects, and dust clinging to the wall. <note>This imagery strongly suggests a grave, hinting that the lady may have been a ghost or that her "home" was a tomb.</note>

<margin>The golden carriage and its attendants came from the west, yet ultimately left no trace.</margin>

Shortly after he had chanted the poem aloud, he suddenly heard the sound of a carriage approaching from the west, moving with great haste. In a moment, he saw a golden carriage accompanied by several riders. Looking at the attendants, he recognized the girl with the double-loop buns <note>A hairstyle typical of young female servants in the Tang dynasty.</note> from before. Startled, he questioned her. The girl stopped the carriage and said to Ao: "In the middle of this busy thoroughfare, I regret that we cannot have a proper meeting." Ao invited her to stay at the inn, but she refused. When he asked where she was heading, she replied: "I am going to..."

<meta>This page begins the narrative of Qin Bang and his encounter with a painting that comes to life. It establishes his character as a scholar-warrior and introduces the supernatural visitor, Ni Huisu, who is a personification of the painting itself.</meta>

# Poetry Moves Qin Bang

<margin>In an ancient painting depicting a female figure, she was but a description within an exquisite study; yet, her gaze transformed and she took on a physical form, with repeating lines <unclear>...</unclear> how many regulated verses were there!</margin>

Qin Bang of Dayuan, whose courtesy name was Bengu, was born with exceptional talent. He dedicated himself to the great teachings of Confucius and Mencius <gloss>the word "great" is pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>. He possessed a heroic spirit and bold strategies. He once built a study about seven *li* <note>a unit of distance, roughly 0.5 kilometers</note> northwest of the city walls, where he spent his time reading. He also enjoyed sword dancing <gloss>"sword" is pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>, and he always kept a blade by his side. In his hall hung an ancient painting depicting a single female figure. Legend said that a "Feathered Man" <note>a Taoist immortal</note> had passed by and gifted it to him. Bang was diligent in his studies; one night, unable to sleep during a fine evening in the ninth month of the *Xinhai* year of the Xuande era <note>1431 CE</note>, just as the water clock had twice dripped its markers, he felt his spirit tire. Just as he was about to retire for the night, he suddenly saw a beautiful woman arrive from outside. She stood beneath the lamp, dressed in plain white clothing and light makeup. Her movements were charming, and she wore a faint, playful smile. Stepping forward slowly, she said: "My surname is Ni, and my courtesy name is Huisu <note>Her name is a pun: *Ni* means mud/clay, and *Huisu* refers to painted silk, the components of a painting</note>. I am a local of this region. Thinking of how I began with white powder and silk and did not care for flashy colors, I relied on the 'Scholar of Pine Stream' to form my flesh and blood <gloss>The 'Scholar of Pine Stream' refers to ink</gloss> and am grateful to the 'Scribe of Hair' for creating my initial form <gloss>The 'Scribe of Hair' refers to the writing brush</gloss>."

<column-break/>

<margin>Though she says she seeks a scholar’s correction, she actually intends to stir a gentleman’s desire to narrate.</margin>

"Since I have been granted a human form, I must know how to move and behave. I hope you, sir, do not despise me for being as lifeless as 'withered wood or cold ashes' <note>a metaphor for someone who has suppressed all desires</note>. I, your humble servant, wish to treat you with the intimacy of 'holding rain and carrying clouds' <note>a poetic euphemism for sexual intercourse</note>." Bang was shocked by her words. He knew in his heart she was a supernatural being, but he was not terrified. Before he could stop her, the woman spoke again: "I have ten 'Bamboo Branch' lyrics <note>a style of folk-inspired poetry often dealing with love and local scenery</note> that I dare to present before a man of letters. Might I receive your correction?" She then recited them aloud. The first went: "My home is by the White Stone Shoal in East Wu; before my door, the flowing water washes my silk clothes. In the morning, I tie the oar of the magnolia boat <gloss>meaning boat and oars</gloss>; as it sets off, it startles the mandarin ducks into flight." The second went: "Outside Stone City lies the riverbank <gloss>.</gloss>; sailing a boat upstream is difficult. The tide has a time to return, yet once the boat departs, when will it come back?" The third went: "My lord travels through the nine provinces for the sake of fame <gloss>the word "sake" is pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>, while my grief rests night and day upon my heart. There is no way for me to be like the white pheasant bird, following my lord's boat wherever it wanders." The fourth went: "I urge my lord to never climb the peaks of Mount Wu; I urge my lord never to travel to Linqiong <gloss>a place name</gloss>. The young wives of Linqiong..."

<vocab>Qin Bang: The protagonist, a scholar-warrior. Ni Huisu: The personification of the painting. Xuande: An era of the Ming Dynasty. Songxi Shi: Poetic term for ink. Mao Zhongshu: Poetic term for a brush. Bamboo Branch Lyrics: A poetic form. Yingzheng: A polite term for asking an expert to correct one's writing. Linqiong: An ancient district in Sichuan.</vocab>
