<margin>The Yin forces of the world cannot overcome the Yang; ghosts cannot overcome humans; and evil cannot overcome the righteous. Thus, though this beautiful woman was a supernatural transformation, she could not violate a righteous man.</margin>

When the "Chant of the Kirin Brocade Belt" was finished, Zhao Bang stepped forward and intended to grasp her wrist to flirt with her <gloss>Grasping Bang’s wrist</gloss>.

Bang’s expression remained calm and composed, showing no sign of being moved. However, he gripped his sword, glared fiercely, and shouted in a stern voice: "What sort of demon-specter dares to insult a righteous man like this? If you do not depart quickly, I shall cut off your head!"

The beautiful woman replied: "Would you be like Liu Xiahui? <gloss>Who sat with a woman in his lap without his heart being disturbed</gloss> <note>Liu Xiahui was a legendary official of the Lu state famous for his extreme self-control and moral purity</note>. Or would you be like the Man of Lu? <gloss>Who closed his door and would not admit his neighbor</gloss> <note>The 'Man of Lu' refused to let a woman into his house during a storm, fearing he lacked the self-control of Liu Xiahui; he preferred to avoid temptation entirely</note>. If you can be like Liu Xiahui, I pray you allow me to stay a moment longer, so as not to betray my original intent in seeking you out tonight. But if you must act like the Man of Lu, then please allow me to depart of my own accord; why must you refuse me with such severe and frightening sternness?"

Bang fell into a great rage. He raised his sword and chased her. She ran around the seat, and as he pursued her more urgently, the beauty found herself with nowhere to go. She rushed toward the wall and vanished, leaving neither form nor shadow. Bang realized then that the woman depicted in the painting was capable of becoming a monster. Consequently, he destroyed the image and burned it, but because of this, she was never seen again.

<column-break/>

<margin>The songs recorded above use objects to convey deeper allegories and satires. This was the established style of the ancients. Although the language is close to the colloquial, it does no harm to the meaning.</margin>

**Lyrics for "Persuading the Guest to Stay"**

At the high peak of Mount Wu, the clouds and rain are thick <note>"Clouds and rain" is a traditional Chinese metaphor for sexual intimacy, originating from the legend of the Goddess of Mount Wu</note>.

**The fifth song says:**
The mountain peach blossoms open, redder than red,
Every morning I worry about the rain and the wind.
The flowers bloom and the flowers wither; it is hard for us to meet.
My resentment is boundless, and the sorrow kills me. <gloss>Referring to myself</gloss>

**The sixth song says:**
The Shu River flows from the west, a long ribbon of water,
The river has no waves, its surface bright as a mirror.
I long resent that human hearts are not like water,
For it is difficult to measure the casual likes and dislikes of people. <gloss>The words "likes" and "dislikes" are both pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>

**The seventh song says:**
The West Lake lotus leaves are a lush, overflowing green,
Heavy with dew and tossed by the wind, they sway lightly.
The lotus leaves are round and clustered, like my feelings,
But the dew-pearls are unstable, like your shifting heart, my dear.

**The eighth song says:**
Do not spread cotton across the bottom of the water,
And I advise you, my dear, do not plant lotuses upon the rocks.
Cotton spread under water rots easily,
And lotuses planted on rocks will never have firm roots.

**The ninth song says:**
In an oil-walled carriage seeking fragrance, it is Lady Liu Su <gloss>named Yao</gloss>.
Her spring shirt has half-sleeves of light silk.
The crabapple blossoms are charming, but their scent is not revealed,
For she fears the many wild bees and wandering butterflies.

**The tenth song says:**
The "lamp-flowers" were dry in the silver lamp-vat last night <note>A "lamp-flower" is a charred wick shaped like a flower, traditionally considered an omen that a guest or good news will arrive</note>,
And the magpies chatter at the gauze window today.
If we can meet within three days, I shall re-embroider...

# Kong Deludes Jingchun

<margin>
In late spring, the weather is mild and the scenery of West Lake is at its most magnificent. The desire to go wandering there is truly joyful!
</margin>

Xu Jingchun of Lin’an <note>Modern-day Hangzhou</note> was the son of the wealthy Xu Dachuan. He was about twenty years old, with a stature that was both towering and graceful, and he possessed a fine reputation <gloss>fame</gloss>. He was skilled at chanting poetry and had an elegant air. He was quite experienced in business management, yet his passion for mountains and rivers was also high. 

It was late spring, and the spring garments were already made. The "Bright Spring" graced me with beautiful scenery, and the "Great Creator" <note>original: "大塊" (dakuai), a Daoist term for the Earth or nature</note> granted me this auspicious time. Jingchun ordered his servant to bring wine and went out to wander upon West Lake. His footprints nearly covered the North and South mountains. After a short while, the sun sank into the western hills and the moon rose from the eastern sea. His excitement exhausted, he turned back, strolling toward home. 

Near Loushui Bridge <note>literally "Leaking Water Bridge"</note>, he suddenly saw a beautiful woman walking with a maidservant in green. Her hair was like cloud-shadows and misty tresses; she was lithe and graceful. Looking at her, she seemed almost like someone from the realm of the immortals. In a single glance, Jingchun’s soul was scattered. He sighed in admiration for a long time. As the beauty walked, she chanted: "The road enters the small grotto-heaven of the Peach Blossom Spring <note>A fabled utopian land in Chinese literature</note>; where the chaotic red petals fly, there is a lovely lady." Jing—

<column-break/>

<margin>
Ever since I once traveled to Gaotang <note>A place associated with a king's dream of a goddess</note> in a dream, I finally believed in the "clouds and rain" <gloss>meaning she becomes clouds in the morning and rain in the evening; a euphemism for sexual intimacy</gloss> of the Yangtai Terrace.
</margin>

—chun sighed and said, "The lakes and mountains are as they were, and the scenery is no different. Yet as times shift and the world changes, it makes one harbor the grief of Qin's departure <note>An allusion to historical separation and loss</note> in vain." 

The scholar stepped forward, bowed, and said, "Why does the lady walk alone? Have you indeed found the scenic beauty all to yourself?" 

The beauty replied, "I was walking with other women to 'tread the green' <note>taqing, a traditional spring outing</note> and enjoy the sights. Amidst the bustling crowds of men and women, I accidentally lost my group. I intended to find my way back, but I have lost my path." 

Jingchun learned her surname, 

<margin>
Parents and brothers are what make a family. There is a woman in this world who has no brothers, yet lives all alone; Jingchun was so charmed by her person that he did not bother to investigate the flaws in her story.
</margin>

and her residence. The beauty said, "My surname is Kong, and my childhood name is Shufang. I am the daughter of an official's family from the lake market. My family's fortunes have fluctuated, and my parents died early. Having neither brothers nor many relatives, I live alone with Wang Mei <note>The maid mentioned earlier</note> near the shores of West Lake." 

The scholar offered to escort her home. The beauty smiled and said, "Would the gentleman be willing to look upon my humble home? It is but a short distance away." Thereupon, the scholar walked shoulder-to-shoulder with her, their intimacy reaching its peak. They arrived at the woman's chamber, where they set out delicacies and wine to drink together beneath the west window. They began to discuss poetry, saying that the people of the Tang Dynasty loved...

<margin>The four rhymes are divided into the four seasons; they are subtle and harmonious in их composition, and written with great speed, yet such mere cleverness is not truly worth esteeming.</margin>

->**The Composition of Palindromes**<-

The composition of <term>Huiwen</term><note>original: "廻文" (Huiwen); a form of Chinese palindromic poetry that can be read forwards, backwards, or in various circular patterns while maintaining meaning and rhythm</note> has been rarely seen in recent times. Jingchun said, "You, my jade-like lady, possess such tender feelings and wisdom <gloss>the word 'thought' is in the departing tone</gloss>; in talk and laughter you create these works. For those of us who are dull and unrefined, we find ourselves at a loss for words." 

The beautiful woman smiled and said, "I invite you to provide a topic." 

Jingchun said, "The 'Four Seasons' would be an appropriate theme." 

The beauty immediately composed a poem, saying:

Several branches of flower blossoms turn red beside the steps,
Ten thousand threads of willow silk gently sway in the wind.
The sunset glow brightens a thousand peaks as the sun dips west,
The moon rises over a lonely village and a single pine tree.
Coolness comes from the green bamboo mat, icing the person with cold;
The teeth are chilled by the clear spring, the summer well is freezing.
The incense smoke curls in the breeze, green threads of mist;
By the paper window, the moon is bright, a white round fan. <gloss>The above is Summer</gloss>
Reed-flowers like snow cover the banks, the autumn water is white;
The willow-wind withers the trees, the evening mountains are grey.
In a lonely lamp, a traveler's dream is startled in the empty hall;
A single wild goose carries a letter to a distant official. <gloss>The above is Autumn</gloss>
The sky is frozen with cold rain, in the morning the doors are closed;
Snow flies and the wind is cold, at night the city gates are shut.
Piled red "beast charcoal" <note>Charcoal pressed into the shape of animals, used by the wealthy</note> makes the stove warm;
In shallow green tea-bowls, the clear infusion is poured. <gloss>The above is Winter</gloss>

Jingchun sighed in admiration at the speed of her recitation. He immediately dipped his brush in ink to harmonize with her, saying:

The fragrant trees spit forth red flowers after the rain has passed;
Flying into the curtains, the white willow-fluff is startled by the wind.
Yellow <column-break/> <margin>Alas! Kong Shufang's vital <term>Yang</term> energy had not yet fully dissipated, so she coupled with a man to commit a sin. When Jingchun encountered her, had he possessed a heart free from lewd desires, then such evil could not have taken hold. Is this not so?</margin> adds to the dawn light, as the green willows stretch;
Powder falls from the fragrant snow covering the pines. <gloss>Harmonizing with Spring</gloss>
Melons float in the water-jar, their coolness dispelling the heat;
Lotus roots are served on a plate, their icy emerald crunching cold.
Leaning against the stones, one points to where the bamboo shoots grow thick;
In the small pond, leaves unfurl as the lotus flowers cluster. <gloss>Harmonizing with Summer</gloss>
The remaining sun is a dazzling red, the frost-covered leaves are crimson;
A thin mist envelops the trees, the evening forest is grey.
The startling letter is hateful, shameful to seal with tears;
A butterfly dream <note>A reference to Zhuangzi’s dream, signifying the blurring of reality and illusion</note> is startled by sorrow, fearing the thought of home. <gloss>Harmonizing with Autumn</gloss>
The wind rolls the snow-covered awning, the cold brings an end to fishing;
The moon shines on the frost-cracked earth, the cold knocks at the city walls.
Thickly scented wine overflows the "rosy-cloud" cup;
The thin shadows of plum blossoms cross the paper curtains in the clear light. <gloss>Harmonizing with Winter</gloss>

The beautiful woman also praised his skill. After lingering together for a long while, they finally partook in the "joys of the pillow" <note>A common euphemism for sexual intercourse</note>, sharing the "happiness of flying together" <note>original: "于飛之樂" (yufei zhi le); a classical idiom for marital or sexual harmony</note>. 

Meanwhile, Jingchun's servant had returned home first with the horse. His parents, fearing that he might have collapsed from drunkenness or sought out a <term>Chu Mansion</term><note>original: "楚舘" (Chu Guan); a literary term for a brothel or house of pleasure</note>, ordered servants to search for him in all directions, but he could not be found.

The following morning, a neighbor named Lin Shiejie was passing by the New River Embankment. Beside a wall, he saw Jingchun lying prostrate on the ground. Realizing he had been "deceived by a ghost," Lin hurriedly rescued him and brought him home. His parents were overjoyed to have him back. Nearby, there stood a gravestone for a deceased young woman named Kong Shufang.

### A Courtesan Meets a Formal Scholar

In Huizhou, there lived a man named Yan Jingxing, also known by his courtesy name Tianrui. He was a master of prose and poetry. During the Tianshun era <note>1457–1464, during the Ming Dynasty</note>, he was traveling by boat one day to visit a friend. Letting the vessel drift as it pleased, he waited for dawn to break. At that hour, large fish could be heard leaping from the waves, and birds returning to their nests chirped along the banks. Jingxing felt a sudden wave of melancholy and began to sing a sorrowful song, feeling as though he had lost something. Suddenly, another boat came cutting through the waves. Seated inside was a courtesan. Jingxing steered his boat aside to avoid her, but the woman laughed and said, "Tianrui, why do you turn away?" She then pulled her boat alongside his, stepped onto his deck, and bowed. "We are fated to meet this evening," she said. <gloss>A leisurely meeting</gloss>. She then ordered her maid to set out wine for them to drink together. Jingxing suspected she had mistaken him for someone else and did not argue, simply drinking with her. After several rounds, the courtesan said, "Sir, you are well-known for your skill in poetry. Why not compose something yourself? <gloss>The word 'yong' here means 'to do' or 'to use'</gloss>. Let us each assemble lines from the poets of the Tang Dynasty <note>This refers to 'Ji Tang,' a popular literary game of 'patchwork poetry' or centos, where new poems are created using famous existing lines</note>. We shall aim for ten poems each. What do you say?" Jingxing agreed. The courtesan recited first: "With skin like hibiscus and hair like green clouds, how many secret feelings can I tell?"

<column-break/>

<margin>
Although these ten rhymes are assembled from old poetic phrases, each one expresses personal feelings quite appropriately, making them quite delightful.
</margin>

"It is hard to hear that springtime makes the melancholy double; do not let your long sleeves lean against the railing." <gloss>To be read in a level tone</gloss>.

"The clouds make one think of her clothes, and flowers of her face; <note>A famous line by Li Bai referring to the beauty of Yang Guifei</note> yet I know not when we might meet again. My body lacks the colorful wings of a flying phoenix, but I am like King Xiang, remembering a dream." <gloss>A reference to a Tang-era dream or romance</gloss>. The courtesan said: "Tossing and turning a thousand times, I am too listless to leave my bed; before the door, the moonlight reflects on the horizontal pond. Being heartless is not as painful as being full of passion; a single song of 'Yizhou' brings several lines of tears." <gloss>The word for 'lines' is pronounced 'hang'</gloss>.

Yan replied: "Her robe is draped in rosy clouds over a skirt of lotus silk; suddenly she departs, following the heavy rains. Ever since the 'Rhapsody on the Gao Tang' was written, <note>A reference to the legend of the Goddess of Mount Wu meeting a king in a dream</note> where else could such a dream-cloud go?" The courtesan said: "South of Xunyang, the tides do not reach; yet the traveler’s path is beautifully long. The bright moon descends with the clear frost; where is the jade-like person teaching someone to play the flute?" Yan replied: "On the southern path, I grieve at the parting of falling leaves; the crickets in the shade chirp incessantly. At dawn, the wind drops blossoms like snow; suddenly coming to the window, I wonder if it is you." The courtesan said: "My sorrowful heart doubles my love for the long-departed; the red trees and green mountains overlook the rushing stream. Outside the gate, the autumn colors of the late clearing are old and unbearable to sing about..."

[The courtesan] said: "Today, the water is like a bright stretch of Xichuan silk." <note>Xichuan (Sichuan) was famous for its high-quality, vibrantly patterned brocade.</note>
Yan replied: "How can I obtain an 'ice knife' <note>A poetic term for a sharp, shimmering blade.</note> to cut and tailor it?" <gloss>The word 'cut' is written as 'tailor' in another version.</gloss>
The courtesan stood up, patted his back, and exclaimed, "A rare talent indeed!"
Yan also sighed in admiration, saying, "The courtesan's talent is so great it could almost surpass even Xue Tao of the White Clouds Villa." <gloss>Xue Tao was a famous Tang dynasty poet and courtesan; both she and the master of White Cloud Villa were renowned for their verse.</gloss> "Though I am slow and dull, how could I not venture to 'knit my brows' <note>An idiom referring to the ugly Dongshi imitating the beautiful Xishi; it means to clumsily imitate someone's superior work.</note> and compose a regulated poem to praise her beauty?"
The courtesan replied, "I would not have dared to ask, but it is exactly what I wished for."
Yan then recited:
"The graceful immortal is no ordinary being,
Far surpassing the famous Wang Siniang <gloss>A celebrated courtesan of the past</gloss> of yesteryear.
Her moth-like eyebrows, newly drawn, put willow leaves to shame;
Her vermilion lips, half-parted, reveal a smile like a bursting pomegranate.
Chanting in the wind and drunk under the moon, her passion is profound;
Enveloping the rain and carrying the clouds, her charm is even more lasting.
If others look on from the side, they must keep a close watch,
For in Suzhou, she once broke the heart of a high official." <gloss>The word 'official' is pronounced here in the departing tone.</gloss>
The courtesan laughed and thanked him, saying, "I am honored by your excessive praise. How could I ever be worthy of such sentiment? This feeling is deeply engraved in my heart."
Before long, a great wind arose. Clouds and mist darkened the world, and the courtesan vanished. He never knew what kind of strange spirit she truly was.

<column-break/>

<margin>These words of love and parting never descend into the common tone of the mortal world.</margin>

Leaning against the Sunset Tower, Yan said: "This has truly become a composition on a tragic fate. I recall searching for flowers at the wilderness temple, though spring had already waned. Where can one find the object of their longing? They do not meet; the clouds of Mount Jiuyi dissipate, and the green mist is scattered."
The courtesan replied: "Meeting you is to feel an immediate kinship; we have remained silent and wordless through several springs. The sorrow of parting grows deeper; where can it be written? As evening comes, I fear this lonely solitude will wound the spirit."
Yan said: "I sorrowfully listen to the winter fireflies as tears soak my robes. <gloss>The word for 'tears' is pronounced 'ti'.</gloss> Alas, what are your intentions in this parting? Do not say there is no messenger for our mutual longing; <gloss>The word 'messenger' is pronounced in the departing tone.</gloss> From far across the waters, a single letter shall come within the 'pair of carp' <note>Ancient Chinese letters were often protected between two pieces of wood carved into the shape of fish.</note>."
When the recitation ended, they clapped and laughed loudly. They undressed and went to sleep. Soon, when their joy was at its peak <gloss>The word 'joy' is pronounced 'luo'</gloss>, the courtesan spoke again:
"Now, let us take 'Spray on the Waves' as our theme and each compose a line to form a linked poem. What do you think, sir?"
Yan said, "I have it."
The courtesan chanted first: "Not descending from the heavens, yet planted with the dew."
Yan followed: "Relying only on the message of the seasonal wind, urged on time and again."
The courtesan said: "A single spray at last is seen moving in a winding path."
Yan said: "Ten thousand blossoms suddenly bloom in a single instant."
The courtesan said: "At Xunyang Wharf, the autumn colors are disordered by the rain."
Yan said: "At Mirror Lake, the colors of spring follow in a person's wake."

**Key Vocabulary**

*   <term>Harmonious Union (良縁)</term>: A predestined or excellent marriage match.
*   <term>Traveler (旅客)</term>: A guest or person on a journey.
*   <term>Inn (客店)</term>: A lodging house for travelers.
*   <term>Prize-winning (錦標)</term>: Originally a silk trophy in dragon boat racing, it refers to reaching the top or being the best.
*   <term>Spring Beauty (春色)</term>: Literally "spring colors," often used to describe a woman's beauty or the vibrancy of life.
*   <term>Hunting Grounds (獵場)</term>: An area for hunting; here, a metaphor for the competitive arena of the world.
*   <term>Saddle and Horse (鞍馬)</term>: A term signifying the hardships and constant movement of travel.
*   <term>Autumn Wind (秋風)</term>: A common literary trope signifying loneliness or the passage of time.
*   <term>Marriage Scrolls (姻牘)</term>: Formal documents used to record a legal and social union between families.

It contrasts the lonely life of a traveler with the social stability of a documented marriage.</summary>

## The Traveling Soul, Zhang Ke

In Yuqian County, there was a local villager named Zhang Ke. While traveling as a peddler <gloss>merchant</gloss>, he entered the city and stayed at a traveler’s lodge. Just as the night watch had ended, the blue sky was clear of clouds, the moon shone brightly without a speck of dust, and the shadows of trees slanted across the window. The fragrance of plum blossoms drifted into his dreams. Zhang, sleeping alone, was at first restless and then unable to sleep at all. His mind became dazed, as if in a trance—he felt asleep yet his heart remained awake. He then dreamed of a woman dressed in fine, ornate clothing, seeking to share his bed. A sound <gloss>the sound came from the side of the bed</gloss> rang out clearly. Once he awoke <gloss>the woman said, “Dissipate”</gloss>, the woman was still vividly present by his side. It was not until dawn that she took her leave.

On the following night, just as the door was bolted and the lamp had not yet been extinguished, she stood before him again, and they once more shared the bed. Zhang asked where she had come from. The woman replied, "I am the daughter of a neighboring house. I live very close to this lodge. I knew you were a noble guest, so I dared to follow you." She then sang a verse:

> The young daughter of the eastern neighbor plies the jasper shuttle, <gloss>“young daughter” is read with a falling tone</gloss>
> From threads of cloud on a phoenix loom, she weaves plain silk.
> How could she lightly grant the passion of rain and clouds? <note>“Rain and clouds” is a classical Chinese euphemism for sexual intimacy, originating from the legend of the Goddess of Mount Wu.</note>
> Even if her teeth were broken, what would it matter? <note>This is a literary allusion to a woman who would rather have her teeth broken than lose her beloved, or a reference to a woman resisting an unwanted suitor to remain faithful.</note>

Zhang was greatly pleased.

<column-break/>

<margin>
Her form was seen many times during the day. She is a vengeful ghost whose roots are stuck here. Because of their intimacy, her spirit has accumulated and will not dissipate.
</margin>

He also responded with a short verse:

> In emerald sleeves and red skirts, a graceful lady,
> Embraced by the mandarin duck quilt, smelling of musk and orchid. <gloss>The word is pronounced "qin," meaning a quilt or coverlet.</gloss>
> Having found love near the shaman’s mountain, <note>A reference to Mount Wu (Wushan), the legendary site of a romantic encounter between a goddess and a king.</note>
> Who says a lonely inn brings only heartbreak?

Their passions grew deep, and ten days passed. Outsiders began to notice and became suspicious <gloss>suspecting she was a ghost or monster</gloss>. Someone told him, "A courtesan once lived here who hanged herself. Her form is often seen during the day. We suspect the one you are consorting with is her, and that you are being bewitched." Zhang kept the secret and refused to speak of it. From then on, he continued his intimacy with the woman at night without any fear. 

One evening, the woman returned. Zhang carefully questioned her with many twists and turns. The woman showed no sign of concealment and replied instantly, "It is true. I was formerly a courtesan and was deeply involved with a client named Yang Sheng. He took two hundred units of my wealth <gloss>money and goods</gloss>, promising to marry me with proper ceremony. But after three years, he did not honor our pact. In my resentment, I fell ill and eventually died by the noose. This inn was actually my former home; that is why I still linger here, unable to bear leaving. Furthermore, Yang Sheng is from your hometown. Do you know him?"

Zhang replied, "I know of him. I hear he has married a wife and his business is flourishing; his household grows more prosperous every day." The woman became dejected and began to sing...

<summary>This page tells the story of Zhang Ke, a merchant who encounters the ghost of a courtesan at an inn; the ghost reveals she was betrayed by a man named Yang Sheng and died by suicide.</summary>
<keywords>Zhang Ke, ghost story, Yuqian County, courtesan, Yang Sheng, vengeful spirit, romance, betrayal</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Zhang Ke, who has encountered a mysterious woman at an inn. After their initial meeting described on the previous page, their relationship deepens through an exchange of poetry and a miraculous discovery, leading to a journey back to Zhang's home and a final act of supernatural retribution.</meta>

<margin>The gold found under the bed as she described truly solidified Zhang’s devotion. This account is similar in style to the ancient records of curiosities <note>original: "乱琛" (luan chen), referring to miscellaneous gems or records of wonders</note>.</margin>

She said: "Life in this world is like interlocking branches or twin flowers on a single stem. Why write empty words on leaves or fruitlessly tie knots in white silk? Lacquer and glue should naturally hold firm; only I know the loneliness of a vacant mat. Be careful not to let grievances arise in the light of day, or you will pointlessly regret the time of parting."

Zhang replied in turn: "By chance I have met a divine maiden, her spirit so pure and her appearance so rare. Like the fragrance of orchids, we share the same tastes; like the pine and cypress, we share our life's hopes. May you forever be the joy of my bedchamber <gloss>Luo</gloss> <note>The gloss "Luo" likely references the Goddess of the Luo River, a standard for divine beauty</note>, and long may we delight in ink and paper. Even if Mount Tai were worn down to a whetstone, my devotion would remain as old as the earth and as high as the heavens."

When the poem was finished, after a long silence, the woman said: "I shall be sincere with you from beginning to end. Long ago, I buried fifty taels of white silver under this bed. No one knows of it, and it can be used to help with your expenses." Zhang searched the ground and found the gold exactly as she had said, without any falsehood <gloss>not a hollow experience</gloss>.

From then on, the woman stayed behind the bed-curtains and did not come out during the day. One day, she whispered: "Staying here long is of no benefit. Can you take me back with you?" Zhang asked how he could carry her. The woman said: "You can make a tablet and write 'Spirit Throne of Such-and-Such' on it. Hide it in your bamboo trunk. Wherever you arrive, open the trunk and call to me softly, and I will appear to meet you." Zhang followed her instructions exactly, left the lodge, and set out on the road home.

<column-break/>

Everyone said that Zhang was deeply possessed by a ghostly aura and would surely perish on the road, but Zhang himself did not doubt her in the least. Wherever they traveled, they stayed together <gloss>read in the falling-rising tone</gloss>.

When they reached his home, he respectfully placed the spirit tablet against the wall to serve her. At first, his wife thought it was a deity and worshipped it without fail. Before long, she saw a woman come out to speak with Zhang. The wife was greatly shocked and questioned her husband: "Who is that person? Are you perhaps kidnapping a daughter from a good family? I hope you are not inviting calamity <gloss>the character 'Jia' (to invite) is pronounced 'gu'</gloss> upon us."

Zhang told her the truth, and his wife's mind was put at ease; they all shared the same room for five days. Then, the woman asked to go to the city market to collect a debt. Zhang agreed and accompanied her to the south of the city. As they were about to cross over, the woman said: "I am very grateful to you, but I fear I cannot stay with you much longer." Zhang began to weep, not understanding what she meant. They entered the city gate as usual, but after he called for her two or three times, she could no longer be seen.

He then visited the lodgings of a merchant named Yang and found the place in a state of great disturbance. He asked a neighbor, who replied: "Mr. Yang had no previous illness, but he just died suddenly with blood flowing from his seven orifices <note>The eyes, ears, nostrils, and mouth; in traditional Chinese lore, this often signifies a violent or cursed death</note>." Zhang was terrified and hurried home. He never encountered the woman again.

<margin>The woman died first and the man Yang died later. Though it was a cycle of grievances and retribution, is it not also a case of 'Heaven’s net being vast and losing nothing'?</margin>

<term>Spirit Throne (神位)</term>: A ritual tablet used to house the soul of the deceased, allowing them to be transported or worshipped.
<term>Seven Orifices (七竅)</term>: The two eyes, two ears, two nostrils, and the mouth. 
<term>Heaven's Net (天網恢恢)</term>: A reference to the Daoist proverb "Heaven's net is vast; though its mesh is wide, nothing escapes," signifying that divine justice eventually finds everyone.

<summary>This page concludes the tale of Zhang Ke and his ghostly companion, who provides him with hidden gold and travels home with him in the form of a spirit tablet. The story ends with a reveal of karmic justice, as the woman apparently takes her revenge on a man named Yang before vanishing forever.</summary>

### The Skull at the Banquet Table

<margin>Lu Guimeng called himself the "Hermit of Rivers and Lakes," and Master Kong calls himself the "Descendant of Guimeng"; from this, one can see Master Kong’s true ambitions. <note>Lu Guimeng was a famous Tang Dynasty poet who lived as a recluse; Kong, though a merchant, clearly wishes to be seen as a sophisticated man of letters.</note></margin>
Kong Xilu of Bingzhou <note>modern-day Taiyuan</note> was a wealthy merchant. Though he spent his days in the marketplace, calculating every tiny gain and loss <term>zizhu</term> <note>original: "錙銖" (zīzhū); ancient units of weight used to describe extreme stinginess or meticulousness in business</note>, and although he had already worn himself out with the "drill and awl" of profit-seeking <note>a metaphor for the sharp, piercing pursuit of wealth</note> while traveling tirelessly between the regions of Long and Shu <note>modern Gansu and Sichuan</note>, he still harbored different aspirations. One day, while traveling for trade, he passed through Taipingdi. Staying at a lodging and feeling bored, he was overcome with deep homesickness. He always traveled with a zither and a sword as his companions and styled himself as the "Descendant of Guimeng."

<margin>He then chanted two poems in the "regulated verse" style; the first began: "Where is my home..."</margin>
"Where is my home? It is in Bingzhou. Across ten thousand miles of mountains and passes, sorrow flows without end. I stand alone in the evening glow of a rustic brewery, as the autumn colors of the city walls drift away with the empty river. My official ambitions are now humbled by this lonely scene <margin>Alluding to Tao <note>Likely Tao Yuanming, the quintessential recluse-poet.</note></margin>; how does my traveler’s state compare to the wanderings of old? <margin>Alluding to Qin <note>Possibly referring to Qin Jia, a poet known for his poems to his wife while traveling.</note></margin> A night of west wind blows my hat tattered; my heart, yearning for home, is sent far off to the head of the Chu River." The second poem said: "Green mountains for ten thousand miles, the waters overflow; a traveler should know how hard it is to vent his grief. The vegetation withers under the heavy autumn dew; the evening wind blows cold over the wandering soul in Hunan. At the edge of the sky, there is a traveler like Zhang Yong <note>A famous Northern Song official known for his martial prowess and integrity.</note>; on the eastern riverbank, who is there like the

<column-break/>

Xie daughter? <note>original: "謝女" (Xiè nǚ); refers to Xie Daoyun, a famed female poet of the Jin dynasty, used here as a symbol of a talented and beautiful woman.</note> Green hair and rosy cheeks are now all changed; pitifully, only a sliver of a loyal heart remains." Having finished his chanting, he leaned against the boat’s awning to rest for a moment. Suddenly, he heard the sound of sobbing—cries of such deep sorrow and perceived injustice that they were painful to hear. Master Kong looked up and saw a woman. Her makeup was light and her dress elegant, dressed in plain white mourning silk without patterns. Yet her natural poise and beauty were

<margin>At the water’s edge.</margin>
strikingly lush and magnificent. Sadly, she reached the water’s edge <margin>She was weeping and scolding, saying: "With you for half a year..."</margin> and, between her sobs and reproaches, cried out: "Our love and duty as husband and wife have been exhausted; how have I failed you that you drive me away? My own life is not worth pitying, <margin>If she did not weep, complain, or throw herself into the water, how could Master Kong have been moved to lend a helping hand?</margin> but how could you be so unfaithful? Even if I die and go to the Nine Springs <note>The Chinese underworld.</note>, I will certainly not stop there. I shall report this to the Arbiter of Fate <term>Siming</term> and the King of Hell <term>Yanjun</term> <note>The supreme judge of the dead.</note> to settle this with you once and for all! Do you think I am merely boasting?" Having spoken, she threw herself into the water. Master Kong, moved by pity, rushed forward to rescue her. He invited her onto his boat and, after a long while, asked: "My lady, why do you hold your life so lightly? If you have suffered some injustice, please try to tell me." She replied through her sobs: "I am the daughter of the Zhang family and the wife of the He family. Because I did not get along with my husband, I was driven out..."

The woman threatens to take her grievances to the King of Hell, setting the stage for a supernatural encounter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Kong Xilu, a merchant with the soul of a poet. Having previously introduced his background and his arrival at a lonely lodging, the story now details his encounter with a mysterious woman who appears to him in his solitude.</meta>

<margin>
The "Touch-Engraved" form transforms into a human; once human, it can speak. It can even compose lyrics, and the melody is quite extraordinary—how strange! How remarkable!
</margin>

> ...in vain, I sigh. How many times have the spring dreams been unclear? Sorrow turns toward autumn as the crickets chirp. The wind blows from the south, then blows again from the north. Once the Lord's favor is lost, it can never be regained. Floating clouds return to heaven in their due time, but once a Lord's heart is lost, how can it ever return? Do not forget the wife of "dregs and bran" <note>A "dregs and bran" wife refers to a spouse who shared one's poverty and hardship.</note>; let the deer-cart and thorn-hairpin <note>Symbols of a frugal but virtuous wife who shares her husband's struggles.</note> grow old together with you.

When the song ended, Master Kong praised her chanting, saying: "My lady, these lyrics deeply possess the spirit of loyalty and sincerity found in the 'Lessons from the States' <note>original: "Guofeng" (國風), the section of the Classic of Poetry dealing with folk songs and moral sincerity</note>."

<margin>
The Lessons from the States.
</margin>

One day, the woman felt a sense of dread in her heart. Weeping, she said to Master Kong: "When I was young, I was terrified by swords and blades, which resulted in a 'heart ailment' <note>likely a chronic nervous condition or trauma</note> that has not been cured to this day. Since you possess such a weapon, please wrap it in its case and hide it away. Do not let me see it, lest it cause a regret that lingers. Will you remember this?" <gloss>To remember or record.</gloss> Master Kong agreed.

A few days later, Kong happened to get very drunk. He completely forgot the woman's words and drew his sword to perform a dance. The woman suddenly cried out in terror and fled. Kong chased after her quickly, praying for her to stay, but there was nothing there but a single touch-engraved skull <term>chulou</term><note>original: "觸鏤" (chulou); refers to a skull that has been carved, engraved, or used as a drinking vessel, common in macabre legends of the borderlands.</note>.

<column-break/>

<margin>
The "Song of Dispelling Troubles" is also ultimately gentle, winding, and full of flavor.
</margin>

"I was about to throw myself into the deep abyss with my resentment, never expecting to receive such pity and be saved from drowning." Kong Xilu comforted her repeatedly, then slowly tested her intentions, asking: "I have no one to manage my household; would you be willing to follow me?" The woman <note>referring to herself as "Qufan," the dispeller of troubles</note> changed her expression and thanked him, saying: "I was cast out by my husband, and all bonds of love and duty were severed. If not for your helping hand, my body would have sunk into the drains and my bones would have rotted in the mud and sand. I would have watched the fish and shrimp pass by, my soul following the flowing water, drifting away. Now I am burdened with the grace of a second life—it is more than I can bear. Receiving your further affection, how could I not wish to serve you with 'broom and dustpan'?" <gloss>To serve as a wife.</gloss> Master Kong was greatly pleased. They consorted with one another, and their love was exceptionally deep. Upon the pillow, the woman composed the "Song of Dispelling Troubles" to express her resentment, saying:

> "Urgent, oh how urgent! Leaving the house, I dare not cry aloud. I remember when I was young and foolish and married you; I told myself we would stay together in life and death. Who knew that midway through, obstacles would arise? You discarded my rosy face as if it were mere dirt. My bow-shoes <note>referring to bound feet</note> are narrow and small, and the thorns are many; I hide my tears while walking, seeking the old path. Toward the flowing water, the flowers fade, silent and heartless; as spring returns again, the sorrow of parting grows. Waiting for the stairs to clear under a fair sky, while the overnight rain lingers all day, I lean against the railing..."

Beautiful Thoughts from the Fragrant Heart
Volume 2

<margin>The year 1449 marked the Tumu Crisis <note>original: "土木之變" - A disastrous military conflict where the Ming Emperor was captured by Mongols.</note>; were there really such wild rebels as Ye Zongliu in Chuzhou as well?</margin>

### The Jade Hairpin as a Token of Faith

In Yiwu County of Jinhua Prefecture, a son of the Zheng family took a daughter of the Shu family as his wife. This woman was exceptionally beautiful, intelligent, and skilled in poetry. At the age of sixteen, she managed the household affairs with the competence of a much older woman. Furthermore, she was filial to her parents-in-law and lived in harmony with her sisters-in-law; everyone both inside and outside the family praised her as a perfect domestic helper. In the fourteenth year of the Zhengtong era (1449), the wild rebel Ye Zongliu gathered a following in the mountains and forests of Chuzhou, plundering the local districts. Lady Shu was captured by them. The rebel leader was pleased by her appearance and tried to entice her in a hundred different ways, but she refused to yield to the end. Later, taking advantage of the rebels sleeping at night, she stole out of the camp gates and hanged herself with a silk scarf. She left behind an orphan who was only one year old. Inside her belt was a poem:

"Tears fall like a pair of jade hairpins <gloss>weeping and flowing</gloss> in the autumn wind,
Just like Lady Zhaojun <note>A famous Han Dynasty beauty sent to marry a barbarian ruler; a symbol of tragic exile.</note> leaving the Han Palace.
I know not what day my dreams of home will arrive;
It is hard to guess when a letter might get through.
A heart full of loyal blood is weighed down by infinite sorrow,
My knitted brows carry a resentment that never ends.
Amidst the chaos of spears and shields, dust fills the eyes;
All my heartbreak lies in what remains unsaid."

In the third year of the Jingtai era (1452), a fellow townsman named Lan Tian was passing by the place where Lady Shu had died. By then, the rebels had been pacified and the area was a desolate wasteland. At dusk, he saw the flicker of a fire and, walking toward it, found a magnificent house. Lan knocked on the door, and a maid came out to ask his business. After Lan explained the truth, the woman led him inside, saying, "The Mistress invites you for tea." Lan followed her in and saw a beautiful woman descending the stairs to welcome him. She invited him to sit as a guest and prepared a feast of fine wine and delicacies <gloss>meat pastes and spirits</gloss>. Lan asked, "What is your honorable surname, and why do you live here?" The beauty wept and said, "Does my neighbor

<margin>
Lady Shu died for her virtue. Her peril makes her virtue even more weighty. Therefore, her spirit is the same as the heroes of Suiyang <note>Referring to the famous Siege of Suiyang, where defenders chose death over surrender.</note>. How do those who would steal a living just to avoid death compare to her?
</margin>

not recognize me? I am your fellow townsman, the daughter of the Shu family and the wife of the Zhengs. I have been dead at the hands of those wild rebels for four years now. The Heavenly Court <note>The celestial bureaucracy in Chinese folk religion.</note> commended my chastity and appointed me as the *Secretary of the Thunder Bureau* <gloss>a title for an official in the underworld</gloss>. My inner sincerity has not yet been fully expressed, and I wished to rely on you, my neighbor, to reach my family and report." Lan said, "What has become of those rebels now?" The beauty replied, "Such treacherous rebels betrayed Heaven and Earth above, and their sovereign and parents below. In the world of the living, they were executed; in the world of the shades, they suffer for their crimes. Through ten thousand kalpas <note>A term for an incredibly long period of time in Buddhist and Taoist cosmology.</note>, they shall never again inhabit a human body." Lan asked, "Does the underworld truly provide such certain retribution?" The beauty...

<summary>This page tells the story of Lady Shu, a virtuous woman who committed suicide to protect her honor after being captured by rebels in 1449. Her spirit later appears to a neighbor to relay a message to her family and affirm the reality of divine justice.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific historical narrative of Lady Shu on the previous page to a broader philosophical and moral discourse regarding karmic retribution. It features a dialogue followed by a series of "judgment poems" describing the transformation of sinners into animals.</meta>

<margin>
Punished as cattle, punished as horses—the monks themselves should surely know what to avoid. If others remain ignorant of these punishments in the Underworld, they will behave lawlessly. This text uses praise and blame to show them; I know they will not dare to sin.
</margin>

Someone said: "Where there is the light of the living world, there must be the shadow of the world of the dead. Where there are humans, there must be ghosts. How can one say they do not exist?" Mr. Tian replied: "If that is so, then do those who spread good and evil truly receive no recompense?" The first person replied: "It is only that you have not seen it yet; in the end, it cannot be avoided. Allow me to speak briefly of the <term>karmic retribution</term> <note>original: "果報" (guobao) - the Buddhist concept of cause and effect where one's actions determine their future fate.</note> of the <term>Underworld Court</term> <note>original: "冥司" (mingsi) - the celestial bureaucracy that judges the dead.</note>." 

A certain monk was greedy and lustful, breaking the sacred laws; the Underworld Court punished him by turning him into an ox. The accompanying poem says:
"In former days, your <term>cultivation</term> <note>original: "脩行" (xiuxing) - spiritual practice or religious discipline.</note> was not firm of heart,
Now, your head and horns stand out sharp and tall.
In years to come, when spring plowing on the ridges is done,
You shall sleep as you please amidst the fragrant grass of the plains."

A certain Taoist priest was addicted to wine and obsessed with lust; the Underworld Court punished him by turning him into a horse. The poem says:
"In your past life, when did you ever sow seeds of goodness?
Now turned into a beast, who is there to pity you?
One day, when you walk with steady steps on the road to Chang'an,
Do not wait for the nobleman to strike you hard with his whip."

A certain man made his family's fortune through <unclear>lewdness</unclear> and filth; the Underworld Court punished him by turning him into a pig. The poem says:
"You harmed others to fatten yourself, acting with wild abandon,
Drinking a thousand bottles of wine and eating ten thousand sheep.
Today, you go happily as a scrawny pig,
Deep within the curved railing of the pen, filling yourself on dregs and husks."

A certain man stirred up and manipulated lawsuits; the Underworld Court punished him by turning him into a sheep. The poem says:
"Throughout your life, your writings and scrolls were the finest in the study; yet your pursuit of profit...

<column-break/>

<margin>
For those who possess wisdom but receive punishment, when the time for that punishment arrives, I know they will not be able to endure the "wind-knives" and the "grinding stones" <note>Traditional metaphors for the agonizing tortures of the Buddhist hells.</note> of the underworld.
</margin>

...has hindered others. Your thoughts did not harbor <gloss>goodness</gloss>. Now you have become 'soft-hair' <note>A traditional poetic alias for a sheep.</note> within this mortal world; if you are skinned and your meat is cooked, you have only yourself to blame."

A certain man was unfilial to his parents and unkind to his brothers; the Underworld Court punished him by turning him into a dog. The poem says:
"You acted as you pleased with hidden evils, disrupting the natural order of human relations,
Like an overturned basin where the light of the sun and moon cannot reach.
Now you lick food from the toilets of others and do not seek more;
From morning to evening, you must diligently guard the gate. <gloss>The evening is so very gloomy.</gloss>"

A certain woman was troublesome and unfilial to her parents-in-law, and she did not respect her husband; the Underworld Court punished her by turning her into a <term>hen</term> <note>original: "牝雞" (pinji) - literally a female chicken. This refers to the idiom "the hen crows at dawn," a traditional metaphor for a woman usurping a man's authority.</note>. The poem says:
"You did not follow the Way of Wives nor cultivate your character,
Arbitrarily seizing your husband's power and hating your kin.
Now as a hen, you must reflect upon yourself;
From this day forward, never again attempt to crow at dawn."

A certain man would drink wine and start fights; the Underworld Court punished him by turning him into a mule. The poem says:
"For half a lifetime, you were a 'hero' among the tangled weeds,
But your greed, lust, and love for fine clothes are pitiable indeed!
If you wish to know why your body is now draped in a coat of hair,
It is because your 'romantic' excesses brought this upon you."

A certain official who held a rank of ten thousand bushels of grain embezzled the people's wealth; the Underworld Court punished him by turning him into a rat. The poem says:
"By <term>kicking the bushel</term> <note>original: "踢斛" (tihu) - a corrupt practice where officials kicked the measuring grain container to make it settle, allowing them to overcharge taxpayers and pocket the surplus.</note> and letting grain spill, you took as much as you desired,
Embezzling government grain to make your private schemes.
Now you have transformed into the 'Star of Emptiness' <note>A reference to the rat's zodiacal associations or its nature as a scavenger of empty spaces.</note>; your millions..."

<summary>This page presents a series of moral warnings through the concept of karmic reincarnation, where specific sins like greed, unfilial behavior, and corruption lead to rebirth as various animals. Each transformation is accompanied by a poem that serves as a divine judgment on the sinner's past life.</summary>

All is based on the Great Mountain <note>original: "大山" (Dashan) - likely referring to Mount Tai, the site of judgment for the dead</note> to distinguish those who achieve benevolence. Your righteousness naturally belongs to the integrity of the dragon lamp; it is better to use it than to let it remain merely complete.

...of loyalty. Therefore, the *Book of Changes* says: "The king's servant is beset by difficulties, but in the end, there is no blame." Following others with steadfastness brings good fortune; it means following one person to the end. I often see those who would rather cut their throats <note>original: "刎首" (wenshou)</note> than serve rebels <gloss>such as the Beauty of Hejian</gloss>, or those who climb over walls to follow lovers <gloss>as the saying goes regarding the Xun River</gloss>. This is the shame of having no shame; truly shameless! Master Wenshan <gloss>Wen Tianxiang</gloss> <note>A famous Song Dynasty loyalist and poet known for his martyrdom</note> said: "Confucius spoke of achieving benevolence; Mencius spoke of choosing righteousness." Only when one's duty to righteousness is fulfilled can the highest benevolence be reached. Therefore, there is no reason for me to cling to life shamefully. 

The *Book of Odes* says: "Alas, my parents! They gave me life with toil and labor." From this, we know the kindness of one's parents must be repaid. The *Book of Rites* says: "Serving one's parents-in-law is like serving one's own parents." From this, we know the virtue of parents-in-law must be compensated. I had my hair pinned at fifteen <note>The "Ji" ceremony marking a girl's coming of age</note> and was married at sixteen. I looked forward to the eternal harmony of the <term>zither and lute</term> <note>original: "琴瑟" (qinse) - a metaphor for a happy marriage</note>. Who would have thought disaster would arise from within the family? Villains like <term>city foxes and altar rats</term> <note>original: "城狐社鼠" - a metaphor for corrupt people who rely on their positions to do evil</note> acted as demons; they committed evils like giant boars and long snakes. Driven by thunder and chased by lightning, my soul trembled with fear. The waters of the stream were parted, and the <term>clouds and rain of Mount Wu</term> <note>original: "雲雨" (yunyu) - a classical euphemism for sexual intimacy</note> were severed. Alas! The days are bleak, the hundred flowers wither, and the reeds grow in chaotic tangles.

Do not steal mindlessly from the granary. As for rebellious subjects and treacherous sons, they shall fall forever into perdition, seeking the heights but never reaching them. Mr. Tian thanked her and departed, riding with the letter to the Zheng residence. The two families returned her sentiments along with the jade hairpins. Her parents-in-law and parents exclaimed: "These are the belongings of the deceased spirit!" Greatly shocked, they read the letter, which said: "I humbly believe that the <term>Three Bonds and Five Constants</term> <note>original: "綱常" (gangchang) - the fundamental moral relationships in Confucianism</note> are the vital energy of the nation and the great foundation of the human body. If the state discards these, it destroys its vital energy and ruins the moral order. If a person discards these, they lose their foundation and follow customs that extinguish Heavenly Principle. This is why Confucius edited the *Spring and Autumn Annals* and Master Zhu <note>Zhu Xi, the preeminent Neo-Confucian scholar</note> wrote the *Outline of the Comprehensive Mirror*. All were intended to uphold Heavenly Principle, suppress human desire, and <term>rectify names</term> <note>original: "正名" (zhengming) - the Confucian doctrine that things should match their descriptions</note> to establish the Bonds and Constants. Furthermore, a woman serving her husband is like a subject serving their lord. When a woman serves her husband with a single heart, it is called <term>integrity</term> <note>original: "節" (jie) - specifically female chastity and moral persistence</note>. When a subject serves their lord with a single heart, it is called..."

How beautiful is this path! Yet one can only gaze toward the white clouds and sigh deeply, or observe the spring trees with distant longing. How can one soar high or leave a lasting legacy so great that it might reach the heights where the "sound of the cock flies to heaven"? <note>original: "翰音之登天" (hanyin zhi dengtian) — a reference to the *Book of Changes*, suggesting an empty reputation or an effort that reaches high but lacks substance. Here, she questions how one can achieve true immortality through virtue.</note> I often say to myself: the poets of old used the metaphor of "wading deep or shallow" <note>original: "深厲淺揭" (shenli qianqi) — from the *Classic of Poetry*. It refers to crossing a river by either wading in or lifting one's skirts, symbolizing how one should navigate moral difficulties according to their severity.</note> to criticize those who elope or act out of lust, for such people lack both distinction and righteousness. 

To master the essential teachings of the "Three Constancies" <term>San zhen: a reference to the lifelong commitment to chastity and moral integrity</term>, one must be prepared to face death. I would rather cast my body into a "Pool of Pearls" <note>A metaphor for a virtuous but perhaps tragic end</note> than settle for the safety of being a "whole tile." <note>original: "瓦全" (waquan) — from the idiom "better to be a broken piece of jade than a whole tile," meaning it is better to die with honor than to live in shame.</note> 

Therefore, amidst the thickets of thorns and brambles, the soul rises and the spirit falls. Beneath the fragrant silk handkerchief, the jade is shattered and the flowers fly <note>Traditional euphemisms for the death or suicide of a virtuous woman</note>. All of this is done to uphold the fundamental social bonds <term>Gangchang: the "Three Bonds and Five Constants" that form the bedrock of Confucian ethics</term> and to shame those of later generations who are disloyal as ministers or unfaithful as wives. 

Writing cannot express all that I wish to say. I only hope for your honorable consideration. The entire family is overcome with grief. My companions and I later visited the place where this occurred, but we found nothing more than lush, towering trees and dense forest. Where, indeed, did that grand house and the beautiful woman go?

# The Demon Fox of the Government Office

<margin>
It had been uninhabited for a long time. Was it due to a cursed aura, or was there something physically possessing the space that made it so?
</margin>

During the Hongzhi era <note>The reign of the Ming Emperor Xiaozong, 1488–1505</note>, Junior Preceptor Ma Wensheng <gloss>Ma Wensheng, whose courtesy name was Wensheng</gloss> was serving as an Investigating Censor <note>A high-ranking official responsible for monitoring government conduct and inspecting provinces</note> and was on an inspection tour of the outlying provinces. One particular government office had been occupied by a demon fox and had remained uninhabited for many years. When Minister Ma arrived at the county, he insisted on staying there. The local officials and clerks all tried to dissuade him by recounting past horrors, but Ma would not listen. He resolutely unlocked the doors and took up residence. At dusk, he sat alone in the hall, pouring himself drinks from a large goblet. <gloss>A *gong* is a type of wine vessel, often carved from animal horn.</gloss> He then composed and inscribed a poem in the "eight-line regulated" style:

> Long have I been a traveler, languishing in illness for a year,
> My eyes strain against the west wind, gazing toward the skies of Chu.
> I cannot bear the autumn scenery that mocks my once-dark hair,
> I only pity how the colors of the season transform the mountains and rivers.
> Fame and merit are but lonely things, drifting beyond the clouds,
> The affairs of the world are fragmented here by the banks of the Han River.
> I remember my old friend and our words at parting;
> Thinking of how last night’s rain passed, my tears fall like a stream.

He sighed and took another drink, showing not the slightest hint of fear. The presiding clerks were all trembling with fright. The areas before the screens and behind the halls were deathly silent; no one dared to make a sound or even breathe loudly. Ma sat for a long time until he was slightly tipsy, then he began to walk through the front and back of the rooms.

<column-break/>

<margin>
Ma Dingchen <note>Another name for Ma Wensheng</note> poured wine and offered it to the demon, and the specter accepted and drank several times. Yet Ma remained as steady as a mountain. This is what is meant by the saying: "If the heart has a master, evil cannot interfere."
</margin>

In the light of the lamps and candles, he saw a folding screen depicting an old pine tree, its shape strangely twisted and ancient. He immediately took up a brush and, by the candlelight, inscribed a poem upon it:

> Leaning against the void, the tall tree stands cold and free of dust,
> As I idly recall the past, my dreams seem ready to part.
> Your emerald hue is best appreciated after the winter frost,
> Your shivering sound is heard most clearly beneath the moon.
> The crying gibbons surely bring the rain from the green mountains,
> And the returning cranes must harmonize with the clouds of the immortal realm.
> Do not go to the eastern garden to join the peach and plum blossoms;
> For the beauty of the spring light still has no room for one such as you.
<note>In Chinese poetry, the pine represents the noble scholar who remains upright in hardship, unlike the "peach and plum" which are beautiful but fleeting.</note>

He returned to the center of the room to continue drinking. After the first watch of the night <note>Approximately 7:00 PM to 9:00 PM</note>, a whistling cold wind blew, and something appeared upon the roof beams. The demon revealed its face, grinding its teeth and stretching its mouth so wide it seemed as large as the ridgepole itself. Ma looked at it for a moment as if nothing were there. The creature then descended from the beam and took on a human shape, looking hideous and extremely terrifying. <gloss>"Hideous" (zhenning) refers to something ghostly and strange.</gloss> It looked at Ma and said, "Since you are drinking alone, would you not give a drop to me, a ghost?" Ma took a vessel, poured some wine, and handed it over. The demon accepted and drank several times. Ma remained completely unmoved. By the fourth watch <note>Approximately 1:00 AM to 3:00 AM</note>, the creature's form gradually shrank. Suddenly, it revealed its original shape—it was nothing but an old fox. Ma immediately seized it, bound it, and stuffed it into his travel chest. The clerks later remarked that this demon had been a plague for a long time. Once the Minister...

<summary>This page tells the story of the courageous official Ma Wensheng, who expels a long-standing demon fox from a haunted government office through his inner strength and composure. The narrative emphasizes that a person of high moral character and a "steady heart" is immune to supernatural terrors.</summary>

<keywords>Ma Wensheng, demon fox, Ming Dynasty, Hongzhi era, ghost story, officialdom, courage, poetry, pine tree symbolism</keywords>

<center>Selections of Refined Conversations, Volume 3</center>

<margin>The report of the spy in the barbarian camp and the warning from the ghostly fox were how it repaid the kindness of not being killed while trapped in the box.</margin>

As the eastern sky began to brighten toward dawn, Ma recited another poem, saying:
<insert>One should leave the lonely lodge as the single lamp fades.</insert>

> The stars fall into the River of Heaven as dawn approaches,
> The celestial cock crows, yet the moon still hangs over Hangu Pass.
> The cries of geese have just passed through the mists of Baling,
> In this drifting life, I have already awakened from Zhuang Zhou’s dream. <note>A reference to the philosopher Zhuangzi, who famously dreamt he was a butterfly, symbolizing the illusory nature of reality.</note>
> My great ambitions are still driven by Zu Ti’s whip, <note>Zu Ti was a general known for his dedication, rising at the first cock-crow to practice his swordplay.</note>
> But today, jackals and wolves block the road and stain my path;
> How could an old fox dare to linger here?

By daybreak, the local officials and clerks were struck with awe and respect. Ma eventually left the post. Later, he was demoted to the Chongqing Guard in Jiangxi <note>A military administrative post</note>. Soon after, an uprising of "barbarians" occurred in Sichuan province. The commander in charge of the troops, knowing that Ma’s reputation was held in high regard by the central imperial court, requested his assistance.

When they reached a certain location, a man came to visit Ma, presenting his name on a calling card. He told Ma: "Tomorrow, when the army reaches a certain spot, there will be a spy from the barbarian camp. You must be prepared; this is the best strategy." Having said this, he immediately vanished. Ma realized this was the "Ghostly Fox" giving him a warning. He informed the commander, and they set a clever ambush. That night, the enemy arrived and suffered a crushing defeat. Because of this, Ma Wensheng's prestige grew even greater. He was eventually recalled to the capital, where he served as the Minister of Personnel <term>Zhongzai: The head of the Ministry of Personnel, often called the "Head of the Hundred Officials."</term> and was granted the titles of the Three Solitaries <term>Sangu: High-ranking honorary titles: Junior Preceptor, Junior Tutor, and Junior Guardian.</term>. He truly became a celebrated statesman of his era.

<column-break/>

<margin>The old fox, bound inside the box, spoke words of prophecy; how could one know then that they would be proven true years later during the campaign in Sichuan?</margin>

"The fox should leave this place immediately," Ma said. From inside the box, the fox wept and said, "Although I have committed offenses, were it not for the fact that you are a great statesman of this generation, protected and sustained by a hundred gods, how could I have escaped being eaten?" <gloss>To be consumed.</gloss> "From this day forward, your rank, title, and longevity will be preeminent in this age. However, in the midst of your rise, there will be a setback. During that time of difficulty, you will surely achieve a miraculous success. At that moment, someone will come to visit you using my name. That will be me. My words will lead you to that great achievement; this is my way of repaying you."

Ma replied, "You need only leave this place, and I shall release you. I do not care for such rewards." The fox said, "I respectfully receive your instruction." Ma then opened the box, untied the bonds, and let it go.

The prison clerk still voiced his doubts, saying the Minister should not have so easily let the creature go. The fox then reassumed human form and said, "The reason I did not dare unleash my venomous mischief was because a famous official was present. If not for him, what would be the difficulty in devouring you lot to fill my belly? Since the Minister has released me, yet you people still mock me, I shall show you my true form." It then leaped into the rafters, revealing its original shape—the same fox as before. A moment later, it vanished. Only then did the clerk regret his spoken words.

<summary>Ma Wensheng releases a captured fox demon after it prophesies his future greatness and a moment of crisis. Years later, the fox returns in disguise to warn Ma of an enemy spy, leading to a military victory that cements his legacy as a legendary statesman.</summary>
<keywords>Ma Wensheng, Fox Demon, Prophecy, Sichuan Campaign, Ming Dynasty Officials, Supernatural Gratitude</keywords>

## The demon fox is struck dead by a thunderbolt, and this man’s case is judged once more in Yaozhou City. <note>In traditional folklore, "death by thunder" represents divine execution by the Heavens for spirits that have overstepped their bounds or deceived humans.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the general introductory themes of divine justice and moral consequences into the specific narrative of the "Fox Enchantress of Xinzheng." <margin>
Huang Xing conspires with his wife; Langu <note>Xiao Yu’s courtesy name</note> is lured by beauty. In one move, both are lost.
</margin>
On his way to take up his new post as the Vice Magistrate of Yaozhou, Xiao Yu passed through Xinzheng. Huang Xing was a cousin to the Xinzheng Magistrate, Peng Zhihe, and so Xiao Yu went to visit him. Magistrate Peng arranged for him to stay at the official post station. Huang Xing, who was performing his duties there, saw that Xiao Yu was young, unrestrained, and possessed a bold, heroic appearance; furthermore, his luggage was quite substantial. Huang then said to his wife: "We are poor, but our fortune is about to change. I have a plan for Xiao Yu." He repeatedly ordered Meiniang to go fetch water from the well. When Xiao Yu saw her, he was indeed delighted and immediately asked to take her as a concubine. Huang said: "If the official truly wishes to marry my daughter, it will require ten times the usual dowry." Xiao Yu did not begrudge the cost and exhausted his funds to complete the arrangement. He then took her with him to his new post. Meiniang was naturally intelligent and, moreover, gentle and obedient. From the wife of the Prefect down to the households of the various officials, she presented each with a piece of fine silk and ten packets of rouge. She served her elders and cared for the young, winning the hearts of everyone. Because of this, she was praised both inside and outside the home; no one had a word of criticism against her. Whenever guests arrived, even before Xiao Yu could give the order, wine and food would appear as soon as he called, with the abundance or simplicity of the meal always being perfectly appropriate. In her spare time, she personally engaged in spinning and reeling silk. She remained deep within the inner chambers, and her feet never—

# The Fox Enchantress of Xinzheng

<margin>
Hu Meiniang creates a scene of distress; her demeanor seems enough to move one to pity her bitter suffering.
</margin>
Huang Xing, a worker at the Xinzheng Post Station, happened to be returning home late one night. While resting under the trees, he saw a fox pick up a human skull and place it on its head. The fox bowed toward the moon, and in an instant, transformed into a woman. She appeared to be sixteen or seventeen years old, possessed of absolute beauty, and she began to weep along the road to Xinzheng. Walking and wailing at the same time, she was followed by Huang Xing, who watched her closely <gloss>the original term means "to peer at or watch"</gloss>. The fox did not realize she was being observed by Huang and continued to strike a charming, delicate pose. Huang thought to himself: "This is rare merchandise worth hoarding." <note>A classic idiom (qihuokeju) meaning to hold onto something unique to sell for a great profit later.</note> He then asked: "Whose daughter are you? Why are you walking alone late at night?" She replied: "I am from Hangzhou, my surname is Hu, and my name is Meiniang. My father was being transferred to an official post in Shaanxi when we were ambushed by bandits at the village ahead. My parents and brothers were all killed, and our wealth was stripped bare. I alone survived by hiding in the wild grass. Now, in my lonely suffering, I wish for death, which is why I am weeping." Huang said: "Can you find a home with me?" The girl held back her tears and followed Huang home. When they arrived, Huang’s wife saw how gentle and submissive she was and treated her well. However, Huang never told his wife the truth of what he had seen. At that time, there was a Graduate <term>Jinshi</term> named Xiao Yu, a man of the Eight Markets <gloss>refers to a bustling urban district</gloss>—

<summary>This page recounts the origin of the fox-spirit Hu Meiniang, who transforms into a beautiful woman to deceive a post station worker and eventually marries an official named Xiao Yu. It highlights her calculated charm and her ability to perfectly mimic the virtues of a traditional wife to gain social standing.</summary>
<keywords>Fox-spirit (Yaohu), Xinzheng Post Station, Xiao Yu, Huang Xing, transformation, filial piety, dowry, deception, Ming dynasty folklore</keywords>

# Drafts of Pure Stories

<margin>
Did Yin Danran know of the demon beforehand because of his keen observation, or had he heard of it before? If the original scheme was used to prey, then it was like a hunter in the bamboo.
</margin>

Even outside the private quarters, whenever people in the district had indecisive matters, they would consult her. She would analyze and solve every issue one by one, fully discerning the truth of every situation. Xiao Yu boasted of having such "inner assistance" <note>A traditional term for a wise and supportive wife</note>, and among his colleagues, she was increasingly believed to be a virtuous wife and daughter-in-law.

Before long, the Provincial Office heard of Yu's talent and commissioned him to supervise grain collection across various prefectures. Meiniang said to Yu: "Strive in your public duties and devote your heart to the King's affairs. I can handle the trivial matters of the inner chambers. You must value your <term>thousand-gold body</term> <note>original: "千金之軀" (qiānjīn zhī qū); a common idiom for the precious health of a person of high status</note> to seek a way to repay my humble devotion." Yu nodded in agreement and departed.

While traveling, he stayed overnight at the Chongyang Temple. There, a Daoist named Yin Danran saw him and whispered privately to Yu’s clerk, Zhou Rong: "The official's demonic aura is very strong. If this is not treated, there will be great sorrow later." Rong told Yu, but Yu simply scolded him. That winter, Yu returned to the public office to handle state affairs, but in <term>mid-spring</term> <note>The second month of the lunar calendar</note>, he fell gravely ill. Medicine and treatments were unsuccessful. Zhou Rong remembered Daoist Yin's words and reported everything to the Prefect. The Prefect immediately sent for Yin. Yin told the Prefect in private: "The family member in the Xiao household is actually an **old fox spirit** from the North Gate of Xinzheng. If she is not removed, the disaster will be unpredictable."

<column-break/>

<margin>
The fox's charm is the same. Huang Xing encountered her and gained a post; Xiao Yu encountered her and suffered calamity. Why is the encounter the same, but the outcome so different?
</margin>

The Prefect was shocked and said: "Lord Xiao’s wife is praised by all as virtuous. How can such a claim be made?"

Danran replied: "Just wait until tomorrow, and you shall see."

He then went to the back hall of the Prefectural Office and established an altar. At noon the next day, Danran brandished his sword and wrote talismans, summoning the **Divine Generals** <note>Celestial warrior spirits in Daoist exorcism rites</note>. Suddenly, black clouds like spilled ink appeared, and white rain fell like overturned basins. With a clap of thunder, Meiniang was struck dead; all was silent. Within the room, the Prefect led his subordinates to look. It was indeed a fox, and a human skull was still resting on its head.

The various family members hurriedly took the gifts she had given them and looked at them: the green silk turned into several banana leaves, and the rouge became a few peach blossom petals. When these were shown to Yu, he finally understood. Yin burned the corpse to end the haunting, and Yu’s illness began to heal. People were sent to Xinzheng to ask about Huang Xing; he had already moved away, and his family was now wealthy. He was no longer a station clerk, having enriched himself through the dowry wealth Xiao Yu had provided.

<summary>This page concludes the story of the Fox Spirit of Xinzheng, describing how a Daoist priest reveals Meiniang's true form, leading to the discovery that her "virtuous" gifts were mere illusions of leaves and petals.</summary>
<keywords>Fox spirit (Huli Jing), Xiao Yu, Yin Danran, exorcism, Daoist talismans, illusion, Xinzheng, supernatural retribution</keywords>

# The Yuan Family of Luoyang

During the Guangde era <note>The reign of Emperor Daizong of Tang, 763–765 AD.</note>, there was a scholar named Sun Ke. Having failed his imperial examinations, he was traveling through Luoyang. While walking by the side of King Wei’s Pond, he saw a grand mansion surrounded by magnificent trees. Passersby pointed to it and said, "That is the residence of the Yuan family." Sun Ke went straight to the gate and knocked on the door <gloss>the door panels</gloss>, but no one answered. Beside the gate was a small room with very clean curtains. Sun Ke, thinking it was a place for guests to wait, lifted the curtain and entered. After a long while, he suddenly heard a door opening. A young woman appeared, her radiance reflecting off the very objects around her. Her beauty was startling—like a pearl newly washed in moonlight, or a willow tree beginning to catch the misty charm of spring. She was as fragrant as an orchid and as pure as polished jade. Sun Ke suspected she was the daughter of the master of the house, but he merely peered at her in secret. The woman picked some <term>Daylilies</term> <note>Original: *xuancao*. Also known as "forget-worry grass," these flowers were symbols of maternal love or a desire to dispel sorrow.</note> in the courtyard and stood for a long time in deep contemplation. She then composed a poem:

<margin>Since he had already slipped inside, he should have remained hidden; why then was he met with the maid’s questioning and the lady’s sudden appearance in all her splendor?</margin>

->Others see this as "forgetting worry," but to me it looks like rotting grass.
Only with the green mountains and white clouds can I truly open my heart.<-

As she finished reciting, her expression became sorrowful. She then lifted the curtain, saw Sun Ke, and retreated inside with a look of startled shame. She sent a <term>maidservant</term> <note>Original: *qingyi*, literally "green-clad," a standard term for a young female servant.</note> to question him. Sun Ke offered his apologies and expressed his regret for the intrusion.

<column-break/>

Sun Ke pressed his request to see her again. Eventually, the woman appeared in her full finery to meet him. She ordered her maids to bring tea and fruit, and said, "Since you, sir, have no lodgings, you may move your belongings into this hall." Pointing to the maidservant, she told Sun Ke, "If you have any small needs, just inform these girls." Once inside, Sun Ke asked the maid, "Whose daughter is she?" The maid replied, "She is the daughter of the late Official Yuan. She was orphaned young and lost her father; she has no other relatives. Only three or five of us servants live here in this mansion." Sun Ke then sent for a matchmaker to propose marriage. The woman joyfully accepted, and he took her as his wife. The Yuan family was immensely wealthy, possessing vast amounts of gold and <term>silks</term> <gloss>textiles</gloss>. Sun Ke, who had long been poor, suddenly

<margin>It is human nature to love virtue and beauty. Sun Ke, a poor scholar, obtained great wealth and a beautiful wife; his joy and envy must have been tenfold that of an ordinary man.</margin>

found himself with brilliant horses and carriages, and his clothes and trinkets were of the most exquisite quality. This raised many suspicions among his friends and relatives, many of whom came to question him, but Sun Ke remained silent. He enjoyed his great wealth for three or four years without ever leaving Luoyang. Later, he encountered a maternal cousin surnamed Zhang, who told Sun Ke that his house was filled with a powerful "demonic air." Zhang gave him a precious sword to take home. When the wife saw the sword, she snapped it into pieces as easily as if she were breaking a tender lotus root. When Zhang heard of this, he was terrified and dared not enter the house again. Sun Ke remained in Luoyang for ten years...

<summary>A failed scholar named Sun Ke encounters and marries a mysterious, wealthy woman from the Yuan family in Luoyang. Despite their domestic bliss, a cousin suspects her of being a supernatural being after she effortlessly destroys a magical sword meant to ward off evil.</summary>
<keywords>Sun Ke, Yuan Family, Luoyang, supernatural marriage, demon, precious sword, Tang Dynasty fiction, Daylily, transformation</keywords>
