<meta>This page continues the story of Sun Ke (referred to here as Ke) and his wife, Mrs. Yuan, whom he met at a mysterious mansion in Luoyang. They have been married for several years.</meta>

# Extensive Records of Strange Things
## Volume 22

<margin>Looking at the green mountains and high pines, she felt a restlessness. The old ape within perhaps could not help itself, and so she eventually returned to her original form.</margin>

Over the passing years, Mrs. Yuan raised two children. She managed the household with great strictness and did not socialize with outsiders. Later, Ke went to the capital, Chang'an, to visit his old friend, the State Chancellor Wang Jin <note>Wang Jin (700–781 AD) was a high-ranking official and the brother of the famous poet Wang Wei.</note>. Wang Jin recommended Ke to Zhang Wanqing, the Military Commissioner of Nankang, to serve as an Administrative Judge <term>經略判官 (Jinglue Panguan)</term> <note>A staff officer position responsible for regional military and administrative affairs.</note>. On the journey to his new post, whenever Mrs. Yuan encountered high green mountains or ancient pine trees, she would stare at them for a long time. Her gaze would become fixed, and she appeared deeply troubled or dissatisfied.

When they reached Ruizhou <note>Located in modern-day Jiangxi Province.</note>, Mrs. Yuan said, "Half a day's journey from here is the Qianshan Temple. My family once had a disciple there, a monk named Hui, who lives in seclusion. This monk can discern the true nature of one's physical form <note>original: "別形骸" - literally 'distinguish the skeletal frame,' meaning to see through illusions.</note> and is skilled at helping one transcend the mundane world. If we stop there to provide a ritual meal for him, it will surely bring blessings to our journey south." Ke agreed and prepared the necessary vegetarian offerings and supplies.

Upon arriving at the temple, Mrs. Yuan happily changed her clothes, arranged her hair, and took her two children to the monk’s quarters. She walked the paths as if she knew them by heart, which Ke found quite strange. She then presented a green jade ring to the monk, saying, "This is an old object from this temple." The monk, however, did not recognize it. After the ritual meal was finished, dozens of wild apes descended from the high pines, linking arms. They ate upon the terrace, then began to howl mournfully, grasping at the vines and leaping about. Mrs. Yuan appeared deeply moved and saddened. Suddenly—

<column-break/>

<margin>To tear one's clothes and transform into an ape—such transformations are part of the natural order of things, yet they are also bound by the fate of the master. That is sufficient explanation.</margin>

—she called for a brush and wrote a poem on the temple wall:

->*Severing the ties of love, from this heart hence,*
*Through many aimless changes, I have drifted deep.*
*Better to follow my companions back to the mountains,*
*With one long howl, lost where the mist and clouds are deep.*<-

She then threw the brush to the ground, stroked her two children, and sobbed several times. She said to Ke, "Live well, live well! We must part forever now." 

She suddenly tore her clothes and transformed into an old ape. With a cry, she chased after the other howling apes and leapt into the trees. As she headed toward the deep mountains, she turned back many times to look at Ke. Ke was struck with terror and shock <gloss>appearance of being shaken</gloss>; his soul felt as if it had flown away, and he was utterly devastated. After a long time, he held his two children and wept bitterly. 

He then questioned the old monk. Only then did he realize that this ape had originally been raised in the temple. As for the green jade ring, <gloss>it was originally donated by a merchant from Heling</gloss> <note>Heling was an ancient kingdom on the island of Java, known for maritime trade with the Tang Dynasty.</note> and had been worn around the ape's neck for a long time. Overcome with melancholy, Ke moored his boat for several days, unable to proceed. Eventually, he took his two children, turned his boat around, and went home, never accepting the commission from the Military Commissioner.

<summary>Mrs. Yuan reveals her true nature as a transformed ape during a visit to a temple, leaving her husband and children to return to the wild. The story concludes with Sun Ke's grief and his decision to abandon his career.</summary>

My life is a constant bustle, but what has been achieved?
I wish to live in seclusion and hide my name.

Remaining clouds carry rain as the spring weather clears.

I pass the years in vain without meeting my match,
Leaving my home, I cherish the land and my idle thoughts.

> My whole body is chilled by the wind and dew.
> By the curved railing, the voices of people are still,

> Then I shall meet face-to-face with the Goddess Chang'e.

> The cool shadow of the Shala tree falls into the ice-pure jar.
> <note>The "ice jar" is a poetic metaphor for the moon or the clear night sky.</note>
> Year after year, I practice the Song of the Feathered Raiment in vain,
> Not knowing if we shall meet again at the third watch of the night.

Shi Ming saw that her beauty was such that he could no longer restrain himself <gloss>read in the level tone</gloss>. Telling himself that no one would know in the depths of the night, he hurried forward and asked her, "Lady, why are you bowing to the moon?" The beauty smiled calmly.

# The Moon-Worshipping Beauty

Shi Ming of Xuyi, whose courtesy name was Huizhi, was in his twenties. He had a handsome appearance and was skilled in poetry and prose. However, his path was frustrated and his "red leaves" had sunk—meaning his luck in love and career had failed him—and he was not yet married <gloss>The text notes that for a man, "room" means taking a wife</gloss>. During the Hongwu era <note>The reign of the first Ming Emperor, 1368–1398</note>, he took his capital and traded goods as a merchant, traveling about. One day, while sailing down the Yangtze River with the current, his solitary sail caught the sun and the wind. He traveled hundreds of miles, passing the Three Mountains and heading toward the capital. At dusk, he moored at Guabukou. That night, a long rain had just cleared. A faint moon reflected on the lonely boat. All around him, everything was silent <gloss>the term means quiet and still</gloss>.

He felt increasingly lonely and clear-headed. For a long time, the sound of the wind shook the bamboo and trees, and dogs barked mournfully <gloss>the term describes a sad, howling cry</gloss>. Ming could not sleep. He left the boat, opened his coat to the breeze, and walked idly, eventually chanting a quatrain:

> The stems of floating-hearts and reed buds blur in the distance,
> White gulls fly back and forth near the traveler.
> By the water's edge, the mossy stones are a deep green,
> The bright moon and reed flowers fill the fishing pier.

Having finished his chant, he walked further and heard the deep, echoing sound of drums from a distant temple...

<summary>A handsome but lonely merchant named Shi Ming, unable to sleep while moored on the Yangtze, encounters a mysterious beauty bowing to the moon and chanting poetry. A marginal note warns that the woman is actually a fox spirit in disguise.</summary>
<keywords>Shi Ming, Moon-Worshipping Beauty, Hongwu Era, Yangtze River, Fox Spirit, Chang'e, Guabukou, Poetry</keywords>

### Page Thirteen
### Volume Three

He spoke to her, saying, "If you wish to find a fine husband, you should pray to the Old Man Under the Moon." <gloss>The Old Man Under the Moon is the deity in charge of the registers of marriage.</gloss> "What is my lady's wish for a husband tomorrow?" The beauty replied, "Having you, sir, is enough." Truly, in this world, some marriages are difficult to encounter yet easy to join; tonight was such an occasion. He invited her to his boat to perform the <term>nuptial rites</term><note>The original text refers to "hejin," the ritual of drinking from a split gourd to seal a marriage.</note>. <margin>Scholar Dou and the beauty performed their wedding ceremony inside the boat. This refers to the act of marrying without the command of parents or the mediation of a matchmaker.</margin> The beauty showed no sign of hesitation and joyfully followed him. Hand in hand, they boarded the boat and drank together under the light of the moon. Afterward, Scholar Dou and she coupled, enjoying the height of pleasure.

The following day, they hurried the boat back to Ganyi. Ming presented the beauty to his parents and the clan, claiming that he had married a woman from the Huang family <gloss>referring to her clan</gloss> at Guabu. Once the beauty entered the Dou household, she was diligent in spinning and weaving, and she prepared the family meals with care. Her parents-in-law called her filial; the clan called her harmonious; her sisters-in-law called her righteous; and the neighbors and servants called her peaceful and forgiving. Everyone, both inside the house and out, spoke of her in unison as a virtuous helpmate.

At that time, the successor to the Celestial Master, the Daoist Master Zhang, was traveling to the capital and passed through Ganyi. He pointed at the Dou residence and said, "There is a demonic aura here; I must go and eliminate it." Those who heard this were shocked and found it strange, not quite believing him. Before this...

<column-break/>

...the beauty wept and said to Ming, "In three days, a great disaster will be upon us. I will surely die." Ming was shocked and asked for the reason, but the beauty concealed it and would not say. She only said, "Do not forget our love. Even if I die in the Nine Springs <note>The "Nine Springs" is a traditional term for the underworld or the land of the dead.</note>, I shall still have a smile on my face."

As calculated, four days later, the Celestial Master suddenly arrived, brandishing a sword at the door. Onlookers gathered until the place was as crowded as a marketplace. The beauty was struck with terror and lost her composure; she tried to leap away to hide, but the Celestial Master shouted, "Demon fox! Where do you think you are going?"

The beauty then prostrated herself on the ground and tearfully recited a regulated poem:

> Once I borrowed the tiger’s awe-inspiring power,
> In the mountains, the hundred beasts could not deceive me.
> I listened to the ice in the deep freezing winter,
> And wandered the wild wastes in the dark of night.
> <margin>The beauty saw the Celestial Master and could not escape, eventually becoming a victim of the blade. This illustrates that evil cannot overcome the upright.</margin>
> After a thousand years, I transformed into a beauty,
> At the fifth watch, I learned to cry like an infant.
> In this era of the Holy Sovereign’s peaceful rule,
> The Nine-Tailed Fox shall surely appear as a lucky omen.

The Celestial Master swung his sword and beheaded her; she was merely a fox. Ming bowed and thanked him.

<summary>Scholar Dou marries the mysterious woman without formal rites, only for her to be revealed as a thousand-year-old fox spirit by the Celestial Master Zhang. Despite her virtuous behavior as a wife, she is executed, fulfilling her tragic premonition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the thematic exploration of "Poverty First, Wealth Afterward" introduced on the previous page. While the previous page used historical icons like Fan Shu and Tao Zhu Gong to illustrate the concept, this page begins a specific narrative about a scholar named Lei Qizhe, whose life serves as a practical example of these shifting fates.</meta>

# Encountering Treasure by the Eastern Wall

<gloss>Scholar Lei’s journey from early poverty to later wealth is a matter of fate. That he stayed in the temple and did not die is fate. That he twice obtained bright pearls and reached a state of great wealth is fate. His rise in status is also fate; can a person truly control these things through their own efforts?</gloss>

In the year of *Xinmao* during the <term>Chenghua</term><note>The reign of the Ming dynasty Emperor Xianzong, specifically the year 1471.</note> reign, there lived a man in Yongping Prefecture named Scholar Lei; his given name was Qizhe. Having lost his parents at a young age, he dedicated himself to the path of a <term>Confucian scholar</term><note>original: "儒業" (ruye), referring to the pursuit of classical education and the civil service examinations.</note>. He spent his days sitting on a cold felt mat, wielding his red-ink brush in study, his feet never venturing beyond his doorstep. However, he had no means of making a living, and as time passed, his household became increasingly destitute. He wished to keep his door closed and maintain his integrity, but how could he endure the hunger of his own body? He wished to look upward and ask others for help, but how could his heart bear such shame? Eventually, he reached a point where he could no longer survive. 

West of the city stood an ancient temple where demons and spirits were said to roam. Those who sought shelter in that temple often vanished, their remains never found. People from far and near dreaded the place, and any gentleman who valued his life would never set foot there. Qizhe, however, was so consumed by his own bitter hardships that he only sought a way to die quickly. Thus, he went to stay the night at the temple. Prostrating himself before the statue of the deity, he prayed: "Qizhe is but a common resident of this city, a lowly scholar of the grass and reeds. I have lived as a humble student, with one robe for winter and one hemp garment for summer. I have never harbored excessive desires; when thirsty I drink, and when hungry I eat, never daring to think beyond my station. Why then is it—"

<column-break/>

<margin>
Generally, poverty binds a person so tightly that even the most wise, heroic, or talented cannot break free on their own. This has been true since ancient times and is not unique to the present world.
</margin>

"—that my path in life is so full of setbacks? I can no longer endure this constant poverty. Dust gathers in my steamer because there is no food to cook, and my situation is as dire as a fish gasping in a dry pot. In winter, even when others are warm, my children cry from the cold; in years of plenty, my wife still wails from hunger. If you could graciously shed the light of the sun and moon upon me and point out a path to survival, I would be most fortunate. But if I am destined to live my whole life in such distress, I would rather return early to the <term>Nine Springs</term><note>original: "九原" (Jiuyuan), a classical literary term for the underworld or the graveyard.</note>; I would sooner close my eyes in death now."

After finishing his prayer, his face was wet with silent tears. He then chanted a poem to himself:

"In olden times, it was said that long life was granted to the virtuous, but Heaven’s plan is hidden. Why is it that the one who feeds the magpies only lives the short years of a dog or sheep? <term>Scholar Jia</term><note>original: "賈生" (Jia Sheng), referring to Jia Yi, a brilliant Han dynasty scholar whose talents were underutilized by the emperor.</note> possessed the talent of Luoyang, and <term>Shudu</term><note>original: "叔度" (Shudu), the courtesy name of Huang Xian, a man of great character who lived in poverty during the Han dynasty.</note> was a worthy of the House of Han. Their noble spirits moved the world, and their names remain untarnished through the ages. Mount Jing produces rare jade, and the finder did not hesitate to offer it repeatedly. If one carries the jade but walks a path of thorns and crossroads, what became of <term>Bian He</term><note>original: "卞和" (Bian He), a man who discovered a piece of raw jade and had his feet cut off by kings who thought it was a common stone, until it was finally recognized as a masterpiece.</note>? In Nanyang, the Sleeping Dragon chanted the *Song of Liangfu*; eventually, he received the three visits that allowed his great name to be remembered forever." <gloss>Zhuge Kongming</gloss>

Having finished his chant, he collapsed under the altar table. His heart was filled with indignation and sorrow, and his body was sprawled out in exhaustion. His arms felt weak, and he could not fall into a peaceful sleep.

<summary>Scholar Lei Qizhe, an impoverished student in the Ming Dynasty, seeks a quick death in a haunted temple due to his extreme poverty. He prays to the gods and recites a poem comparing his unrecognized talent to historical figures like Zhuge Liang and Jia Yi.</summary>
<keywords>Scholar Lei (Lei Qizhe), Ming Dynasty, poverty, fate, haunted temple, Jia Yi, Zhuge Liang (Zhuge Kongming), Bian He, Confucian scholar</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the story of Scholar Lei Qizhe, who, driven by extreme poverty, has taken refuge in a reputedly haunted ancient temple. Having previously resigned himself to fate, he now encounters a supernatural manifestation that marks the turning point of his fortunes.</meta>

# Pure Conversations from the Wicker Basket

<margin>The four poems on pearls, jade, gold, and coins each reflect their own nature and sense of self-contentment.</margin>

Suddenly, four men were seen coming in from outside. One wore a yellow robe and a <term>scholar's cap</term><note>original: "幞頭" (futou), a traditional headcloth or cap worn by officials and scholars.</note>; one wore a white robe and a red headscarf; one wore a purple robe with a leather belt; and one wore plain dress with a Taoist cap. Upon entering the temple, the man in the yellow robe suspected the presence of a living person's breath. The man in the purple robe quickly stopped him, saying, "Our Master is here; do not speak overmuch." 

The man in plain dress was the first to speak, saying, "I suggest we each recite a <term>regulated verse</term><note>original: "一律" (yilu), a technically demanding eight-line poem with specific rhyme and tone patterns.</note>. Friends, do not hide your talents." His poem said:

> Several grains, round and bright, with an auspicious aura of treasure,
> Presented by <term>merfolk</term><note>original: "鮫人" (jiaoren), mythical sea-dwellers whose tears were said to turn into pearls.</note>, their value is extraordinary.
> If they were not produced within the womb of an ancient oyster, 
> Then they must have been hidden beneath the chin of the <term>black dragon</term><note>original: "驪龍" (lilong). Ancient legends claim the most precious pearls were guarded by dragons.</note>.
> Lustrous and beautiful, in the morning they breathe out radiance from the depths; 
> Brilliant and dazzling, they follow the stars and glow at night.
> They have returned to <term>Hepu</term><note>A reference to a famous story where pearls disappeared from the Hepu region due to corrupt officials and returned once an honest governor took office.</note> without need for searching, 
> For in this quiet place, our hearts strive to emulate <term>Lord Mengchang</term><note>A famous warring states statesman known for his hospitality and many talented retainers.</note>.

The man in the white robe recited:

> Named as the supreme treasure, its virtue is warm and smooth,
> Without blemish or flaw, its essence is firm and pure.
> When carved from the <term>Jing stone</term><note>original: "荆石" (jingshi), referring to the famous 'Jade of He,' a legendary gemstone of great value.</note>, its brilliance rivals the sun; 
> Where planted in <term>Lantian</term><note>A mountain famous since antiquity for producing high-quality jade.</note>, a warm mist rises like smoke.
> Its value is that of several cities, a treasure without peer, 
> Hidden deep within a casket for many a year.
> I especially remember the man of Chu who wept while offering it, 
> Suffering the loss of his feet—a truly pitiable sight<note>A reference to Bian He, who according to legend had his feet amputated by two kings who didn't believe his raw jade stone was real, before a third king finally recognized its value.</note>.

The man in the purple robe recited:

<column-break/>

<margin>At the moment they vanished by the eastern wall, the wealth obtained by Qizhe finally reached its destination.</margin>

> Born of the <term>Li River</term><note>original: "麗水" (lishui), a river in Southwest China historically famous for its gold deposits.</note>, its color is brilliant and bright,
> Of <term>double-south value</term><note>original: "雙南" (shuangnan), a term for high-quality gold currency.</note>, its fame has passed down through generations.
> Though picked from the sand, its form is quite unique; 
> Cast within the furnace, its substance grows even firmer.
> When <term>Mencius</term><note>The famous philosopher who was famously cautious about accepting monetary gifts unless they were proper.</note> received it, he did so with caution, 
> While the King of Yan used it to attract the worthy to his side.
> To the filial <term>Guo Ju</term><note>One of the twenty-four paragons of filial piety; legend says Heaven rewarded him with a pot of gold while he was digging a grave for his child to save food for his mother.</note>, Heaven surely granted this gift, 
> Leaving his name on the green bamboo scrolls for ten thousand years.

The man in the yellow robe recited:

> Crafted in square and round, it can communicate with the spirits,
> Unlike gold, and unlike silver.
> Since ancient times, the <term>Qingfu</term><note>A mythical insect. Legend said if the blood of a mother and child insect were smeared on coins, the coins would always return to each other, symbolizing wealth that never leaves.</note> has been famous among scholars, 
> And even today, the <term>True Man of White Water</term><note>original: "白水真人" (Baishui Zhenren), a literary nickname for coins, originally a pun on the birthplace of the Han Emperor Guangwu.</note> is a rare sight. 
> Accumulating <term>Brother Square</term><note>original: "方兄" (Fangxiong), a personification of the traditional Chinese coin which had a square hole in the middle.</note> is enough to make one rich; 
> Collecting <term>mothers and children</term><note>original: "母子" (muzi), a technical term for large and small denominations of currency.</note>, one need not fear poverty.
> If allowed to flow like a spring across the land, 
> Then under all of heaven, my people would have plenty.

When the recitations were finished, the four men walked together toward the eastern wall and vanished. Qizhe then went to that spot and obtained several <term>hu</term><note>A traditional unit of dry measure, roughly 50 liters.</note> of pearls, several boxes of jade, a hundred bars of gold, and a pit full of coins. Later, he became the wealthiest man in the district of Fu, and to this day, people still praise and speak of his story.

<summary>This page concludes the story of Scholar Lei Qizhe, who is visited by four spirits personifying different forms of wealth (Pearls, Jade, Gold, and Money) who recite poems before bestowing a massive physical fortune upon him.</summary>

### Rare Treasures from an Ancient Tomb

In Jingjiang, there lived a man named Nguyen Van Hung <note>Nguyen Van Hung (Ruan Wenxiong) is the protagonist, depicted as a wealthy and romantic scholar.</note>. His family was extremely prosperous, and he was a man of broad mind who was deeply devoted to the elegant pleasures of landscapes. In the autumn of the *Yichou* year of the Shaoding era <note>1229 AD, during the Southern Song Dynasty.</note>, during the time when rents were being collected at his estates, Master Nguyen took the opportunity to seek the joy of sightseeing. He took one or two servants and rowed a leaf-like small craft <gloss>a small boat</gloss>, lightly paddling along the water's edge. At that time, white duckweed and red knotweed bloomed, while withered reeds and the remnants of lotuses floated. The clear mountain mist towered in emerald green, and distant trees were shrouded in clouds. Under the hanging sun, he listened to the singing birds and watched the leaping carp. Wherever his eyes met a beautiful scene, he would stop to admire it. Drifting along, the boat reached Seven-Mile Bay, and before he realized it, the sky had grown dark. Looking around, no one lived nearby. Suddenly, a pavilion appeared ahead, standing firm like a craggy rock. He ordered his servants to move the boat closer. Just as the boat was moored, he suddenly heard a burst of laughter from the pavilion. Master Nguyen peeked inside and saw three beauties leaning on the railing, frowning and smiling. One look and he could not keep his heart steady. He then recited a poem loudly from the boat:

<column-break/>

十九

<margin>
Indeed, the feeling of dislike is common to all; the beauty's self-narrative touches upon this.
</margin>

> Sadly I lean on the creek-side tower looking afar,
> Because I see the bright moon;
> If the bright moon has an appointment,
> Why does it mistakenly trigger the sorrow of parting?

Hearing this, a beauty in the tower recited in response:

> The thin grass turns green when spring arrives,
> Quiet flowers turn red after the rain.
> Longing for you but unable to meet,
> Full of melancholy east of the painted tower.

Master Nguyen’s restlessness grew even stronger, regretting that he could not ride the wind like a celestial being <term>Fengxu</term> <note>Refers to "riding the void," a Daoist concept of flying through the air like an immortal.</note>. Soon, the beauties lowered a red silk rope into the boat. Master Nguyen climbed up the rope. A beauty smiled and said, "Is the young master going to be a 'gentleman on the beam'?" <note>A "gentleman on the beam" is a traditional Chinese idiom for a thief.</note> Master Nguyen laughed and replied, "I wish to follow the ancient example of 'plucking the lotus'." <gloss>Refers to the story of Xie Kun</gloss> <note>Xie Kun was a famously free-spirited scholar; "plucking the lotus" refers to a story of him attempting to woo a neighbor's daughter.</note> They then shared the joys of the bed, their laughter continuing until dawn, their affection growing deeper. One beauty said, "To follow the six rites of a matchmaker and then betroth is the way of humans. To preserve the original energy of Great Harmony and wait for the right time is the nature of things. We are not like mountain pheasants or wild herons that can be tamed <gloss>to become accustomed to</gloss>, nor like roadside willows or wall flowers that can be plucked. Because we were moved by the seasons, we took on different forms. Since nature behaves this way, how can humans avoid feeling sorrow when seeing the scenery? Therefore, we would rather violate the three feet of... <note>The "three feet" usually refers to the law, as laws were once written on three-foot bamboo tablets.</note>

<summary>A wealthy scholar named Nguyen Van Hung encounters three mysterious beauties in a secluded pavilion while traveling by boat. After exchanging romantic poetry, he joins them for a night of passion, leading to a philosophical discussion about human nature and destiny.</summary>

"The law is often used to indulge the private whims of the six desires <note>A Buddhist concept referring to the desires arising from the six senses: sight, sound, smell, taste, touch, and thought.</note>. If you, sir, do not mind the ease of 'joining skin' <note>A metaphor for physical intimacy.</note> and can maintain the steadfastness of the 'golden brake' <note>original: 金柅 (jin ni). A reference from the I Ching symbolizing stopping or restraining something firmly; here it suggests a loyal or firm commitment.</note>, and do not despise our humble, adorned appearances, allowing our feelings to flow forth like a continuous spring, then we sisters may die tonight and be content." One beauty said, "The 'graceful lady is a fit mate for the gentleman' <note>A direct quote from the first poem in the *Classic of Poetry* (Shijing).</note>. Today’s joy is exactly this; how can we be without poetry?" Everyone agreed. One beauty then began by chanting: "The talent of Mount Yiyang <note>Mount Yiyang was famous for producing the fine paulownia wood used to make the best *qin* zithers.</note> has long been valued as highly as 'Southern Gold.' The craftsmanship balances Yin and Yang with deep intent. Heavenly sounds arise like a solitary crane's cry to the sky, or the roar of an old dragon amidst a thousand acres of cold waves. It plays the pure and thick righteousness of the era of Shennong <note>original: 義農 (Yi Nong). Refers to the mythical Emperor Shennong, credited with inventing the zither to cultivate virtue.</note>, washing away the decadent sounds of Zheng and Wei <note>Ancient states whose music was considered licentious or 'modern' compared to the pure ritual music of old.</note>. I lament that after Zhong Ziqi passed away, no one remains who truly understands the heart of Bo Ya <note>Zhong Ziqi and Bo Ya are the quintessential symbols of soul-friendship; when Ziqi died, Bo Ya broke his zither because no one else could truly 'hear' his music.</note>." Another beauty chanted: "A song of 'Cloud Harmony' <note>original: 雲和 (Yun He). A mountain famous for producing wood for zithers (Se).</note> remains from ancient times to now; among the fifty strings <note>The *Se* zither traditionally had fifty strings, often associated with themes of sorrow or the passage of time.</note>, elegant thoughts gather. The flowing water is clear and cold at the Xiang River bank in the evening; the sad wind soughs through Lake Dongting in autumn. One is startled to hear the auspicious crane dancing toward the clouds, or listens quietly as the fine fish swim out from the mountain stream. The records of Lu <note>The State of Lu, home of Confucius.</note> mention the Spirit of the Xiang River <note>Xiang Ling, the goddess of the river.</note> in the final two lines: such a person shall take the top honors in the imperial examinations <note>original: 占鰲頭 (zhan ao tou). Literally 'standing on the turtle's head,' a metaphor for ranking first in the civil service exams.</note>." Finally, the last

First, he gains the intimacy of the beauties in the pavilion, and then he gains the high price of the zithers and lutes. Master Ruan’s encounter is a "fortunate misfortune."

beauty chanted: "The dragon’s head and cloud-shaped tip are skillfully crafted; the bridge is shaped like a praying mantis, light and clear. As the jade-like fingers suddenly pluck, a single sound rings out, startling the Silver River <note>The Milky Way.</note> and echoing with all the sounds of nature. Before long, the night colors were about to fade and the morning light was beginning to scatter. The beauties hurriedly helped Master Ruan up, saying, "You must go on your long journey immediately; do not let outsiders discover this." In a fluster, Master Ruan returned to his boat and ordered his servants to prepare his luggage, thinking of a plan to stay longer. Suddenly, he turned his head to look back, and the pavilion and the beauties had vanished without a trace. Master Ruan was greatly terrified and went to the spot to investigate. He saw only an old, neglected tomb, with a hole in the side serving as an entrance for foxes and rabbits. Looking inside, he found a *Qin* zither, a *Se* zither, and a *Pipa* lute. He took them back and sold them, obtaining a very high price.

<term>Master Ruan (Ruan-sheng)</term> The protagonist, a wealthy scholar-traveler.

<term>Bo Ya</term> The legendary zither master.

<term>Old Tomb (Gu-zhong)</term> A common trope in ghost stories where a beautiful mansion is revealed to be a grave.
<term>Foxes and Rabbits (Hu-tu)</term> Animals associated with abandoned or supernatural places.

### The Pipa in Front of the Window

<margin>The "Six Rites" <note>The traditional procedures for a formal marriage in ancient China.</note> must be completed before a virtuous woman acts; how can there be one who brings her own bedding to offer herself in bed?</margin>
...so cold and clear, how can I endure? 

After the girl finished her song, she knocked on the door and said, "I have heard of a refined and elegant scholar, and so I have risked breaking the rules to seek you out. Are you now going to follow the example of the ancients by closing your door and refusing to let me in?" 

<gloss>A reference to the "Man of Lu," a legendary figure who famously refused to let a neighbor woman into his house during a storm to maintain his purity.</gloss> 

Upon hearing these words, Scholar Jin could not bring himself to refuse her. He slowly opened the door, and the girl had already pressed her way forward to the bedside. 

Scholar Jin said, "On such a fine night, meeting such a beauty—but alas, the candle is extinguished and the wine jar is empty. We cannot enjoy even a single song or drink together." 

The girl replied, "I have brought my own bedding to offer myself to you; our time together will span months and years, so why be so stubborn about tonight? Besides, 'the old drunkard's true interest is not in the wine.'" <note>A famous literary allusion meaning that one's true intention lies elsewhere—in this case, in their physical intimacy rather than the drinks.</note>

Thereupon, they undressed and entered the bed-curtains together, enjoying the height of intimate pleasures. When the cock crowed outside the window and the neighboring temple bell rang, the girl gathered her clothes and rose, saying, "I must return." Jin urged her to stay time and again. The girl said, "Say no more; if I do not teach you, you would have to sleep alone," and then she quietly departed. 

The next night, Scholar Jin prepared wine and delicacies to wait for her. The girl indeed came wandering in. They drank together until they were merry and relaxed, and the girl sang the same lyrics as the night before.

<column-break/>

<margin>In a narrative, first mentioning a character's love for music and books provides a foundation for what follows.</margin>
Scholar Jin of Dengzhou, named Heyun, was of a refined disposition and loved the zither and books. He was highly praised by his peers. During the Hongxi era <note>1425 AD, a short reign period in the Ming Dynasty.</note>, he traveled to Xiuzhou and stayed at a wealthy family's estate. His bedroom was adjacent to the Zhaoti Temple <gloss>The name of the temple.</gloss>.

Across the wall, there were often sounds of singing—sometimes distant, sometimes near, sometimes high, and sometimes low. At first, he was suspicious of it, but as it continued night after night, he eventually paid it no mind. 

One night, when the moon was bright and the breeze was gentle, and the night was deep and quiet, he unexpectedly heard the sound of singing coming from outside the window. Peeking out, he saw a girl of about seventeen or eighteen years old. With her hair elegantly disheveled and her beauty graceful and many-sided, he suspected she was a concubine from the host's family who had slipped out in secret. He did not dare open the door, but leaned in to listen to her song:

"Sound, sound, sound, you are heartless, you are truly heartless. You have failed me until now. I remember back then, the low singing and the shallow pouring of wine; one song was worth ten pieces of gold. Now, it is lonely in the shadow of the old wall. The autumn wind, the wild grass, and the white clouds are deep. Where can the broken bridge and flowing water be found? So mournful..."

<summary>This passage introduces Scholar Jin, who encounters a mysterious, beautiful woman singing mournful songs outside his study near a temple. Despite the breach of social etiquette, the two share an intimate night, beginning a secret nocturnal romance.</summary>
<keywords>Scholar Jin (Jin Heyun), Zhaoti Temple, Ming Dynasty romance, Hongxi era, Pipa, supernatural encounter, courtship etiquette</keywords>

### Ten Thousand Records of Pure Talk: Volume Three
#### Page Thirteen

<margin>She claimed to be the daughter of Governor Cao, having achieved immortality in the Jiang Grotto-Heaven <note>A "Grotto-Heaven" (dongtian) is a sacred site in Daoist mythology, thought to be a terrestrial paradise inhabited by immortals.</note> but was banished to the mortal world. These were merely the lies of a strange spirit seeking to justify herself.</margin>

Scholar Jin said, “In the presence of a new companion, one should not sing old tunes. At a place of joy, how can one speak of sorrowful feelings?” Following the rhyme of the previous song, he sang:

<gloss>Sound, sound, sound, sound—I know you have a heart. Knowing you have a heart has enticed me until this very moment. Most precious is this night; by the window, beneath the flowers, we pour wine. A single smile is worth a thousand pieces of gold. Suddenly, "clouds and rain" <note>A traditional literary euphemism for sexual intimacy.</note> play within the spring shadows. The "Jade Mountain" <note>A metaphor for a refined person’s body; here it suggests collapsing into bed or a state of blissful exhaustion.</note> topples deep within the crimson curtains. Where else could one find such joy?</gloss>

Upon hearing the song, the girl rose and thanked him, saying, “You have truly turned my sorrow into new joy.” Their mutual passion grew deep. A month passed; they met when the moon was bright and parted when the breeze was clear, their excitement becoming hard to restrain. <gloss>to hold</gloss> As of yesterday, it was beyond control. <gloss>reach</gloss>

In this way, half a year passed by quickly, and few people knew of their affair. Suddenly, one night, the girl arrived and began to weep. Jin consoled her and asked what was wrong. The girl said, “I am originally the daughter of Governor Cao. I was fortunate enough to learn the immortal arts and wander the Grotto-Heavens, but because my mortal heart was not yet quelled, I suffered this banishment. Moved by our predestined bond, I have long served your pleasure. Now my allotted time is exhausted. However, your future path is vast; our meeting in Jinling <note>Modern-day Nanjing.</note>...

<column-break/>

<margin>The later meeting in Jinling and the old connection at Jiashan prove that people have fixed destinies, and even objects possess foreknowledge.</margin>

...is not far off. I only hope you take good care of your health.” She was overcome with grief. At the fourth watch <note>Between 1:00 AM and 3:00 AM.</note>, Jin gave the girl some gold as a parting gift, and she left. Not long after, a torrential rain fell like water being overturned from a basin. With a great clap of thunder, the old wall outside the window completely collapsed. Scholar Jin’s spirit was shaken, and the next day he did not stay.

Two years later, while building a wall at the old foundation, he dug up a stone box and found a <term>Zither</term> <note>Specifically a "qin," a seven-stringed traditional Chinese instrument associated with scholars and refinement.</note> inside. Jin never understood the reason why it was there. Later, when Scholar Jin was serving as an official in Jinling, someone who knew of his love for the instrument brought a zither to present to him. Jin saw the zither’s radiance was dazzling, and he knew it was made of no ordinary material. He joyfully accepted it and placed it on a stone bed. Looking at it from a distance, it appeared to be the girl from before; but when he approached to touch it, it was still just a zither. Only then did he realize the girl was a zither spirit. He was both shocked and delighted. 

Later, he moved to Chuzhou. When Scholar Jin fell gravely ill and was near death, he commanded his family to bury the zither with him. The prophecy he had heard in Xiuzhou was thus fulfilled.

<summary>Scholar Jin discovers that his mysterious lover was actually the spirit of an ancient zither buried beneath his home. After a career as an official, he is eventually buried with the instrument, fulfilling a destiny foretold earlier in the text.</summary>

### The Lamp Spirit • The Ruined House

->**The Lamp Spirit's auspicious eye casts a brilliant light, shooting high toward the regions of the Dipper and the Ox.** <note>The "Dipper" (Dou) and "Ox" (Niu) are two of the Twenty-Eight Mansions of the Chinese constellations; here, they represent the celestial heights to show how powerful the spirit's light is.</note><-

<column-break/>

<margin>1</margin>
**Dwelling within a ruined house of mere cubits <term>xunci: a traditional unit of measurement for a very small, cramped space</term>, I am ashamed to have no plan for my daily bread.** <note>The phrase "no plan for morning and evening" suggests a hand-to-mouth existence or a lack of financial security.</note>

<note>This page contrasts the scholar’s physical poverty—living in a "ruined house"—with the extraordinary, celestial light of the "Lamp Spirit" who visits him. In East Asian folklore, lamp spirits often appear to lonely scholars to provide companionship or inspiration, though they are often revealed to be the essence of an object rather than a human soul.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the supernatural identity of the scholar's visitor as a Lamp Spirit and illustrates the scholar's humble, impoverished living situation. While the previous notes referred to a "Scholar Jin," this page identifies the protagonist as Liang Dong (also known by his courtesy name Dacai). # Manifold Notes for Pure Discourse

## The Lamp Spirits Under the Moon

In Yizhou, there lived a scholar named Liang Dong, whose courtesy name was Dacai. His family had been scholars for generations, yet he possessed no talent for business or the pursuit of wealth. He often found himself in dire straits, facing the "jade grains and cassia firewood" <note>An idiom meaning food is as expensive as precious jade and fuel is as costly as rare cassia wood; it signifies an extremely high cost of living during a famine or personal poverty.</note> situation. The ancients once spoke of "nine meals in thirty days" and "no savings in the jar," and his life certainly neared such a state. Furthermore, his dwelling was but a grass hut with a bamboo fence—one that let in the sun by day and the stars by night, providing no shelter against the wind and rain.

Out of necessity, Liang found an abandoned mansion in the village and moved there temporarily. However, his heart was set on becoming a preeminent scholar in the Imperial Court. Thus, the ancient classics on his desk were never closed; he read them without rest, heedless of the shifting seasons. <gloss>The ancients spoke of "wearing through an inkstone made of iron," and he was nearly at that level of dedication.</gloss> As for his pressing poverty, he eventually put it out of his mind entirely.

One day, as he sat alone near midnight, feeling a slight fatigue, he chanted a poem to himself:

> Sitting alone by the scholar's window, the lamp is half-shrouded,
> Its light opens the depths of my heart, where a myriad of sorrows dwell.
> I am ashamed to have no peck or bushel of grain for my daily bread,
> While in vain, a heroic light shoots toward the Dipper and the Ox constellations. <note>The "Dipper" and "Ox" are celestial regions; the "heroic light" represents the scholar's untapped potential and ambition shining toward the heavens.</note>
> I leave the bright moon over the Five Lakes beyond my concern,
> For ten thousand miles of blue clouds <note>The "blue clouds" (qingyun) are a common metaphor for high social status and a successful career in the imperial bureaucracy.</note> hang upon my heart.
> To overturn the heavens and shake the—

<column-break/>

十五 <note>Page fifteen</note>
<meta>Volume Three</meta>

<margin>
Han Jingmu is the Han Algae Lamp.
Zhu Minglong (Bamboo Bright Dragon) refers to the character for "basket," which is also a type of lamp.
When light is used for reading, it is called a "book lamp."
When a servant boy holds it for clarity, it is called a "Buddha lamp."
The "Yuanliang" style is a fisherman’s lamp.
As for the "Shu Torches," this also refers to the character for "candle."
</margin>

—earth: these are the deeds of a man. Why should I settle for being a mere Lord of Ten Thousand Households?

Suddenly, six men appeared from behind the house. Each held a lamp, and they were so bright it seemed like broad daylight. Liang was startled and asked them: "Gentlemen, where do you come from, and why have you arrived so late at night?"

The six men bowed and said: "We are your neighbors from the surrounding houses. We heard that a 'Guest of the Blue Clouds' <note>A term of respect for an ambitious, high-achieving scholar.</note> was here, with a sharp resolve and a willingness to endure hardship. Therefore, we have specifically come to offer our lamps and candles, to provide you with the means to continue your work into the day." <note>The text notes the idiom "continuing the night into the day," meaning to work tirelessly around the clock.</note>

Liang bowed in thanks and invited them to sit. He said slowly: "My household lacks a mistress to manage the kitchen; I fear I cannot offer you proper hospitality. I hope you will not take offense?"

The six men laughed and said: "Pure conversation <term>qingtan: a refined style of philosophical and literary debate favored by scholars</term> is quite enough. Why worry about external matters?"

One of them added: "In the presence of a man of letters, let us not speak of crude things. Shall we each compose a poem?"

Han Jingmu <note>His name literally means "Han Respected-Wood," personifying a tall wooden lampstand.</note> was the first to chant:

> Lord Han is eight feet tall, mighty and strong,
> At a distance of two feet, he is intimate and bright.
> He does not shun the grease and oil that keep him moist,
> For he always receives the lamplight to glow with brilliance.
> He is used to being carried forth when the times are clear,
> But cast aside and hidden in dark places when dismissed.
> In years of wealth and status to come, he should not be abandoned,
> But moved onto the patterned banquet table to shine upon the silver trays.

<margin>
Are they not all lamps? These names are all formed by splitting or rearranging characters to create puns; their meaning is easily understood by the reader.
</margin>

**Zhu Minglong** <note>The name literally means "Bamboo Bright Dragon," personifying a lantern with a bamboo frame.</note> chanted: 

"Hollow, round, and thin, remarkably light and clear;
Surely crafted by a master’s skillful hand.
A thousand windows on the outside, yet partitioned;
At the center, a single point of bright light.
Moonlight coldly reflects off the mica panes;
Rosy colors warmly hold the red crystal.
How many times, after the King’s spring banquet ends,
Have I lit the way back to the purple paths at the third watch?" <note>The "third watch" is midnight. The "purple paths" refer to the roads of the capital or the palace.</note>

**Shu Yongguang** <note>The name "Books Use Light" personifies a scholar’s desk lamp.</note> chanted: 

"Below the window, the silver jar is a foot long;
All day long, it accompanies me as I practice my writing.
Brilliantly it illuminates a thousand lines of text;
<gloss>The word 'line' (*hang*) is pronounced here to rhyme with a rank or row.</gloss>
Splendidly it burns, a one-inch flame.
Often, because of the night rain on the wicker cage, 'flowers' bloom; <note>In Chinese lore, a sparking wick is called a "lamp flower" and is an omen of good luck.</note>
It does not seek to flatter the spring sun.
Those in olden times who reflected the snow or bagged fireflies <note>Refers to famous scholars Sun Kang and Che Yin, who were too poor to buy oil and studied by the light of reflected snow or fireflies.</note>
Would do well to join this fragrant grove and visit the Hall of Confucius."

**Seng Yiming** <note>The name "Monk of Enlightenment" personifies a votive lamp in a Buddhist temple.</note> chanted: 

"A single point of eternal brightness before the ancient Buddha;
Even more so when the 'red rain' night feels like a year.
Red light pours out, filling the lapis lazuli bowl;
Crimson flames blossom into a water lily.
Shining through the dark paths of the underworld, one should progress;
Illuminating the Dharma realm, it is good for practicing Zen.
This old monk sits facing it as the night grows late;
Having finished chanting the *Lankavatara Sutra*, the light still shines brightly."

**Yu Yuanliang** <note>The name "Fisherman of Primal Light" personifies a lantern on a fishing boat.</note> chanted: "A drop of pine...

<column-break/>

<margin>
Scholar Liang initially suffered in a poor alley, leaving his traces in this 'Phoenix Residence.' Afterward, he passed the highest imperial examination. Truly, even those in poverty and low status can achieve greatness; it is a matter of the waves of fate bringing success.
</margin>

...resin at night feels like a year.
Red light reflects on the water, and the water is like the sky.
Many times, through dark rain at the Yan Rapids,
Long wisps of cold smoke passing the Wei River.
Beneath the waves, it reflects the fading red;
The carp leap, and by the sandy shore, sleeping gulls are startled awake.
A guest on a lonely boat pushes back the canopy to watch;
Wondering if it is a meteor, shining so brilliantly."

**Shu Huoju** <note>The name "Sichuan Torch" refers to a candle or torch made from Sichuan wax or resin.</note> chanted: 

"Resin from the cliffs melted into a sheet of silver;
Several branches poured out, most round and even.
Tears flow drop by drop, as if in a sorrowful night; <note>Refers to "candle tears," the wax dripping down the side.</note>
'Flowers' form brilliantly, without waiting for the spring.
Entrusted to the elegant banquet to befriend the wine-pouring guests;
In the jade hall, becoming close to the one reading books.
The sagely lords of the Tang Dynasty bestowed many favors,
Especially granting 'Golden Lilies' to the close ministers." <note>The "Golden Lily" (Jin Lian) was a ceremonial candle given by the Emperor to favored officials to light their way home.</note>

Before long, the sound of the bell from the ancient temple faded. From the ten thousand dwellings, the sounds of screens and cages moving were heard, and a rooster crowed in the mountain village. Startled and scattered like the stars in the sky, the visitors each went their own way, and it is not known where they went. 

Afterward, Scholar Liang’s learning grew daily and his virtue advanced daily. At the great ceremony, he finally passed the *Jinshi* <term>The highest level of the imperial examination system, granting the title of "Presented Scholar."</term> and achieved his rank.

**Right Column:**
<margin>
The *Night Chat of the Lamp Demon* is an old tale. Why has it been recorded again? It is because the narrative is grand and the prose is so fluid that it is sufficient to clear the confusion of those chasing fame and fortune, while stirring the souls of those with spiritual sensitivity. This is a piece from the "Forest of Phrases" <note>original: 詞林 (cilin), a collective term for the literary world</note> that must not be discarded when choosing friends.

# Night Chat with the Lamp Spirit

Zhang Yidu, from Jiaxing, went by the courtesy name Nanxun. He lived contentedly in poverty, much like the sages of old who lived on a single bowl of rice and a ladle of water. He was a devoted student of antiquity and had mastered the works of various schools of thought, having browsed through the writings of many philosophers. Although he harbored the ambition to be the greatest scholar in the land, his circumstances never quite matched his goals. During the Zhiyuan era <note>A regnal period of the Yuan Dynasty (1264–1294)</note>, while he was reading late one night, a "lamp flower" <note>A carbon deposit on a candle or lamp wick that glows like a blossom, traditionally seen as a lucky omen</note> suddenly flew off and landed among his books. Zhang quickly brushed it away. This happened repeatedly, until it seemed like a solitary moth throwing itself into the flames. Zhang thought to himself: "So it is, so it is! You and I have helped each other for years. Now, unable to display the light of my writing, I am confined to this study, lonely on a cold night. My heart is nearly turned to ash. No wonder you act this way. I should compose a poem to thank you; why must you labor to provoke me?" He then composed a poem:

->A single spark of eternal light, uniquely close in spirit. For years you have joined me<-
->discussing literature at night. In the cold window, a thin flame spreads with smoke; against a broken wall, fragrant oil burns amidst the rain. Having finished<-
->the tattered scrolls, my spirit alone is encouraged. I beg for the new fire to be shared by hand first. When the time comes, the brilliance will fade into the mundane; how can it compare to<-
->following a fisherman among the water and clouds?<-

Day one. Half a lifetime of mutual understanding, never weary of poverty. The cold light shimmers.

<column-break/>

**Left Column:**
<margin>
These three regulated poems are close to the "Yesterday's Errors" style of writing.
</margin>

Chanting in solitude. I delighted in the flickering "flower" of the wick, yet it held no true meaning; I pity the lamp’s devotion to its master, though it has a familiar heart. The taste of pickled vegetables <note>original: 虀鹽 (jiyan), a metaphor for a scholar's impoverished lifestyle</note> has been burned through completely; for many nights, the sound of the bells and drums has reached me deeply. This clear light can still assist in diligent study; why must one labor to bore through a wall to find light? <note>A reference to the Han dynasty scholar Kuang Heng, who was too poor to buy oil and bored a hole in his wall to use his neighbor's light</note>

Day three. Reflecting on the sword and the sounding zither, the light brushes against the screen. You truly understand the flavor of a Confucian household. By the plum-blossom window, the light flickers as the rain stops; at the bamboo door, the moon is just beginning to rise in the gloom. My learning is shallow, yet to be rewarded like the old man with the "blowing staff" <note>An allusion to a legend of a spirit providing light to a scholar</note>; in times of hardship, I feel for the one who gathered fireflies to read. I pity those who rely on the company of pipes and songs, only loving the springtime found within the sounds of idle chatter <note>original: 唼喋 (shadi), representing the sound of birds feeding or idle gossip</note>. Having completed the three regulated poems, he was about to strike the five rhymes. His spirit felt quite transcendent. 

He was just about to use his arm as a pillow <gloss>bending the arm</gloss> <note>A reference to Confucius, who found joy in simple poverty</note> and entrust his dreams to a butterfly <gloss>Butterfly Dream</gloss> <note>A reference to the philosopher Zhuangzi’s famous dream where he could not tell if he was a man dreaming of a butterfly or a butterfly dreaming of a man</note>. Suddenly, a woman emerged from behind the lamp. She was graceful and multifaceted, though her form was shimmering and indistinct. Zhang stood up and shouted, "What kind of monster are you, that you dare to offend a gentleman?" The beauty opened her mouth and said, "I have a long-standing connection with you; it has not been merely for one night..."

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Zhang Yidu (Zhang Nanxun), a scholar from Jiaxing. In the previous page, Zhang was visited by a "lamp flower" that transformed into a supernatural entity after he composed a poem. This page records the dialogue between Zhang and this mysterious beauty, who critiques the scholar's obsession with fame and the harsh reality of the civil service examination system.</meta>

<margin>
The two words "fame and fortune" lure people like a seductive estate. To fail to attain them is as difficult as ascending to heaven. They have misled many. These are extremely cynical words; though they may be excessive, there are some who will find truth in them.
</margin>

"Just now, sir, you were chanting your bitter poems, and the honor of your verse reached even my humble, clumsy self. Therefore, I came to listen. Why are you so deeply surprised?" Zhang Han <note>Presumably Zhang Yidu's formal name used in this section</note> replied to her: "I live in a secluded and quiet place, and my nature is lazy and mediocre. We have no reason to be acquainted; how could you speak so boldly and practice such deception?"

The beauty clapped her hands and laughed, saying: "Have you forgotten, sir? We have been intimate for a long time, yet suddenly you deny me? It is truly strange indeed!" Zhang was flustered and could not remember her. He tentatively replied: "If that is so, then do you know what it is I do?"

The beauty frowned and spoke: <gloss>The word "kneeling" refers to the feet being bent</gloss> "Alas! I have been familiar with your conduct for a long time. Now that you speak like this, what is it you are so stubbornly holding onto? You boast of your status, but do you not realize that 'fame and fortune' are merely demons that enchant the souls of men? Your profession is your burden. What more do I need to say?" Zhang became increasingly bewildered. Trapped in his empty study late at night, he thought this beauty must be an evil spirit. He secretly gripped a sharp sword and waited. Then he said to her: "Please explain the truth of the matter. If your words are reasonable, then so be it. If not, I shall have something to say." The beauty calmly replied: "Among today's scholars who live on a single ladle of water <note>A reference to the poverty-stricken but virtuous lifestyle of Yan Hui, a disciple of Confucius</note>, who does not wish to scale the clouds <note>original: 梯雲 (tiyun), a metaphor for passing the highest exams</note> and wear the purple and blue robes of nobility? But—"

<column-break/>

<margin>
Master Zhu Wen said: In pursuing scholarship, sustaining one's life must come first. However, if one's heart is split between making a living and studying, one inevitably falls into the trap of starting and stopping. How then could one find the inspiration to pick up a brush and compose elegant lines about the water, clouds, flowers, and the moon?
</margin>

"—the standards of literature are held by the officials, while success or failure depends on one's fate. Therefore, those who toil in their prime only to reach old age without success are many. Of those who seek the 'secret nod of the Red-Robed God' <gloss>The Red-Robed God nodding his head</gloss> <note>A legendary deity said to sit behind examiners and nod when a passing essay was read</note>, how many succeed out of a hundred?

I have often seen those misled by the scholar's cap: they wear rags like quails' feathers and patched shoes, living in hovels with mats for doors and ropes for hinges. They never eat their fill, and their clothes cannot keep out the cold. Looking up, they have nothing to provide for their elders; looking down, they cannot support their families. The sounds of sighing, resentment, and weeping tug at their hearts and press against their ears. To endure it is to have no purpose; to be content is impossible for survival. Unable to advance or retreat, their will and strength are both exhausted. Even heroes are left helpless, with no door at which to beg for help. The ancients said: 'When hunger comes, not a single word is fit to be boiled for food.' It is truly pitiable!

<gloss>This passage refers to those who live as guests of the wealthy to teach students.</gloss> The schoolroom is like a prison, a self-imposed lock without a rope. They must look to the master's whims to decide when to yield or stand firm. They must speculate on the students' moods to decide when to be happy or angry. Every movement is a cause for concern; coming and going is difficult. If the student succeeds, the father claims the glory; if the student fails, the teacher bears the blame. As for rising early by the 'cold window' or staying awake at the 'snowy desk' <note>Common tropes for the extreme hardships scholars endured while studying</note>, huddling one's spirit under a small mosquito net..."

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Zhang Yidu and the supernatural beauty who appeared from his lamp. Having criticized his obsession with fame, she now provides a series of tragic examples—Scholars Yu, Xu, and Feng—to illustrate the physical, mental, and financial ruin brought about by the pursuit of the imperial examinations.</meta>

<margin>
No one could surpass them in integrity. Those two men, Dong and Lü, in their character and benevolent personal conduct, spoke such words that make one feel ashamed upon reflection.
</margin>

By a cold lamp, mourning one’s own shadow. Birds cry and flowers fall. These things only increase ten thousand bushels of sorrow. Amidst green waters and blue mountains, who can provide the joy of a forest stroll? One’s home is ten thousand miles away, connected only through the fleeting medium of dreams. With wind and rain battering half the window, one can only sigh and pity oneself in vain. Toil, hardship, and desolation—these are things I have experienced personally in both heart and body. Alas! Even if one eventually gains some success, it is still not enough to compensate for this suffering. Furthermore, the official salary for a teacher is fixed and meager; in the end, it is difficult to provide for the needs of a family. The ancients said that "a belly full of essays cannot cure hunger." How truly mournful!

In another case, there are those who are young and possessed of excellent learning, selected among the top ranks. They speak grandly and dress extravagantly, looking down on others with an arrogant air. They hunt for reputation and aim for fame, boasting of their extraordinary talents. Yet, they repeatedly meet with disaster at the <term>Thorny Gates<note>The "Thorny Gates" (Jiwei) refers to the high-walled, heavily guarded provincial examination halls.</note></term>. They find themselves "behind Sun Shan" <note>An idiom meaning to fail the exam; Sun Shan was the last name on the passing list, so to be "behind" him was to fail.</note>, returning year after year with "ink on their foreheads" while watching others pass the examinations. Because of this, their timing is gradually lost, their affairs gradually fail, their ambition withers, and their spirit is blocked. Their former vitality is completely extinguished. Once the pressures of heat and cold close in, and they lack even the clothes and food to cover their skin, their thoughts become even more distracted. Lonely, stagnant, and unlucky, they are besieged by both sorrow and illness. To work as a laborer would bring shame to their scholar’s robes and cap; to become a thief would destroy their sense of integrity and shame. Their youthful 

<column-break/>

ambition is ground away, and their hopes for a high reputation come to nothing. <gloss>Those who held high reputations in the past are now mocked by others on the road.</gloss> Their bodies grow old and impoverished, waiting to rot in a ditch. The ancients said: "Wealth and honor do not come, but youth departs." How tragic!

<margin>
Scholar Yu, Scholar Xu, and Scholar Feng were all contemporaries of Zhang. Thus, he mentions them here by the lamplight as evidence, though it is a pity their full names have not been passed down.
</margin>

Have you not seen Scholar Yu? He was a beautiful youth. His father loved him partially and supervised his studies with extreme strictness. He toiled day and night until he developed <term>consumption<note>Likely tuberculosis (laozhai), a common "scholar's disease" attributed to overwork and lack of physical activity.</note></term>. His studies were not yet complete when he died prematurely, leaving his father in a state of irremediable regret and mourning. 

Have you not seen Scholar Xu? He was a man of great diligence in learning. However, his household stores were empty. To him, "grains were like jade and firewood like cassia" <note>A metaphor for the prohibitively high cost of living in poverty.</note>; he often had to report that his supplies were exhausted. <gloss>The word "exhausted" (kong) is pronounced in the departing tone.</gloss> While his desk was piled high with letters and books, how could they save him from hunger and cold? When he faced such dire straits that he knew not what to do, he and his wife would face the corner of the room and weep together. This led to a mountain of worries in his heart; he died of a suppressed spirit. He left behind an orphan and a widow, who to this day remain cold, hungry, and without support. 

Have you not seen Scholar Feng? He was the son of a wealthy family. Because he failed to meet expectations in the annual school examinations held by the literary instructors, he covered his eyes and stamped his feet in frustration, coughing up several mouthfuls of blood. Upon returning home, his eyes remained fixed in a daze. His hall was filled with shamans and doctors, but a hundred schemes were of no effect. On the night his illness became critical, his usual brushes and inkstones...

<summary>The narrative describes the "Seven Tragedies of the Scholar," using the examples of Scholars Yu, Xu, and Feng to illustrate how the civil service examination system leads to physical illness, poverty, and premature death. It serves as a grim critique of the scholarly life and the heavy price of seeking social advancement through the state exams.</summary>

### Pure Talk, Part 3

<margin>
Even when one’s status is high, one's ability to protect oneself is insufficient to rely upon.
</margin>

<margin>
This passage discusses how the harm of achieving fame and success is actually quite severe. It emphasizes that the life of a scholar cannot be relied upon in this way.
</margin>

All his writings were burned before him. He called his son and instructed him, saying: "These things are what steal one's life; you must be careful with them." Having finished speaking, he passed away. These three scholars did not deserve such early deaths, yet it was the scholar's life that ultimately killed them. Your own career, sir, is of the same sort as those three; I fear that I will not even have time to worry for you. Is this really something you should boast about? Not only this, but there are also those who achieve success and fame, who carry <term>Copper Seals<note>Small copper seals were the official insignias of rank given to government officials.</note></term> and stand in the middle of the road, looking quite illustrious. Yet their tracks are merely the external burdens of those three scholars. One might call this "glory," but if one ever offends the Sovereign, one dies at Court; if one commits a crime, one dies in prison; if one braves the wind and frost or encounters dangers on the road, one dies at one’s official post. At the moment of their deaths, did they love their own bodies any less than those three scholars did? It is simply that they wished to return to being a "Farmer of the Field-Huts" <note>An idiom for a retired commoner living a simple life.</note> and could not. This "illustriousness" is nothing more than <term>Sinking on Dry Land<note>A philosophical metaphor meaning to be lost or "drowned" in the world of human affairs and official duties, despite appearing successful.</note></term>. Can the scholar's path truly be relied upon? Therefore, rather than holding a pen while suffering hunger and cold, would it not be better to hold a hoe and be well-fed and warm? Rather than "Understanding the Classics" <note>The <term>Mingjing</term> was a specific category of the imperial examinations focused on the memorization of Confucian texts.</note> and inviting disaster, would it not be better to learn a craft and leave behind a legacy of peace? Moreover, a human life is expected to last a hundred years, but reaching seventy has been an <term>Ancient Rarity<note>A reference to a famous poem by Du Fu: "Since ancient times, those who reach seventy have been rare."</note></term>. I see that your years, sir, are already more than half-spent, yet the opportunity for success has not yet—

<column-break/>

<margin>
This depicts the wretched state of an impoverished scholar, expressing their bitterness to the fullest. For those in this profession, how could this not add ten thousand-fold to the heartache?
</margin>

—arrived. Holding the <gloss>rhythmic beat</gloss> of the weaving loom, if some misfortune is encountered, I fear this body cannot wait for you any longer. Alas, how tragic! To use your limited years to gamble for a fame that is hard to hope for; to exchange your insufficient spirit for a future wealth that may never come. I privately suggest that you, sir, should not choose this path. Furthermore, fame, success, and wealth are but a puppet show. Once they pass before the eyes, they become a void, and they only serve to make a person grow old. Look at the bones in the burial mounds today; they were all the "cunning competitors" of years gone by. Think on it, sir: why not love yourself instead of causing yourself such suffering?

When Zhang finished hearing this, he fell into a great rage and said: "A man's household, and yet I am being lectured by a demonic woman!" He secretly drew his sword and struck at her. The beauty vanished at the movement of his hand as the lamp toppled and was extinguished. He called his servant to bring a fresh candle, but when he looked, he saw only the lamp on the table had been sliced into two pieces. Zhang then had a realization and said: "This lamp was passed down from my grandfather and has been in the family for nearly two hundred years. When an object exists for a long time, it can transform its shape like this." Because of this experience, he recorded the entire event.

<header>The Brush Ghost</header>

### The Long Chant of the Brush Ghost

<margin>This is a general sketch of Scholar Qu’s character. Note by Brother Jing.</margin>
Scholar Qu of Yuanzhou, whose given name was Fushen, lived in the woods without pursuing worldly gains <gloss>referring to business or profit</gloss>. He believed that what a man establishes for himself should be through skill in poetry and prose. In his leisure time, he would often sing to amuse himself. He admired the ancients of high antiquity and did not follow the common customs of the vulgar world. He esteemed the virtues of the Seven Sages of the Bamboo Grove <gloss>a group of 3rd-century Chinese scholars, writers, and musicians</gloss> but found their wild abandonment unsightly. He cherished the loyalty of the Minister of the Three Gates <note>original: "三閭" (Sanlü), an office held by the poet Qu Yuan</note> but found his drowning to be excessive <gloss>referring to the poet Qu Yuan, who drowned himself in protest</gloss>. He recognized the wisdom of the hermit of Mingyi’s departure from public life, yet found his later pursuit of wealth to be circuitous <gloss>referring to Fan Li, a politician who became a legendary wealthy merchant</gloss>. When moved by the scenery, he would immediately call for wine and sing for himself. He did not drink to excess, nor did he sing according to formal melodies. When tired, he would lie down; he did not let the weary paths of the world annoy him. Why should he alone lack the formal constraints of society? On the rivers and lakes, even when the tides of the Wu region rose, he would not take a boat. He had a refined love for cloudy peaks; even where the moss was slippery and the stone steps dangerous, he rarely hesitated or turned back. He was a solitary and upright man, and his reputation was held in high regard. One day, while sitting idly by candlelight at home, his elderly servant reported, "A 'Secretary Mao' is at the door to visit." Fushen ordered the servant to show him in. When the visitor arrived, he saw an old man whose hat

<column-break/>

<margin>This passage traces the history of the brush from its origins to its fame; this was something Scholar Qu already knew well.</margin>
was round and long, and his body was dressed in plain green clothing. Fushen bowed to him and invited him to sit. He asked calmly, "May I ask your honorable name, elder?" The old man said, "My surname is Mao <note>The surname 'Mao' means 'hair' or 'bristle,' hinting at his true nature as a writing brush</note>, and my name is Yuanying <note>Yuanying literally means 'Round Point'</note>. 'Secretary' is my official title. Because you possess such literary elegance, I did not hesitate to visit even in the dark of night. I recall with deep respect that my ancestor once served the First Emperor of Qin and was granted the title of Secretary and General of the Household <gloss>Meng Tian is credited with first inventing the writing brush</gloss>. By the time of Lu Ying of Wuxing, our traditions flourished greatly <gloss>Lu Ying was a famous maker of high-quality brushes</gloss>. Other virtuous gentlemen have often cherished us, such as when Jiang Yan received a colorful brush in a dream, or when Li Bai dreamed of a brush blooming into a flower <gloss>the 'Dream of the Flowering Brush' is a metaphor for a sudden burst of literary inspiration</gloss>. These are all glorious legends of my clan." He then chanted a poem:

> A tip as sharp as a sword, entrusted to the scholar's house,
> With an ivory tube and frosty bristles, the craftsmanship is fine.
> Within the purple jade pool <note>The inkstone</note>, mists and rains are gathered;
> Upon the silver paper, dragons and snakes <note>Cursive calligraphy</note> gallop.
> Jiang Yan was delighted to see colors flow from his verses,
> And for Li Bai, an auspicious flower bloomed within his dream.
> Who says these minor arts provide no great benefit?
> Within the golden palace, we draft the imperial edicts.

Fushen, being clever, realized he was...

<summary>Scholar Qu, a refined recluse, is visited by a mysterious old man named Mao Yuanying. The visitor reveals himself through a poem to be the personification of a writing brush, recounting the legendary history of his "clan" in Chinese literature.</summary>
<keywords>Qu Fushen, Mao Yuanying, writing brush, Meng Tian, Li Bai, Jiang Yan, calligraphy, personification, folklore</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Scholar Qu and his mysterious visitor, who has just been identified through his poetic skill and supernatural nature as a "Brush Ghost."</meta>

# Selected Works from the Pavilion of Pure Reading, Volume 3

### Jing 3

<margin>
This collection from an old family contains four poems. Reciting them, one finds they possess a very ancient style; compared to his other verses, they are in a class of their own.
</margin>

Scholar Qu realized this was a <term>Brush Spirit</term> <note>original: "筆怪" (bǐguài), a supernatural entity formed from an object—in this case, a writing brush—that has gained a soul through long years of use</note>, but he kept his silence. Instead, he flattered him, saying: "I am but a humble scholar, fortunate to receive your kind attention. Listening to your discourse, you are surely no ordinary man. If you have more fine verses, I beg you to recite them once more; please do not grudge me your favor." The old man pondered for a long while, then slowly said: "I have composed four poems titled *Miscellaneous Delights of the Farmstead*. Please, listen."

He then began to loudly recite the first:
> The eastern wind fans a gentle harmony, and the hundred grasses grow lush and green. In the woods, the wood-pigeon calls; the spring scenery is truly warm and bright. The farmer rejoices that spring has arrived, leading one another to plant the fine grain. The seasonal rains passed by last night <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss>, and the new sprouts cluster in vibrant green. The old man returns carrying firewood on his back, while the young child feeds the yellow calf. We are fortunate to find the government offices at leisure; recently, there has been little <term>corvée labor</term> <note>original: "徭役" (yáoyì), forced state labor required of peasants</note>. Let us work hard at our plowing and weeding, lest we become those starving in the empty valleys.

The second poem said:
> Wearing my clogs, I walk out from the eastern valley; the south wind blows by the creek at noon. By chance, I come beneath the pine trees, standing still until I calmly forget all worldly schemes. The village elder sees me arrive; with close affection, we lean toward one another. The mulberry leaves are ready and the silkworms mature; the wheat is being steamed as the young pheasants first take flight. Looking up and down, I contemplate the principles of nature; wandering at ease, I chant my verses and return. I send my thanks to the likes of <term>Wang Liang</term> <note>A legendary master charioteer; here he represents those who chase after worldly speed and ambition</note>; for such empty fame is pursued in vain.

<column-break/>

<margin>
A brush is but a single object of the scholar's studio, yet it too can become a spirit. Generally, when an object endures for a long time, it transforms; the principle is the same for all things.
</margin>

The third poem said:
> The farmstead has no ties to the outside world; my only wish is to manage a full belly. This autumn, we are fortunate to have a small abundance; everywhere the rice and grains are ripe. The rent and taxes are delivered to the government granary, so the village bailiff does not come knocking at night. We set the feast and gather friends and guests; chicken and millet are served with pears and dates. Facing one another, we enjoy a cup of wine, becoming joyfully drunk until we "collapse like a mountain" <note>An idiom for being blissfully and heavily intoxicated</note>.

The fourth poem said:
> Under thatched eaves on a sunny winter day, the feelings in my heart are truly not bad. The brushwood gate only opens when the sun is high; chickens and dogs scatter by the fence. This morning no guests have arrived; we idle folk gather for our own pleasure. Beside the bed, the new wine has ripened; for now, it is enough for us to drink together. I sing loudly to comfort myself; what do I care if I end up dead in a ditch?

Without noticing, the slanted moon sank into the west and the remaining stars grew scattered; the glimmer of dawn was already faint in the east. As they bid each other farewell, Qu followed closely behind him and threw a stone at him. The old man cried out and fled, dropping his hat. When Qu went to inspect it, he found it was actually a brush cap <note>The protective sheath used to cover the bristles of a Chinese writing brush</note>.

<vocab>Brush Spirit (筆怪), Miscellaneous Delights of the Farmstead (田家雜興), Selected Works from the Pavilion of Pure Reading (淸讀閣選), Brush Cap (筆帽), Corvée Labor (徭役), Chicken and Millet (雞黍), Ditches and Gullies (溝壑)</vocab>

<summary>Scholar Qu tricks the mysterious visitor into reciting four poems about the simple joys of rural life before discovering the visitor is actually the spirit of a writing brush. The encounter ends at dawn when the spirit flees, leaving behind a physical brush cap as proof of its identity.</summary>
