<margin>This opening poem describes the layout of the Temple of Flowing Sunlight; it is written with the beauty of a vigorous, experienced hand.</margin>

# The Four Demons Appear in the World

In Baoding, there is a temple called the Temple of Flowing Sunlight <note>original: 華陽觀, Huayang Guan</note>. Its central hall stands majestically, preceded by heavy gates and followed by tiered chambers. To the left and right, it is flanked by wings with five pillars and three rooms each. From the north door of the hall comes a cool breeze, providing a defense against the summer heat. The southern eaves gather the winter sun <gloss>where one stands, the daylight is short</gloss> <note>A reference to the winter solstice</note>, so the bitter cold is of no concern. The woodwork is finished but not painted red; the walls are plastered but not whitened. The foundations are all made of polished stone. For shade, they use screens of paper and bamboo. Inside the hall are plain screens and wooden couches, and collections of scriptures are kept ready. Outside, the tall bamboo grows thick and the ancient trees stand dark and deep. It is truly a grand sight. However, the roofs and rafters are cluttered, and many rooms sit empty. Legend has it that demons and spirits haunt the place at night, so people rarely dare to go there.

At that time, there was a Daoist priest named Xiong Fei. He was a man of strategic mind and was skilled in the art of writing talismans and chanting incantations. One evening, he spoke out boldly, saying, "While others fear to go, I shall go alone." Carrying a sword, he hid in a side room to watch. During the twilight hours, there was no sign of anything. But just as the first watch ended <note>around 9:00 PM</note>, he suddenly saw a figure. Clad in yellow clothing and possessing three legs, the creature came hopping

<column-break/>

<margin>The four creatures describe their own names; they are all word-play riddles using character components to hide their true identities.</margin>

and dancing. It entered from outside and introduced itself as "Hut-with-Fire" <note>original: 廬以火, Lú yǐ huǒ. The characters for "Hut" 廬 and "Fire" 火 combine to form the word "Censer" 爐.</note>. Soon after, three others followed. They were called "Metal-Finish" <note>original: 金竟, Jīn jìng. These characters combine to form "Mirror" 鏡.</note>, "Stone-Visible" <note>original: 石子見, Shí zǐ jiàn. These combine to form "Inkstone" 硯.</note>, and the "Black Daoist" <note>original: 黑道士, Hēi dàoshì. A personification of an inkstick, which is black and used by scholars/priests.</note>. All were just over a foot tall, and each had a distinct appearance. The four met and sat in a circle, laughing and talking freely. Among them, one person looked dejected and unhappy. The others comforted him and asked what was wrong. He replied, "Our kind is about to be sent out to be used by the world; we cannot avoid falling into the hands of others. That is why I am unhappy." The others asked when this would be decided. He answered, "It will be settled tomorrow." Only "Hut-with-Fire" spoke up: "Coming and going has its time, and the length of one's life is fated. Why be gloomy and worry so much beforehand?" He then chanted a poem:

> I’ve been crafted for years unknown,
> An ancient, wondrous thing the world makes known.
> With the number of Heaven, three feet in height,
> Like a mountain peak, a rounded sight.
> Red glows the fire in the Treasure Duck's heart,
> Pure smoke from the Golden Lion’s mouth does part.
> Wisps of drifting silk cannot reach my height,
> In the Jade Hall I dwell, from morning till night.

Then "Metal-Finish" chanted:

> Cast in the red furnace of the Yellow Emperor <note>original: 軒后, Xuanhou</note> of old,
> Polished with labor until the color turns clear and cold.
> Reflecting one's form, easily seen...

<summary>A brave Daoist priest named Xiong Fei stakes out a haunted temple and witnesses four "demons"—personified scholar's objects like an incense burner and a mirror—discussing their fate. The beings use word-play riddles to hide their identities as they lament being pressed into human service.</summary>
<keywords>Huayang Temple, Xiong Fei, personification, scholar's objects, word-play, incense burner, bronze mirror, Baoding, Daoist magic</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the supernatural encounter in the Temple of Flowing Sunlight. The Daoist priest Xiong Fei is witnessing the personified "Four Treasures of the Study" (and a mirror) manifesting as spirits to recite poetry. This transition from prose to poetry is typical of Ming-dynasty "tales of the strange," where objects reveal their histories through verse.</meta>

Whether the face be beautiful or ugly, the heart remains hard to know; so too with the sentiments of good and evil.
When the jewel box opens, the "Toad’s Cave" <note>A poetic reference to the moon</note> glows with brilliance;
Where the jade terrace hangs, the lunar disk shines clear.
While the beautiful lady pauses to arrange her rosy countenance,
The wandering poet is startled to see white hair appearing.

### The Song of the Stone

The **Stone** <note>The personification of the inkstone</note> stepped forward and chanted:
"Refined from the stone bones of Southern Stream, firm and strong,
This new 'Toad' pattern cost much effort to carve.
Its 'horse-liver' texture <note>A dark, smooth reddish-purple color highly prized in inkstones</note> is moist with the waters of the vast sea,
Its 'myna eyes' <note>term: 鴝眼 (Quyan); circular stone inclusions in Duanxi inkstones that resemble bird eyes, considered a mark of the highest quality</note> are clear, reflecting the green mountain spring.
In the Golden Palace, the Noble Consort once held it in service;
In the Jade Hall, the Academicians of old wore it through with grinding.
I have lived as a neighbor to scholars for a long time,
Transmitted forever as a supreme treasure of the scholar’s studio."

<gloss>The Black Daoist’s seven-character ancient-style verse ending is actually superior to his previous poems.</gloss>

The **Black Daoist** <note>The personification of the ink-stick</note> deeply praised the beauty of the verse and then composed a seven-character ancient-style poem, saying:
"Fragments of ancient pine bones from Mount Culai <note>A mountain in Shandong famous for pines used in making high-quality ink soot</note>,
In a bamboo hut, the fragrance of smoke fills the air.
The Daoist rises at dawn and searches the bedside,
Sweeping up two or three bushels of 'dark frost' <note>A poetic name for ink soot</note>.
In the empty mountains, the sound of iron pestles echoes;
The Moon Maiden and the gentle wind pound the mixture until it is smooth.
Suddenly, I see dragons leaping across the desk,
I suspect a rainbow light is flashing before my eyes.
The young scholar and the eminent gentleman are talented and worthy;
For such treasures, I would offer heavy sums of gold.
The Magpie Hut <note>The workshop of a famous craftsman</note> is gone, and the father sighs;
But the 'Magpie Light' <note>The luster of the ink</note> lasts through the long days, truly worthy of—"

<column-break/>

"—pity.
I am stubborn and talentless <note>original: 戇無能 (zhuàng wú néng)</note>, what can I do about this?
All day long, the treasures of the studio soak in the pool of the ink-well.
Who is it that stands at the shield’s edge to draft a call to arms?
Whose name will remain after the brush is moistened?
Sometimes, beneath the window, I test a grind—
Ink splashing like clouds and mist, churning the river waves."

*Wu Mozhi* <note>Likely a name or reference to a specific ink-maker</note> ceased his efforts, and the old man... <unclear>text obscured</unclear> ...brushed the tip to record the official history. He regretted that there were not 'ninety thousand sheets of Shanyin paper' <note>A reference to Wang Xizhi, the Sage of Calligraphy</note>, for his practiced robes were old and could not withstand washing for many years.
"Call the boy to sweep the wall, and I shall write a poem;
The color of moss fills the wall while the autumn rain falls."

When the chanting ended, they all laughed together beautifully, unaware that the eastern sky was already turning white.

<margin>If the Eastern Stove was not dug up later, then this story clarifies it; the saying that these things are 'meant for the world' is indeed true.</margin>

Seeing the dawn break, the group dispersed. Xiong Fei marked the place where they vanished near the foot of the eastern wall. The following day, he ordered several men to dig. At a depth of only one or two feet, they discovered:
*   One ancient bronze incense burner
*   One bronze mirror
*   One **Duanxi inkstone** <note>The most famous type of Chinese inkstone, from Guangdong</note>
*   One **Songxi ink-stick**

From that point on, the hauntings in the temple ceased entirely.

<summary>The personified scholar's tools finish their poetic contest as dawn breaks and they vanish. Xiong Fei digs up the physical objects—an incense burner, mirror, inkstone, and ink—effectively ending the haunting of the temple.</summary>
<keywords>Four Treasures of the Study, inkstone, ink-stick, Duanxi, supernatural tale, poetry contest, Xiong Fei, Daoism</keywords>

# Three Elders and the Night Stroll
<note>The title "Night Stroll" (夜逸, Yeyi) refers to a common trope in Ming-dynasty literature where a scholar wanders out at night to encounter supernatural beings or spirits of the natural world.</note>

### The Gathering of the Elders

->**Gray-haired old men, beneath the bright moon and clear stars, chant poetry together.**<-
<note>original: "吟哦" (Yin'e) — a specific style of rhythmic, melodic recitation used by scholars when composing or appreciating verse.</note>

<column-break/>

### The Solitary Lady of the Pine

->**In the dark and desolate outskirts, the cry of a crane and the whistling of the wind add to the loneliness.**<-
<note>original: "鶴唳" (Heli) — the cry of the crane. In classical Chinese aesthetics, the crane's call is often associated with high-minded recluses or, as seen here, a mournful sense of isolation in the wild.</note>

***

### Key Vocabulary for this Passage

*   **Three Elders** <term>三老 (Sanlao); often referring to the personifications of the three stars of Blessings, Prosperity, and Longevity, or simply a group of respected senior spirits.</term>
*   **Night Stroll** <term>夜逸 (Yeyi); a leisurely or escapist wandering under the cover of night.</term>
*   **Rhythmic Chanting** <term>吟哦 (Yin'e); the classical way of intoning poetry.</term>
*   **Loneliness** <term>寂寞 (Jimo); a profound sense of solitude, often used in poetry to describe the atmosphere of a landscape.</term>
*   **The Big Dipper** <term>北斗七星 (Beidou Qixing); the "Seven Stars of the Northern Bushel," a vital celestial marker in Daoist and folk cosmology.</term>
*   **Crane’s Cry** <term>鶴唳 (Heli); a sound signifying both purity and the melancholy of the wilderness.</term>

The scene uses celestial symbols like the Big Dipper and the moon to establish a mystical, scholar-literati atmosphere.</summary>
<keywords>Three Elders, Night Stroll, poetry recitation, Big Dipper, crane's cry, melancholy, Ming-dynasty literature, supernatural encounter</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Strange Encounters" theme from the previous section. While the previous page featured the spirits of writing tools, this narrative shifts to the outskirts of Pizhou, where the protagonist, Scholar Xiang, encounters three elderly figures who are the personified spirits of ancient weapons (a sword and a bow).</meta>

# A Strange Encounter with Three Elders

Xiang Qi, a scholar from Pizhou, was exceptionally brilliant from a young age. As he grew, he attended school where he was known for his diligence, agility of mind, and mastery of poetic composition. He once traveled with friends as a wandering gallant to the Shrine of Marquis Han in Huaiyin—this is a true account. One of his friends said to Qi, "Give us a poem; why not compose a regulated verse to express your ambitions?" Without the need for painful deliberation, Qi flourished his brush and wrote upon the wall:

<margin>The poetic meter reflects his own mourning for the man of Huaiyin.</margin>
<note>The "Man of Huaiyin" refers to Han Xin (d. 196 BCE), a brilliant general who helped establish the Han Dynasty but was later executed. He is a symbol of tragic genius.</note>

> A hero’s image remains, solemn through a thousand autumns,
> While the shrine looks desolate, shaded by ancient, gloomy trees.
> With a crouching sword, he once hunted the deer of the Qin,
> <note>original: "秦氏鹿" (Qin shi lu) — hunting the deer is a classic metaphor for competing for the imperial throne.</note>
> But did his merit lie merely in the titles of the Han court?
> In late spring, the fragrant grass still grows in sorrow everywhere,
> At sunset, the Great Huai River sobs and ceases to flow.
> Long-past events are too distant to ask about;
> The resentful blood beneath the blue sky remains a sorrow today.

His friends marveled in admiration. One day, while visiting an old acquaintance <gloss>meaning a long-time friend</gloss>, Qi was walking through the countryside when evening fell. At that time, a pale moon shone dimly, a whistling wind blew, and the rustling of grass and trees created a haunting sound. For more than ten miles, there were no human dwellings; mist and fog seemed to house spirits, and tangled thorns confused the eyes. Qi was gripped by sudden alarm, finding no

<column-break/>

good plan in his heart. Suddenly, he saw three elders approaching with laughter. Their appearances were all distinct. They met Qi on the path, bowed, and said to him, "Why have you come so late, sir?" Qi replied, "It is because I was visiting a friend." The three elders then sat down with Qi on the brushed-aside grass. One elder said, "On such a fine night, to have found such an esteemed companion! We have nothing with which to make merry <gloss>pronounced 'le'</gloss>. Shall we each compose a poem?" I agreed to this. Thereupon, one old man first chanted:

<margin>At the third watch, he chants a poem that also recounts his own origins.</margin>

> In what year did Heaven's smith forge this blue dragon?
> <note>original: "蒼虬" (Cangqiu) — literally 'Blue-Green Horned Dragon,' a poetic name for a legendary sword.</note>
> Wandering the human realm, uncounted autumns have passed.
> To crush the rebels, I must return to a great general’s hand;
> To serve the sovereign, I would first strike off the sycophant's head.
> Cold light leaves the scabbard, bright as frost and snow;
> Purple energy pierces the heavens, aiming for the Big Dipper.
> <note>original: "斗牛" (Dou-Niu) — the Dipper and Ox constellations; in Chinese lore, a sword's aura reaching these stars indicates a weapon of divine quality.</note>
> In today's era of peace, there is no place for my use;
> I ask you to carry me along for a stroll through the Five Tombs. <gloss>Five Tombs is a place name.</gloss>
> <note>The Five Tombs (Wuling) was an area near the Han capital inhabited by wealthy families and adventurous young men.</note>

Then another old man chanted:

> With horn from Yan and glue from the Qilin, the wood of Chu is made firm;
> <note>These materials—horn, glue, and wood—are the traditional components of a high-quality composite bow.</note>
> The state employs such things only to bolster military power.
> Where the arrowhead flies, it is as swift as a shooting star;
> When the string is drawn, it is as round as the full moon.
> The tiger-squadrons were startled back to the northern borders;
> The 'Bird-Cry' bow brought things down by the side of the Tripod Lake.
> <note>original: "鳥號" (Niaohao) — the name of the Yellow Emperor's legendary bow. "Tripod Lake" (Dinghu) is where the Yellow Emperor ascended to heaven.</note>
> But now, empty-handed, my dappled horse rests;
> I lie deep in sleep by the Fusang tree...
> <note>Fusang is a mythical tree in the East where the sun rises.</note>

***

### Key Vocabulary for this Passage

*   **Three Elders** <term>三老 (Sanlao); here, personifications of ancient weapons who appear as elderly men.</term>
*   **Scholar Xiang** <term>項生 (Xiang Sheng); the protagonist, a gifted poet who encounters the supernatural.</term>
*   **Huaiyin** <term>淮陰; the home of the famous general Han Xin, serving as a setting for reflections on history and tragedy.</term>
*   **Shrine of Marquis Han** <term>韓侯祠 (Han Hou Ci); a temple dedicated to Han Xin.</term>
*   **Blue Dragon** <term>蒼虬 (Cangqiu); a metaphor for a masterfully forged sword.</term>
*   **Five Tombs** <term>五陵 (Wuling); a region associated with the elite and the military culture of the Han Dynasty.</term>
*   **Yan Horn and Lin Glue** <term>燕角麟膠 (Yanjiao Linjiao); quintessential materials used in the construction of legendary bows.</term>
*   **Bird-Cry** <term>鳥號 (Niaohao); the specific name of the bow belonging to the mythical Yellow Emperor.</term>
*   **Tripod Lake** <term>鼎湖 (Dinghu); the site of the Yellow Emperor's divine ascension.</term>

<summary>Scholar Xiang Qi encounters three mysterious elders in a desolate landscape who reveal themselves through poetry to be the spirits of a legendary sword and a bow. The narrative uses these personifications to reflect on themes of past military glory versus current times of peace.</summary>
<keywords>Xiang Qi, Han Xin, personification, sword spirit, bow spirit, Pizhou, military history, Ming dynasty fiction</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Scholar Qi (identified as Li Qi in the source collection Jingshi Tongyan) and his encounter with supernatural entities. While the previous page established his poetic talent and his journey through the countryside, this page concludes his meeting with three mysterious elders who are revealed to be the spirits of ancient weapons from a forgotten war.</meta>

# The Song of the Frontier and the Ancient Spirits

<margin>Even the "Song of the King of Chu" by Xiang Yu followed this rhythmic tone; thus the three elders spoke in such a manner.</margin>

...old trees stood frequently. Another elder then chanted: "The black banners are unfurled to their limit, opening in the midst of battle. The Eight Arrays <note>The 'Eight Arrays' refers to a legendary tactical formation attributed to the master strategist Zhuge Liang.</note> of the commander-in-chief take their position, like eagles and ospreys soaring. Shadows move across the autumn frontier as the dragon and snake formations coil. A thousand miles of northern wind commands the troops; the ranks are solemn for a time, and the general's heart is heroic." <gloss>The word "general" here is pronounced in the departing tone to denote his rank.</gloss> "Now in times of peace, all weapons are withdrawn and utterly useless. The barbarian tribes are now swept clean from the earth."

Qi deeply admired the poem. The elder said, "The three of us are exceedingly clumsy, and it has been long since we practiced the craft of poetry. We wish to hear your instruction; what say you?"

Qi then composed a section of the *Song of Yan* <term>Yan Ge Xing: a classic poetic form used to describe the hardships and glory of military life at the northern frontier</term>, using it to praise the three elders:

"Yellow elms and white grass stretch across the northern frontier, where the eyes meet only yurts and barbarian foes. Martial energy flies through the empty camps, reaching the clouds; brave warriors look at one another, their faces losing color. The commander receives the decree as the palace gates open; between the brocade tents and ivory-topped banners, the fortifications are set. Standing alone with a single banner, wielding the authority of the ceremonial axe, who could settle the Tianshan Mountains with but three arrows?" <note>This refers to the Tang Dynasty general Xue Rengui, who famously defeated a massive force with only three arrows, causing the enemy to surrender in awe.</note> "The Chanyu <note>The title for the supreme leader of the Xiongnu nomadic confederation.</note> was raised on the lands of joy, leading cattle and sheep through the autumn rain around his tents. In moments of leisure, they gather to sing the songs of Liangzhou; when drunk, they rise to perform the Whirling Dance of the West." <note>The 'Hu Xuan Wu' (Whirling Dance) was a fast, spinning dance introduced to China from Central Asia during the Tang Dynasty.</note> "Ancient groves of trees stand by the banks of the Yulong River..."

<column-break/>

<margin>In the ancient battlefield, corpses covered the fields and a martial aura filled the air; it is no wonder these three objects became spirits.</margin>

"...Hegan Mountain, where flying birds are rare. The 'purple-bearded barbarian' children have eyes of double emerald; the 'big-bellied Xiongnu' have waists ten spans wide. Master Xu has long been stationed at the distant frontier, even more so as a descendant of the noble families from the Eastern Sea. To harmonize with the strategies of 'Tigers and Leopards' to defeat the enemy, his secondary merit should earn him a place among the portraits of the Qilin Hall." <gloss>This refers to the practice of recording the likenesses of the empire's greatest meritorious officials in the Qilin Gallery; the poet laments that such high character is rare today.</gloss> "People do not know; such effortless fame and merit do not exist today. In the clear autumn, a long sword leans against the southern sky; at midnight, the solitary city faces the Big Dipper. Do not say the crowd looks on with disdain; wait to see your merit recorded high upon bamboo and silk <note>Ancient writing materials used before the widespread use of paper, symbolizing permanent historical records.</note>. Have you not seen how the Marquis of Ye <note>Li Mi (722–789), a brilliant strategist who served four Tang emperors.</note> recognized a true man of the state? Ascending the altar, he was finally appointed Grand General."

Shortly thereafter, the sky gradually brightened. The three elders looked at each other in startling realization and vanished. Qi followed behind them and saw them sink into the mud. He ordered men to dig there, and they found a precious sword, a carved bow, and banners that had already rotted away. Upon inquiring with the local elders, he learned that this was an ancient battlefield.

<summary>Scholar Qi exchanges martial poetry with three mysterious elders who vanish at dawn, leaving behind ancient weapons buried in the mud of an old battlefield. The passage highlights the transformation of physical objects into spirits through the lingering "martial energy" of historical conflicts.</summary>

<keywords>Li Qi, Ancient Battlefield, Spirits, Poetry, Military Allusions, Qilin Hall, Xue Rengui, Tang Dynasty, Folklore</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Selected Drafts of Pure Conversation," a collection of supernatural tales and refined literary anecdotes. This specific entry, "The Six Vessels of the Zen Chamber," follows a common folk motif where everyday objects come to life as spirits, a concept similar to the Japanese *tsukumogami*.</meta>

->Selected Drafts of Pure Conversation<-

<margin>
It is fitting that a monk should take shelter in a temple; however, his account of these six objects manifesting as spirits is quite beyond one’s wildest imagination.
</margin>

# The Six Vessels of the Zen Chamber

In the early years of the Zhengde era <note>The reign of the Ming Emperor Wuzong, 1506–1521.</note>, a monk from Xinzhou named Lüren <note>His name literally means "Walking in Benevolence."</note> was returning home at dusk when he was caught in a downpour. Finding an ancient temple along the road, he sought shelter inside. Upon entering, he found the grounds overgrown with mugwort and weeds, with no sign of any human presence—it was an abandoned ruin. Only the main hall remained standing, where a golden Buddha statue shone with a brilliant luster. A single lamp flickered before the altar, casting a faint light. Out of necessity, Lüren rested there, intending to nap until dawn. At that moment, the clouds parted to reveal the moon's radiance, and the stars circled the Big Dipper. A chilly wind began to sough, and the cold air felt as if it were piercing his skin. Feeling uneasy, Lüren composed a poem in the "regulated verse" style:

> Amidst the wild weeds of this ancient shrine, the world’s waves recede,
> Stone steps wind through the void where only birds might pass.
> At dawn, the ten-fathom golden body opens against the green cliffs,
> In the shrine, a single lamp flickers behind the misty vines.
> Clouds arise as the guest arrives, soaking his clothes with damp;
> Flowers fall as the monk meditates, carpeting the floor.
> Since I have no brothers here to form a fellowship,
> What will it be like when I finally return to the dusty world?

After a short while, as the first watch ended, he saw a figure dressed in yellow garments and a black hat. The figure had only one leg.

<column-break/>

<margin>
These six poems are not of high literary quality, but they serve only to describe the nature of the objects themselves.
</margin>

The figure came hopping toward him. Terrified, Lüren tried to hide, but the person said, "Do not be afraid. You and I are of the same family. It is raining tonight, so I have come to keep you company." Before he had finished speaking, five more figures arrived hand-in-hand. One wore thread-bare clothes and had a square body; one wore black garments and had a slender body; one had a narrow waist with a square top and a rounded bottom; one had a square frame and a wide, large circumference; and finally, an old man in white garments arrived with a fluttering, elegant air. They bowed to one another and sat down, exchanging glances as they began to discuss poetry. The figure in the yellow clothes and black hat chanted:

> Bamboo serves as my bones, and paper as my skin;
> I fear not the drenching wind or the driving rain.
> I follow you for a thousand miles, a constant shadow;
> Opening and closing at will throughout the four seasons.
> When my round wheel is overhead, I offer shelter and shade;
> When my long handle is in hand, I require your support.
> My sound is like shivering jade or rain hitting plantain leaves;
> Do you truly understand this scene and this feeling?
<note>This poem describes a paper umbrella.</note>

The figure in threaded clothes with a square body chanted:

> With pearl-dusted foil and silver hooks, tied with colored rope;
> Exquisite and translucent, clear through all seasons.
> In the painted hall, I am rolled high, smooth as glaze;
> By the vermilion door, I hang low, light as kingfisher feathers.
> Through the long days, a cool breeze passes through my fine mesh...
<note>This poem describes a bamboo screen or curtain.</note>

<summary>A monk seeks shelter in an abandoned temple and is visited by a group of spirits who are actually personified household objects, beginning a poetic exchange to reveal their identities.</summary>
<keywords>Zhengde era, monk Lüren, supernatural encounter, personified objects, paper umbrella, bamboo screen, Chinese ghost stories, Zen chamber</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the monk Lüren from the "Selected Drafts of Pure Conversation." Having found shelter in a ruined temple, he now witnesses the supernatural manifestation of common objects as they reveal their spirits through poetry.</meta>

->Floating World Collection: Volume One<-

<margin>
Fei Changfang <note>A legendary Han dynasty occultist.</note> of the Jin dynasty once threw his staff into Ge Pond, where it transformed into a dragon and flew away; hence the poem refers to this.
</margin>

Deep into the night, the clouds cleared, allowing the moonlight to leak through in all its brilliance. In ancient times, it was said that Lady Jia peeped at the young clerk Han <note>A reference to a famous romance where a daughter spied on her father's handsome subordinate.</note>, a story whose fame has endured for a thousand years. Amidst this stillness, a **man in black robes with a slender frame** stepped forward and recited:

> "I was gathered as a nine-jointed cane from the peaks of Penglai <note>The mythical island of the immortals.</note>,
> Used by elders to support them as they walked at leisure.
> My head is carved into a pigeon’s shape, crafted to sit above the brow;
> My 'crane-knee' joints are light to carry in the hand.
> I have accompanied monks to wild temples under the moon,
> And entered mountain cities to gather medicinal herbs.
> I advise you, sir, do not cast me away into the pond,
> For I have already seen myself transformed into a blue dragon."

Then, a **man with a slender waist** recited:

> "I lie like a tiger or a coiled dragon upon the ivory bed,
> Ensuring that idle sorrows never reach the 'Village of Dark Sweetness' <note>A literary euphemism for deep sleep.</note>.
> My form is curved like the morning moon, as smooth as coral;
> My bones are cold as autumn clouds, scented with amber.
> I startle the sleeper from the 'clacking wood' <note>A reference to a 'warning pillow' used by scholars to wake up and study.</note> so the night is not wasted,
> For how long can the day last when the yellow millet is cooked? <note>Refers to the 'Yellow Millet Dream,' a story about the fleeting nature of worldly ambition.</note>
> I have troubled myself to see the ten continents and three islands of the immortals,
> Yet after one such journey, my thoughts remain vast and misty."

Next, a **man in square robes** <note>The OCR reads "方休人," likely a transcription of "方衣人" (square-clothed man), representing the mat.</note> recited:

> "Woven from the bamboo of Qi <note>Qizhou was famous for its high-quality bamboo mats.</note> to rest upon the ivory bed,
> I am like shorn leaves reflecting a person's light.
> My moisture surpasses the jade pillow during the five-watch rain;
> My coldness soaks through the silk curtains like June frost.
> My patterns are like the ripples of the Xiang River <note>Refers to 'mottled bamboo,' said to be stained by the tears of the goddesses of the Xiang River.</note>,
> In the shadows, I seem like a piece of the wild...

<column-break/>

<margin>
These six vessels were able to manifest as humans and compose poems under the moon in this manner; how strange that their essence remains the same as their physical forms.
</margin>

...clouds stretched across the southern window, fending off the oppressive heat. When one lies upon me in the pavilion, I cause ten thousand worries to be forgotten."

Finally, a **man in white robes** stood alone and recited:

> "Heaven and earth are a furnace where the cruel summer steams,
> Who took pure white silk and skillfully tailored my form?
> My 'dragon bones' <note>The ribs of the fan.</note> are carved from sturdy frosted bamboo;
> My 'white crane feathers' are cut from light, snowy paper.
> When shaken, I unfurl like a half-wheel of the bright moon;
> I summon a refreshing breeze to rise from beneath one's arms.
> As autumn deepens, I only fear the parting of our ways,
> But what can be done? The world’s favor changes with the heat and the cold."

Lüren listened to them all, noting that the rhythm and melody of each speaker was flawless. In his heart, he doubted they were human, for the mountain temple was deserted; yet he doubted they were ghosts, for the style of their poetry did not resemble the verses of the dead. 

He waited until the cock began to crow and the sky gradually brightened. The six figures then calmly dispersed. Lüren immediately went to the spot where they had been sitting to search for traces of them. He found only a broken umbrella, a ruined flute, a staff, a fan, a pillow, and a mat—all of them discarded items, buried under dust and abandoned on the ground for a long time. 

Lüren had a great realization. He walked away quickly and did not look back.

<summary>This page concludes the tale of the "Six Vessels," where a monk witnesses a staff, pillow, mat, and fan manifesting as spirits to recite poetry before discovering they are merely discarded household objects. The story serves as a meditation on the hidden life of objects and the ephemeral nature of the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>animism, Ming dynasty, supernatural poetry, Buddhist allegory, Fei Changfang, Yellow Millet Dream, tsukumogami</keywords>

### The Five Spirits of the Lu River

<margin>Leaning against the window of the boat's awning, I rested my chin and gazed out; who could have imagined that I would happen to see five figures passing by and catch a glimpse of them...</margin>
Half a year has now passed <gloss>the original indicates this follows a "level tone" rhyme scheme</gloss>. My desire for a political career is thinner than water. The passing of the seasons urges a man forward as fast as the waves.
Wild geese are startled by the autumn rains gathering on the rivers and lakes; cattle and sheep crowd the countryside under the many glows of the evening sun. Throughout my life, I have held a refined desire for solitude; but now my hair is white as the stars—what can be done about old age? <gloss>Following the rhyme: leaning against the boat window and gazing idly, I suddenly saw five people.</gloss>
They were barely over a foot tall, gathered together on the riverbank. One of them said, "You all may be happy as you please, but what of my own lofty purity? Long ago, I lived in a humble alley and shared songs and laughter with Master Yan <note>The speaker refers to Yan Hui, the favorite disciple of Confucius, who was famous for living happily in extreme poverty.</note>; I also visited Master Xu <note>Xu You, a legendary hermit of the golden age who refused the offer of the throne to live in the mountains.</note> <gloss>This refers to Xu You.</gloss> at Mount Ji to invite him for a wander."
Another said, "You speak of your own height, but can you match me? I am square and round, upright and straight, the very embodiment of the compass and the square. I have not the slightest bend or crook. Furthermore, those I associate with are all the most distinguished scholars of this age."
Another said, "You two are merely boasting. I was born in the mountain ravines, pure and clean. I was fashioned into a tool to benefit the ten thousand people—is this not the beautiful path of 'broadly bestowing and aiding the masses'?"
Another said, "A 'wise merchant hides his goods as if his store were empty,' and 'the great virtue of a gentleman makes his appearance seem as if he were a fool.' <note>A quote from the 'Zhuangzi,' suggesting that truly wise people do not show off.</note> Why..."

<column-break/>

<margin>This describes the quiet and elegant refinement of the study, depicting every detail without omission.</margin>
Lan Song of <term>Wuxi</term> <note>A historic city in Jiangsu province, known for its culture and the scenic Lake Tai.</note>, whose courtesy name was Tingxiu. During his youth <gloss>when he was a child</gloss>, he studied for the imperial examinations. He had a fine study in a secluded spot on the southern slope of <term>Mount Xi</term> <note>Also known as Huishan, famous for its spring water and scholarly retreats.</note>, called the "Mount Xi Retreat."
The place was tranquil and remote, with water and stones of a quiet elegance. It was enclosed by several layers of high walls and shaded by a few stalks of tall bamboo. The study was built upon a foundation of stacked stones, its beams rising out of the forest.
The rapids reflected against the eaves, and a spring flowed around the steps. Moonlight and the wind through the pines graced the courtyard where grass grew in lush patterns. The sunlight and clouds shimmered together, and stars were scattered like a net in the sky. Drifting mist and the breath of the heavens unrolled and curled there. Peach and plum trees mingled with pines and cypresses to create a vibrant green.
Song studied there. When he failed to grasp a concept, he felt frustrated; when he succeeded, he was joyful. He was seemingly unaware of his own fatigue. Eventually, his studies were completed, and he became renowned for his deep knowledge of antiquity.
When he entered government service, he held the post of <term>Prefect of the Imperial Fields</term> <note>Jitian Ling, an official responsible for managing the lands where the Emperor performed ritual farming to ensure a good harvest.</note>. At that time, his mother <note>The text uses "Xuan-tang," literally "Daylily Hall," a poetic term for a mother.</note> was over eighty years old.
Song said, "If I cannot serve my parent, how can I fulfill my duty to the Emperor? His Majesty's kindness is urgent, but my duty to my mother is paramount." He resigned his post to return south. His boat stopped at the <term>Lu River</term> on the first day of the eighth month.
There, he composed a poem: "In the eighth month, I move my boat out from the Lu River; the traveler’s journey begins again..."

<meta>This page continues from Lan Song’s observation of five small figures by the river. These figures are revealed to be the personified spirits of household objects—a gourd ladle, a paperweight, a rush mat, a hearth, and a teapot—each reciting a poem that reveals their identity and history.</meta>

<margin>
The poems of these five "Yellow Guests" each describe their own nature as objects; none are merely frivolous.
</margin>
"With such boasting, will there not be critics who follow?" one of them said. "On such a fine night, let us indulge ourselves. Why not use rhymes and 'strike and push' <note>A traditional term for composing and refining poetry through rhythmic recitation.</note> to see what is truly worthy of praise?" All of them agreed. One stepped forward to chant first:

> "I grew at the head of mountain forests and water caves; my waist was bent for many springs and autumns. In a poor and lowly alley, I was the companion of Master Yan <note>Yan Hui, the favorite disciple of Confucius, who lived happily in poverty with only a single gourd for a cup.</note>; later, I sought the hermit Xu You at Mount Ji. For travelers on the road, I am a necessary vessel; for monks crossing the sea, I serve as a floating cup. Since the day my round form was split in two, I have held half of heaven and earth within myself."

Then another chanted:

> "Fine wood was cut to form my several-feet length; during the day, my use is found in the scholar's study. I press down the books as a single straight bar; I keep the paper steady with my four square corners. I follow the path of the ink-line without any crooked intent; whether vertical or horizontal, I allow the scrolls to be divided into rows. <gloss>The sound of the word 'rows' here is 'kang'.</gloss> Even the 'ink slaves' <note>A playful term for pens or scribes.</note> understand how to maintain discipline under me, for I ensure the 'crow soldiers' <note>A metaphor for black ink characters on the page.</note> do not cross the borders."

Another chanted:

> "Vines from the creek were cut and densely woven; crafted with skill into a circular pattern. On a stone bed, I am spread out as light as a lick of flame; on a wooden couch, I am often as soft as cotton. I am most suited for the halls of a Daoist temple,"

<column-break/>

<margin>
The "Five Yellows" are all spirits transformed from objects used by humans; thus, their names refer to their physical forms. Lan Song also used this designation to refer to them.
</margin>
> "or a monk’s cell, providing a restful night of Zen. How many times have I sat alone in the place where words are forgotten, while the 'Lord of Heaven' <note>A philosophical term for the human heart or mind.</note> remains naturally at peace?"

Another chanted:

> "I was chiseled directly into the ground to serve as a furnace. Let the wind and snow fill the mountain city; in several places, the light smoke of brushwood scatters as old wood knots are added to keep the 'living fire' burning. Tea is brewed until 'crab-eye' bubbles <note>A traditional description of the first stage of boiling water, where small bubbles resemble the eyes of a crab.</note> appear in the cauldron; 'Dragon Bud' tea is simmered with snow in the pan. The whole family, old and young, sits together in a circle—who could know a greater peace than living in the mountains?"

The last one chanted:

> "Taller than a bottle and cleverer than a pit; one must know that with a small capacity, I am easily filled. When the 'living fire' is added, the 'crab-eyes' roll; where the clear spring water boils, the sound of 'buzzing flies' <note>A poetic description of the high-pitched sound of boiling water.</note> emerges. I rinse the remnants from 'stone teeth' <note>A metaphor for the tea leaves or perhaps the spout's edge.</note> as the waves break; my call pierces the silence like mountain winds through the bamboo. How many quiet nights in a snowy house have I washed the ears <note>An allusion to the hermit Xu You, who washed his ears to cleanse them after being offered a government position; here it implies spiritual purification through tea.</note> of men until the third watch?"

Lan Song listened intently to these five poems and found them exceedingly strange. He hurried forward to look, but he saw nothing there at all.

# Loss of Virtue | The Young Monk

### A beauty stays the night in a monk's residence; what hidden passion stirs within the dark?
<note>In Chinese literature, "spring" (chun) is a common metaphor for romantic or sexual desire. The "hidden spring" suggests a secret or illicit affair beginning in the darkness of the monastery.</note>

<image-desc>A woodblock print illustration depicting a woman with her hair styled in a bun, wearing traditional robes, and sitting on a low stool. ### A disciple of Buddha <note>original: "釋子" (shizi), literally 'Son of Shakyamuni,' a term for a monk.</note> gives way to wild Zen; he studies, yet knows not that emptiness is form.
<note>This is a play on the famous "Heart Sutra" line "Emptiness is form, and form is emptiness." In this context, "form" (se) also means physical beauty or lust. "Wild Zen" (kuangchan) refers to an unorthodox or undisciplined approach to Buddhism that ignores traditional precepts.</note>

<meta>The narrative transition is now complete. We move from the poetic "Yellow Guests" to a specific supernatural tale set in the Lingyin Temple during the Jingtai era of the Ming Dynasty. This page introduces the monk Zhanran and his fateful encounter with a mysterious woman.</meta>

# Secrets of Pure Words <unclear/>

<margin>She recounts her history and where she has been, yet she is deceiving him with things that only seem plausible in principle.</margin>

Regarding her name, the woman said: "I am your humble servant, a daughter from a family neighboring this temple. My parents loved me dearly and I was late to marry. Now, I have a secret lover, and thus I have several times <gloss>twice</gloss> slipped out unnoticed. Passing by here, my affections have shifted toward you. However, you must keep this matter strictly confidential so that our relationship may endure. Otherwise, we shall both be disgraced." The monk happily agreed and followed her command. From then on, she left at dawn and came at dusk; there was no night they did not meet. This continued for nearly a full year, and the passionate monk <note>original: "火僧" (huoseng), likely referring to the monk consumed by the "fire" of lust.</note> did not realize his body was becoming withered and thin. His breathing became labored and he gradually lost the will to live. Although his fellow monks tried a hundred different medical treatments, their efforts were in vain. 

An old monk in the temple said to him: "I have examined your pulse. You are suffering from labor-exhaustion <note>original: "勞瘵" (laozhai), a traditional term for a consumptive illness or tuberculosis, often associated in folklore with depletion of essence due to ghosts.</note> combined with a severe attack of 'yin-shadow evil' <note>Evil energy associated with ghosts or spirits.</note>. You must have suffered some great depletion. If you do not speak clearly, there is no way to save you." Zhanran was terrified and forced himself to recount the past events. The crowd of monks said: "That is it! If this haunting is not removed, your illness will not be cured. Tonight, if she comes again, you must wait for her to leave and then track her; only then can we apply the arts of healing." That night, the woman arrived, and the monk again coupled with her. As she was about to leave, he wished to rise and see her off, but the woman stopped him, saying: "Monks live in clusters; at night, people might—"

<column-break/>

<margin>At first, she pressed close to him as if wanting to enter the door, but he could not reach her. Then, she passed before him and he rose quickly to see her, yet again he could not reach her. This 'inability to reach' her made him want to possess her all the more; she intentionally made it difficult to entice him.</margin>

### The Monk Stirred by Evil Fire
During the Jingtai era <note>The reign of the Ming Emperor Daizong, 1449–1457.</note>, there was a young monk at the Lingyin Temple in Zhejiang named Zhanran. His nature was perceptive and quick, and his appearance was exceptionally handsome <gloss>possessing talent and fine features</gloss>. The monk's quarters where he resided were particularly secluded and quiet. One evening during the summer heat, while sitting alone in the courtyard, he saw a beautiful woman. She was slender but did not wear long skirts; she walked with a light and swift step, and her graceful posture <gloss>charming and lithe</gloss> was captivating. She wore little makeup or adornment. The monk wished to approach and question her, but she had already moved away. The next night, while he was going to the latrine, she passed before him again. He rose quickly to look at her, but again could not reach her. Anyone else in this situation would find it unbearable—how much more so for a monk? How much more so for a monk in the heat of his youth? 

From then on, he was deeply confused and distressed, his lustful passions drawing him in. He could not stop his obsessive thoughts. Two days later, she again walked slowly by his side. The monk hurriedly grabbed her clothes. The woman then feigned a look of shame and timidity. After he pleaded with her repeatedly, she finally entered his room. Once they were seated, the monk pressed his body close to hers and gradually began to flirt and tease. Eventually, they fulfilled the "clouds and rain" <note>A classic Chinese euphemism for sexual intercourse.</note>. Zhanran then calmly asked her where she lived.

<summary>This page tells the beginning of a ghost story where a young monk named Zhanran at Lingyin Temple is seduced by a mysterious woman. Over the course of a year, the affair drains his life force, leading to a diagnosis of spiritual haunting by an elder monk.</summary>
<keywords>Zhanran, Lingyin Temple, Jingtai Era, ghost seduction, labor-exhaustion, clouds and rain, monastic discipline</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of the monk Zhanran at Lingyin Temple. After being confronted by an elder monk about his declining health and suspected supernatural encounter, Zhanran has confessed his secret relationship with the mysterious woman. We now see the community's plan to trap the entity.</meta>

# The Monk is No Longer Deluded

<margin>Artful words used to mask oneself so that no one suspects a monster; this is precisely why such a creature is considered a demon.</margin>

Where the beauty offered her charms, there was joy indeed, and his desires were fully satisfied. Why, then, was he so utterly deluded and clear-headed yet unable to force himself to stop? The next day, he informed the assembly of monks. For the sake of his well-being, the elder monk devised a plan, saying:

> "Tomorrow night, when she comes, you must treat her as you usually do. Secretly place an object upon her head. The rest of us will hide outside the room. When she departs, knock on the door as a signal. We will all follow closely behind her and must not stop until we have caught her. Only then can this curse be broken."

The elder monk <note>original: "大僧" (daseng), referring to the head or senior monk of the monastery.</note> gave his word. Two nights later, Zhanran felt his mind grow hazy and his spirit wander. As he lay alone leaning against his bed, the woman pushed open the door and entered once more. The monk engaged in intimacy with her, and her affections were even more tender and detailed than before. At the first crow of the cock, the woman took her leave. The monk secretly pinned a velvet flower <note>A traditional Chinese hair ornament made of silk floss.</note> into the woman’s hair. He then playfully knocked on the door three times. The assembled monks heard the signal and rose together to investigate. They saw only a woman drifting slowly away. They rang bells, recited incantations, grasped their monk's staffs <note>original: "錫" (xi), referring to the khakkara or 'ringing staff' carried by Buddhist monks.</note>, and took up weapons to pursue her. She fled directly to a small room behind the Abbot's quarters, where she vanished. This room was rumored to be the place where the Third-Generation Patriarch attained his transformation <note>The place where a high-ranking monk died or reached a state of permanent meditation.</note>; it was opened only once a year for offerings.

<column-break/>

<margin>Evil monsters that delude people are destined for destruction; that the young monk narrowly escaped should serve as a warning to all!</margin>

The room remained sealed. Knowing the woman had hidden her tracks there, the monks eagerly broke through the window and entered. They saw nothing at first, but behind the Buddhist cupboard in the northwest corner, there was a flickering, tiny light. They rushed to inspect it and found only a worn-out broom. The bamboo of the broom was smooth and moist, and its bundle of twigs was fresh and bright; it was clearly an object that had existed for several decades. While they were still doubting if this could be the culprit, they saw the velvet flower that the young monk had secretly pinned, now stuck to the broom's handle. Convinced now of the truth, they carried it to the front hall. They snapped off one of the broom's twigs <note>original: "箠" (chui), a switch or rod-like branch of the broom.</note>, and fluid dripped to the ground. The monks were even more shocked and strangely unnerved. They snapped another, and the same thing happened. They continued until every twig had been broken. The monks then held up a bright lamp to examine the fluid closely; the two dripping leaks were not water at all, but were actually vital essence <note>original: "精" (jing), the stolen life-force or "seed" of the monk, which the object had siphoned from him.</note>. Seeing this, Zhanran was filled with remorse and realization. Overcome by terror, he could not contain himself. Consequently, the object was beaten and burned in a fierce fire, and the ashes were scattered over the lake. The monk was treated with potent medicines, and after a long time, he finally achieved a full recovery.

<summary>This page concludes the tale of Monk Zhanran, revealing that his lover was actually an ancient broom that had gained supernatural powers to steal his life force. The story ends with the destruction of the object and the monk's recovery through repentance and medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Lingyin Temple, exorcism, spirit possession, vital essence, repentance, Ming Dynasty folklore, transformation</keywords>

# A Supernatural Intrusion upon a Scholar

In Liyang <note>A historical region in present-day Jiangsu province.</note>, there lived a Confucian scholar surnamed Ji, named Gang, with the courtesy name Tingsu. From his youth, he carried great ambitions, and as he grew, he became a devoted lover of learning. Because of this, he repaired a thatched hut to serve as his study. In front of it, he cleared a channel to lead in spring water, the current so clear one could see the bottom. Behind it, high peaks reached into the clouds, and the stone walls of the two flanking ridges glowed with five colors. Green forests and emerald bamboo were present in all four seasons. When the morning mist began to fade, the apes and birds sang in harmony; when the evening sun was about to set, the fish competed in leaping from the depths. 

Scholar Ji read there daily. Amidst his yellow scrolls and under the green lamp, he forgot his fatigue by day and night. One day, having read until midnight, he felt a slight chill. He draped a cloak over his shoulders and sat alone, suddenly missing an old friend from Suzhou <note>original: 吳門 (Wumen), an ancient name for the Suzhou area.</note>. Thereupon, he composed a regulated poem:

　To Suzhou’s mist and moon, we traveled once together,
　Maple leaves and reed-flowers, sharing a passenger boat.
　Gathering and parting have their time; the clouds drift north,
　Floating or sinking, there is no plan; the waters flow east.
　A single cup of wine is finished as the green mountains dim,
　A letter from a thousand miles arrives as green trees turn to autumn.
　Where is this mutual longing? We do not see each other,
　Only the palaces of the Capital <note>original: 鳳城 (Fengcheng, or "Phoenix City"), a poetic name for the imperial capital.</note> viewed from a river tower.

Before he had finished polishing his rhymes, there was suddenly a knock

<column-break/>

<margin>
The woman offers a poem, which is also a way to subtly narrate her own circumstances.
</margin>

at the door. He opened it to see a woman of graceful and lithe posture, her face as bright as wintry jade. She smiled and said to Gang: "I am a daughter of your neighbor. Having heard your high poetic rhymes, I have impulsively intruded. My intention is to ask for your instruction." 

Ji Gang, seeing her, was greatly delighted. He took her hand and they entered, sitting shoulder to shoulder. The woman said: "I wish to offer a poem." Ji Gang said: "Excellent." The woman recited:

　The frost is cold, the autumn high at White Emperor City <note>A legendary city in Chongqing, often associated in poetry with the "White Emperor" of the West and the autumn season.</note>,
　Within the women's quarters, strength is spent, and resentment hard to calm.
　In the west-wind courtyard, the *ding-dang* sounds ring out <note>These sounds refer to the "fulling" of cloth—pounding fabric on stones to soften it, a traditional task for women often associated with longing for absent husbands.</note>,
　From the towers tomorrow night, the intermittent echoes will rise.
　Pounding to pieces the heart for home, sorrow is about to knot,
　Startling the guest's pillow, a dream is hard to complete.
　It only seems that these sounds do not reach the ears of those at feasts,
　Vainly troubling the Jade Pass <note>Yumen Pass, a remote frontier post. The poem suggests the woman is like a wife longing for a soldier far away.</note> with infinite emotion.

Ji Gang recited the poem and praised it, and was about to make an amorous advance. The woman resisted him, saying:
"To be betrothed is to be a wife; to elope is to be a concubine—this is the maxim of the ancients. I am not made of wood or stone; do I not know that chastity is laudable and that illicit elopement is shameful? Why do you look upon me so lightly and attempt to offend me?" 

Ji Gang pleaded with her again and again. Only then did she suddenly change her expression and say: "The intent of <term>clouds and rain</term> <note>A classical euphemism for sexual intercourse, originating from the legend of the Goddess of Mount Wu.</note> is something common to all people..."

<summary>Scholar Ji Gang is visited by a mysterious, beautiful woman while studying late at night. After exchanging poetry, Ji Gang attempts to seduce her; she initially refuses on moral grounds but eventually begins to relent.</summary>
<keywords>Ji Gang, Liyang, poetry, supernatural encounter, courtship, morality, Suzhou, autumn imagery</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced Scholar Ji Gang and his encounter with a mysterious woman who visited his study late at night. The narrative continues with their conversation and the revelation of her supernatural nature.</meta>

“It is not that I do not wish to be submissive,” she said, “but it is easy to lose one’s life and difficult to preserve a good reputation. This is why I would rather offend your feelings than bring about my own ruin.” 

Thereupon, she retired to bed with Gang. The woman then chanted:

> You dwell at the mouth of Bamboo Ravine,
> My home is at Peach Blossom Ferry.
> Passing back and forth, we knew each other not;
> The green mountains must laugh at us so.

After their passions were spent, Gang said to her, “For three months, one should not deviate from benevolence <note>A reference to the *Analects*, where Confucius praises his disciple Yan Hui for not deviating from 'ren' (benevolence/virtue) for three months.</note>; today I have deviated.” 

The woman replied, “For three months, the Master did not know the taste of meat; today, I have known its flavor.” <note>Another reference to the *Analects*; Confucius was so entranced by the Shao music that he lost his sense of taste for three months. The woman is wittily using the Sage's words to justify their tryst.</note>

Gang then asked her which village she was from and what her family name was. The woman replied, “My surname is Shi, and my given name is Zhan.” 
<gloss>The word ‘Zhan’ (占) here is a play on the word for a laundry stone, ‘Zhan’ (砧).</gloss> 
“My family home sits facing south toward a tree; let that be your marker.” 
<gloss>A tree facing south (the direction 'Wu') forms the character for 'Pestle' (杵, chu). The woman is subtly revealing her true form.</gloss> 
“I am from the same village as you. If you truly do not reject me, you should visit tomorrow.” 

Gang asked, “Can you sing?” 
The woman replied, “Only enough to provide a rhyme.” 
Gang then took “Thinking of You” and “Since Parting” as themes and commanded her to compose a song. Without a moment's hesitation, the woman composed a song on the spot to answer him. The song went:

> Thinking of you since we parted,
> Twice have I seen the leaves fall.
> Yellow leaves stretch across a thousand miles,
> Each of us at a different corner of heaven.
> I wish to fly but regret having no wings;
> I wish to cross but the waves have no bridge...

<column-break/>

<margin>
A laundry stone and pestle are but stubborn, inanimate objects. Looking at this, even among the myriad things of plants and animals, some are exceedingly ignorant, yet they can still be used to illustrate such troubles as these.
</margin>

> In the past, we were firm as glue and lacquer,
> Why now are we like the stars Shen and Shang <term>Shen and Shang: The constellations Orion and Scorpius, which are positioned such that one sets as the other rises, never appearing together—a classic metaphor for separation.</term>?
> An official of high rank on the road to Chang’an,
> Like the man Ma Zihui;
> How can you not think of our intimate bond <gloss>friendship</gloss>?
> Vain fame has finally led us astray.
> How can the Southern Sieve be used to winnow?
> The Ox-herd star cannot pull a carriage. <note>References to the *Classic of Poetry* (Shijing), describing stars that bear the names of useful objects but are actually useless for those tasks.</note>
> Sorrow comes and cannot be stopped;
> Clutching my breast, I pace alone in hesitation.
> Truly, if one lacks a heart of gold and stone,
> I shall be forever forgotten by you.

Soon after, the rooster crowed three times, the <gloss>sound</gloss> of the strikes became frequent, and the fifth watch was struck with urgent rhythm. Hearing this, the woman hurried to rise and put on her clothes. She said to Gang, “Please take care of yourself, my lord. I shall return tomorrow <gloss>the word 'return' is in the level tone</gloss> without waiting for an invitation.” 

Gang was determined to keep her. “Since you are already hidden here, why must you go?” he asked. 

The woman said angrily, “I have parents at home. If this affair is exposed and ruined, where will the blame fall? Not only would it stain my reputation, but it would also be disadvantageous to you.” 

Gang would not listen. As the woman tried to run away with all her might, Gang wrapped her in a quilt and held her tight. After a long time, she stopped moving. When he opened the bundle to look, there was only a **laundry stone and a pestle**.

<summary>Scholar Ji Gang engages in a poetic and romantic encounter with a mysterious woman named Shi Zhan, who eventually reveals herself through wordplay and a sudden transformation to be the personification of a laundry stone and pestle.</summary>
<keywords>Ji Gang, Shi Zhanniang, laundry stone, pestle, transformation, Analects, supernatural encounter, Ming dynasty fiction</keywords>

## Solitude

<image-desc>A woodblock print illustration of a scholar in a long robe and high cap standing in a landscape. He is looking downwards in a contemplative pose. Above him, a crescent moon and the seven stars of the Big Dipper constellation are depicted, connected by faint lines. ->The road ahead is long and winding; I travel it solely to seek out a dear old friend in the Peach Garden.<-
<note>Peach Garden (Taoyuan) is a classic literary allusion to a hidden paradise or a place where one finds a reclusive, often supernatural, beauty.</note>

<note>
**Key Terms from this passage:**
*   **Village House** (村舍, *cunshe*): A humble rural dwelling.
*   **Solitude** (寂寥, *jiliao*): A sense of lonely, quiet emptiness.
*   **Heart's desire to return** (歸心, *guixin*): Often used to describe the longing for home or a place of belonging.
*   **Strange spirit** (竒妖, *qiyao*): A mysterious or supernatural entity; in this context, it refers to the woman who is actually the spirit of a laundry stone.
*   **Peach Garden** (桃园, *taoyuan*): A reference to a secluded, idyllic place.
*   **Old friend** (故友, *guyou*): A term of endearment, though here he uses it to refer to the woman he has only just met.
</note>

# The Lady Tang and the Two Daughters-in-Law

<margin>The three demons intend to seduce the man; therefore, the old woman appears first, followed by the two wives. This marks the beginning of the encounter.</margin>
The first poem says:
*In Guangzhou as the sun sets, few travelers are seen,*
*The landscape is hazy and grey, stirring a traveler’s heart.*
*The village feels as quiet as the Cold Food Festival,*
*Countless wild flowers grow atop the crumbling walls.*
<note>The Cold Food Festival (Hanshi) was a traditional holiday where no fires were lit, creating a somber, quiet atmosphere often associated with mourning or solitude.</note>

Another poem says:
*Beneath the bamboo, a cold spring flows of its own accord,*
*Piercing the clouds and spraying snow, it echoes against the rocks.*
*The sound washes my bones all night, leaving me sleepless,*
*How much more so with the autumn moon shining through the west window?*

Xifu, having no other choice, went to knock on the door of the house. After a long while, an old woman emerged holding a candle and asked, "From where does the honored gentleman come?" Xifu replied, "I am a student of the Guangzhou academy. Because it is late, I seek a place to stay for the night." The old woman consented. Xifu followed her inside to the rear hall, where they sat as guest and host. She prepared wine and snacks for them to drink together. Xifu asked for her surname. The woman said, "This humble person is named Tang." Xifu asked about her sons, and she replied, "Both of my sons died young." Xifu then asked how she supported herself, and she said, "My two daughters-in-law—one is skilled at the loom, and the other is talented at composing and measures <gloss>the word "measures" here is read in the departing tone</gloss>—so we manage to live well enough." The woman then called the two daughters-in-law to come out and meet him. They were dressed in light makeup and plain white clothing, yet they possessed national beauty. She commanded them to sit on either side, serving wine and ordering them to compose poetry. The old woman—

<column-break/>

<margin>This pair of responses is not only clever in its "word-splitting," but its moral sense is also complete and elegant, illustrating the subject's quick wit.</margin>
# The Lady Tang and the Two Daughters-in-Law

In Guangzhou, there was a student named Du, given name Xifu. He was born with extraordinary intelligence, and his father loved him dearly. When Xifu grew up, his father hired a tutor to teach him the classics. His father once sat drinking with the tutor, whose surname was Wu. The father playfully offered a couplet: "Mr. Wu [吳] drinks wine; as soon as it reaches the mouth [口], he swallows [吞]." <gloss>This is "splitting characters" to find a hidden meaning.</gloss>
<note>original: "吳先生飲酒到口便吞". This is a visual pun. The character for "swallow" (吞) is composed of "mouth" (口) over "heaven" (天), while the teacher's name Wu (吳) is composed of "mouth" (口) over a similar base. The joke implies the teacher is "consuming" his own name.</note>

He ordered Xifu to provide a matching line. Xifu immediately responded: "Officer [大夫] Xie [謝] wants money; he pulls his body [身] away [去] to ask for it."
<note>original: "謝大夫要錢抽身去討". This mirrors the father's pun. The character for "Xie" (謝) contains the components for "body" (身) and "go/away" (去). Xifu uses another surname to create a perfect intellectual counterpoint.</note>

His father was greatly amazed by this. Later, during the Spring Flower Festival, Xifu went outside the city walls with his father for a stroll. They saw a sign on a commoner's house that read: "This House For Sale." His father told him to compose a literary commentary on those four words. Xifu broke the phrase down, saying: "Leaving the peaceful dwelling and not living in it; seeking a good price and selling it."
<note>Xifu is quoting from the *Mencius* and the *Analects* to elevate a common real estate sign into a philosophical statement about virtue and value.</note>

His father was even more astonished. At the age of eighteen, Xifu was admitted as a student of the prefecture academy. He was repeatedly held in high regard by the Education Commissioner and various eminent officials; furthermore, his conduct was upright and never careless. One day, returning from a long journey, he reached a village settlement at dusk. It was already dark, and the lamps were too dim to see by. He planned to find a place to sleep and looked around at the desolate surroundings. Feeling a sense of melancholy, he began to chant a poem to himself—

<summary>This page introduces Du Xifu, a child prodigy and disciplined scholar, who becomes lost at night and finds lodging in the home of a widow, Mother Tang. The narrative emphasizes Xifu's wit through linguistic games before transitioning into a classic "encounter with beauties" trope in a remote setting.</summary>

<keywords>Du Xifu, Mother Tang, character splitting, wordplay, Guangzhou academy, supernatural encounter, widows, hospitality</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Du Xifu, who has taken shelter in a mysterious house inhabited by an old woman named Tang and her two daughters-in-law. This section reveals the supernatural nature of the characters as personifications of household objects.</meta>

<margin>
The two women strongly desired to seduce Du, and though they forced themselves upon him, he refused. Ultimately, they could not violate him. Thus we know that when the heart is righteous, the harmony of spirits cannot be easily broken.
</margin>

She brought a large cup of wine to Xifu and said, “This old woman’s family is poor, and our daily needs are not met. These two daughters-in-law of mine are quite beautiful. If you do not find them unworthy, please choose one to serve you as a <term>green tunic and yellow skirt</term><note>The phrase "green tunic and yellow skirt" (lǜ yī huáng sháng) is a classical allusion from the *Book of Songs* referring to a concubine or a secondary wife, as yellow was a primary color and green a secondary one.</note>. This would be my great fortune.” 

Xifu was a man of upright character. He firmly declined, saying, “I am grateful for your kind intentions and your desire to form this bond. However, I have a humble wife at home, and it would be against my principles to take another woman so casually. Therefore, I cannot obey your command.” 

Old Lady Tang called out to the two women and urged them, saying, “Do not let this young gentleman escape!” 

The two women grabbed Xifu, attempting to force a sexual encounter. Xifu’s expression turned stern as he resisted them, and he composed a poem that said:

> One’s inner heart should not be clouded,
> For who dares say the gods do not exist?
> Holding a candle, I watch until the dawn,
> To see what kind of man I truly am.

They continued to talk back and forth until, without them noticing, the sky began to brighten. In a trance-like state, the surroundings suddenly vanished. All that remained was a single rafter of a ruined house, a <term>Tang-po</term><note>A "Tang-po" (literally "Tang’s Wife") is a traditional Chinese bed-warmer—a ceramic or metal vessel filled with hot water to warm the feet.</note>, and a one-foot ruler.

<column-break/>

<margin>
Old Lady Tang and the two women each chanted a poem, each praising their own virtues.
</margin>

The old woman first sang:

> My whole life I have possessed a warm heart,
> And so officials have titled me by the name of Tang.
> I provide warmth deep in the night without needing a fire,
> And in the depths of winter, I drive away the cold to return the spring.
> Though <term>Madam Xu</term><note>A reference to "The Half-Applied Makeup of Madam Xu," a historical figure who remained sexually attractive in her old age.</note> has grown old, her affections remain deep;
> Her clothes must be warmed <gloss>When Fan Shu came himself, the gift of the silk robe was not forgotten</gloss> by the morning soup.
> I tell the old man of the mountain to cherish me dearly,
> And do not let me experience the change from heat to cold in the days to come.

One woman sang:

> My delicate hands are slender and beautiful,
> By the bamboo window, I hear a woman’s toil.
> The sound of the golden shuttle never ceases as it goes to and fro,
> One frequently hears the creaking and groaning of the jade loom-axle.
> When <term>Mencius’s Mother</term><note>A famous story where Mencius’s mother cut her weaving on the loom to teach her son that stopping his studies was like cutting a functional cloth.</note> cut the cloth, it was to teach her son;
> Where <term>Gongyi</term><note>Gongyi Xiu, a minister of Lu, burned his own silk and pulled up his vegetables to ensure he didn't compete with the livelihood of common weavers and farmers.</note> burned the weaving, he left a lasting name.
> To produce three thousand bolts of five-colored cloud-brocade,
> Consider how much effort must be spent to weave it to completion.

The other woman sang:

> I was first crafted during the time of the Yellow Emperor,
> When <term>Ling Lun</term><note>The legendary founder of music in ancient China, who also established standard measurements.</note> cut the pitch-pipes to determine the proper scales.
> Once established, inches and tenths should be the standard,
> Setting fixed rules without the deviation of a hair’s breadth.
> Whether comparing or measuring, there are always rules for fairness <gloss>The word "measure" (du) is in the entering tone</gloss>,
> When weighing length and shortness, there is no room for doubt.
> In all the world from ancient times to now, who can be compared to me?
> I am held constantly in the hands of clever wives and skilled craftsmen.

When the singing was finished, Old Lady Tang—

<header>Songs of the Southward Journey</header>

<column-break/>

<margin>A traveler’s reflections are naturally melancholy. Sitting in idleness only intensifies these feelings. It is only natural that he could not sleep and instead gave form to his emotions through poetry.</margin>

# The Three Brothers of Jianye

Scholar Mao, whose given name was Youzhi, was a man of Wu <note>The region around modern-day Suzhou</note>. He was accomplished in Confucian learning and well-versed in the rules of poetry; thus, many among the literati held him in high regard.

At the beginning of the Hongwu era <note>The reign of the first Ming Emperor, 1368–1398</note>, he rowed a small boat to Jianye <note>Modern-day Nanjing</note>. One night, he anchored beneath the Stone City <note>A famous ancient fortress in Nanjing</note>. His thoughts as a traveler were desolate and his heart was troubled. Pushing open the boat's awning to gaze at the moon, he felt even more restless. It happened to be the season of the Qingming Festival <note>The Tomb-Sweeping Festival</note>, and from the surrounding fields came the mournful sounds of many people wailing for the dead. Consequently, he chanted a poem to himself:

*The seasonal cycles urge the traveler onward,*
*The scenery of the South is truly pitiable.*
*At the lakeside pavilion, the water has newly risen,*
*In the homes of the swallows, the cooking fires are forbidden once more.*

->The Cold Food Festival<-

*A few petals fall after the Cold Food Festival,*
*Several tall willows stand before the setting sun.*
*In the morning, I wipe my spring robes of fragrant silk, white as snow,*
*A handsome horse neighs playfully by a saddle of white jade.*
*Green waters and verdant mountains seem bright as day,*
*Warm clouds and fragrant grass are best for sleeping.*
*A guest with pine-mash wine invites the village society,*
*But at whose family’s grave are the offerings of wheat-rice sprinkled?*
*I am ashamed to be like the master of the Yangzi cottage by the roadside,*
*For ten years, I have kept my doors closed while the ghosts howl.* <gloss>After Cen Fu finished</gloss> <note>A reference to a literary figure or an allusion to the poet Cen Shen.</note>

<column-break/>

<margin>A great gale arose and the wind blew wildly. The river water boiled and surged, waves piling up like mountains. One could see nothing, and the river made a fierce, roaring sound. Youzhi was astonished. He crouched by the fire to comfort himself and peered out from the boat window.</margin>

Then he saw three objects leaping and frolicking in the water. One had a pointed head and a large belly, and its body was very long. One had many eyes and a square body, and its form was very black. The last one was thin and long. Soon, they suddenly spoke in human tongues, saying: “We have roamed the rivers and lakes for many years. Catching fish and breaking through the waves—what have we not been able to do? Alas, now we are all cast aside as useless.”

One object said: “We at least remain unharmed. We should celebrate the fact that we have avoided ending up in the bellies of fish!”

Another object said: “Tonight the moon is bright and the wind is clear; it is a truly fine evening. Why don’t we each recite a poem to enjoy our journey?”

Thereupon, the object with the pointed head and large belly recited first:

*A small boat <gloss>Zemeng refers to a small boat</gloss> drifts like a single leaf,*
*Traveling between the ends of Chu and the head of Wu.*
*Breaking through the misty waves at the ferry crossing at dawn, the oars...*

<margin>The "pointed head and large belly" is the boat; the "many eyes and square body" is the net; the "thin and long" one is the fishing rod. These three poems are actually factual descriptions of their own forms.</margin>

<summary>During a restless night at the Stone City in Nanjing, the scholar Mao Youzhi witnesses three personified fishing tools—a boat, a net, and a rod—discussing their lives and reciting poetry during a storm.</summary>
<keywords>Mao Youzhi, Hongwu era, Nanjing, Stone City, Cold Food Festival, personification, Chinese poetry, folklore</keywords>

<header>Selection of Ten Thousand Pure Talks, Volume 3</header>

<margin>
The Second Path of Poetry:
The Carp
</margin>

Breaking the reflection of the autumn moon in the heart of the river, drying the net securely on the willow-lined bank; the sound of the rowing-thump <note>A sound made by fishermen hitting the side of the boat to drive fish into nets</note> echoes far across the banks of reed flowers. The old fisherman relies on this for his livelihood and would not trade his place for the title of a Marquis of Ten Thousand Households <note>A high-ranking noble title in ancient China</note>.

<gloss>An object with many eyes and a square body [A fishing net]</gloss>
The object chanted:

> Thousands of knots and hundreds of ties, densely entwined,
> Forever tending to the labor of the fishing family.
> With eyes <note>Referring to the mesh of the net</note> spread wide across the river estuary, 
> I escape the constraints of the weave as the autumn moves through sea and sky. 
> Where the fishy scent rises, I am gathered in; 
> Beyond the mist, in the dappled sunlight, I am dried on the shore. 
> How many small fish and shrimp suffer from this cruel design? 
> I know not who first devised such a clever scheme.

<gloss>An object with a thin and long body [A fishing rod]</gloss>
The object chanted:

> A single silk thread hangs long from the green jade <note>A poetic term for a bamboo rod</note>, 
> Often found between the Wei River and the Dongting Lake <note>Famous locations associated with legendary fishermen like Jiang Ziya</note>. 
> In the shade of the willows, startled by jumping fish; 
> Among the fragrant smartweed flowers, accompanying the idle gulls. 
> Not yet has a royal messenger come to recruit me for service, 
> Instead, I am held fast in the grip of an old fisherman. 
> Long occupying the misty waves and the bright moon, 
> This body should surely transcend the dusty world of men.

The entities laughed loudly. Zhao Youzhi’s boat nearly capsized, and the boatman was filled with great terror, fearing a disaster beyond measure. Youzhi assumed a stern expression and rebuked them, saying: "I am originally a Confucian scholar from the region of Wu. I have no quarrel with your kind. Why do you display such power to treat me as an enemy?"

<column-break/>

The three objects <note>The "Three Brothers" likely refers to the fishing net, the rod, and perhaps the boat or the "Carp" mentioned in the margin</note> laughed and said: "We were merely playing with you, sir; we did not truly intend to harm you." Then, they all vanished together into the water. The next day, Youzhi related the incident to the local elders. They all said: "Last year, a fisherman’s boat capsized at this very spot. Those beings you saw must have been the spirits of his fishing gear."
