<meta>This is the opening page of the manuscript "Rare Blooms of Suiyang," establishing the setting in the Ming Dynasty and introducing the protagonist Yu Changzuo and his miraculous garden.</meta>

# Rare Blooms of Suiyang

<margin>Opening a garden, building pavilions, and planting all sorts of flowers and trees for leisure; Master Yu is indeed a man of grand style!</margin>
During the Tianshun era <note>1457–1464 AD</note>, there lived a man in Suiyang named Yu Changzuo. He belonged to a family of great wealth, with lands stretching across the countryside and a lifestyle rivaling that of princes and dukes. Blessed by the spiritual grace of the Sui River, he was the wealthiest citizen in the prefecture. He once chose a site where mountains and waters embraced one another to build a vast garden, approximately six or seven *li* in circumference. Within the garden, pavilions and kiosks stood like a forest, and every variety of fine tree and rare, exotic flower was planted there. All thrived with vibrant beauty. In his leisure, Yu Changzuo would wander through it, sometimes bringing friends or inviting companions to share cups of wine and enjoy the scenery. When the weather was clear, a refreshing breeze would blow, and the harmonious songs of fine birds could be heard; when it rained, he watched the golden carp leap in the beautiful ponds and listened to the mountain springs splashing against jade-like stones. It was truly a magnificent garden. He employed a caretaker to guard it, known as the "Garden-man." This Garden-man, while keeping watch over the locked gates, often saw a group of women playing within. Their beauty was striking and seductive, and they danced with a light, graceful air. However, whenever they encountered a person, they would scatter and vanish without a trace, leaving no clue as to where they had come from. The Garden-man reported all of this to Changzuo, but Changzuo did not believe him.

<column-break/>

<margin>One name is "Loyal Heart," the second is "Image of a Loyal Minister"; these terms praise its character as truly exceptional.</margin>
Nevertheless, he went to observe for himself. It was the hour when the sun was setting, and the lingering clouds were tinged with fresh color. Suddenly, he saw a group of women, each dressed in silks and embroidery, approaching from the outside. Their appearances were even more beautiful than described, and they laughed and chatted playfully. One woman said, "On such a fine day and amidst such beautiful scenery, when all things are competing in their splendor, why should we use the Jie Drum to urge the flowers?" <gloss>A reference to an anecdote about the Tang Emperor Xuanzong, who supposedly beat a drum to make flowers bloom in winter.</gloss> The other women laughed and said, "Living in today's world, why discuss the ways of the ancients? Let us each compose a poem to express our thoughts, so as not to waste this beautiful time." The others all agreed. One woman then stepped forward and was the first to recite:

> Year after year, the buds open toward the summer heat,
> The love bestowed upon them is truly extraordinary.
> A few green leaves hold the summer rain,
> While a single "Loyal Heart" <note>original: 丹心 (dan xin), referring to the red center of a flower or a heart of loyalty</note> faces the morning sun.
> Its fragrant posture compares to the "Image of a Loyal Minister,"
> Its elegant quality is just like the "Garb of a Daoist Immortal." <note>original: 羽士 (yu shi), literally "feathered scholar," a term for a Daoist priest</note>
> Seeing its lofty purity surpass all other plants,
> Why should one need to toast it with cups of wine? <gloss>A self-composed poem on the Sunflower</gloss>

Another woman recited:

> Red thorns and green stems, fashioned in a clever style,
> Its fragrance lingers from spring through the height of summer.
> Beautiful as woven gold, it hangs from the high trellis,
> Casting a dense, cool shade to cover the small hall.
> As deep red as an orangutan, the blossoms lean upon the dew...

<summary>Wealthy landowner Yu Changzuo builds a magnificent garden in Suiyang, only to discover it is frequented by mysterious, beautiful women who compose poetry about the flowers they embody.</summary>
<keywords>Yu Changzuo, Suiyang, Ming Dynasty, flower spirits, Chinese gardens, poetry, Sunflower, Tianshun era</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the description of Yu Changzuo’s magnificent garden. While the previous page introduced the "Garden-man" witnessing mysterious women, this page captures the specific moment Yu Changzuo encounters these spirits—who reveal themselves through poetry—and the supernatural aftermath of their discovery.</meta>

# The Spirits of the Garden

<margin>Referring to the hometown of Zhu Maichen <note>A famous Han dynasty official who rose from poverty</note>; his surname was Zhu, and this poem describes his character.</margin>

Lighter than swallows, soaring with the wind. Many times in the bright day, their beauty is seen. One might suspect it is Maichen returning to his hometown.

<margin>She identifies herself as the Multiflora Rose.</margin>

One woman recited: "I bloom only after the God of Spring has departed; a rare variety mostly planted in wealthy homes. The sun bakes me into a fiery red, like a spraying fire; the warm breeze scatters my crimson, like burning clouds. Radiant and grand, I am no different from the vivid red of 'chimpanzee blood' <note>a traditional Chinese poetic term for a brilliant, deep red</note>; brilliant and pure, like the cinnabar atop a crane’s head. I heard I was born long ago in the land of Parthia <note>Anshiguo, an ancient kingdom in modern-day Iran</note>, and was brought to the Middle Kingdom by the explorer Zhang Qian."

<margin>She identifies herself as the Pomegranate.</margin>

One woman recited: "I do not waste the labor of spring on pruning; this rare flower chooses instead to bloom in the summer heat. My secluded fragrance belongs not to the mortal world; this exotic species originally came from the Land of the Buddha. Emerald leaves shimmer, holding the rain and dew; my icy petals are crystalline and pure, far from the dust of the world. An old monk facing this is as clear as water; at times, fragrance rises from the Altar of Wisdom <note>the 'Prajna Terrace,' a Buddhist reference to spiritual insight</note>."

<margin>She identifies herself as the Jasmine.</margin>

One woman recited: "A spiritual sprout growing in the Emperor’s domain; the colors of the sunset in the blue sky brighten my literary grace. I do not follow the spring colors into the Gold Valley Garden <note>the famous luxury garden of the wealthy official Shi Chong</note>; I am often bathed in Heaven’s grace near the Jade Hall. When the sun shines on the palace

<column-break/>

courtyard, I am bright and elegant. The wind carries my scent through the government offices, scattering a clear fragrance. Sitting alone at dusk with no companion, I chant poems toward the lush blooms, my spirits rising high."

<margin>She identifies herself as the Crape Myrtle.</margin>

One woman recited: "With a soul of snow and a form of ice, I am untainted by the vulgar; who was it that transplanted me into the shade of a small window? Were it not for the Sister of the Moon’s golden bracelet, it would be hard to buy the Weaver Girl’s white jade hairpin. The lovely colors of dawn reach the desk and mat; the autumn fragrance slowly permeates one's robes. A recluse finds joy here among the forest and springs, occasionally catching the clear scent to moisten a poem."

<margin>She identifies herself as the Jade Hairpin Flower <note>also known as the Hosta</note>.</margin>

<margin>From the moment the master rebuked them, the various flowers immediately died. This is proof that 'evil cannot overcome the upright.'</margin>

Changzuo listened closely to them. Knowing there were no such women in the garden, he realized they must be supernatural creatures. He stepped forward quickly and let out a great shout. The women instantly dispersed, and no one knew where they went. Returning to the garden several days later, he saw that the Sunflower, Multiflora Rose, Pomegranate, Jasmine, Crape Myrtle, and Jade Hairpin flowers had all died. Changzuo found this strange. Reflecting on the meaning of their poems, he finally realized that those women were the spirits of those very flowers.

***

<summary>Yu Changzuo discovers that the mysterious women in his garden are actually the spirits of his exotic flowers, who vanish and wither after he confronts them. The text uses their sophisticated poetry to reveal their identities and origins, framed by a moralizing commentary on the triumph of the upright spirit over the supernatural.</summary>

<keywords>flower spirits, Yu Changzuo, Chinese poetry, Rose, Pomegranate, Jasmine, Crape Myrtle, Hosta, supernatural encounter, Ming Dynasty literature</keywords>

## The Plum Spirit
<note>original: "梅妖" (Mei Yao)</note>

->In the Shi manor of the capital, Chang'an, the phantom soul is often swept away by the violent wind.<-

Her robes are elaborate, with long ribbons that suggest celestial movement. Above her, a crescent moon and the seven stars of the Big Dipper <note>known in Chinese as the "Northern Dipper," often associated with the transition between the mortal and spirit realms</note> hang in the night sky. She appears in a quiet courtyard, surrounded by the geometric patterns of tiled floors and decorative paneled screens.

## Startled and Scattered
<note>original: "驚散" (Jing San)</note>

->By the official river of Suide, the plum's shadow turns through the night following the bright moon.<-

<meta>This page continues the narrative involving supernatural encounters and historical figures. While the previous page depicted the appearance of a Plum Spirit in a dream-like or ghostly setting, this page provides the "historical" framing of the encounter, involving the Ming dynasty general Shi Heng and a woman named Mei Fanghua who embodies the spirit of the plum blossom.</meta>

# The Plum Blossom of Suide
<note>original: "綏德梅華" (Suide Meihua). The OCR reads "緩" (Huan), but historical context and the text itself indicate "綏德" (Suide), a garrison town in Shaanxi.</note>

<gloss>Reciting two poems with a grand, heroic spirit; it seems that General Shi was also a master of poetic meter.</gloss>

At the beginning of the Jingtai era <note>1450–1457 CE</note>, the Regional Commander Shi <note>whose taboo name was Heng</note> was returning in triumph from a western military expedition. It was the day of his victorious return, and his boat was moored at the official river of Suide. At that time, the troops were resting, the armor was rolled up, and the banners were furled. During this quiet halt, he did not realize the sun was setting in the west and the sky was growing dark. Shi sat alone in his boat with no one to converse with. He began to beat time on the side of the vessel and loudly recited two poems in the "eight-line" style.

The first poem said:
"The Great Ming is unified in a time of lasting peace;
How could the people within the four seas not flourish?
The celestial horses <note>referring to the famous 'Heavenly Horses' of the Western Regions</note> nibble flowers among the alfalfa;
The rustic villagers offer wine made from ripe grapes.
The rain from the 'Ninefold Heaven' <note>the Emperor's grace</note> passes, moistening the rivers and mountains;
The clouds for ten thousand miles recede, and the sun and moon shine high.
I bow my head, fearing to fall behind in the great military ranks;
As the General of the West rolls up his battle robes."

The second poem, "Reflecting on the Past," said:
"In the north, it swallows the Ba and Han regions; in the south, it controls the Xiao and Xiang;
The anger is like mountain ranges reflected in the mirror-like water.
I already recognize the integrity of the youth Zhong Jun;
Playing at <gloss>The Story of the Wei Emperor</gloss> <note>referring to Cao Cao</note>, one should know that 'throwing down the pen' <gloss>The Story of Fu Jian</gloss> <note>actually referring to the idiom of Ban Chao leaving civilian life for the military; the gloss mentions Fu Jian, who famously lost a battle despite his massive army</note> is even more absurd.
Through a thousand autumns, the fishing boat sings to the bright moon;
For ten thousand miles, the sand-gulls play in the evening light."

<column-break/>

<gloss>This explains that her original betrothal was the reason she refused to marry another; the writer intends to move us with this logic, and indeed, such reasoning is not false.</gloss>

"The poplar flowers dance in the clear dust, yet how lonely it is;
The good wind belongs only to the merchants coming and going."

Just as he finished his recitation and was feeling self-satisfied, he suddenly saw a woman approaching, weeping piteously. Three times she cried out, "Save me!" Shi immediately ordered his soldiers to rescue her. Looking at her appearance, she was exceptionally beautiful and refined, far beyond the ordinary. When asked for the reason for her distress, the woman wept and said:

"My humble surname is Mei, and my given name is Fanghua <note>meaning 'Fragrant Flower'</note>. I was originally betrothed to the Yin family of the same village. After a year, their family fell into ruin. My parents, loathing their poverty, pressured me to break my word and marry another. I would not follow their command. My parents were enraged and beat me with a cart-whip, to the point where I could no longer survive. For this reason, I threw myself into the water. Fortunately, I have met with your lordship's compassion and been saved. This is truly a case of bringing the dead back to life and putting flesh back on my bones."

Shi questioned her, saying, "Do you wish to return home to your parents, or will you become my concubine?"

The woman said, "Returning home is not what I desire. I wish only to hold the 'basket and broom' <note>a humble way of saying 'to serve as a wife or concubine'</note> for your lordship."

Shi was greatly pleased. He had her change into new clothes, placed her in the rear carriage, and returned home. After arriving at the Shi mansion, she served the First Wife with great respect, treated the other concubines with utmost sincerity, managed the servants with kindness, and welcomed guests with proper etiquette.

<summary>General Shi Heng, a prominent Ming military leader, rescues a beautiful woman named Mei Fanghua from a suicide attempt during his return from a western campaign. She becomes his virtuous concubine, though her name and ethereal nature suggest she is the spirit of the plum blossom.</summary>

<keywords>Shi Heng, Suide, Mei Fanghua, Ming Dynasty, poetry, filial piety, betrothal, concubinage, supernatural encounter</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of General Shi Heng and his mysterious companion, Mei Fanghua. While the previous page established their meeting and her role in his household, this page describes her refusal to meet the righteous statesman Yu Qian, revealing her supernatural nature as a plum blossom spirit and her eventual departure from the mortal world.</meta>

<margin>
Fanghua was merely a plum blossom, transformed into a strange and marvelous form. This record of a wonder among the "Hundred Valleys" <note>likely a reference to a collection of marvelous tales</note> was written based on the whim of a moment to be left to future generations.
</margin>

Lord Shi entrusted all matters of the household—from the grandest banquets to the daily meals—entirely to her. She performed these duties without fail, meeting every expectation with perfect timing and grace. Shi cherished and doted on her immensely. Both within and outside the household, people competed to praise her ability and her virtue, and everyone longed to catch a glimpse of her beauty.

Whenever princes, dukes, or high-ranking officials visited the residence, Shi would command her to come out and greet them. Fanghua would comply obediently, which led to even greater praise from the guests and even more favor from Shi. In the winter of that year, the Minister of War and Junior Guardian of the Heir Apparent, Lord Yu <gloss>whose taboo name was Qian</gloss> <note>Yu Qian (1398–1457) was a famously upright Ming dynasty statesman known for his integrity and for saving the capital during a Mongol invasion.</note>, happened to visit Shi’s residence. Shi, wishing to show her off, prepared a grand feast for Lord Yu and ordered Fanghua to dress herself elaborately and come out. 

Upon hearing the command, Fanghua acted quite differently than she had in the past. She knit her brows with a look of great difficulty. Although Shi insisted, she would not obey. He ordered the servant girls to hurry her; messengers hurried back and forth along the paths, yet Fanghua refused to appear. Lord Yu eventually took his leave and returned home. Shi, feeling humiliated and dejected, went to call her himself, but she still refused to come out. Left with no choice but to see Lord Yu off, Shi returned in a great rage and scolded her, saying: "Of all the noble guests who frequent my estate, you have seen many."

<column-break/>

<margin>
"Evil cannot overcome the upright, and the false cannot disrupt the true." These are truly ultimate words. One should not dismiss the truth of the statement just because the speaker is a spirit.
</margin>

"Why is it that when it comes to Lord Yu, you alone refuse to see him?" Fanghua did nothing but weep, offering not a single word in response. Shi, being a man of military temperament, grew furious. He drew his sword to strike her. Fanghua suddenly fled into the wall, and from within it, she spoke: "I have heard that <term>evil cannot overcome the upright</term> and the false cannot disrupt the true. I am not of the mortal world, but the spirit of a plum blossom. Having accidentally stolen the essence of the sun and moon, I took human form within this great universe. Now, Lord Yu is a pillar of the state and a 'vessel for the altars of the land' <note>a metaphor for an indispensable statesman</note>. He is an upright gentleman whom both gods and men revere. How could I dare to see him? Have you not heard the story of the concubine who would not see Di, the Duke of Liang? <gloss>This refers to a story involving Wu Sansi's household.</gloss>" 

She then said, "I must now bid you an eternal farewell," and chanted a long poem:

> The old gnarled branches lean beside the water’s edge,
> Year after year, the first to lead the hundred flowers' bloom.
> Amidst ice and frost, they sense the warmth of early spring;
> With the color of snow, they brave the cold as the valley opens.
> Their sparse shadows turn with the bright moon at night;
> Their hidden fragrance follows the pleasant breeze as it arrives.
> When the fruit forms at the top, it shall return to the palace halls;
> Only then will it be known that there is <term>great talent to season the imperial broth</term> <note>original: 和羹 (hegeng). This is a classical metaphor for a talented prime minister or advisor who perfectly "seasons" or balances the government.</note>.

The woman was never seen again after this.

<summary>This page concludes the tale of General Shi Heng's concubine, revealing her to be a plum blossom spirit who vanishes because she cannot face the overwhelming moral purity of the statesman Yu Qian. The story uses supernatural elements to highlight the historical reputation of Yu Qian as an exceptionally upright official.</summary>

<keywords>Shi Heng, Yu Qian, plum blossom spirit, Ming Dynasty, folk supernatural, morality, Di Renjie, metaphors of statecraft</keywords>

The five women are spirits. Each describes their own woody flower <note>This likely refers to the "spirit" or essence of specific flowering plants</note>.

Then again, there was more sighing and sobbing.
<gloss>Having finished the poem, they moved the boat again to the east of the pond. The two scholars—</gloss>
—shared a cup of wine. Just as they ordered the servant to pour more of the brew, they suddenly looked up and saw a tower several dozen feet high. It reached into the clouds and mists, with windows on all four sides. Inside sat five women of extraordinary beauty. They leaned against the windows, relaxing and playing; their laughter and chatter could be heard even in the highest heavens. One woman took the lead and said, "On such a moonlit night, with the air so clear and refreshing, how could we not have a poem for our own enjoyment?" The others replied, "Indeed." Thereupon, one woman recited:
"Emerald sleeves and a yellow crown, playing the role of a god,
Before the peach and after the plum, I alone welcome spring.
In the Crystal Palace, a guest visiting the Primordial,
Fragrant and intoxicated in the mountains, a person who has found the Way.
Silken socks move lightly, stepping on the moonlight,
Icy skin and plain appearance, distant from the dust of the world.
When shall I be moved to the Purple Hall <note>original: 紫宸殿 — The Inner Palace of the Emperor</note>,
To be a close neighbor to the official plum blossoms?"
<note>original: 仙自詠 — The immortal sings of herself. This poem describes the Narcissus, known in Chinese as the 'Water Goddess'</note>
Another woman recited: "Stately and quiet, rooted in the realm of water and clouds, untainted by the silt, I am distinct from all other fragrant flowers. The rosy clouds reflect in the water as I tilt my head; facing the wind, the cloud-patterned brocade envies my palace makeup. My delicate and beautiful nature..."

The two scholars, He and Mao <note>The main text identifies the first scholar as Ji, but the margin notes him as 'He'</note>. These people said such and such. A small moon [is carried] on this vessel.

### Appreciation on a Boat at Wei Pond

Ji Shiheng, from Huating, was a man of letters. He was close friends with Mao Guinian, a man from the same town, and both were equally renowned for their literary talent. When these two scholars sat, they were knee-to-knee and sleeve-to-sleeve, searching for the right phrases and composing lines together. When they traveled, they went side-by-side in the same carriage, admiring the scenery and expressing their feelings in writing. Their neighbors compared them to the famous friendships of Chen and Lei, or Guan and Bao.
<gloss>Referring to Chen Zhong and Lei Yi, and Guan Zhong and Bao Shuyu—legendary pairs of devoted friends in Chinese history.</gloss>
One day, the two scholars traveled together to Wei Pond on business. It was a summer night, and the sky was sweltering and humid. However, a slight breeze brought a sense of clarity. Looking up, the moon was bright and the stars were brilliant; looking down, the lotuses rested upon the water, their fragrance rich and overflowing. How could the two scholars not feel a sense of appreciation in their hearts? They immediately set out wine on the boat to enjoy the lotuses and together composed a poem in the "palindromic" style.
<note>term: 回文詩 (Huiwen) — A 'circular' or palindromic poem that can be read forwards and backwards to create different meanings or verses.</note>
"The mountains are green, the dew is thin, and scattered clouds fly,
The eyes reach the distant void where the half-sun sets.
Leisurely reflecting the evening glow, red petals piece by piece,
The water borders spring trees, green branches swaying low.
Hearing the rooster, one sleeps near the small fishing boat,
The weary horse's hooves bring sorrow to the traveler's path.
To conquer the barbarian caves, there are many buildings,
The high stockade is far away, and illness is frequent..."

<summary>This page introduces the scholars Ji Shiheng and Mao Guinian during a boat trip to Wei Pond, where their poetic evening turns supernatural as they witness five flower spirits reciting poetry from a celestial tower.</summary>
<keywords>Ji Shiheng, Mao Guinian, Wei Pond, lotus, flower spirits, palindromic poetry, Huating, Narcissus poem, literary friendship</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the supernatural encounter of the scholars Ji Shiheng and Mao Guinian at Wei Pond. Following the first poem by the Narcissus spirit on the previous page, the four remaining flower spirits—the Red Lotus, the Lotus Leaf, the Red Knotweed, and the Reed Flower—recite their verses. The narrative concludes with the scholars' realization that their beautiful companions were the personified essences of the local flora.</meta>

<margin>The five poems each fit their subject perfectly, being neither vulgar nor superficial, and possess a profound flavor.</margin>

"Like Xi Shi’s <note>One of the legendary Four Beauties of ancient China</note> delicate and graceful form, or like Liulang <note>original: 六郎 — Zhang Changzong, a Tang dynasty official famously compared to a lotus for his beauty</note> whose face is as fine as a lotus flower. Going toward the Stream Pavilion to pursue the best views, for her sake, I open the wine and draw in the clear fragrance." <gloss>The Red Lotus sings of herself</gloss>

->One woman recited:<-

"Fengyi <note>The God of the Yellow River</note> holds it up from among the water and clouds; without blemish or flaw, each one is alike. It turns like a kingfisher-green plate holding the morning dew; floating lightly, silken socks trail a fragrant breeze. Over the waves, it has already become an umbrella for the perch; to wash away the summer heat, it serves as a tube for sipping wine. <gloss>The Lotus Leaf sings of herself</gloss> A message for the silk-washer: do not pluck it; diligently leave it as a canopy for the Crystal Palace."

->One woman recited:<-

"The sharp air of autumn chills the marsh country; the Malan flowers bloom all across the sandbars. Below Fuchun Mountain stand rows of fishing huts; by the side of Caishi River <note>A famous site on the Yangtze River</note>, it is reflected in the wine taverns. Deep in the thickets under the night moon, the silver dew is cold; in the dim shade of the setting sun, the 'brocade scales' <note>A poetic term for colorful fish</note> swim. The young nobleman, waking drunk, must be deeply puzzled: he sees a red silk carpet spread out and not yet gathered." <gloss>The Red Knotweed sings of herself</gloss>

->One woman recited:<-

"River and sky grow old in autumn, and things wither; the flowers spit out golden 

<column-break/>

<margin>The two scholars met five women laughing and talking in a tower on a fine night; it is truly extraordinary. It can be compared to the beauty of the legends of Tiantai and Lanqiao <note>Famous literary sites of encounters between mortal men and celestial goddesses</note>.</margin>

half-dried leaves. On a moonlit night, the frost on the beach is bright and white; in the west wind, the snow on both banks is vast and swirling. To fill the void, one envies the joy of the birds' feathers; who pities the filial son in his single garment of reed-fluff? <note>A reference to Min Ziqian, a disciple of Confucius whose stepmother cruelly stuffed his winter clothes with useless reed-fluff instead of warm cotton; he bore it without complaint</note> Forgetting to sleep within the thickets of the small boat; at dawn, I stand lost in thought, gazing at the jade-colored waves." <gloss>The Reed Flower sings of herself</gloss>

When the recitation ended, the five women laughed together, each reaching the height of joy. The two scholars sat in their boat, greatly delighted, saying that no encounter could be more extraordinary than this. After a short while, they heard a voice from the tower say, "How beautiful are these two youths in the boat! What a pity that we are as far apart as the ends of the earth and cannot speak a single word with them." Before she had finished speaking, each of the five women tossed a small object down into the boat as if in play. Hearing the sound of them landing, the scholars ordered their servant to light a torch and search for them; they found only a lotus seed, a joint of lotus root, and similar items.

The two scholars were so entranced by the pleasure of the moment that they forgot to return. When they finally turned their oars back, they did not realize the dawn bugle was sounding from the towers and the forbidden morning calls were echoing, while a faint light began to shimmer over the vast waters. They commanded their servant to mark the spot. The next day, they went back to visit again, but they saw only the water lilies shining brightly, the red lotuses shimmering, lotus leaves pressed against the water, the white of the reeds, and the red of the knotweed. The towers and the beautiful women of the previous night were nowhere to be seen. Only then did the two scholars reach a great realization.

<summary>The five flower spirits conclude their poetry and playfully toss botanical gifts to the scholars before vanishing at dawn. The scholars return the next day to find only the actual flowers, realizing they had experienced a spiritual vision.</summary>

# Flower Spirits of the Wild Temple

<margin>
Scholar Yao was far from the city and the sun had already set. He could neither reach his destination nor return home. He stopped for a brief rest, though he truly did not know what kind of place this was.
</margin>

Heyang is a major district. About eight *li* <note>A traditional Chinese unit of distance; eight *li* is approximately 2.5 miles or 4 kilometers.</note> from the city, there stands an ancient temple dedicated to the True Lord <note>A "True Lord" (Zhenjun) is a title given to high-ranking Daoist deities or perfected immortals.</note>, facing south. Within the hall sits a sculpted statue of the True Lord, guarded by various generals whose awe-inspiring countenances resemble those found in a formal government office. Below the steps of the hall, on both sides, enthusiasts have planted four famous types of flowers: the Lily Magnolia, the Shirley Poppy, the Peony, and the Banana Shrub. The temple is magnificent and the flowers are exquisitely beautiful; onlookers cannot help but admire them in their hearts.

One day, a scholar surnamed Yao, named Tianlin, a native of Heyang, was returning from visiting a friend. He had traveled far and was unable to reach the city walls before the sky turned dark. He could neither go forward nor retreat. In his anxiety, he looked into the distance and saw an ancient grove. He hurried toward it and found several small buildings within the trees, which he assumed must be a private residence. He quickly approached the gate. Upon arrival, he saw an old servant standing outside. Tianlin bowed and greeted him, saying, "Is this not an inn?" The old servant laughed and said, "You are mistaken! This is a grand residence; how could it be a common inn?"

<column-break/>

<margin>
The black headcloth and scarlet robes are the usual attire for the True Lord within the temple hall; only this has not changed.
</margin>

"Whose residence is it then?" asked Tianlin. The old servant replied, "This is the home of the True Lord of Heyang." Tianlin then asked the servant to introduce him to the True Lord. The servant did not refuse and led Tianlin through several gates to the base of the steps. There, he saw an old man wearing a black headcloth <term>Futou: A traditional headcloth or cap worn by men in ancient China, often signifying status or scholarship.</term> and scarlet robes <gloss>The black headcloth and scarlet robes are the usual attire for the True Lord within the temple hall; only this has not changed.</gloss> sitting regally at the head of the hall. Tianlin bowed deeply and said, "I, a commoner of Heyang named Yao Tianlin, have lost my way and arrived here. I humbly beg for your hospitality."

The True Lord gestured for Tianlin to rise and said, "A man of letters should not be overly formal." Tianlin stood, and the True Lord guided him up into the hall, inviting him to sit in the guest’s seat. He then ordered the old servant to bring wine and fruit to share with Tianlin. After several rounds of wine, the True Lord became increasingly cheerful. He said to Tianlin, "In my house, there are four maidens who are skilled in song and dance, and especially gifted in poetry. I wish to have them come out to offer a toast, but I fear they might be mocked by a great scholar like yourself."

Tianlin left his seat to express his thanks, saying, "I am deeply honored by your elegant gift; how could I dare to mock them?" The True Lord summoned them, and after a short moment, the four maidens appeared. Their beauty was peerless, and their graceful bearing was like that of immortals descended to the human world. The True Lord...

<summary>Scholar Yao Tianlin, stranded at night, seeks refuge in what he discovers is the residence of the deity of Heyang. The deity welcomes him as a guest and prepares to entertain him with four beautiful maidens who represent the temple's famous flowers.</summary>
<keywords>Yao Tianlin, True Lord of Heyang, Daoist deity, flower spirits, supernatural encounter, hospitality</keywords>

# The Command to Compose Verse: The Four Maidens’ Request

The True Lord first commanded that poems be composed. The four maidens requested a theme. The True Lord said, “Each of you may use your own name as the subject.”

The first maiden was named **Lily Magnolia** <note>original: "Xinyi" (辛夷), the *Magnolia liliiflora*. Its buds are famously shaped like the tip of a calligraphy brush.</note>. She recited:

> The peach and apricot blossoms have drifted away; spring has ended,
> But fragrant buds newly sprout within the jade balustrade.
> The brush-tips trail purple powder, not by human effort,
> But the buds break into rosy clouds, like the work of a god.
> Dew-drenched, their clear fragrance condenses as if dipped in water,
> The wind blows their sharp points as if to paint the void.
> When should they be plucked and brought into the scholar's study,
> To sweep away a thousand enemies with the strategy of a hero?

The second maiden was named **Beautiful Spring** <gloss>Note: "Beautiful Spring" (Lichun) is another name for the Poppy; specifically, the variety known as "Monthly Red," which comes in red and white colors.</gloss>. She recited:

> From one single root come several kinds of flowers,
> After the rain, red and white colors quietly mingle.
> Their spirit is not yet as unrestrained as Zhao Feiyan,
> But their color is exactly like that of Zhang Lihua. <note>Zhao Feiyan and Zhang Lihua were legendary imperial consorts famous for their transcendent beauty.</note>
> Tired, they lean on the spring wind, flushed as if from last night’s wine,
> While warm morning dew dots them like sacred cinnabar.
> The God of Spring is naturally a guest from a noble house,
> Who fears he might overstay while chanting verses before these fragrant clusters.

The third maiden was named **Jade Stamen** <note>original: "Yurui" (玉蕊). There is much historical debate among Chinese scholars as to exactly which flower this refers to, often associated with rare, white, snow-like blossoms.</note>. She recited:

> Gardenias, willow catkins, and mountain alum—
> It was difficult even for the ancient sages to distinguish their varieties.
> A few blossoms are adorned like bright flakes of ice,
> A thousand branches carved from the cold of snow-flowers.
> At the Tangchang Temple, one no longer sees the original stock,
> For it was planted by a celestial maiden—

<column-break/>

> —Search for the fragrance, pluck a branch, and behold.
> Looking back, the God of Spring seems never to have been here,
> As the wild wind covers the ground with broken jade.

The fourth maiden was named **Smiling Shrub** <note>original: "Hanxiao" (含笑), the *Michelia figo*, known for its heavy banana-like scent and petals that never fully open, looking like a demure smile.</note>. She recited:

> Heaven gave rouge to dot those crimson lips,
> With the east wind full upon her face, she smiles radiantly.
> Her fragrant heart is naturally filled with joy,
> Her drunken face forever holds a fresh air of happiness.
> Her world-toppling beauty is full of emotion, favoring the guest,
> Facing the sun in silence, she seems to flirt with men.
> In the world of red dust, there are so many with worried brows;
> They would do well to enter this flower grove and become close neighbors.

When the recitations were finished, <gloss>The True Lord commanded them to sing.</gloss> <gloss>The True Lord commanded them to dance.</gloss> Their songs were like the melodies of orioles warbling in tall trees; their slender waists moved in dance like willows sleeping in the Forbidden City. Tianlin drank until he was thoroughly intoxicated. Amidst the scent of incense and the remains of the feast, he suddenly realized the sky had turned bright. He looked around and could no longer see where the True Lord or the four maidens were; only a clay statue stood there solemnly. The inscription on the temple hall read: "Temple of the True Lord of Heyang, Local Deity of this District." On both sides were four kinds of flowers: the Lily Magnolia, the Poppy, the Jade Stamen, and the Smiling Shrub. Tianlin returned home in awe and wonder, and from then on, he cultivated his virtue even more diligently.

<margin>
Having reached a mature age, Scholar Yao cultivated his virtue even more, for he finally began to realize that there are spiritual beings three feet above one's head.
</margin>

# Beside the Fence

<image-desc>An illustration depicting a scene titled "Beside the Fence" (籬邊). Two women in traditional Chinese dress are standing and talking near a large, jagged ornamental rock—a "scholar's stone"—and a flowering branch. Behind them is a low trellis or decorative fence.</image-desc>

The fourth maiden was named **Beside the Fence** <note>original: "Libian" (籬邊). This refers to the Chrysanthemum, famously associated with the poet Tao Yuanming and his fence.</note>. She recited:

> Bearing rain and draped in frost, one recalls the old sentiments beside the fence in Pengze <note>Pengze was the county where the famous recluse-poet Tao Yuanming (365–427 CE) served as magistrate before resigning to live a simple life growing chrysanthemums by his "eastern fence."</note>.

<header>Strange Chrysanthemums of Hezhou</header>
<page-num>Eleven</page-num>

Taoists favor yellow for their ritual clothing, thus it is known as "Taoist-style makeup."

Two scrolls of white chrysanthemums depicted flowers and leaves of such refreshing beauty that the spirit of autumn seemed to overflow from the brush-tip. Dai Jun’en was greatly delighted. He turned to the two beauties and said, "The chrysanthemums painted on these walls are so skillfully done, I cannot fail to offer them a verse. Why don't we each compose a regulated poem?" Thereupon, the beauty in the yellow dress first recited a poem for the Yellow Chrysanthemum:
> The fragrant clusters shine brightly, weary of the autumn light,
> Delicately leaning against the west wind in their Taoist-style makeup.
> Just like the man who lived in the era of Yixi <note>referring to the poet Tao Yuanming (365–427 CE), who retired during the Yixi reign of the Jin Dynasty</note>,
> Through cold mist and biting rain, how many Double Ninth Festivals have passed?

Jun’en then recited:
> Throughout your life, you are best at enduring the frost and dew,
> Tao Qian of Pengze <note>another name for Tao Yuanming</note> was your old friend who truly understood your "tone."
> The butterflies do not realize that autumn is already ending,
> Still fluttering through the fence, longing for the lingering gold of your petals.

The beauty in the white dress then recited a poem for the White Chrysanthemum:
> Beside the fence in the tender cold, several branches bloom,
> Powdered with dew and blowing fragrance into the wine cup.
> I can't help but laugh at that crazy old man of the Tao family <gloss>referring to the poet Tao Yuanming</gloss>,
> Whose blurred eyes mistook the white flowers for a person in white coming to visit.

Jun’en responded with a poem for the White Chrysanthemum:
> Faint fragrance fills the courtyard as the morning frost grows thick,
> The powdered butterflies fly near but find no trace of color.
> In your loneliness, who recognizes your late-season integrity? <note>original: "wanjie" (晚節). This refers both to the flower blooming late in the year and the moral integrity of a scholar who remains principled in his old age.</note>
> Like white hibiscus blooming upon the autumn river.

The three of them finished their recitation and were stroking—

<column-break/>

<margin>
The beauty's name is White Chrysanthemum; she is also called "Yellow Flower." <note>In Chinese poetry, "Yellow Flower" is often a generic term for all chrysanthemums.</note>
</margin>
<margin>
*Meng* [甍] refers to the ridge of a roof; *meng-meng* means high and grand.

# Strange Chrysanthemums of Hezhou

->The Hanshan Villa in Hezhou<-

Amidst farmlands and rustic huts, the view in all four directions was vast and open. Vegetation flourished there, and though spring flowers and autumn birds marked the passing years, few people ever visited. During the Hongxi era <note>The short reign of the Emperor Renzong of Ming, 1425 CE</note>, a scholar named Dai Jun’en was traveling elsewhere when he lost his way and happened upon this place. He saw grand, towering <gloss>repeated twice</gloss> red gates and many-layered <gloss>twice</gloss> elegant pavilions. Enveloped in ethereal mist and clouds, the sight looked just like a painting. Jun’en was astonished, thinking that such a magnificent house should not exist in such a remote place. He stood still for a long time until he saw two beauties emerge from the gates—one dressed in yellow and one in white. They greeted Jun’en with smiles, saying, "You, sir, are a man of talent. Won't you please take a seat and favor us with a visit?" Jun’en was charmed by them and followed. The beauties led the way while Jun’en followed behind, passing through multiple gates and ascending high steps until they reached the central hall. They exchanged formal greetings and invited him to sit, laying out fine fruits and serving him rich wine. The atmosphere was extremely lush and elegant. When Jun’en was half-drunk, he began to stroll around the hall and saw hanging upon the four walls—

<summary>A scholar named Dai Jun’en discovers a mysterious, grand villa inhabited by two personified chrysanthemum spirits. They share wine and compose poems that honor the flower's historical association with the recluse poet Tao Yuanming.</summary>
<keywords>Dai Jun'en, Tao Yuanming, Chrysanthemum personification, Hezhou, Ming Dynasty literature, floral poetry</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Dai Jun’en and the two mysterious women, who are revealed to be the spirits of the yellow and white chrysanthemums described on the previous page.</meta>

<columns>2</columns>
<header>The Record of Overflowing Waves</header>

<margin>
Every word is steeped in rouge and fragrance; every phrase is full of deep emotion. What kind of women are these beauties, truly?
</margin>
They clapped their hands and laughed loudly, and both parties forgot their inhibitions. Jun’en then spoke leisurely, saying, "My ladies, living alone behind these secluded curtains, do you not feel the sorrow of things that touch the heart?"

The beauty laughed and replied, "Of all things, humans are the most spiritual. How could I be like a bitter gourd? <note>A reference to the *Analects* (17.7), where Confucius says he is not a gourd to be hung up and not eaten; she means she is not meant to stay unused and isolated.</note> How could I be tethered without being 'consumed'? How could I avoid the feelings of sorrow when things touch the heart? Now that I have seen my gentleman, my heart is at peace. <note>original: "我心則降" (my heart is lowered/calmed), an expression of relief and joy from the *Classic of Poetry*.</note> We shall play the zither and lute together forever <note>original: "琴瑟" (qin and se), a classic metaphor for marital harmony</note>, so what doubt could there be?"

That night, the two beauties shared a bed with Jun’en, and their love and affection grew even deeper. One beauty joked, "Our love was conveyed by the red leaf; we are not like the 'Jade of Shujun' seeking a buyer." <note>The "red leaf" refers to a famous legend where a palace lady sends a poem on a leaf down a stream to find a husband. The "Jade" reference implies they chose him out of love rather than seeking a transaction.</note>

Jun’en replied, "My feelings were moved by the plain zither; I am not like the one who climbed over the wall to follow." <note>A reference to the romantic poet Sima Xiangru, though here Jun'en claims his devotion is more refined.</note>

The next day, Jun’en bid them farewell to return home. The beauties wept, saying, "Our love is not yet fulfilled, and the pillows are not yet warm. How can you bear to abandon us and go far away?"

Jun’en said, "Truly, I cannot bear to leave, but my family’s eyes are watching anxiously. If I go now and come back later, perhaps both duties can be fulfilled without harm." Thereupon, the beauty in the yellow dress took out a gold hair-cover <note>A type of traditional ornamental hair net or mesh</note> to give as a parting gift, and the beauty in the white dress took out—

<column-break/>

<margin>
These two poems about parting, though somewhat colloquial, describe the emotion of separation both concisely and thoroughly.
</margin>
—two silver phoenix hairpins to give as a parting gift. They said, "Please accept these two items as a small token of our hearts. We beg you to look at these objects and think of the people; do not forget us from morning till night."

The beauty in yellow wept and recited:
> "The mountains are clear, the waters flow;
> Talking of parting at the crossroads, I cannot bear the sorrow.
> Outside the Gate of Xianyang <note>Xianyang was the ancient capital; its gates were a standard literary setting for scenes of parting</note>, the thousand willow branches
> Cannot tether my darling’s departing boat."

The beauty in white also wept and recited:
> "Telling you to go, you depart in such haste,
> This parting sorrow is endless.
> Before my eyes, the fallen leaves are so red—
> They are all dyed by the tears of a grieving person."

Jun’en sighed and sobbed, unable to compose a rhyming verse in response. The three of them parted, each with eyes full of tears. When Jun’en returned home, he constantly missed them; they appeared in his dreams and in his poems. Privately, he was filled with uncontrollable joy.

By the next year, he had cause to travel that way again. Passing by the villa, Jun’en thought he could see the beauties once more. But when he went to visit, he could not find the place. Jun’en was startled and thought they must be spirits. He quickly took out the hair-cover and the phoenix hairpins to look at them—they were all actually the yellow and white petals of chrysanthemums.

# Preface to Clear Talk Selected from Ten Thousand

## Wei Ken

While the Sage Son of Heaven <note>A respectful term for the Emperor</note> is upon the throne and the realm has been purified and made vast, the families of the scholars <note>original: 博士 (boshi), historically referring to the "Erudites" or court academicians</note> clap their hands and converse; every man grasps the "Spirit-Snake Pearl" <note>A legendary gem said to be given by a grateful snake to its healer; it symbolizes rare and precious literary talent.</note> and every family cradles the "Uncut Jade of Mount Jing." <note>The raw stone from which the most famous jade disc in Chinese history was carved, representing hidden, unpolished genius.</note> Then one day, a person arrived with a manuscript in hand to visit me and request its printing; it was titled *Clear Talk Selected from Ten Thousand*.

<summary>This preface introduces a collection of "clear talks"—a genre of elegant, witty, or philosophical conversation—praising the current era as a time of such great peace and talent that every scholar possesses the brilliance of legendary gems.</summary>
<keywords>Clear Talk, scholars, Spirit-Snake Pearl, Uncut Jade of Mount Jing, Sage Son of Heaven, literary talent, preface</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Wei Ken for the collection "Clear Talk Selected from Ten Thousand," moving from a general praise of the current era into a philosophical defense of "Clear Talk" as a medium for the Way.</meta>

->漢<-
<meta>catchword: Han</meta>

Now, "talk" means to converse; it is to speak. The words of the <term>Six Classics</term> <note>The source text uses 六徑 (Six Paths), likely a variant or substitution for the 六經 (Six Classics), the foundational texts of Confucianism.</note> are supreme, and through them, the Way is made clear. In the Han dynasty, the governance between Heaven and Man was stable, and Royal Mandates were issued to the armies. During the Jin, Wei, Tang, and Song dynasties, literary masterpieces like the *Rhapsody on the Goddess of the Luo River* or the *Rhapsody on Mount Gaotang* <note>Two of the most famous poetic works in Chinese history, dealing with divine encounters and romance.</note> are too numerous to fully record. Yet, I have never heard of prioritizing **"Clear Talk"** <note>original: 清談 (Qingtan). A sophisticated style of philosophical conversation and wit that flourished during the 3rd to 6th centuries among the Chinese elite.</note> or selecting specific conversations and labeling them "Clear." If the **"Dark Learning"** <note>original: 玄 (Xuan). Referring to Xuanxue, a Neo-Daoist school of thought focused on the metaphysical nature of reality.</note> and the Way were as distant as the states of Yan and Yue <note>A common idiom meaning things are as far apart as the north and south.</note>, perhaps one might privately harbor doubts, much like the Minister over the Masses during the Jin dynasty. However, upon examining these <term>bamboo slips</term> <note>original: 簡策 (jiance). Referring here to the manuscript pages.</note>, I see...

<column-break/>

...characters of extraordinary spirit. There is talk of physical things manifesting as illusions and mysteries; there is talk of whether to take a public post or remain in seclusion; there is talk of the laws of music and rhythm; there is talk of the transformations of **Yin and Yang**. Here is the expansion and contraction, the coming and going, the dark and the bright, the hidden and the manifest, and the gain and loss that serves as exhortation and warning. Every word is meticulously detailed; every part is orderly. This might well be the ringing of the **clapper-bell** <note>original: 鐸 (duo). A bell used by ancient heralds to command attention; a metaphor for a teacher or text that awakens the public's moral conscience.</note> that rouses the populace; it might well be the vessel that carries the Way. To defend the Way is found herein, and the path to it is also found herein.

<note>This passage argues that even though "Clear Talk" was often seen as frivolous or purely metaphysical in the past, this specific collection serves a moral and educational purpose similar to the classics.</note>

<summary>Wei Ken defends the collection by arguing that these "Clear Talks" are not mere idle chatter, but actually contain the moral "Way" and serve to awaken the reader's spirit through various philosophical and natural topics.</summary>
<keywords>Clear Talk (Qingtan), Six Classics, Yin and Yang, Dark Learning (Xuanxue), morality, Han Dynasty, literary defense</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the philosophical introduction of the previous page to the formal title and table of contents of the collection. It also includes an inscription by the Hermit of Mount Taihua, praising the literary quality of the work and its connection to the historical traditions of the Wei, Tang, and Song dynasties.</meta>

# Newly Engraved and Fully Illustrated Edition with Commentary: Selections of Ancient and Modern Pure Conversations in Ten Thousand Categories, Volume 1
->Published by Zhou Jinquan of Jinling<- <note>Jinling is the historical name for Nanjing, a major center for publishing during the Ming Dynasty.</note>

### Table of Contents: Volume 1
Human Character and Supernatural Wonders, Part One
Fifteen entries in total
Marquis Zhang Returns to Life
Scholar Zhang’s Banquet

<column-break/>

...from the Wei, Tang, and Song dynasties are found here. Among ten thousand selected, ten thousand hit the mark; how could the "three burrows" <note>A reference to the idiom "a wily hare has three burrows" (狡兔三窟), suggesting that while some are merely clever for survival, this collection possesses a deeper, more singular excellence.</note> of the cunning be worth mentioning? Alas! It is like using a silk thread to pull a giant turtle’s tail or lifting a heavy stone; the road is long for a mediocre horse <note>original: 駑駘 (nudai). A metaphor for someone of limited talent attempting a great task.</note>. Yet, here is **"Clear Talk"** <note>original: 清談 (Qingtan). A refined style of philosophical wit and conversation popular among the elite.</note> preserved in ink.

The Sagely influence shapes these works. Upon opening the book chest, the brilliance of the brush-tip <note>original: 豪芒 (haomang). # The Rebirth of Lord Zhang

Zhang Yaowen, a successful candidate of the *Xin-you* year [1561] from Xingan County, Jiangxi, was the younger brother of Zhang Kewen, an official in the Ministry of Works. There were six biological brothers in total; Kewen was the eldest, and Yaowen was the second. Along with their fourth brother, they all studied under Kewen’s guidance. They worked together day and night on the Classics and Histories; even in their sleeping and eating, they were in perfect harmony. Kewen and Yaowen both passed the provincial examinations <term>provincial examinations: the second level of the imperial exam system</term>. In the summer of the first year of the Longqing reign [1567], they prepared to travel to the Southern Palace <note>original: "Nangong"; a poetic term for the Ministry of Rites, where the Metropolitan Examination was held</note> for the final imperial trials. Their younger brothers, filled with affection and reluctance to part, accompanied them for part of the journey. Five friends from the same region were also traveling for the exams; among them was a close friend from the same county who had passed the exams in the same year as Yaowen. They agreed to charter a boat together to head north. On the first day of the seventh month, the boat docked at Huai’an Prefecture. Yaowen began suffering from dysentery, though he expected to recover within ten days. On the twenty-fifth day, they reached Qinghe. Suddenly, in the middle of the river, the boat lost its steering oar, and they faced the terror of the vessel sinking. Everyone on board cried out in fear for rescue. Yaowen, weakened by his illness and startled by the sudden crisis, fell into a state of mental confusion. Fortunately, a transport ship was nearby, higher than the sinking boat

<margin>
There must certainly be a reason for this; how could it merely be the fulfillment of a wish? It happened shortly after the peace was restored.
</margin>

by about three feet. Everyone scrambled to crawl onto the other ship. Kewen alone carried Yaowen on his back using a heavy blanket. As if aided by divine wings, he leaped and boarded the transport ship. Once aboard, Yaowen’s illness became even more severe. A physician examined him and said, "His pulse is scattered; nothing more can be done." Kewen looked toward the Sanyi Temple <note>The "Temple of the Three Righteousness," dedicated to the sworn brothers of the *Romance of the Three Kingdoms*: Liu Bei, Guan Yu, and Zhang Fei.</note> in Taoyuan from afar. Weeping, he prayed: "My brother Yaowen is critically ill and on the verge of death. Now we are stranded on this arduous journey. If my brother indeed dies, I cannot live on alone. If we brothers die one after another and the news reaches home, our parents will be unable to bear the grief, and I fear further tragedies will follow. Our fourth brother and the others would surely weep themselves to death." <gloss>Estimating the emotions that would inevitably follow.</gloss> "The fate of our entire family hangs on this day's turn of events. The voice in the sky spoke again: "Old Mr. Yan has departed. From now on, Yaowen's corpse will remain as it is." Kewen could not bear to nail the coffin shut. # Ten Thousand Selections of Pure Loyalty and Respect, Volume One

...Indeed, the soul seemed to have returned. However, because his vital energy <term>qi: the circulating life force or "breath" in traditional Chinese thought</term> was only just beginning to stir, it was vital not to startle him suddenly. They waited until the dawn of the sixteenth day. Ke rushed to report the news at the Temple of the Three Righteous Ones <note>original: "Sanyi Ci"; a temple dedicated to the heroes of the Three Kingdoms—Liu Bei, Guan Yu, and Zhang Fei—who represented the pinnacle of fraternal loyalty</note>. Returning to the room, he took Yao’s hand. Yao responded by lightly pinching Ke’s hand with his finger. Ke then used his hand to gently lift Yao’s eyelids, which at first could not stay open. After a long while, they opened slightly, and Yao was able to recognize Ke. Ke asked him softly, "What time is it?" Ke told him it was the fifteenth day of the eighth month <note>This is the Mid-Autumn Festival, a traditional time for family reunion</note>. He asked again, "Where is this place?" Ke told him it was Taoyuan <note>literally "Peach Spring," a district in Jiangsu province</note>. Yao said, "I was just in Huaian <gloss>where Yao first suffered from dysentery</gloss>. How did I get to Taoyuan?" He then fell silent once more. Over the course of the eighteenth day, he began to murmur to himself in a low voice, "I have come back, but every part of me is not my own <gloss>his physical form did not resemble his former self</gloss>. Initially, my elder brother loved me with such great affection, but I have departed and left everything to him. Since every part of me is no longer mine, how can I truly return?" On the nineteenth day, he said again, "I cannot return now, yet I cannot leave. If every part of me is not my own, I must depart again." Ke surmised <gloss>pronounced in the entering tone</gloss> that when the soul first entered, it possessed its original appearance, but now that the soul was returning after the body had withered, the flesh had fallen away and no longer resembled his former shape.

<column-break/>

<margin>If the Ministry of Water <note>original: "Shuibu"; a reference to Zhang Ke’s official title or department</note> had not been able to calmly stabilize the true body and move the gods with ultimate sincerity, then in the space of a single breath, even the spirits and ghosts could not have pulled him back and kept him.</margin>

Thus, Ke comforted him, pressing his hand against Yao’s heart and asking, "Is this part truly yours?" After a short while, Yao replied, "This part, at least, is mine" <gloss>the heart does not change</gloss>. Ke said, "If this part is yours, then every part is yours. Your brother has guarded everything well for you. You must not leave again; if you go now, you will never be able to return." Ke added, "If you leave again, I shall follow you." He spoke with such sincerity that it moved Yao’s true nature; Yao surely could not bear to have his brother die with him <gloss>living together might prevent his departure</gloss>. On the twentieth day, Yao finally asked for a mirror to look at himself. Seeing his reflection with his own eyes, he smiled slightly and said, "It is me." His soul having finally settled, he was given congee to drink. After a few more days, he ate <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss> cooked rice. A few days later, he ate a small amount of meat <gloss>pronounced as written</gloss>. He required no medicine at all. He felt his breath gradually return and his flesh gradually become firm. He asked if he could sit, and then gradually he could walk, following every stage of growth just like an infant. By the end of the ninth month, his form was exactly as it had been in the past, though his facial features remained slightly different. In the tenth month, Ke and Yao wept bitterly as they took their leave.

At the Temple of the Three Righteous Ones, they boarded a merchant boat to continue their journey north. Whenever Ke heard his brother mutter something while in that death-like state in the room, he questioned him about it, and every instance had a cause; each had its own explanation, though Ke could not bear to recount them all. When the boat arrived at the capital, they learned that their fellow travelers were staying in a temple. Yao went on ahead.

The principles of life and death, the hidden mechanisms of the gods and spirits, the resonance of brotherly love, and the rewards for a family’s accumulated virtue—are they not exactly like this?

# Scholar Zhang’s Meeting in the Spirit Realm

In Hecheng, there was a man surnamed Zhang, a scholar without official rank. He lived by Mandarin Duck Lake <note>Yuanhu: a famous scenic lake in Jiaxing, Zhejiang Province</note>, where he found joy in a simple, solitary life. <gloss>pronounced 'le', meaning joy</gloss>
He was free from the burdens of high office and frequently visited Buddhist monasteries. He conversed with monks about Zen and possessed a deep understanding of the true essence of the scriptures. <gloss>He understood the principles of the Zen sutras</gloss> In the autumn of the thirty-third year of the Wanli reign <note>roughly 1605 CE during the Ming Dynasty</note>, he observed a butcher in his village and the butcher’s wife. 
They were both proclaiming the verses of the Luo Sect <gloss>they were promoting a heterodox path to deceive and incite the people; the men and women following them were numerous, misleading the public and deceiving the commoners</gloss>. Zhang felt the urge to roll up his sleeves and denounce them, but his wife stopped him, saying, "You can barely look after your own life; what leisure do you have to manage the affairs of others?" Zhang laughed and dropped the matter. Later, one night while sitting alone facing the moon, he suddenly saw two mighty warriors <gloss>military men</gloss> wearing yellow turbans and embroidered jackets. They stepped forward and bowed, saying, "The Heavenly King Vaisravana <note>one of the Four Heavenly Kings who protect the world and the Buddhist law</note> invites the scholar for a meeting." Zhang was startled <gloss>Zhang arrived of his own accord</gloss> and before he could even ask a question, a person suddenly led a green lion forward. The King pulled the embroidered saddle and bridle as it knelt on the ground. The warriors helped Zhang mount the beast. In the hazy atmosphere, he felt the roar of wind and thunder in his ears, while clouds and mist dazzled his eyes.

<column-break/>

<page-num>1</page-num>
<margin>Though it cannot be historically proven that Zhang Wujin was the previous incarnation of Layman Pang, one can be certain that his heart, which held fast to the truth and detested heresy, remained the same across both his past and present lives.</margin>

In a short time, they arrived at a golden city. Guards were lined up in a dense formation. They passed through multiple <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss> gates before reaching the hall. 
The Heavenly King had the head of a pig and the nose of an elephant, appearing just like the statues molded in temples. Zhang bowed twice, and the Heavenly King returned the greeting, gesturing for him to sit by his side. With a calm <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss> composure, the King said to him, "I am the head of the Solar Clan and a protector of the various Buddhas. 
Currently, the Luo Sect spreads falsehoods across the world, bringing chaos to our true teachings. Although the light of a firefly can hardly challenge the sun, weeds in the field will inevitably harm the beautiful crops. <gloss>These two lines are metaphors for the previous statement</gloss> How could those who defend the Law not feel chilled at heart? 
Formerly, Zhang Wujin <gloss>a person's name; also known as Zhang Shangying, a famous Song dynasty official and defender of Buddhism</gloss> was your previous incarnation. You once defended the vehicle of the Law with great merit. Now, seeing what is right but failing to act, 
I fear that your previous merits will be discarded because of this. <gloss>The merits gained from defending the Law in his previous life</gloss> The reason I have invited you here is that I wish to trouble your 'historian’s pen' to correct these many heresies." He then ordered his attendants to bring forth a white jade inkstone, a rhinoceros-horn brush, and more than ten feet of "cloud-pattern" paper, which were placed before Zhang. Zhang bowed his head and followed the command. With a single stroke, he composed a poem: "The fragrant ink of the palm-leaf scriptures flows from India; the White Horse galloped from afar to enter China. If you wish to know the path ahead, [cough—]" <note>original: "欬". This is a catchword referring to the first word of the following page, likely part of the word 'kàisù' (to explain or sigh).</note>

<summary>A scholar named Zhang is summoned by the deity Vaisravana to the spirit realm. The deity reveals that Zhang is a reincarnation of a famous Buddhist protector and tasks him with writing a refutation of the "Luo Sect," a popular heretical movement of the time.</summary>
<keywords>Vaisravana, Luo Sect, Zhang Shangying, Ming Dynasty, reincarnation, heterodoxy, Jiaxing, Mandarin Duck Lake</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Scholar Zhang’s visionary encounter. Having been commissioned by the Celestial King to expose the corruption of the Luo Sect, Zhang now presents his critique. The text shifts from a narrative description of his journey to a polemical poem (the "Luo Path Chapter") that he submits to the divine court before returning to the mortal realm.</meta>

<margin>This describes the state of the world where people trust in heresy, filled with the deceits of the Luo Path. It makes clear that there is blood and principle involved; thus, it is fitting that it moved the Dragon Face <note>The Emperor's or a high deity's countenance</note> with joy. It serves as a guide for removing evil. It is not even necessary for the events to be literally true for one to believe that the underlying principles are valid.</margin>

They constantly command men and women to disregard the Three Bonds <note>The Confucian fundamental relationships: ruler-subject, father-child, and husband-wife</note>, offering incense and flowers as if to true saints. Yet there is not the slightest evidence of efficacy; it is all absurd. <gloss>Proclamations made over incense</gloss> They are even more skilled at using honeyed words to make demands. At a single call, a hundred people promise their support. Compared to the two great rebel groups of the past, they are even more unrestrained. <gloss>Their harm is worse than that of the Yellow Turbans or the Red Turbans. They are greedy for both wealth and sex, their lust as insatiable as a deep ravine.</gloss> I now sing this "Chapter on the Luo Path," hanging the Mirror of Dharma high in the middle of heaven to clearly expose the inner workings of the Luo Path. Even if there are false teachings, who will dare to spread them now? When the writing was finished, it was presented at the Palace of the King of Heaven. The King was greatly pleased and said, "Scholar, this work can be said to thoroughly expose these heterodox practices <gloss>pronounced in the rising tone</gloss> and greatly turn the Wheel of Dharma. The blessings you shall receive, Scholar, are immeasurable." After these words were spoken, those who were to escort the Master waited at the foot of the palace. There were many followers leading the way with banners, parasols, music, and incense. In an instant, he arrived home, and the crowd suddenly dispersed. Zhang felt as if waking from a dream. At that time, there was someone living with Scholar Zhang who was familiar with these events and recorded them once.

<column-break/>

<margin>Writing with a direct pen to shatter the very core of these bandit heretics—this section serves that purpose. One must know how to value oneself; if those who are drowning in these false methods can also learn to restrain themselves, they may find salvation for generations to come.</margin>

One must know that the source of true teaching comes from the power of the True Vehicle <note>The authentic path of Buddhism</note>. Nowadays, these teachings have mostly collapsed into the "Luo Path," also called "Non-Action" <note>Wuwei</note>. They spread their seductive songs and chants throughout every village and town. The virtuous are scolded into bowing to them, while the wicked follow their lead. They claim to speak of the speed of descent into hell, yet they wantonly create new lyrics to entice and mislead ignorant women. Once these women believe, they slight their husbands and loathe their children, rushing to welcome the sect. They stand to burn incense, never suspecting that a spell was first cast into the incense itself. Even the unattractive are made to seem beautiful through "karmic affinity," and they are permitted to come to the secret chambers morning and night. There, lewd and filthy acts are committed. People of the time simply call this "Navel Rubbing." <note>A derogatory term for a ritual associated with these heterodox sects</note> It is a tragedy that they lose their virtue and yet do not repent, still using their chants to fool their husbands. Their methods also involve looking into mirrors and gazing into water. Those who cannot join the assembly feel deep shame. This trend pollutes our customs and does great harm to people; it is a massive plague upon the world. <gloss>pronounced 'sui'</gloss> Do you not see the great bandits of the Han dynasty called the Yellow Turbans? <gloss>Yellow Turban was their title</gloss> They hid behind such excitement to move millions of people. Do you not also see the Red Turbans who gathered crowds and brought chaos to the end of the Yuan dynasty? <gloss>The White Lotus Society</gloss> Like a spark or a small stream, they eventually could not be contained. And now, the Luo Path flourishes in all four directions.

<summary>Scholar Zhang presents a scathing poetic critique of the Luo and Wuwei sects to the King of Heaven, comparing their deceptive practices and moral corruption to historical rebel groups like the Yellow Turbans and the White Lotus Society. After receiving divine praise, Zhang awakens from what seems to be a visionary dream, and his account is recorded by a contemporary.</summary>

<keywords>Luo Path (羅道), Wuwei Sect (無為教), Yellow Turbans, Red Turbans, White Lotus Society, Scholar Zhang, religious polemic, Ming Dynasty, Three Bonds, heterodoxy</keywords>

# -> Chanting Linked Verses on an Autumn Night <-

<image-desc>Woodblock illustration of a scholar in a long robe and cap standing beneath a pine tree branch, holding a brush and writing on a large stone monument or wall. Cursive script is visible on the surface of the stone.</image-desc>

<margin>
Drunk with wine and unrestrained in poetry, throughout this hundred-year life, I wish to ascend to immortality. <note>original: 酒酣詩放百年身世欲登僊. The term "ascend to immortality" (dengxian) refers to the Daoist concept of becoming a celestial being.</note>
</margin>

<unclear>Not seeing this gentleman, also...</unclear>

<column-break/>

<image-desc>Woodblock illustration of five scholars in traditional robes and caps standing together outdoors at night, looking upwards at a crescent moon and a constellation connected by dotted lines. Stylized clouds and a flock of birds are visible in the sky.</image-desc>

<margin>
The moon is bright and the breeze is clear; the eternal mountains and rivers remain just as they were of old. <note>original: 月朗風清萬古山河渾似舊. This phrase evokes a sense of timelessness, suggesting that while human religions and sects may rise and fall, the natural order is constant.</note>
</margin>

**Key Vocabulary:**
*   <term>Autumn Night</term> (穐夜): A traditional setting for scholarly gathering and poetic composition.
*   <term>Linked Chanting</term> (聯吟): A social literary practice where poets take turns composing lines of a single poem.
*   <term>Ascending to Immortality</term> (登僊): The spiritual goal of transcending the mortal coil.
*   <term>Bright Moon and Clear Breeze</term> (月朗風清): An idiom describing a peaceful, beautiful night, often used to signify a clear and virtuous mind.
*   <term>Eternal Mountains and Rivers</term> (萬古山河): A reference to the enduring landscape of the nation and the world.
*   <term>Wine-tipsy and Unrestrained Poetry</term> (酒酣詩放): The classic image of the "drunk poet" whose inspiration is freed by wine.

<summary>This page depicts a transition into a contemplative, poetic mode following the scholar's divine mission, using imagery of a moonlit gathering to emphasize the permanence of nature over human turmoil. The scholar expresses a desire for Daoist immortality through the medium of wine and verse.</summary>
<keywords>Autumn Night, Linked Chanting, Immortality, Nature, Scholar-Bureaucrats, Daoist Transcendence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous scene of spiritual reflection. Having moved beyond his critique of the Luo Sect's corruption, the protagonist—now identified as the scholar Shang Zhi—is depicted in a moment of quiet, scholarly solitude that transitions into a supernatural encounter. The theme shifts from religious polemic to the timeless tradition of "linked verse" (lianju), a collaborative poetic practice.</meta>

<margin>On this Mid-Autumn night, encountering eight gentlemen to compose linked verses—the literary flavor is delightful, and the meeting is truly remarkable.</margin>

# Chanting Linked Verses with Shang Zhi

Shang Zhi of Xianyang (whose courtesy name was Shizhong) was a man of letters who loved books so much he was rarely seen without one in his hand. He built a study at his Nanzhuang Villa <gloss>pronounced *shu*</gloss>. During the Tianbao era <note>original: 天寶 (742–756 CE). This was the era of the Tang Dynasty Emperor Xuanzong, a period famous for its high culture but also the eventual An Lushan Rebellion.</note>, Shang Zhi moved there to live, reading day and night without ceasing. One year, during the Mid-Autumn Festival, he saw the Milky Way bright in the sky and moonlight washing the earth, perfectly dividing the autumn scenery. It was truly a beautiful night. Shang Zhi pushed open the window to face the moon and began drinking alone <gloss>pouring wine</gloss> to satisfy his heart. He then composed a seven-character regulated poem:

-> The autumn colors are split even, the moon is perfectly round; <-
-> At the South Tower, high spirits <gloss>falling tone</gloss> soar beyond the sky. <-
-> Clouds retreat from the Phoenix Court, a golden mirror hangs; <-
-> Water fills the Dragon Pond, where white lilies float. <-
-> Mountains and rivers of old remain through ten thousand ages; <-
-> In this hundred-year life, I wish to ascend as an immortal. <-
-> When shall I call upon the old hermit of the forest and banks, <-
-> To share a flask of wine and let our boat drift on the river? <-

When the poem was finished, he chanted it aloud several times <gloss>contemplating the flavor to suit his mood</gloss>. Lifting his head to look around, he suddenly saw eight people beneath the moon.

<column-break/>

<margin>These eight men were likely distinguished writers during their lives; therefore, their heroic spirits did not perish along with their physical remains.</margin>

Some were old and some were young; they were all different. They wore rustic clothing and "Lunjin" headbands <note>A traditional silk headband or scarf, often associated with the strategist Zhuge Liang or scholarly recluses.</note>, each with their own distinct appearance. Hearing Shang Zhi's chanting, they bowed and stepped forward, saying, "We are residents of a nearby village. Since it is such a beautiful night, we were taking a stroll to enjoy ourselves when we suddenly heard a man of letters <gloss>referring to Shang Zhi</gloss> chanting here. We were filled with endless admiration, so we have come specifically to visit." 

Shang Zhi believed their words and felt no fear <gloss>terror</gloss>. He ordered his servant to wash the cups so they could drink together. The eight men looked at each other and said, "This evening is so beautiful; should we not have a poem to record it?" The group replied, "Why don't we compose linked verses? Each of us shall contribute one rhyme." 

One man began the chant: "The Wei River flows east, the sun sets in the west." 
Another responded: "The autumn colors of Xianyang are lost in the misty view." 
A third said: "At the ancient ferry in the desolate mist, few people come." 
A fourth said: "By the withered willows of the empty city, a horse neighs on its own." 
A fifth said: "Hegemony has vanished in the fires of the third month" <gloss>amidst lush grass and broken tablets</gloss> <note>original: 三月火. A reference to the burning of the Qin palaces by the conqueror Xiang Yu, which reportedly lasted for three months.</note>. 
A sixth said: "A broken stone tablet still bears a few lines of writing." 
A seventh said: "Where is the man who led his dog through the East Gate?" <note>A famous historical allusion to Li Si, the Prime Minister of the Qin Dynasty, who lamented as he was led to execution that he wished he could still hunt with his dog outside the East Gate of Shangcai.</note>

<summary>The scholar Shang Zhi is visited by eight mysterious, scholarly figures during a Mid-Autumn night. They begin a collaborative "linked verse" poem that reflects on the tragic history and fallen glory of the ancient capital, Xianyang.</summary>
<keywords>Shang Zhi, Mid-Autumn Festival, Linked Verse (Lianju), Xianyang, Tang Dynasty (Tianbao era), Ghostly Encounter, Li Si allusion</keywords>
