<page-num>Thirty-<unclear>one</unclear></page-num>
<sig>Tiger</sig>

<margin>
Regarding Shang Zhi’s poetry, the elders certainly knew the weight of his writing, but perhaps they also highly valued it to borrow Shang Zhi’s vitality <note>original: 生氣 (shengqi). In Chinese folklore, ghosts often seek the "living breath" or "vital energy" of scholars through literary interaction.</note>.
</margin>

A dog barked at the East Gate. The last person said: "In vain I see the green grass even every year." Having finished his chant, the eight people raised their wine cups and came before Shang Zhi, saying: "We have long lived in this village school <gloss>a wild</gloss> <gloss>place</gloss>. Yet Zhou Ke <note>Possibly a reference to a local spirit or a deceased scholar.</note> had knowledge. We only see spring flowers blooming and autumn grass withering <gloss>scattered</gloss>.

Spring follows autumn; heat and cold push each other; swallows and wild geese take turns in their migration. There are no carriages or horses crowding our door; only sparrow nets could be set <note>A classical idiom (men ke luo que) describing a place that is deserted and lonely.</note>. Desiring to renew our old ways <gloss>level tone</gloss>, we must obtain fine words <gloss>referring to Shang Zhi's poetry</gloss>. If you do not disdain the dust of our forest and spring, we hope you will generously grant us a regulated poem <gloss>of gems and pearls</gloss>.

We rustic folk would be fortunate. Our humble hut <gloss>pronounced *bi*</gloss> would glow with light. It would make onlookers see beauty and the writer feel honored. We would be most fortunate indeed, most fortunate indeed." Shang Zhi agreed. He called his servant and asked for <gloss>to take</gloss> paper and brush, then wrote a regulated poem to give to them. The poem says:

> My secluded home is much like the Pavilion of the Invited Gentleman. The door faces the green mountains, closed <gloss>opening by itself</gloss> throughout the day. From one couch, tea smoke drifts in the whistling wind <gloss>whistling and fluttering</gloss>; amidst the dim pine rain, I wake from a dream. <gloss>level tone</gloss>. The official of the Hongdu Academy <note>A prestigious Han Dynasty institution for artists and poets.</note> is well-placed, but ashamed of his brushwork...

<column-break/>

<margin>
Initially, the chanting of the eight people was profound and lingering. The feeling of being touched by it was so timely; how could his heart not be moved?
</margin>

> ...The temple gate is deep; I am too lazy to discuss sutras. Lying alone in the empty forest, I realize the year is late. No one says there are few <gloss>departing tone</gloss> tiny stars.

After he finished writing, he handed it to the eight people. Each was greatly pleased. They each took brushes and ink from their sleeves and approached Shang Zhi to thank him. <gloss>Before the desk, we were blessed with your precious words; we cannot express how much we admire this poem.</gloss>

"We now offer these brushes and ink to assist your study. We humbly hope you will accept them with a smile. This is our gratitude." Soon the slanting moon hung over the cliff. Fading stars were scattered. Frost and dew had fallen. The light of dawn was hazy, and without realizing it, the East had already turned white. The eight people bowed and took their leave, then departed. Shang Zhi sat down and looked at the brushes and ink they had given him. They were made of mud. He was suddenly struck with a great realization. Looking afar at a secluded forest, Shang Zhi walked slowly through it. Thorny brambles filled the path, and deserted graves were piled high <gloss>scattered and overgrown</gloss>. The poems he had given them were still hanging on the treetops. Shang Zhi was amazed, dared not look back, and hurried back home.

<summary>Shang Zhi composes a poem for his eight mysterious visitors, who reward him with brushes and ink before vanishing at dawn. He later discovers the gifts are actually made of mud and finds his poems hanging over a cluster of ancient graves, revealing his companions were ghosts.</summary>
<keywords>ghost stories, classical poetry, Shang Zhi, supernatural gifts, graveyard, literary encounter, mud pens</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical sketch of Master Wu, a local figure who embodies the "hermit-scholar" ideal. It emphasizes his rejection of political status in favor of a quiet, disciplined life in nature during the early Ming Dynasty.</meta>

<margin>
Someone asked: "Is Master Wu an immortal?" I replied: "Why speak of immortality? The essence of the Nine Immortals is to have a heart without lingering fears and a body without lingering attachments. Now, Master Wu shares his spirit with the heaven, earth, lakes, and mountains; his soul is clear and vast, and he treats flowers and birds as his close companions. Since he has forgotten both body and mind, is he not an immortal among immortals?"
</margin>

# A Visit to the Immortal Scholar Wu

During the Hongwu era <note>The reign of the first Ming Emperor, 1368–1398.</note>, there was a man named Master Wu, who took the pseudonym "The Contented Immortal." He loved poetry and was fond of wine, yet he never succumbed to arrogance or disorderly conduct. In his daily life, he was modest and respectful, appearing like a simple or even weak man; however, in his heart, high office and noble rank <note>Literally "carriages and caps," symbols of high-ranking officials.</note> were as worthless as weeds, and gold or jade were as mere dust. He looked down on wealth and status. Though his family's means were not abundant, his heart was joyful. In the main hall, he had parents to serve; at his knees, he had children to enjoy. Happiness <gloss>pronounced in the entering tone</gloss> was found right here. What benefit is there in seeking outward? 

He built a thatched hut in a secluded spot by South Lake. A stream flowed by his door, and ten thousand stalks <gloss>of bamboo</gloss> stood behind the house. Inside the room, scrolls were lined up in order. Upon his desk, he daily placed the *Records of the Grand Historian* <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>, the prose of Su Shi and Han Yu <gloss>the great masters of literature</gloss>, the poetry of Li Bai and Du Fu <gloss>from selected anthologies</gloss>, and the ink-paintings of various famous artists, which he would study and enjoy. 

Whenever he gained some insight, he would take up a fine brush and compose a poetic rhapsody. Having finished writing, he would lean against the pine or locust trees, watching and listening to the flowing water while pondering his word choices <term>qiaotui: literally "knocking or pushing," a reference to the meticulous editing of poetry</term>. He was so busy interacting with dancing butterflies, buzzing bees, wagtails, and pelicans that he had no time for anything else. When tired, he would remove his cap and go barefoot—

<column-break/>

<margin>
Immortality is found right here. Why must one shed their mortal coil to ascend to the clouds, or practice "refining the form" and "abstaining from grain" <note>bigu: a Daoist dietary practice of avoiding five-grains to achieve longevity</note> before being called an immortal?
</margin>

—barefoot, he would rest briefly within his "plum blossom" bed-curtains. Upon waking <gloss>pronounced 'jiao'</gloss>, he would leisurely <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss> sit on a bamboo couch. He would stir the stove and tend the fire, burn a piece or two of fine incense, drink half a bowl of bitter tea, and play a single tune on a "stringless zither" <note>A reference to the poet Tao Yuanming, implying a love for the spirit of music over the technicality of notes.</note>. 

Then he would step out into the courtyard to pick up fallen flowers or feed the goldfish. Occasionally encountering a fishing boat, he would discuss the joys <gloss>pronounced in the entering tone</gloss> of the rivers and lakes and the methods of catching fish, never tiring of such simple talk. Soon, as the setting sun filled the trees and the new moon hung over the eaves, he would order his young servant to roast garden bamboo shoots and cook radishes and beans. He would call his crane to join him; one dance, one drink. When slightly tipsy, he would slap his belly and sing to himself, walking dozens of steps in the moonlight. 

The fire served as his lamp. Opening the doors and windows, he would write a few lines for me, take a few more sips of bitter tea, roll up his scrolls, and lie down by candlelight. Body and spirit were both forgotten. As dawn approached, the melody of birds and the wind in the bamboo would wake him from his deep dream. He would lie there listening for a while, his heart and breath at ease, then slowly rise, put on his robes, and practice his breath-work and stretching <term>daoyin: ancient physical and breathing exercises similar to Qigong</term>. 

Once finished, he would attend to his inner cultivation <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss> by the window, washing and combing. At that moment, the tender silk-like willows would hang across the gate, and the new red flowers would seem to smile at him. The long stream was a quiet emerald, and the distant villas were a dense blue. It was almost...

<summary>This page describes the daily life and philosophy of Master Wu, a hermit who lived during the early Ming Dynasty. He finds spiritual "immortality" not through esoteric Daoist rituals, but through the simple pleasures of nature, family, poetry, and tea.</summary>
<keywords>Master Wu, Hongwu era, hermit life, Daoism, poetry (Li Bai, Du Fu), Daoyin, South Lake, immortality, simplicity</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Master Wu (the "Contented Immortal"). Having established his hermit-like life in his thatched hut by South Lake, the text now transitions from his daily routine to a supernatural encounter with two Daoist immortals who arrive to compose poetry with him.</meta>

<margin>
Alas, the people of the world! They chase the weary dust of carriages and horses and brave the bitter dangers of the rivers and lakes. How can they not bow their heads before Master Wu and feel a sense of shame?
</margin>

He was no longer aware that his body resided within the mortal world. One day, just as the rain on the lotuses had ceased and the wind began to stir the parasol trees, the Contented Immortal <note>The pseudonym of Master Wu.</note> felt a stirring in his heart. He began to hum a "Swallow Song": *Fallen leaves cover the lattice windows; the wind gathers floating clouds to mend the gaps in the wall.* 

As he finished the couplet, he suddenly saw a female Daoist. She wore a "Free-and-Easy" cap <term>逍遥巾 (Xiaoyao jin): a traditional headcloth worn by scholars and Daoists symbolizing a relaxed lifestyle</term> and a crane-feather cloak. Her clothing was unadorned yet naturally refined; her eyebrows were not painted yet were naturally charming. Moving with a composed <gloss>as above</gloss> manner, she stepped forward and asked: "Is the Contented Immortal happy <gloss>pronounced *le*</gloss>?" 

Master Wu recognized that she was no ordinary person. He bowed low with his head to the ground and led her inside to be seated, saying: "My humble thatched hut is crude and clumsy; why has your celestial carriage graced me with a visit?" 

The female Daoist sat cross-legged on the floor <gloss>seated on the ground</gloss> and said: "I and the Daoist of Perfect Clarity <gloss>a pseudonym; some say he traveled to Mount Kuaiji</gloss> have shared our fly-whisks atop the peaks of Yunmen, spread our robes beneath the Great Yu’s Cave, gathered the blossoms of the Orchid Pavilion, and washed in the currents of Mirror Lake. <gloss>These are all famous sites of scenic beauty in Zhejiang.</gloss> We were about to return our banners to the Kunlun and Tai mountains, but I saw you chanting here in your solitary seclusion. Thus, I have come to join you in the joy <gloss>pronounced *le*</gloss> of poetic harmony <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>."

Master Wu replied joyfully: "Your visit is a startling honor to my clumsy feet. Although I dare not race alongside noble steeds, if it is a matter of following your poetic sentiments, I shall endeavor to add my cry, like that of an autumn earthworm or a spring oriole." 

The female Daoist then untied a gourd from her back and tapped the ground with her finger, saying: "Daoist of Perfect Clarity, you may come and join the appreciation." As she spoke, an old Daoist appeared, wearing a yellow cap and rustic sandals. His hair and beard were white as frost, and he held a jade fly-whisk. He entered the door with a fluttering, ethereal grace, saying: "The Lady of Perfect Emptiness is already here!" 

He sat facing them with his arms around his knees and said to Master Wu: "Let us speak of our feelings through the scenery before us. Shall we take 'Late Autumn Village Joys' <gloss>pronounced *le*</gloss> as our theme?" 

They all agreed. The Daoist of Perfect Clarity was the first to lead with a verse:

<column-break/>

<margin>
If one carefully savors these twenty-one poems on "Late Autumn Village Joys," though they were produced during a single session of poetic harmony, they are in no way inferior to the works of Pi and Lu from the Tang Dynasty. Indeed, are they merely equal to Pi and Lu? <note>Refers to Pi Rixiu and Lu Guimeng, famous Tang poets known for their "harmonizing" poems written in response to one another.</note>
</margin>

> In the quietude of the wild path,
> The gate is reached only by deep waters.
> Trees by the creek host the mysterious birds,
> Flowers are pierced by the lazy, busy bees.
> It is hard to recognize those racing across the ferry,
> But along the banks, I occasionally meet a monk.
> The brushwood gate is usually never opened,
> Sealed away, piece by piece, by the wild clouds.

The Lady of Perfect Emptiness remarked: "Only when the body is at leisure is there true joy <gloss>le</gloss>." She then recited:

> Day after day, I lean against the vine-covered window,
> Where clouds break to reveal solitary peaks.
> Frost shines as the myriad leaves descend <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss>.
> The gate to the woods is closed, yet it draws out my inspiration <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>.
> I listen to the flute of the fields, played without a melody.
> My dreams are broken, though not by any warning,
> Leaving only a void...

<summary>This page describes a mystical encounter between Master Wu and two Daoist immortals, the Lady of Perfect Emptiness and the Daoist of Perfect Clarity, who visit his hut. The three engage in a "poetic harmony" session, composing verses on the theme of autumn village life and the joys of reclusion.</summary>

<keywords>Master Wu, Lady of Perfect Emptiness (Zhen Xu Jun), Daoist of Perfect Clarity (Liao Ran Dao Ren), Late Autumn Village Joys, Daoist Immortals, Ming Dynasty Poetry, South Lake, Pi Rixiu, Lu Guimeng</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Master Wu’s encounter with celestial visitors. Following the arrival of the female Daoist on the previous page, this section records a series of poems and dialogues shared between Master Wu (under the name Zidezi) and his guests, Zhenxujun and Liaoran Daoren, as they celebrate the virtues of a secluded, rustic life.</meta>

<column-break/>

<margin>
The preceding passage conveys the idea that there is no other plan or scheme within the dream.
</margin>

Sleeping, yet caught in Zhuang Zhou’s dream <note>original: "莊周夢"; referring to the famous parable of the philosopher Zhuangzi dreaming he was a butterfly</note>; by the window, the red wine’s effect has not yet faded <gloss>even tone</gloss>. Oxen and sheep sleep in the hazy grass; gulls and herons gather on the secluded shore. Resting wearily among the trees of the forest; leaning idly upon the pavilion over the water <gloss>a moment of peaceful self-contentment</gloss>. I study the subtle mysteries behind closed doors—the gates are shut, the brushwood entrance closed. 

**Zhenxujun** <note>original: "貞虛君", The Gentleman of Pure Emptiness</note> says: "Pears and beans hang ripe along the dike; the gate of thorns opens toward the temple. New rain fills the ponds; crows return carrying the sunset. Idle butterflies follow the children; I move flowers and learn to plant them. Do not mock one who embraces solitude; since ancient times, who has truly pitied talent?" <gloss>written with feeling</gloss>. 

**Zidezi** <note>original: "自得子", The Contented One, a pseudonym for Master Wu</note> says: "Strolling along with the old man from next door; the calls of secluded birds break the silence. A bamboo fence winds along the curving water; a reed-covered bank leads to a high bridge. To predict the year’s harvest, I look to the fields; to divine the winds, I ask the local folk songs. They sing that this year’s autumn harvest is good; everywhere there is joy in the village’s abundance." 

**Liaoran Daoren** <note>original: "了然道人", The Enlightened Daoist</note> says: "Once I arrive to pay my respects at this residence <gloss>referring to a place of visitation</gloss>, I find I have a natural affinity for these woods and springs <term>林泉 (Linquan): a common literary term for a hermit's retreat</term>. Green moss spreads in the daylight; green trees echo the chirping of cold cicadas <gloss>describing the current scene</gloss>. I visit recluses to pass the long days, seeking out hidden spots as the year <gloss>season</gloss> comes to an end. Tonight I shall be thoroughly drunk <gloss>intoxicated</gloss>, without need for the money at the head of my staff." 

**Zhenxujun** says: "The farming family has much leisurely charm."

<column-break/>

<margin>
Whether it is the literary refinement of Lu Ji’s "Awl and Hammer" or the miraculous rebirth of Lu, I believe it is nothing more than this.
</margin>

A jar <gloss>lamp</gloss> hangs upon the wall. 

**Zidezi** says: "I have found the joy of the hills and gardens; I seek out travels while the time is right. Having finished an old game of Go, I inspect the bamboo to carve new poems. The rain washes the autumn air, making it even lighter; the flying clouds of dusk arrive somewhat late. I linger by the fields and forget to return; the boat drifts along, though not out of madness." 

**Liaoran Daoren** says: "Lotus flowers fall along the banks; the autumn scenery is faint and thin. I sweep the path and move the playing-stones; I divide the sand and set up a fishing weir. A man with a hoe and a single straw hat; a sail sent off across the rainy lake. Everything that enters my sight becomes a subject for poetry; I cannot restrain <gloss>even tone</gloss> my idle thoughts <gloss>departing tone</gloss> from taking flight." 

**Zhenxujun** says: "Fortunately, my body is free of burdens; why would I need a carriage drawn by four horses? In the empty courtyard, I tame the broad-winged cranes; from the stone spring, I startle the fish. There is a temple where I can discuss sacred verses <gloss>Zen Buddhist sayings</gloss>, and though I have no neighbors, I can borrow books. Leisurely, I attain the natural spark of life; only now do I understand **Changju** <note>original: "長沮"; a famous ancient recluse from the Analects who preferred farming to the chaos of politics</note>." 

**Zidezi** says: "I have already understood the charms of the village; what harm is there in lying down..."

<margin>
The ancients said: "What is harder to forget than being under the flowers? What is better than a wine vessel?"
</margin>

The scholar’s cleverness is mocked by the spider mending its web; for sport, one leads the ants to find the bran. The sky is still, with clouds serving as a curtain; the hall is empty, and the man stays within. In a single cup, there are no other plans; let the world and its affairs remain a blur <gloss>drifting through the world with light unconcern</gloss>. 

**Liaoran Daoren** says...

Their verses celebrate the aesthetics of reclusion, the beauty of the autumn harvest, and the spiritual freedom found in a life unburdened by social status or political ambition.</summary>

Having established their retreat from the world on the previous page, they now engage in "Pure Conversation" (Qingyan), a traditional form of witty, philosophical dialogue, focusing on the aesthetic joys of their rustic, secluded lifestyle.</meta>

# Seven Sayings of Pure Conversation

In a remote and desolate village, the path is naturally open to all. Leaves twist and turn; the crickets' burrows are cold and damp, and the fallen swallows' nests are empty <gloss>all feeling resentful</gloss>. Do not say that the reds and purples of spring have vanished; the red maples ask <gloss>departing tone</gloss> the green paulownia trees about the changing seasons.

**Liaoran Daoren** <note>original: "了然道人"; The Insightful Daoist</note> says: "In these scattered, quiet places, the smoke swirls back; a winding 'small seclusion' <gloss>a small dwelling</gloss> is best. Golden oranges lean against the curved balustrade; the 'Cinnamon King' <note>likely referring to a large Osmanthus tree</note> leans against the empty steps. I watch the geese piercing the clouds until they are out of sight; exploring the banks <gloss>where one waters fruit trees and the wind and frost are mild; the rhythmic smartweed comforts the hidden heart.</gloss>"

**Zhenxujun** <note>original: "貞虛君"; The Gentleman of Pure Emptiness</note> says: "Where the path twists <gloss>curving and bending</gloss> and the bridge is broken; the fragrance of wild crabapples fills the cottage. In the quiet <gloss>quietly hanging</gloss> wind, 'scorpion-traces' appear on the wall <note>refers to cracks or patterns in a wall that resemble calligraphy</note>. The sun is high; the chickens peck at the <gloss>threshing</gloss> floor. I pity the broken branches that look like sparrow-umbrellas; I weave thorns together to protect the new bamboo shoots. Having no business to attend to, I labor only to open my scrolls; the sound of the wind <gloss>a sharp, resonant sound</gloss> fills the winding pond."

**Zidezi** <note>original: "自得子"; The Contented One, Master Wu’s pseudonym</note> says: "My obsession is the joy of village living; <gloss>each</gloss> seeking the spirit of the overgrown <gloss>departing tone</gloss> results in a bold heart. Opening the seal, I pour the <term>Green Ants</term> <note>original: "綠蟻"; a poetic name for unfiltered rice wine with floating bits of yeast</note>; rising, my fingers peel the <term>Yellow Mash</term> <note>original: "黃虀"; here referring to the succulent meat of a crab</note>. The sound of the laundry mallet in the moonlit forest is delicate; the chime of a wild stone gong meets the wind. Sleeping drunk, I achieve a solitary gain; while in the city, the crowds are surging in a chaotic tide."

**Liaoran Daoren** says: "Distinct and pure, I stay close to the kingfishers across the stream; chanting as I <term>Wash my Hat-Strings</term> <note>original: "濯纓"; a literary reference to the 'Canglang Song,' symbolizing a scholar's decision to withdraw from a corrupt world to live in purity</note> to cleanse myself."

<column-break/>

<margin>The three men call and respond to one another; every word depicts the scene; every word carries a sense of leisurely self-contentment.</margin>

Meeting by chance at the thatched hut; wild trees, wind, and mist merge together. Overgrown fences where vines intertwine; flowers of the village occasionally serve as garden stones, and bamboo shoots gathered at night become evening dishes. Silent, with no one to disturb us; we only allow the magpies to build their nests.

**Zidezi** says: "Elegant and refined, this is no world of dust; many should call this a hidden dwelling. The stream winds, making one suspect the water is possessed of a special spirit; the wilderness is vast, making one feel the sky is low. Craving simple flavors, I pick from the vegetable garden; greedy for fragrance, I watch the chrysanthemum plot. <term>Jicai</term> <note>original: "季才"; likely referring to the Jin dynasty scholar-general Liu Kun</note> had bold ambitions, but whether sitting or rising, I am now ashamed to hear the cock's crow <note>referring to the practice of 'rising at the cock's crow' to pursue a career; Zidezi prefers to sleep in</note>."

**Liaoran Daoren** says: "A villa is truly a place where one can hide; arriving here, wild emotions are many. I cut bamboo to build a new house and clear away thatch to patch the old thicket. The wind scents the chrysanthemums on the secluded steps; frost falls on the lotuses in the small creek. The autumn scenery is not purely desolate and lonely; the neighbors bring wine as they pass by <gloss>level tone</gloss>."

**Zhenxujun** says: "For a hidden heart, only you are superior; I wish to plan a place for planting hemp and mulberry. The land is remote, the autumn wind wails through the leaves; the forest is deep, and in the day, flowers fall. Pushing <gloss>level tone</gloss> the window, I startle the wild egrets; leaning on my pillow, I listen to the pond frogs. This moment is truly worthy of joy; the people of <term>Luoyang</term> <note>original: "洛"; the capital city, representing worldly status</note> <gloss>level tone</gloss> should know there is nothing left to boast of."

**Zidezi** says: "A dwelling with <term>Rope Hinges</term> <note>original: "繩樞"; a symbol of a humble, impoverished home</note> opens to a bamboo grove; the land is elegant and meets the secluded wind. The place is remote, the heart..."

<summary>This page records a "Pure Conversation" through poetry between Master Wu and two Daoist companions, celebrating the sensory details and spiritual freedom of their rustic seclusion. They contrast their simple joys—drinking wine, eating crabs, and watching nature—with the noisy, ambitious life of the city and the capital.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the narrative of Master Wu’s (Zidezi) encounter with the two mysterious figures, Liaoran Daoren and Zhenxujun. # A Myriad Selections of Pure Conversation
## Volume I
### Page 19

<margin>
Divine craftsmanship is in heaven and also not on earth; immortality lies in my heart alone. The ancients could be immortals in their hearts, so they did not care where they went, nor did they call it "walking upon the earth."
</margin>

Pulling fish to prepare a delicious dish of sliced raw meat <note>original: "鱠" (kuài); a dish of thinly sliced raw fish or meat</note>, frying rice and loving the fragrant stalks. <gloss>the day of Geng-chen</gloss> The butterflies in the meadow already know the path; the returning geese do not yet recognize the shelters. Village elders beneath the trees by the fence; facing one another, they have already forgotten worldly passions. To obtain the true joy of the village, Master Zhenxujun says: "A small villa hides human traces, specifically causing <gloss>level tone</gloss> secluded thoughts <gloss>departing tone</gloss> and high spirits to rise. Dense shade gathers fine grass; a secret fragrance steams from the drawn water. Watering new taro, moving the boat to pick wild water chestnuts. <gloss>describing the scene</gloss> The most captivating sights are in the misty dusk, with a few fishing lamps scattered across the opposite shore."

Master Zidezi <note>the host, Master Wu</note> says: "One need only find the interest within; village life is always joyful. There is a unique, self-attained pleasure that is hard to forget—drinking the 'Golden Dew' <note>a poetic name for fine wine or celestial nectar</note>, the 'Jade-Pot Sky' <note>a metaphor for a private, mystical world</note> never ends. The crickets know to pity me; the fish and geese have their own words to say. Stroking my beard, I break into a broad smile; who would envy an 'Earth-walking Immortal' <note>original: "地行仙"; a Daoist term for a low-ranking immortal who remains in the mortal world</note>?"

Regarding these three men, none relied on forced thinking, yet over twenty chapters were completed in an instant. They then clapped their hands in great delight, their physical forms and spirits both forgotten. Thus Master Wu said to his guests, "Though these lyrics are not yet refined, the scenery has been fully captured. Can today's gathering be called anything but excellent? May I keep these writings?" The guests simply nodded in agreement. As the feast concluded and thanks were given, Master Wu rose and brought tea—

<column-break/>

<margin>
The gait of his walk was like those who fly; reaching one hundred and nineteen years of age was entirely due to the accumulation of his flourishing vital energy.
</margin>

—but then he could not see them. Master Wu was astonished and helpless; he hurried to track their path. Where they had gone was empty and vast, with nothing to be found. Returning to the bridge at the lake, he only saw two cranes dancing across the sky, lingering and turning as if they were looking back. Thus he sighed and said, "It is so, it is so!"

He returned and mounted the poems and ink-wash calligraphy on scrolls, cherishing and admiring them daily. He truly believed he had encountered immortals. What he called "self-attainment" became even more profound. His study of self-attainment grew more authentic. Thereupon he ate sparingly to nourish his vital energy <term>Qi: the life force or breath</term>, discarded fame to seek leisure, and completely cut himself off from worldly affairs. 

After a long time, even amid the dust of the world, his gait was as if he were flying, and his complexion became more radiant. He spent his days wandering leisurely through the village villas, living until the age of one hundred and nineteen. When he passed, two cranes again flew and cried out as he died while sitting upright. Later, his descendants often showed the collection of poems to others, and thus many people have transmitted and recorded the story.

It emphasizes the Daoist theme that immortality is found through inner peace and "self-attainment" rather than external searching.</summary>

# An Li Transmits the Qin

<margin>
Yuan, the scholar, loved the zither.
Unfortunate not to be born
In the age of Bo Ya <note>Yu Bo Ya (c. 386–301 BC) was a legendary zither master whose music could only be fully understood by his friend Zhong Ziqi.</note>,
Yet how fortunate to accompany
A banished immortal of the Way.
What a blessing of years,
And what luck to receive
A master’s teaching on a moonlit night!
Suddenly, his brush captures
The spirit of a thousand years.
No longer sorrowful or 
Unrecognized by men,
He understands this encounter
Is no ordinary thing, but a path
Through the name of the Sage’s <note>Referring to Confucius.</note> teachings.
Indeed.
</margin>

Yuan Anli of Chengdu was a man who possessed the Way; he was skilled in chanting poetry and took great pleasure in the music of the zither and psaltery <term>qin and se: ancient Chinese stringed instruments often played together to symbolize harmony</term>. Many scholars of the Shu region <note>Modern-day Sichuan.</note> looked up to him and followed his example. 

In the final years of the Yanxing era of the Han dynasty <note>The final reign period of the Shu Han state, circa 263 AD.</note>, Anli was traveling on the road to Mount Qingcheng and took lodging in a village hut. That night, the moonlight was as bright as a painting. Anli took a walk in the nearby outskirts when he heard the faint, distant sound of someone playing the zither. He stood still to listen; the fingering technique was exquisitely marvelous. His heart filled with joy <gloss>happiness</gloss> and a deep sense of longing and admiration. 

He hurried toward the sound and saw lamps and candles shining brightly. It was a magnificent mansion. The halls were painted white, and books and scrolls lined the four walls. In the center of the room sat an old man with thick eyebrows and white hair <gloss>his appearance was ancient and venerable</gloss>. He wore a deep-sleeved robe and a headband <gloss>it is said he was dressed thus</gloss> while leaning over a desk to play the zither. Two young attendants stood below the steps: one held a wish-fulfilling jewel, and the other held a bundle of scrolls. Each attended to their duties and stood with great solemnity, as if in a formal government hall. Anli rushed in <gloss>acting</gloss> with haste <gloss>on impulse</gloss> and bowed deeply until his forehead touched the ground. He said: "I am but a humble man from Chengdu, yet I have a passionate love for elegant music."

<column-break/>

<margin>
The previous section concerned the cultivation of the self.
</margin>

"I have yet to receive the true transmission of the art. Hearing you play the zither just now, I feel immeasurably fortunate. Because of this, I have ventured to intrude upon you so abruptly. I prostrate myself and hope for your instruction <gloss>may you graciously grant this</gloss> <gloss>I shall repay you generously</gloss>. Furthermore, hearing your elegant performance just now, I could not contain my joy and happened to compose a short poem. I beg you to grant it your wise attention." 

The poem said:
*On a moonlit night in the empty mountains,*
*A guest sits by the stringed zither.*
*Carefully taking up the "Clear Shang" melody,* <note>The "Shang" mode in traditional Chinese music is often associated with autumn, strength, or a sense of melancholy.</note>
*He plays the sounds of high antiquity.*
*Deep among the flowers, the northern swallows chatter;*
*Amidst falling leaves, the monkeys of Chu wail.* <gloss>Describing the scene and the mood</gloss>
*Though Zhong Qi understood the music,* <gloss>Referring to the ancient listener who truly 'knew the sound' of the master Bo Ya</gloss>
*A thousand years have passed in silence,*
*Leaving the heart lonely.* <gloss>Lamenting the lack of those who truly understand</gloss>

The elder said: "I am but an old man of the forests and mountains, extremely rustic and unrefined <gloss>words of modesty</gloss>. I happened to play the zither merely to stir my spirits. I did not expect to be overheard by someone so diligent and knowledgeable. You are truly an expert in music! <gloss>Indeed, you understand the sound</gloss>. Stay, and I shall speak with you <gloss>the scholar discusses music</gloss>." 

He continued: "In the past, Emperor Shun played the five-stringed zither and sang the 'Poem of the South Wind,' and there was an atmosphere of harmony and joy. Confucius praised it <gloss>referring to the Shao music</gloss> as being perfectly beautiful and perfectly good <gloss>perfectly good</gloss>. When Zilu, a student of the Sage, played the rigid and forceful psaltery, he possessed the harsh spirit of the northern frontiers..."

<summary>This page recounts how the scholar Yuan Anli, traveling near Mount Qingcheng, follows the sound of a zither to find a mysterious, venerable elder. The two begin a dialogue on the spiritual and historical depth of music, referencing the sage-kings and Confucius.</summary>
<keywords>Yuan Anli, Guqin, Mount Qingcheng, Emperor Shun, Confucius, Bo Ya, Zhong Ziqi, music theory, Taoism, Han Dynasty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the story of Yuan Anli's miraculous meeting in the Shu region. Having entered the mysterious mansion, he now receives a lecture on the philosophy of music and direct instruction from a figure who is eventually revealed to be the legendary zither master, Bo Ya.</meta>

# The Master’s Teaching and the Vanishing Shrine

<margin>
Instruction for Ye Youzong:
The tone of one's writing reflects the intent of their zither playing.
That there are masters like Bo Ya <note>Yu Bo Ya, the semi-mythical zither virtuoso of the Spring and Autumn period (770–476 BC).</note> is no empty boast.
The Great Meaning exists beyond words,
just as Bo Ya found inspiration by the sea.
</margin>

Confucius once expressed a dislike for certain vulgar sounds; he even questioned why Zilu <note>One of Confucius's disciples, also known as Zhong You.</note> played his zither at the Sage’s own gate, implying the music lacked the proper spirit of restraint. Since the time of the Later Han dynasty, when the scholar Cai Yong rescued a piece of paulownia wood from beneath a cooking fire <term>Cuàn xià zhī tóng: famous wood used to make the "Scorched-Tail" zither</term> to craft a magnificent instrument, the techniques of the zither became increasingly refined. However, since those days, true masters have been few and far between. <gloss>Those who truly "understand the music" are rare.</gloss> 

While basic skills can be transmitted through words, the highest levels of mastery must be realized through the enlightenment of the heart. When the heart attains it, the fingers respond; once this is achieved, all other techniques can be mastered by extension. <gloss>The rest can be inferred by analogy.</gloss> The elder then taught Anli the methods of plucking the strings and demonstrated the wonders of the art. <gloss>He taught him personally.</gloss>

Soon, the bells rang and the ancient rooster crowed. The elder of the mountain village, seeing the first light of dawn approaching, composed a poem in pentasyllabic regulated verse to gift to Anli. The poem read:

“In this land of Shu <gloss>referring to the Kingdom of Shu</gloss>, where silkworms were first raised,
The three rivers appear before my eyes <gloss>right now</gloss>.
Gate towers and cities connect to distant forts,
And the border outposts command the scenic heights.
The sun sets over the lands of the southern tribes,
While the autumn wind blows through the White Emperor City.
Glistening clouds fly over the far reaches of the river,
And light rain dims the fragrant islands.
I once wrote of the thatched hut’s craftsmanship,
In the shrine where the Minister of State <gloss>Zhuge Liang, also known as Wuhou</gloss> is remembered.
Willows droop by the steps of the Lu River,
And flowers bloom by the banks of the Rang...”

<column-break/>

<margin>
Scholar Yuan received
the divine assistance
of Bo Ya. This was
truly a miraculous 
encounter of the age.
For Bo Ya to find 
Scholar Yuan and 
transmit his art 
was to find a soulmate 
after a thousand years.
</margin>

“...The Ten-Thousand Mile Bridge still stands to the west,
While the waters of the Three Ba regions flow on their own.
Rocks the size of fists hang from the cliffs,
As boats are hauled a hundred feet up the rapids.
The spray of the Salla <gloss>name of a river</gloss> rises higher than a horse,
And the Min and E mountains <gloss>mountain names</gloss> coil like dragons.
Apes wail in the woods of the mountain passes,
And the cuckoo cries out in the desolate wastes.
This remote land holds the ancient Zither Platform,
Deep in the mountains where the Sword Gate Pass lies in shadow.
Wild flowers offer themselves to the traveler’s mood,
While the river grass calls forth a sense of sorrow. <gloss>There is much melancholy in these lines.</gloss>
The envoy Sima once stayed his carriage here <note>Referring to the poet Sima Xiangru.</note>,
And I imagine the celestial raft floating once more <note>Referring to the explorer Zhang Qian.</note>.
The white clouds seem just as they were of old,
And the brocade-like trees remain forever.
It is as if I have climbed these heights before,
Wandering vaguely through the vast mists.
Unrolling this map, I heave a long sigh,
My spirits take flight toward the western corner <gloss>nook</gloss>.”

Anli listened to these teachings with his own ears and his heart was won over by the elder's excellence. He bowed twice and took his leave, believing he had experienced a rare and miraculous encounter. Suddenly, he looked back, but the elder and the young boy were nowhere to be seen. There was only the desolate sight of a ruined ancient shrine. Upon inquiring with the locals, he discovered it was the ancient Shrine of Bo Ya. From that moment on, Anli possessed divine assistance; his zither technique surpassed all others in the world, and his music was praised as a masterpiece beyond compare.

<summary>Yuan Anli receives a musical transmission and a commemorative poem from a mysterious elder who is revealed to be the ghost of the legendary master Bo Ya. This supernatural encounter establishes Anli as the greatest zither player of his time.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the "Scholar" archetype, moving from the symbolic imagery of the previous page into a specific literary anecdote involving the famous Yuan Dynasty poet Jie Xies Si. It illustrates the scholar’s ideal of the "literary encounter"—a serendipitous meeting between kindred spirits in a refined, secluded setting.</meta>

Miscellaneous Records of the Clear Waves, Volume 1

# Xies Si’s Literary Encounter

<margin>He is called a famous scholar because his literary talent and moral conduct were both high. If one's virtue is true, then both the "root" (character) and "branches" (works) are complete; he is truly an excellent gentleman.</margin>
During the Yuantong era <note>The Yuantong era (1333–1335) occurred during the reign of Toghon Temür, the last emperor of the Yuan Dynasty.</note>, Jie Xies Si <note>Jie Xies Si (1274–1344) was a prominent statesman and one of the "Four Masters of the Yuan" known for his poetry and calligraphy.</note> was a renowned man of letters. Initially recommended by his friend Cheng Jufu, he studied at the Imperial Academy to master <gloss>literary pursuits</gloss> his craft. His prose and moral conduct <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss> were the highest of his generation, and scholars everywhere vied to admire him. Those who arrived at his gate to request poems or essays were counted by the hundreds daily. One day, he went to visit a friend and traveled about twenty *li* <note>A *li* is a traditional Chinese unit of distance, roughly equivalent to 500 meters or one-third of a mile.</note> outside the capital. His travels lasted ten days, and it was evening by the time he began his return. At that time, the clouds were gloomy and the night was pitch black. Looking in all four directions, he felt lost and did not know where to go. His horse and servant were exhausted, which only added to his hesitation and <gloss>fear</gloss> anxiety. In the distance, among a thin grove of trees, he caught a glimpse of a faint light. Jie thought it must be a residence and hurried toward it. When he arrived, he found a magnificent house of several rafters with two bright candles burning; it was a grand estate. Jie was overjoyed beyond his expectations. Just as he was about to request an audience, a young boy suddenly emerged from within. Jie said to him: "I am a commoner from Shandong, a <gloss>scholar in plain cloth</gloss> <term>poor scholar</term>. On my way back from visiting a friend at dusk, I lost my <gloss>road</gloss> way. I seek a place to stay the night; would that be possible?" The boy gave his assent.

<column-break/>

<margin>Sitting upright in his cap and sash, his elegant appearance showed that even though he was young, he was the master of the house.</margin>
After a short <gloss>moment</gloss> while, the boy returned with an invitation to enter. Jie straightened his robes and followed the boy inside. There he saw elegant windows, vermilion gates, and a collection of <gloss>contained within</gloss> zithers, chess sets, books, and paintings. A rare fragrance filled the air, and the entire setting was exquisitely arranged. In the central hall, a man sat perfectly upright wearing a high cap and a broad belt; his appearance was dignified and awe-inspiring. Seeing Xies Si arrive, he rose and said: "I live in this remote and humble place, dwelling among wood and stone and wandering with the deer and swine. I never expected a guest to visit, yet you have favored me with your presence. This must be a <term>connection from three past lives</term> <note>original: "三生有縁" (sān shēng yǒu yuán). A Buddhist-influenced concept suggesting that a chance meeting in the present is the result of karma from three previous incarnations.</note>." He then ordered the servant boy to set out wine. They were near a stream where the fish were fat, and the wine brewed from the spring water was clear and fragrant. The host then said to the Scholar Jie: "You are a man of letters. I live a carefree and idle life and have composed a <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss> poem; I dare ask for your correction." His first poem read: "I selected this site to build my porch and railing, / My heart opens, and joy naturally arises. / The moss condenses into a tender green, / The forest leaves shed a light red. / The moon shines bright on the edges of the eaves, / The spring flows clear through the rock crevices. / Since my secluded interest is sufficient, / I never cared for the pursuit of fame." His second poem began: "Before getting old..."

<summary>This page recounts a legendary encounter where the famous Yuan scholar Jie Xies Si gets lost at night and finds refuge in a mysterious, refined estate. The story emphasizes the "literary encounter" (wenyu), where the hospitality of a mysterious host is centered around shared poetic sensibilities and the beauty of a secluded life.</summary>
<keywords>Jie Xies Si, Yuan Dynasty, literati culture, hospitality, sān shēng yǒu yuán (karmic connection), nature poetry, Cheng Jufu</keywords>

# Ten Thousand Selections of Pure Conversation, Volume 1

<margin>
First, it is said that when fame and success are achieved, one can preserve a good reputation. It is truly believed that misfortune does not spring from wealth alone.
Second, it is told that Little Tianhong laughed and said: "My surname is Xu, and for generations we have been called 'Scholars.' Your fame and success will be far-reaching, beyond what our kind can reach. In the future, you will surely become a high minister to the Son of Heaven. How can you let a temporary grief dampen your aspirations?" Later, the man indeed achieved great things; he prepared offerings of meat and wine for a sacrifice before departing.
</margin>

**The Seventh Verse:** 
The gate is closed against the green moss, purely secluded without a single speck of dust. In leisure, I only delight <gloss>pronounced *le*</gloss> in the Way; often ill, I hold a deep pity for talent. Bamboo always pierces the fence, leading the way; flowers are often "taxed" from the earth for planting <note>A poetic way of saying he has claimed a small plot of land for his garden.</note>. A single flute carries me on an evening ride; the wandering knights of old no longer come. 

**The Eighth Verse:** 
Withdrawing to live beneath a simple gate, lingering in freedom. Plum blossoms hang over a small canopy; lotus leaves float like a light boat. My career gradually becomes like the singing phoenix <gloss>My life is entrusted to the scholar's whisk; why should there be another "clear stream" outside of the master?</gloss> 

**The Ninth Verse:**
<margin>Mr. Zhang says: With the brush-tube closed, I sing long songs, drunk then waking again. Grass grows by Yuanliang’s well; lotus covers Ziyun’s pavilion.</margin>
All day long, the brush-tube is closed; singing long songs, drunk then waking again. Grass grows by Yuanliang’s <note>Tao Yuanliang, better known as the poet Tao Yuanming (365–427), the quintessential Chinese recluse-poet.</note> well; lotus covers Ziyun’s <note>Yang Ziyun, the philosopher Yang Xiong (53 BCE – 18 CE), known for his humble lifestyle.</note> pavilion. At midnight, the lute's tuning pegs are like autumn gold; the ink-stone screen is cast. I turn all the memories of old travels into a single smile, as the evening mountains turn blue.

**The Tenth Verse:** 
Leisure has become a habit; I am eccentric and unwilling to follow the crowd. In the deep forest, there is much green moss; in this remote place, there is little "red dust" <note>A common Buddhist and Daoist metaphor for the distractions and filth of the material, secular world.</note>. The wine is served slowly, frequently detaining guests; when a poem is finished, I envy those who can leave the table. Endless joy in the clear breeze, the wine jug...

In the future, you will surely become a high minister to the Son of Heaven. How can you let a temporary grief dampen your aspirations?" Later, the man indeed achieved great things; he prepared offerings of meat and wine for a sacrifice before departing.

<column-break/>

<margin>Ten Verses on Leisurely Living <gloss>The responsive couplet says: Finding interest in the quiet gate is a great benefit; one who attains this will surely advance in officialdom.</gloss></margin>

**The Second Verse:**
Stealing leisure in a small study of a few rooms. Pouring from a shell cup, wearing a purple cap and garments of spotted deer skin. Leaning on a table, I hear the crying birds; hooking back the curtain, I see distant mountains. As autumn arrives, there is not a single chore; my robes and pendants are all scented with orchid.

**The Third Verse:** 
The whole world is in a long, drunken stupor; who can ask how to stay sober <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss> alone? No "phoenix" character <note>A reference to an anecdote where the character for "phoenix" (*feng*) was written on a gate to mock the host as a "common bird" (*fan niao*).</note> is written on the gate; my bag contains the "Sutra of the Illuminated Embankment." Once planted, the flowers form a brocade; moved here, they serve as a screen. In leisure, they provide a sight to enjoy; meeting them eye to eye, they seem particularly "blue" <note>To look with "blue eyes" (as opposed to the whites of the eyes) is a classical idiom for showing favor or friendship.</note>.

**The Fourth Verse:** 
A single bed is cleaner than <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss> if washed; secluded <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss> and not among the crowd. Purple moss grows everywhere I walk; yellow birds are heard while I sit. The lute plays the moon through the pine branches; the stone is struck by the clouds. The water in the cattail pond is newly warm; falling flowers cover the light ripples.

**The Fifth Verse:** 
Knowing for myself that purity is a delight, who says the mundane can defile? Spring water boils the tea from Jianxi; incense smoke forms clouds like Mount Bo <note>Refers to a "Boshan" censer, an incense burner shaped like a legendary mountain.</note>. In the cold moon, the soul of the plum blossom is thin; in the clear wind, the crane’s dream is solitary. Recently, I have become very sparse and lazy; I have no desire at all for the official "cap and pins" <term>zanzu: the ceremonial headwear and fastenings that represent a career in the imperial bureaucracy</term>.

**The Sixth Verse:** 
Clamor and confusion compete with one another, but my heart opens as if I do not hear. My calligraphy follows Wang Yishao <note>Wang Xizhi (303–361), the "Sage of Calligraphy."</note>; my poetry matches the excellence of Bao Canjun <note>Bao Zhao (c. 414–466), a famous poet of the Southern Dynasties.</note>. Beating the tea-drum among the bamboo; overturning the wine jug before the flowers. Ji Sheng <gloss>Ji Kang</gloss> <note>Ji Kang (223–262), a philosopher and musician who was one of the "Seven Sages of the Bamboo Grove."</note> was naturally of noble character; how could he possibly stay among a "flock of chickens" <note>A metaphor for a person of superior talent among ordinary people.</note>?

It emphasizes themes of seclusion, calligraphy, tea culture, and the rejection of worldly ambitions in favor of nature and ancient scholarship.</summary>

The waves are still and the woods are calm; ten thousand acres of lake-light fall within a single gaze <note>The original phrase "歸指顧" (guī zhǐ gù) literally means "returns to a point and a glance," suggesting that the vast landscape is easily surveyed or commanded by the viewer's eyes.</note>.

# Xisheng Meets Lin

Yan Xisheng of Siming was a great scholar. At the end of the <term>Xianchun era</term><note>1265–1274 AD, the final years of the Southern Song Dynasty.</note>, he traveled to the capital of Hangzhou for his studies and resided outside the Qiantang Gate. One day, during the Mid-Autumn Festival, a heavy white dew lay across the river and the light of the moon met the sky. The waves were still and the woods stood open across ten thousand acres of emerald water <gloss>the light of the lake</gloss>. Yan took a boat to enjoy the lake and stayed for several nights. He sang loudly beneath the flowers, "A jug of wine among the flowers, drinking alone without a companion" <gloss>A poem by Li Bai</gloss>. Drifting along in self-contentment, he knew not where to stop. Thinking back on the noble character of <term>Lin Hejing</term><note>Lin Bu (967–1028), a famous hermit-poet of West Lake known for his "plum wife and crane children."</note>, he composed two <term>regulated verses</term><note>A strict eight-line poetic form.</note> while in the boat to mourn him. 

The first said: 
"He fled to the solitude of <term>Gu Hill</term><note>Solitary Hill on West Lake.</note> <gloss>Inside West Lake</gloss>, 
Idly spending his body as the years were forgotten. 
The peaks and ponds cast empty mountain shadows, 
While the cool water rises toward the quiet pavilion. 
Rain moistens the slow strings of the <term>qin</term><note>A seven-stringed zither.</note>, 
And the wind carries the scent of the medicine mortar. 
The hermit's pure delights were as fine as his calligraphy <gloss>level tone</gloss>, 
With never a worry reaching the halls of the court."

The second said: 
"The wandering heroes who pass by <gloss>level tone</gloss> 
Are fewer now <gloss>level tone</gloss>, and the gate is always closed. 
The scent of grass invades the green of the seat, 
And mountain colors rise into the blue of the tower. 
In his rustic robes..."

<column-break/>

"...cutting lotus and water-caltrop, the garden lies wild <gloss>level tone</gloss>, 
While he boils <term>fu ling</term><note>A medicinal fungus (Poria cocos) often eaten by Taoist practitioners.</note>. 
Residing far beyond the dusty markets, 
His home is no less grand than the Pavilion of the Summoned Gentleman." 

Before his chanting had ended, he suddenly saw through the mist and dew a boat slowly approaching. A man wearing a silk cap and holding a feather fan appeared, possessing an elegant and leisurely grace. As the graceful vessel approached from the west, the prows of the two boats were linked. 

"May we travel together?" the figure asked. 

Yan's expression did not waver. With a calm <gloss>level tone</gloss> and composed manner, he bowed from his boat and said, "Sir, you are peerless in this world. That I might now travel with your spirit is truly a stroke of fortunate beauty." 

Lin smiled and said, "I am a man who said his farewells long ago; I have been dust and earth for a great while. I am fortunate that you do not despise me and allow me to join your excursion. This is truly one of the great and fine encounters <gloss>meaning a meeting</gloss> of this clear night." 

Yan did not wish to fall short of the occasion, and requested that they each compose a poem of one stanza. 

Yan thanked him, saying, "Sir, you are like a miraculous steed of extraordinary speed, surpassing the whole herd. I am but a small, dull jade; how could I dare to follow in the dust of your footsteps?" 

Lin laughed and said, "You are mistaken <gloss>deluded</gloss>. He is a man, and I am a man; why should I fear him? What kind of man was the Sage <term>Shun</term><note>A legendary ethical ruler of ancient China.</note>? And what kind of man am I? He who exerts himself will also be like him."

<summary>This page recounts a mystical encounter on West Lake between the scholar Yan Xisheng and the spirit of the Song dynasty hermit Lin Bu. After Yan composes poems in Lin's honor, the two share a boat and discuss the pursuit of sagehood and literary excellence.</summary>
<keywords>Yan Xisheng, Lin Hejing, Lin Bu, West Lake, Gu Hill, poetry, Southern Song Dynasty, Qiantang Gate, Shun, Fu Ling</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative encounter between the scholar Yan Xisheng and the spirit of the poet Lin Bu on West Lake. After their initial meeting, they engage in a poetic dialogue—a traditional "matching of verses"—as they drift through the moonlit night.</meta>

<margin>
Though Lin’s poems possess a living spirit, from this we know that he is not like the wood of a tree, which must inevitably rot and decay.
</margin>

"This humble self is indeed one who shall rot and decay alongside the plants and trees; why should I fear? <note>Lin Bu is reassuring Yan that although he is a spirit, there is no cause for alarm.</note> Please, do not persist in your polite refusals and waste this fine encounter." Yan thanked him again, and findng he had no choice, he was the first to chant:

> I bought a small boat, steady and sure,
> Much like the famed vessel of the Zhu family. <gloss>self-contentment</gloss>
> I sit in my thatched-roof cabin to watch the mountains,
> And sleep in the pillowed tower to wait for the moon.
> The gulls and egrets find particular delight here,
> While fish and shrimp are caught without thought of cost.
> The south wind has been kind these ten days,
> No need to weary oneself with the heavy tow-rope.

Lin then chanted:

> Departing, departing for the imperial capital,
> A boat laden to the brim with maps and books.
> Red maples line the path to the water-station,
> White birds vanish into the river mist.
> At dawn, the station officials beat the morning drums,
> At night, the fishermen’s songs tap against the hull.
> In these prosperous times, an official’s life is pleasant;
> How is it any different from becoming an immortal?

Yan chanted again:

> Autumn waters are clearer than <gloss>unstained</gloss> silk;
> How fitting to be in this single-leaf skiff.
> Boat-waves and light daze the grassy pavilion,
> Mountain colors reach the thatched window.
> A sudden drizzle falls on me in my solitude,
> Through thin mist, a pair of egrets descends.
> These traveler’s thoughts are truly desolate;
> With whom shall I overturn the wine jar?

Lin chanted:

> I lift my head toward the road to Chang'an, <note>Chang'an here serves as a poetic stand-in for the capital city.</note>
> How many days’ journey for this solitary boat?
> Clouds over the mounds confuse the traveler's dreams,
> The river moon shines upon the grief of parting.
> Distant waters meet the white of the sky,
> The High River <note>The Milky Way.</note> lets down its dew...

<column-break/>

<margin>
One person on a moonlit night, one chanting and one responding; how could one know that life and death follow such different paths?
</margin>

The clarity was almost unbearable as the fine night lingered on. Crickets and cicadas chirped close to the men. <gloss>Yan chanted:</gloss>

> A solitary crane pulls my boat along,
> As white dew floats across the horizontal river.
> The sky is vast, the autumn waters wide,
> The clouds clear as the evening glow recedes.
> A measure of wine offers a brief consolation;
> These rivers and mountains are enough for a grand journey.
> The moon tonight through the thatched window
> Should be just like the one seen at Huangzhou. <gloss>A reference to Su Dongpo's famous excursions to the Red Cliff; spoken in a lower tone.</gloss>

His heart felt naturally expansive, as if the green mountains themselves should give way. Lin chanted:

> As dusk falls, I push open the cabin window and sit. <gloss>lower tone</gloss>
> The station is small; white egrets dot the waves like circles.
> In the vast wilderness, the wind creates a natural music;
> On the clear river, the moon draws near the boat.
> The night grows late, and I am entirely without sleep;
> Time and again, I wish to take an immortal by the arm and fly away.

The two men continued to respond to one another <gloss>departing tone</gloss>, each following his own inspiration. After a while, they washed their cups and drank once more, eventually falling asleep pillowed against each other in the boat, unaware that the east was already turning white with dawn. When Yan rose and looked around, Lin’s whereabouts were lost to him. Yan was both startled and saddened; from afar in the boat, he bowed toward Lin’s tomb and departed. From that point on, Yan’s literary achievements and character improved greatly, and his fame spread far and wide. He was eventually promoted to the position of Collator in the <term>Secretarial Archive</term> <note>Bishu Ge: The imperial library and archive of the Southern Song Dynasty.</note>. This story comes from the book he authored, titled *Meng Lin Lu* (Records of the Dream of the Forest).

<vocab>Meng Lin Lu, Yan, Lin, Chanting in response, Small boat, Zhu's Boat, Excursion to the Red Cliff, Collator of the Secretarial Archive</vocab>

->The moon shines bright over Baixia; beside the Qinhuai Bridge, the waters flow leisurely by.<-
<note>original: "月明白下秦淮橋畔水悠游". Baixia is an ancient name for the city of Nanjing. The Qinhuai River, which flows past its famous bridges, was the cultural heart of the city, renowned for its nightlife, literature, and scholarly gatherings.</note>

## The Opposite Shore

->Spring fills the Southlands; before the Empty Valley Temple, the flowers bloom in brilliant profusion.<-

<meta>This page continues the theme of supernatural encounters through poetry. While the previous page featured a dialogue between a living scholar and a famous historical spirit, this page documents a contemporary encounter (for the author) between a merchant-poet and the ghost of a drowned youth near the city of Nanjing.</meta>

# Hu Yicheng Listens to the Chanting

Hu Yicheng, a merchant from Shaanxi, traveled to Jinling <note>original: "金陵", a historical name for Nanjing</note> for trade. One night, he anchored his boat mid-journey. <gloss>Tapping the side of the boat, he chanted to himself. Though Master Hu was a merchant, he was remarkably talented in poetry; his rhymes were elegant, and he possessed the refined air of a handsome youth.</gloss> The light rain had just cleared, and thin clouds veiled the moon. Hu tapped the side of his boat and chanted:

> At dusk, I reach Fushan Post, its purple gates spread wide;
> Drinking in the evening glow, as the setting sun tangles in the tall pines.
> The leaves are dark, the grass lush, and the day-lilies sparse;
> A startled dog barks at a stranger, while crows fly wing-to-wing.
> They roost, then startle once more—
> Loneliness reigns outside the hedge, as the lamp light slowly dims.

As he finished his poem and was about to sleep, he suddenly heard someone chanting from the opposite shore:

> Jinling, a land of beauty, its scenery ever-changing;
> The city walls are strong as molten gold, the rivers and mountains like an unrolling scroll.
> An evening breeze carries a flute from the tower; spring waters lap the ferry boat.
> Melancholy fills the places once visited; how many years have passed since then?

Hu assumed it was another poet among the merchants and was not overly alarmed. He chanted again:

> Since ancient times, a land where kings arose; its prosperity more than enough to boast.
> Famous is Fighting Goose Lane, though the alley is small; old is the distance of Horse Pasture Square.
> The young children ride bamboo horses in spring; the gardeners...

<column-break/>

<margin>Peering through a gap in the boat's awning, he saw a figure standing still in the shade of the pines; then he knew it was no human being. What must Master Hu have been feeling in his heart at that moment?</margin>

...sell flowers in the late afternoon. The palaces of the Han Dynasty are magnificent; the kingly aura gathers like clouds and mist.

From the opposite shore, the voice chanted again:

> Remembering the lands of Jiangdong; the scenery is truly lovely.
> The Yu Gate <note>A reference to high-ranking official residences or the prominent Yu family of the Jin Dynasty</note> is as deep as the sea; the imperial road as straight as a string.
> The courtesans wear hairpins of swallow-design; children fly their paper kites.
> Since leaving, I have always looked back; on what day shall I compose my "Return to the Fields"? <note>A poetic trope for retiring from public life to the countryside.</note>

Hu hid himself, peeking through a gap in the boat's awning. There, he saw a beautiful young man standing still beneath the shade of the pines. Hu thought to himself, "In the middle of such a quiet night, how could anyone be here? If it is not a ghost, it must be a demon." He then extinguished his candle, leaned against his small table, and lay down. He heard the voice chanting once more:

> Jianye <gloss>Jinling</gloss> is a place for a brief sojourn; wandering into dreams, it feels so distant.
> The moon hangs beside the Qinhuai Bridge; flowers bloom before the Empty Valley Temple.
> Silver candles light the market-place; blue banners <gloss>curtains marking a wine shop</gloss> wave for the taverns.
> In times of peace, the people are joyful; the sound of flutes and drums marks the passing years.

When the chanting ended, there was a long sigh, and the figure entered the water and vanished. Hu was greatly amazed. The next day, he inquired among the local residents. They told him, "This was a talented young scholar who drowned here while intoxicated. His noble spirit appears from time to time."

<summary>A merchant named Hu Yicheng engages in a poetic call-and-response with a mysterious figure across a river, only to discover the stranger is the ghost of a drowned scholar.</summary>

<meta>This page begins a biographical narrative concerning the scholar Bei Qiong and his close friend Tan Jing. It establishes their lifelong bond and their shared disappointment after failing the imperial examinations, leading to a reflective journey at West Lake.</meta>

# Bei Qiong Meets an Old Friend

<margin>
Master Bei was renowned from a young age for his great talent and reputation. He entered the Imperial Academy and served in the History Office; his career was brilliant and radiant. The local histories, village monuments, and books on filial sons all agree on this; it is no exaggeration. Though his songs may be attributed to "Bei," his conduct and character are certainly well-known.
</margin>

Bei Qiong of Chongde, Zhejiang, was a man of the Hongwu era <note>The reign of the first Ming Emperor, 1368–1398</note>. He was a friend of Tan Jing; their families had been neighbors for generations, both continuing the profession of Confucian scholarship. Thus, Qiong and Jing played together as children, studied together as youths, and as adults, they shared the same "inkstone and brush" <note>meaning they were classmates or writing partners</note>. Their bond of friendship was stronger than "metal and orchids" <note>an idiom for a friendship so firm it can cut metal and is as fragrant as orchids</note>; they were sworn brothers ready to die for one another. People of the time considered them the reincarnation of Guan and Bao <note>Guan Zhong and Bao Shuya, China's most famous historical pair of friends</note>, a rare sight indeed. Furthermore, they were fond of surveying the world with a critical eye, judging the figures of the past and present. Though they hid their emotions within poetry and wine, they both possessed great and noble ambitions. During the decline and chaos of the late Yuan dynasty, they lived in seclusion and taught students. When our **Holy Ancestor** <note>The founding Ming Emperor, Zhu Yuanzhang</note> opened the civil service examinations, the two men first began to dream of being clothed in the robes of a "Great Peace" <note>meaning serving in a stable, prosperous government</note>. That year, they "battled in the North" <note>competed</note> at the Autumn Exams <note>The provincial level examinations</note> but failed to pass. Their spirits were heavy and unhappy. Consequently, they set a boat adrift on West Lake to dispel their gloom. The "red fragrance" <note>scent of lotus blossoms</note> clung to their robes, and lush greenery greeted their eyes. Fishing songs accompanied their wine, and water birds welcomed them. Hand in hand, they went toward the Willow Pavilion...

<column-break/>

<margin>
Looking at the majestic landscape and sighing deeply—though they had the high-mindedness to "cast off their shoes" <note>abandon worldly status</note> for fame. It is also said: "How much light and shadow remains?" This reflects the ancient sentiment of "holding a candle to travel at night" <note>the idea that life is short, so one should enjoy it while possible</note>.
</margin>

...at the pass, grieving for the trees of the Su Causeway, mourning the refined character of Lin Bu <note>a famous hermit-poet of the Song dynasty</note> at Solitary Mountain, and grieving for the loyal soul of Yue Wumu <note>The patriotic general Yue Fei</note> at the same location. <gloss>General Yue Fei</gloss> They watched the flying clouds of the Three Tianzhu Temples, <gloss>The Three Tianzhu Temples</gloss> <gloss>washing in the rapid shallows of the Six Dynasties</gloss> <gloss>fluttering and drifting</gloss>. 

Qiong felt a sense of "rising into the void" <note>a feeling of spiritual transcendence</note>, while Tan sighed deeply and said: "<gloss>A great man</gloss>... our physical bodies are instead made fools of by material things. I wonder, how much time do we have? Half a life spent in mindless toil, busy for nothing amidst worries. One does not realize that if you look at a human life from the perspective of Heaven and Earth, how is it different from "heroes" fighting on the tip of a snail’s horn, or "battling wits" inside an ant’s nest? <note>Classical metaphors for the insignificance of human struggles compared to the vastness of the universe</note>. How tiny and small it is! Is it not lamentable!" He then took up a large wine cup and sang two quatrains with a great voice:

*Holding my scrolls, I traveled to the Dragon Gate for years,*
*Only now do I regret seeking a noble title.*
*The years have flowed away, leaving only the lake water;*
*Since ancient times, how many heroes have reached the end?*

*With visiting cards in my sleeve, untendered, the world’s paths are hard,*
*Amidst the dust of fame and merit, an old man’s face appears.*
*The Tomb of Yue—<gloss>Yue Fei's Tomb</gloss>—challenges Solitary Mountain's pride;*
*Is the "Pavilion of Peace" <note>Hejing Pavilion, associated with the hermit Lin Bu</note> with its sheathed sword any better than the leisure of "releasing the cranes"?*

Bei Qiong saw the tragedy in his poem and the strangeness in his tone. He immediately composed a melody to comfort him, but in secret, he felt very uneasy about it.

<summary>This page introduces the scholars Bei Qiong and Tan Jing, detailing their deep friendship and their shared despair after failing the imperial examinations. While visiting Hangzhou's West Lake, Tan Jing expresses a profound sense of world-weariness and the futility of seeking political fame, troubling his friend Bei Qiong.</summary>

# The Supernatural Reunion at Purple Micro Mountain

<margin>
Master Bei <note>original: 貝公, Bei Gong</note>, whose name was Mingzhong, was a man of great repute. He was a student of the Grand Duke Sun. He was summoned for his great integrity to serve as an official; his reputation remained consistent throughout his life.
</margin>

Upon reaching home, Tan eventually died of illness. Although Bei mourned him deeply with great sorrow, he ultimately resigned himself to the workings of the Creator. As the autumn winds repeatedly changed and the spring grass grew anew, he looked upon the scenery and felt the lingering emotions one has for an old friend.

While Bei happened to be staying for business at the foot of **Purple Micro Mountain** in Xiashi, the rain cleared one day. He saw the western hills casting a vibrant blue-green hue and the waters reflecting a lush green, like a painting. He immediately called for his young servant to carry wine and began to ascend the mountain. After walking only a few steps, he heard the sounds of shouting and clearing the way <note>The "he-he" sounds made by attendants to clear the path for an official</note> approaching. A grand procession <term>zouzong|騶從 — the mounted attendants and guards of a high official</term> appeared, displaying a magnificent array of ceremonial guards.

Bei assumed it was a high-ranking official from the prefecture and quickly stood to the side of the road under the eaves of a gate to wait for them to pass. When the carriage arrived, the face of the person inside looked exactly like Master Tan <note>original: 談生, Tan Sheng</note>. Bei was shocked and bewildered; he used his fan to cover half his face and peeked out cautiously. The person in the carriage shouted, "Old friend, we have been apart so long! I regret that we cannot stop to converse, yet you hide your face to cut me off? Is that not going too far?"

Flustered, Bei did not know how to respond. Tan immediately descended from the carriage and grasped his hand, saying, "In my life, I failed you by leaving forever; in death, I harbor the desire to repay your 

<column-break/>

<margin>
Traveling by Ge Hong’s well, sitting on the stones as if seeing him before my eyes. Recalling the poems of our parting, the dust of our travels—one truly cannot know how life and death divide us.
</margin>

sincerity. A thousand apologies! I cannot speak fully here on the road. Please, let us climb the mountain together and speak of what is in my heart." Hearing these words, Bei finally believed it was truly Tan. He complied and followed. Tan ordered his attendants to leave <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>.

Together with Bei, he walked up the mountain. By the side of **Ge Hong’s Well** <note>A site named after the famous 4th-century Taoist alchemist Ge Hong</note>, they sat on stones and drank joyfully, chatting and laughing just as they had in life. 

Bei said to Tan, "I remember when we were officials at the Lake Bridge; it feels like yesterday. But now, you are surrounded by horses and carriages, while I am still wading through the mud of the mundane world. The distance between us is now as far as heaven and earth. Might you tell an old friend how you attained this state?" 

Tan replied, "I had intended to end my days in seclusion at Lake Quan, but unexpectedly, my fate was such that the Supreme Emperor <note>The high deity of the celestial bureaucracy</note> secretly selected me. After my coffin was closed, I was decreed the **Chief Inspecting Commander** <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss> of your district. Although I am lonely, I am fortunate to hold a pure and important office. Your mourning for me provides me with some comfort."

Bei asked again, "May I hear about the duties of your office? Are they complex or simple? Does your heart and body feel weary or at ease?" 

Tan replied, "As for the duties I humbly hold..."
