<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Master Bei Qiong and the spirit of his friend, Tan Jing. Tan, now a celestial official in the afterlife, explains the administrative structure and the absolute justice of the underworld.</meta>

<margin>
If those who govern the Underworld offices have no need to flatter or accept bribes like this, then is it only in the grand and bright world of the living that we suffer the sorrow of "midnight deals" and open corruption? <note>The "sorrow of midnight" refers to secret bribes and backroom deals.</note>
</margin>

My rank is one level above a **City God** <term>chenghuang|城隍 — a tutelary deity who protects a specific city and judges the local dead</term>. Whether the work is complex or simple is hard to predict, but the good and evil of an entire prefecture are recorded here. As for the appointment of officials and the granting of titles, we only value a single category: the selection of the virtuous. It is not like the mortal world, where positions can be obtained through influence <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss> or profit. Therefore, there is no anxiety over the hope of promotion, nor any schemes of solicitation. The habit of flattery is never practiced, and private gains from bribery <term>baoju|苞苴 — literally "wrapped in straw," a classical term for a hidden bribe</term> are never obtained. Although the position is a heavy responsibility, the duties are quite easy <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>; beyond public business, there is nothing else to attend to. What toil could there be?

Bei then asked: "Although the laws of the Underworld are strict, in a prefecture of ten thousand different circumstances, if one wishes to hold people accountable using only your own eyes and ears, your wisdom might find it difficult to distinguish everything. What method do you truly use to investigate?"

Tan replied: "The laws of the Underworld are indeed strict. Great criminals and those of extreme evil are abandoned by the masses. As for other matters such as deceit or financial ruin, the gods of the **Five Sacrifices** <note>The "Five Sacrifices" (wusi) refer to the household deities of the door, well, stove, path, and hall who observe daily behavior.</note> report monthly to the Celestial Emperor. Patrol officers report periodically to the supervisory officials. We weigh the law and examine the circumstances; the calamity that follows matches the crime exactly. As for minor flaws and small faults, we occasionally grant pardons."

Bei then asked: "But what about retribution in the living world?

<column-break/>

<margin>
These words point out that the laws of the Underworld are established without the slightest error, which is enough to make the souls of those who failed to embezzle give way and shatter the courage of the treacherous.
</margin>

It seems there are still things that are not quite consistent? Why is that?" 

Tan smiled again and said: "The laws of the Underworld are established without the slightest error. Let me explain it so that you, brother, may remember <gloss>to record/note down</gloss> it. Those who are rebellious and unfilial are remanded to the **Capital of Thunder**. The greedy, lecherous, and evil are sent to the **Fire Department**. Those who are manipulative and treacherous are handed over to the **Water Palace**. Thieves and silver-tongued <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss> sycophants are gathered in the **Bureau of Pestilence**. 

If there are cases where justice seems not yet done, it is not that Heaven has overlooked them. It is either because there is hope that the person might repent, and so they are temporarily tolerated while we wait; or because their crimes are so extreme that a much heavier punishment is being prepared for them. There are also cases where the individual is intentionally spared so that the disaster falls upon their descendants. The ingenuity of Heaven's design lies precisely in the timing—the balance of progress and delay, of speed and slowness. Can you really take these instances as evidence that the **Vast Net of Heaven** <note>original: "Tianwang huihui," a reference to the Laozi: "The net of heaven is vast; though its mesh is wide, nothing escapes."</note> is failing? 

But it does not stop there. There are also those who have granaries full of wealth but end up holding a begging bowl in their twilight years; those of lowly birth who serve as grooms and drivers but suddenly receive official appointments later in life. Households that accumulate good deeds may suffer frequent illness, while homes that practice treachery often enjoy wealth and glory. Fields and houses merely wait for their true master <gloss>referring to the rightful owner</gloss>...

The Way of Heaven is mysterious;

There are the innocent who are entangled for years in **prisons** <term>lingyu|囹圄 — an ancient term for confinement or jail</term>, and those who commit no crime yet suddenly and mistakenly meet the blade of a sword. Cases like these are as diverse as scales and fur, impossible to list individually. In essence, it is the Mind of Heaven that adjudicates them. The hidden and the manifest are inconsistent; misfortune and fortune are never fixed. Complex and contradictory, they entangle the "nine emotions" <note>the fundamental human feelings such as joy, anger, and grief</note>—this is what makes it "Heaven." If one finds error or inconsistency in this, it is a great delusion. You must understand this, brother."

Bei asked again: "If that is so, then does **Hell** truly exist or not?"

Tan replied: "Though Hell exists, human life is like a great dream. I say death is the same. It depends on the state of one's heart, and Hell follows accordingly. Thus, those fond of wine dream of drinking; those fond of litigation dream of disputes; those who delight <gloss>pronounced 'lu-wu'</gloss> in weapons dream of knives and bows <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>; those who occupy <gloss>rising tone</gloss> themselves with literature <gloss>entering tone</gloss> dream of books and histories. If one dreams of drowning, one thinks of rescue; if one dreams of chess, one...

<margin>
A conversation at Xiashan.
The Buddhist and Taoist currents
are all present.
Words on "Writing in Silence"
and similar
uninspired talk.
</margin>

...thinks of winning; if one dreams of a tiger, one feels fear; if one dreams of an enemy, one knows to avoid them. What is created in the day takes shape again at night. When death comes, the intent returns and the animal soul <note>the *po* (魄), the part of the soul associated with the physical body and instincts</note> descends. The spirits that once danced and moved <gloss>pronounced in the departing tone</gloss> fall still <gloss>originally</gloss> as the ride ends. The single spirit reflects upon itself, and the grievances and sins of a lifetime gather at that moment. If in the past one imposed the **knife and saw** <gloss>pronounced in the level tone</gloss> upon others, then the Hell of Knives and Saws appears before one's eyes. If one previously unleashed the venom of snakes and dogs, then the Hell of Snakes and Dogs is visited upon one's body. If one formerly plotted with boiling water and fire, then the Hell of Boiling Water and Fire sears the flesh. Where life was, death receives its due. Though these hells were never intended to entrap people, people cast themselves into them. How could it be an empty hell? <gloss>Detailed note: Hell is in the heart.</gloss>

Bei said again: "The burning of spirit money and silk, the offering of meals and congees, the lighting of lamps, and the chanting of sutras for repentance—do these truly have a use? Or are they merely empty rituals that provide no help?"

Tan replied: "They are truly empty rituals. When a person's allotted span of life and fate ends, the "Impermanence" <note>the *Wuchang*, messengers who escort the dead</note> sets a limit that cannot be crossed. When the time comes to pursue the spirit and seize the soul, not even a moment's delay is possible. At this time, even **Zhang Liang** <gloss>Zhang</gloss> or **Chen Ping** <gloss>Chen</gloss> would have no strategy, and **Fang Xuanling** <gloss>Xuanling</gloss> or **Du Ruhui** <gloss>Ruhui</gloss>... <note>Tan Jing names the most brilliant administrative and strategic minds of the Han and Tang dynasties to illustrate that even the greatest human intellect cannot outwit the timing of death.</note>

<summary>This page presents a philosophical argument that Hell is not a physical location but a psychological projection of one's own earthly sins and conscience. It also critiques common funerary practices, suggesting that ritual offerings cannot alter the fixed moment of death or the soul's self-judgment.</summary>

The meaning of these words is one of regret;

How foolish! As for the matter of chanting sutras and performing repentance rituals: if a person is kind and virtuous while alive, they have no need for such things. In today’s world, those who oversee litigation often act according to their own whims, letting people in or out of prison based on personal gain and accepting bribes. Bei cupped his hands in respect and said: "I have received your profound discourse; you have taught me much. Since my destiny is likely already known to you, would you try to speak of it? Let me know what to pursue or avoid, so as not to waste this meeting."

Tan then nodded his head and said: "It is indeed my wish. However, I do not dare speak too much detail to you, brother. I hope you will cherish yourself. However, in the very near future, there will be a sudden change that will be unfavorable for your followers. There was no difference from the ritual etiquette and guards of an official in the world of the living. In an instant, they vanished into the clouds.

Bei returned to his residence, greatly astonished by these events. Recalling the warning of a "change," he suspected it might mean a raid by bandits. Consequently, he entrusted his travel funds to a mountain monk and moved his family to the market town. Within ten days, rumors spread that the foothills of the mountain had suddenly collapsed, and the civilian houses were all buried. The dead and injured were very numerous. Bei went to see it; his lodging had already **collapsed and fallen** <gloss>crumbled</gloss> amidst the yellow mud and scattered rocks. It was truly miraculous!

In that same year, Bei was recommended for the **Mingjing** <term>Mingjing|明經 — "Understanding the Classics," a category of the civil service examinations during the Ming dynasty</term> degree and was decreed by the Emperor to help compile the *History of Yuan*. His sons Gao, Pian, and Hui were all appointed to offices based on their talents. The family’s scholarly tradition continued unbroken, exactly as Tan Jing’s words had foretold.

<meta>This page begins a narrative about the scholar Xia Shangzhong and his encounter with a monk named Benyuan. The text blends classical Buddhist and Confucian themes regarding the nature of existence and the choice between public life and spiritual retreat.</meta>

<margin>
The Way of the world is nothing more than choosing between public service and private retreat. Thus it is said: "In failure, cultivate your own virtue; in success, bring goodness to all the world." <note>original: "窮則獨善其身，達則兼善天下" — a famous quote from the Mencius</note> Those who cling to a great reputation or chase after profit will lose their way; those who abandon society entirely also separate themselves from humanity.

Suddenly, a monk arrived from afar and called out, "Old friend, old friend! Why has it been so long since we last parted?" 

Shangzhong heard these words and recognized the voice. This "old friend" was Benyuan, a monk from the local temple. At first, Shangzhong did not realize that the monk had already passed away <note>original: "辭凡" — literally "left the mortal world"</note> the previous autumn. He replied, "I have been longing to see you and intended to visit tomorrow. I did not expect we would meet tonight." <gloss>a chance encounter</gloss> He then ventured to ask, "May I ask where you have come from, Master?" 

The monk replied, "I was traveling through this region and happened to hear the sound of you reciting poetry. I recognized it as the voice of an old friend, and so I did not hesitate to come and see you." Shangzhong was overjoyed. They sat together with their legs crossed <gloss>sitting in a cross-legged position</gloss> and shared a drink under the thatched roof of the boat. 

The monk sighed deeply and said, "The ancients said <gloss>referencing Li Han</gloss> <note>Though the text attributes this to Li Han, this famous passage is from Li Bai’s "Preface to a Feast on a Spring Night in the Peach Blossom Garden"</note> that 'Heaven and earth are the inn of all living things, and time is the passing traveler of a hundred generations.' Since this floating life is like a dream, how much joy can one truly find? The ancients wandered at night while holding candles <gloss>meaning one should enjoy life while they can</gloss>; how profound these words are! Should they not serve as a warning to those who rush about preoccupied with worldly affairs?" 

Shangzhong was astonished and said, "In the past, Master, you always encouraged me to strive for official rank and fame. Why do you speak of such things now?"

# Master Xia Meets a Monk

Xia Shangzhong of Suzhou <note>A major city in Jiangsu province known for its high culture and scholars</note> was a man of letters whose poetry was famous throughout the world. He was a long-time friend of a monk at a temple in Wujiang County.
<margin>According to records, Benyuan was refined in literature and excelled at poetry <gloss>falling tone</gloss>, and he was very disciplined in his conduct. He was considered the most elegant and refined of those in the monastic orders. If he has truly returned without scattering into idle talk, he is indeed a true practitioner.</margin>
The monk’s name was Benyuan. Although he served in the "Empty Gate" <note>A poetic term for Buddhism</note> and was well-versed in Buddhist teachings, he was also highly skilled in literature and particularly excelled at poetry. His conduct was pure, and at the time, people regarded him as a "True Son of Buddha." He associated with famous scholars, and people from near and far respected him. 

One day, he gathered his disciples and said to them, "You all must respectfully follow the teachings. I am going to peacefully return to the original state of creation." <note>A euphemism for death; "造化舊物" refers to the elemental matter of the universe</note> Having said this, he passed away <gloss>falling tone</gloss>. 

Shangzhong was unaware of this event. The following year, he had business elsewhere and his boat arrived at Wujiang. Midway through the journey, the sky grew dark <gloss>dusk</gloss>. Feeling lonely while traveling, he recited a regulated poem to console himself. The poem went: <gloss>mooring the boat</gloss>

*I moor my boat by the willows on the bank,*
*Melancholy fills the ancient capital of Wu.*
*The temple’s distant bell sounds fade away,*
*While the city’s high moon leaves a lonely shadow.*
*Alone I sleep among the river maples,*
*While the geese call to each other from the rushes.*
*An infinite sense of desolation—* <gloss>falling tone</gloss>
*The cold tide washes over the withered weeds.*

Just as he finished reciting and was about to go to sleep...

# Volume One

<margin>This statement that "nothing is acceptable" refers to the source of Li Han’s words. Was he speaking out of some specific motivation? <note>original: "此罔無一可者本源引李翰林之言" — referring to Li Bai, often called Li Hanlin, and his famous philosophical stance on the fleeting nature of life.</note></margin>
"Why do your words sound so full of regret?" Shangzhong asked. The monk bowed his head and did not answer. After a short while, he chanted a poem in a clear voice to reveal his inner state:

"I have realized the meaning of the Coming from the West, <gloss>A reference to Bodhidharma bringing Buddhism from India to the 'Western Regions'</gloss>
At the gate of Zen, I nourish my spiritual nature.
The shade of vines fills my seat,
The color of bamboo greens the pond.
Washing my bowl, fish swallow the leftover rice,
Passing the lamp <note>A metaphor for the transmission of Buddhist teachings from teacher to student</note>, deer listen to the sutra.
Naturally free from the fires of fame and profit,
I follow the 'Inscriptions on the Resting Mind'." <gloss>A state of pure heart and simple living</gloss>

Soon, the starry sky grew vast and the Milky Way shifted across the heavens <note>indicating the deep passage of the night</note>. Shangzhong was delighted by Benyuan’s enlightenment; he wished to keep him longer to enjoy the pleasure of their boats tied together throughout the night. However, Benyuan knew that the path between the hidden world and the manifest world <note>the worlds of the dead and the living</note> was obstructed, and he could not stay for long. He rose suddenly and took his leave, saying, "Radiance does not last; the sorrow of parting is hard to fully describe. I must return first with my companions. If you still care for our old friendship, please honor me with a visit tomorrow morning. I shall have the mats swept and ready for you." Having said this, he left in a hurry.

<margin>This expresses the joy followed by the unbearable sorrow of parting.</margin>
The next day, Shangzhong set out. He chanted: "The guest boat sets off early, hurrying to prepare for the long journey. Shadows of geese cross the autumn colors, the sound of roosters disturbs the bright moon. Buddhist lanterns glow in the distant tower, the woodcutter’s drum ceases in the high city. Gradually..."

<column-break/>

<margin>On the journey, he was startled by his friend's appearance; at the ancient temple, he presented his grief to the disciples. Alas! Master Benyuan has cast off his debts and departed. His spirit is gone, yet remains.</margin>
"...plants and human smoke draw near, the traveler's mood becomes leisurely and smooth." As the boat moored at the Wu River Temple, Shangzhong chanted again:

"A small boat upon the water,
Ten miles to seek the Zen forest.
The land is remote so spring still lingers;
The pavilion is secluded and the grass grows deep.
Birds call out words from the sutras,
The lake receives the sound of the chime.
Sitting hand-in-hand with an old friend,
Every word touches upon the Pure Mind."

He intended to wait for Benyuan. After finishing his chant, he straightened his clothes and entered the abbot's quarters. He was met only by Benyuan’s disciples. Shangzhong asked after their master. The disciples wept and said, "Our master passed away <term>transformed|脫化 — a Buddhist term for death, suggesting the soul shedding the physical body like a husk</term> last autumn. Did you not know, sir?" 

They then recounted his final instructions and the grace of his passing. Shangzhong was shocked and skeptical. He described in detail what he had seen the previous night and the poem that was recited. The disciples were terrified and amazed, saying, "Yesterday, our master told us: 'Tomorrow an old friend arrives; do not treat the meeting lightly.' Hearing your words, we know it was no lie!" Shangzhong was deeply moved and grieved. He offered a sacrifice at the coffin and then departed.

<summary>This page concludes the supernatural encounter between Xia Shangzhong and the ghost of the monk Benyuan, who reveals his death and spiritual peace through poetry. Upon visiting the temple, Shangzhong confirms the miracle when the disciples reveal the monk's prophetic final words.</summary>
<keywords>Xia Shangzhong, Benyuan, Wu River Temple, ghost story, Zen poetry, enlightenment, afterlife, mourning rituals</keywords>

# Clear Conversations... Volume One

<margin>Seeking wealth while ignoring benevolence has been common throughout history. That Zheng was wealthy yet remained deeply committed to brotherly duty is a rare sight indeed. Because of this, even when he walked into the dangerous and rebellious days of the war, the bond between the brothers at the edge of life and death was such that they were spared the sorrow of a final parting.</margin>

### Zheng Rong and His Younger Brother

In Xiangyang, there was a man named Zheng Rong, a person of great wealth. His younger brother, named Hua, had also accumulated vast riches through his holdings. The brothers lived in harmony, exhausting every measure of brotherly love. They were not only known as the wealthiest in the prefecture but were also celebrated for their peaceful and harmonious nature, embodying the benevolent spirit of the region. <gloss>Wealthy yet observant of ritual</gloss> The local people held them in high esteem. During the Kaixi era <note>The Kaixi era (1205–1207) was a period during the Southern Song Dynasty marked by a failed military campaign to reclaim northern territories from the Jin Dynasty.</note> of the Song Dynasty, as the North and South took up arms and the region between the Yangtze and Huai rivers was thrown into turmoil, Rong frequently traveled to Huaian <gloss>The name of a commandery</gloss> <gloss>planning a business trip</gloss> to trade goods. Hua advised him, saying: "In these times of chaos and separation, beacon fires are linked one after another, and the clash of weapons meets the eye everywhere. The elderly and weak are cast into the ditches, and the strong are scattered to the four winds. What kind of days are these? How can you, brother, not value your own life and family, but instead walk into such peril?" <gloss>Speaking of the chaos to stop his brother's journey</gloss> Rong laughed and said: "This is something you do not understand. As the ancients said, 'If one does not enter the tiger's den, how can one obtain the tiger's cub?' <note>A famous proverb meaning that one must take risks to achieve great rewards.</note> Furthermore, a man is born to use the mulberry bow and mugwort arrows <note>original: "桑弧蓬矢" (sanghu pengshi). This refers to an ancient custom where a bow made of mulberry wood was used to shoot arrows in six directions at a boy's birth, symbolizing his ambition to serve far and wide.</note> to shoot in all directions. How can one simply stay in the old garden and grow old in vain?"

<column-break/>

<margin>The inability to bear his brother’s distant departure is a sign of the younger brother's supreme affection, which he could not restrain.</margin>
<margin>The description of the military chaos is so sorrowful it is as if one is seeing it firsthand.</margin>

Hua advised him again: "You, brother, are skilled at managing difficulties <gloss>managing the troubles caused by lesser men</gloss>, but I am one whose 'four limbs are unaccustomed to toil and who cannot distinguish the five grains.' <note>A self-deprecating quote from the Analects of Confucius, suggesting a sheltered or scholarly life.</note> Is this acceptable? I beg to go in your place to express my brotherly devotion." Rong said: "That is not right. I have long been a traveler of the rivers and lakes and have tasted every danger and obstacle. <gloss>Experienced in the ways of the world</gloss> You are still 'yellow-mouthed' <note>A term for being young and inexperienced, like a fledgling bird.</note>; how could you handle such a long journey? Do not speak of it again." Hua wept and said: "I truly cannot bear your distant departure. If that is so, then what if we travel together?" <gloss>Begging to go along</gloss> Rong had no choice but to agree. The next day, the brothers hired a boat, packed their goods, and traveled as guests to the Huai region. They had been traveling for some days when, before long, the Jin army crossed the Huai <gloss>The Huai River</gloss>. The soldiers moved in ranks, lining up their wagons and feeding their horses; weapons filled the roads. The constant sound of clashing iron and steel was heard as the soldiers charged forward. They lived amidst the disturbance of war, with the frequent beating of drums shaking the very heavens. Banners fluttered in the wind and rain, and corpses lay covering the wilderness. The number of killed and wounded was immense, and the sound of weeping was everywhere. Blood dyed the desolate countryside, a scene of bitter and tragic misery. For every life, there were ten thousand deaths; nine out of ten houses were empty. <gloss>No one living there</gloss> Wolves howled and cranes wailed as the mounds of the dead rose up.

<summary>This page recounts the story of the Zheng brothers from Xiangyang who, despite their great wealth, prioritize their mutual devotion during the Kaixi era wars. The elder brother's ambition leads them into the war-torn Huai region, resulting in a harrowing encounter with the invading Jin army.</summary>
<keywords>Zheng Rong, Zheng Hua, Southern Song Dynasty, Jin-Song Wars, Huaian, brotherly love, filial piety, Kaixi era</keywords>

# Clear Conversations... Volume One

<margin>
The sunken boat is recovered.
</margin>
<margin>
Hua’s words proved true, yet in his heart, why would he ever have found joy in his words being proven right in such a way?
</margin>

The wind howled; those who saw it felt their noses tingle with sorrow, and those who heard it felt their hearts turn cold. The tracks of travelers and merchants had vanished, <gloss>as they were avoiding the chaos</gloss>. How unfortunate it was that the boat of the two Zheng brothers happened to meet the enemy in the middle of the stream. They dared not advance to assess the danger, nor could they retreat in time to escape. The Jin soldiers <note>The Jurchen Jin Dynasty, which ruled northern China and was frequently at war with the Southern Song during this period.</note> plundered their goods, bored holes in the boat, and sank it. Hua drowned, while Rong was tossed about in the waves like a seagull touching the water, appearing and disappearing, nearly meeting his end. <gloss>Certain death</gloss> Fortunately, he clung to a single plank to reach the shore. He crawled onto the bank, but Hua was nowhere to be found. Weeping without ceasing, he lamented to himself: "I regret not listening to my brother’s words. <gloss>He urged me to value my life and family</gloss> Now I have indeed met with this cruel calamity. <gloss>The sinking of the boat</gloss> I am fortunate enough to have survived for a brief moment, but I do not know if my brother lives or has perished." That night, a mournful wind whistled and gloomy clouds darkened the sky. Rong sat alone, overwhelmed by grief, and composed a poem to console himself:

->*The Huai River's right bank is a place of lingering; at dawn, we are distant, each in a separate world.*<-
<gloss>Traveling on camels, their hairstyles changed, risking their lives alone</gloss> <note>This likely refers to the "barbarian" customs or disguises forced upon those in occupied territories.</note>
->*Night rain floats the southern leaves; the autumn wind blows over the tombs.*<-

<column-break/>

<margin>
When a person dies, the *hun* spirit <note>The "cloud-soul" associated with the mind and heaven.</note> ascends to heaven and the *po* spirit <note>The "white-soul" associated with the body and earth.</note> descends to the earth; they become silent and formless, perishing together. Yet why is it that they often do not vanish? It is because the essence of the young and strong gathers and does not disperse. Furthermore, when one meets an untimely death, their heart remains unsatisfied.
</margin>

->*Since leaving the fields, there are only empty dreams; in my dejection, I cannot return.*<-

After reciting the poem, he wept and prayed: "My brother, we were always so close. Tonight, whether you are alive or dead, I beg to see you once more. If not, I too shall leap into the Huai River and die, for I do not wish to return alive." Before he finished speaking, there was the sound of weeping in the darkness. As it drew closer, he began to recognize the figure. He realized it was Hua. They wept facing each other. Rong said, "I did not listen to your brave words, and in the end, I met this disaster." <gloss>However, the manner of his escape</gloss> <gloss>was mostly unexpected</gloss> <gloss>Regarding this change, Hua said: Zilu suffered the fate of being minced into meat, and Wu Zixu suffered the fate of being placed in a leather wine-sack.</gloss> <note>original: "子路有醢之難亦有伍員受鴟夷之危" — These are historical references to loyal men who met gruesome deaths. Zilu was a disciple of Confucius; Wu Zixu (Wu Yuan) was a famous general whose body was thrown into a river in a leather bag.</note> <gloss>Reduced to ash</gloss> "A gentleman simply accepts his fate." Then, Hua himself recited a poem:

->*Ten thousand miles north of the Huai River, mournful clouds cover the view.*<-
->*The snow is deep, the barbarian horses are strong; the moon is dark, and the apes of the border wail.*<-
->*Distant garrisons are linked by beacon fires; the cold sands are vast and overgrown.*<-
->*My wandering soul cannot yet return; my heart breaks as the sun sets in the west.*<-

As the rhyme ended, he suddenly vanished. Rong’s grief intensified until he was as thin as a skeleton; he fainted and regained consciousness several times. The next day, Hua’s body floated to the surface of the water alone, looking just as he did when alive. Rong provided a lavish burial for him and returned home. At the time, people marveled at these strange events.

<summary>This page recounts the tragic drowning of Zheng Hua after a Jin military raid and the subsequent ghostly encounter between the two brothers, ending with the miraculous recovery of Hua's body.</summary>

Scholar Ni had already traveled far from the records of the official; in the midst of his seclusion, a monk said: "The falling petals of heaven fill the void as snow accumulates. Their form gathers, appearing like an outer face of jade. A refined laugh fills a carriage; truly, the great transformations since the Song dynasty have been esteemed and followed. One can only stop after experiencing it once or twice, and thus it is recorded here."

### Scholar Ni on a Snowy Night

In the city of Hecheng <note>modern-day Jiaxing in Zhejiang Province</note>, there was a man named Ni, a Confucian scholar. He was staying at the Jinming Temple in the city, at the home of a monk <gloss>a famous spot within the temple</gloss>. He lived near the "Lake-Sky Sea-Moon" Buddha Pavilion. Beneath the pavilion stood a statue of Lord Fan Li <gloss>The Grand Official of the State of Yue</gloss> <note>Fan Li was a famous 5th-century BCE strategist who, after helping the King of Yue, retired to a life of wandering the lakes</note>. Outside the pavilion was a lake known as Fan Li Lake. Its waters were clear, rippling, and pure green. Although it connected to the outer lakes, its color remained distinct, as if separated. Scholar Ni had wandered there for many days. One evening, when the snow had cleared, the long mists vanished from the sky, and the bright moon shone for a thousand miles. One could see only "jade trees" lost in the distance, and jasper palaces rising like blue peaks. The cold air <term>Qi (炁): the vital energy or atmosphere of the environment</term> seemed to lock the silver bridge in place, while the white-plastered battlements <gloss>a battlement is a wall</gloss> of the lake circled around. Near and far, everything was a single color; wherever he looked, it was like a painting. It was truly what one calls a "widening of the heart and a delight of the spirit," a view that one could never finish gazing upon. The Scholar then composed a poem on the spot:

"Six-petaled wondrous flowers dance busily in the north wind,
Mountains and rivers caught in silver waves, the world inside a flour dumpling.
Filled to the brim to satisfy a hungry guest <gloss>one who loves snow</gloss>,
Like the old man fishing alone on the cold river <gloss>a classic snowy scene</gloss>."

<column-break/>

<margin>
How is this strange? For heroes who retreat from the world, their purpose is profound. The wind has not weakened, yet all has vanished; if it were not so, how many would simply wither and rot alongside the common grass and trees?
</margin>

<gloss>The Old Man with the Bamboo Hat</gloss> <note>Li Weng (笠翁) is a pen name for Li Yu, a famous 17th-century dramatist and publisher</note> "One should know that three feet of depth ensures ten years of harvest." Just as the poem was finished, Scholar Ni suddenly heard the sound of laughter outside the pavilion. He was surprised that in the deep of a snowy night, there were others who had anticipated his own heart's desire. He leaned on the railing and looked closely, seeing an imposing man <gloss>the character 'ang' is pronounced like 'yang,' meaning to look up</gloss>. He wore a head-cloth and rustic clothes, and beside him was a youth of exceptional beauty. The tall man said loudly, "The moon is clear; I did not realize the night watches were ending." The beauty replied, "The snow is deep; who asks what the road is like?" The man said, "For a hundred years, Heaven and Earth have been heartless." The beauty replied, "Do the mountains and rivers before our eyes have a master?" The man spoke again, "The crane's cry and the ape's wail make the throat cold." The beauty also said, "When dragons fight and tigers struggle, the bloodstains are many." The man sighed deeply, "A laugh with a bristling beard is just a casual thing." The beauty also smiled, "Looking back at the west wind, our temples have turned white." They walked and chanted to themselves, their steps as light as if flying. Soon, a strange fragrance wafted through the air. Without needing to be called, a small boat without oars came from the outer lake, traveling against the current to the shore. The two of them boarded composedly, letting the wind carry them away, their laughter echoing across the cold pool.

<vocab>Ni Sheng (Scholar Ni), Fan Li Lake, Li Weng (Old Man with the Bamboo Hat), Snowy Night, Lord Fan, Jinming Temple, Cold Qi (Atmosphere), Young Hero/Beauty, White Battlements</vocab>

<summary>This page narrates Scholar Ni's encounter with two mysterious, refined figures—possibly the spirits of the historical Fan Li and his companion—on a snowy night at Jinming Temple. The dialogue and poetry emphasize themes of historical sighs, the transience of power, and the stoic beauty of the winter landscape.</summary>

<margin>
Gusu <note>modern-day Suzhou</note> lies to the northeast of Hecheng. The beauty frequently points toward the northeast; do the willows there also grieve for the ruins of the Su Terrace? <note>A reference to the fallen glory of the Kingdom of Wu, where the beauty Xi Shi was famously sent.</note>

# Essential Records of Idle Talk: Volume I

...amidst the waters, talking beneath the moon and the flowering trees. They shared cups and toasted one another, lingering together. One simply did not know the name of the wine. <gloss>The word "First" refers to the wine.</gloss> After a short while, they left the boat and followed the wall <gloss>the city wall</gloss>, sitting across from one another upon the snow.

The beauty gazed at the moon and sighed deeply, pointing frequently toward the northeast with an expression of great sorrow. The Great Official took her hand and leaned close, his voice and words seeming to offer her comfort. When they finished speaking, they whispered together. Scholar Ni thought to himself: "Venturing out to the lake and treading through such danger amidst the evening clouds and the deserted ruins of a temple <gloss>a Zen temple</gloss> is no ordinary matter." He tried calling out to see if he could stir them, shouting loudly: "You man and woman atop the wall! You seem merry enough, but are you not lonely?"

The pavilion and the city wall stood facing each other, separated by no more than a few feet. The two continued their whispered conversation, acting as if they had heard nothing. The scholar shouted again, but they remained as before, not moving in the slightest. The scholar began to feel a sense of alarm and wonder, suspecting they were divine spirits. He steadied his steps and descended from the pavilion. The pavilion door gave a creaking "yi-ya" sound. He saw the statue of the Great Official of Yue standing before an offering table; a single lamp burned with a clear, lonely light. The Great Official's statue looked imposing and lifelike, with its beard and eyes appearing as if alive. Scholar Ni bowed his head, not daring to look directly at it, and hurried away.

<column-break/>

<margin>
There were giant tracks in the snow, and within those giant tracks were the tiny imprints of "bow shoes" <note>referring to the small, arched footwear of women</note>. This garden did not only manifest itself to the scholar, but also showed these signs to the city dwellers.
</margin>

Upon returning to his lodgings, he reached a great realization. The man he had seen was the Great Official of Yue <note>Fan Li</note>, and the beauty he accompanied was the girl of Huansha Stream <note>Xi Shi</note>. Their boarding of the small boat was a manifestation of their legendary, elegant departure to wander the Five Lakes. 

The next morning, the people of the city were all talking about how there were giant footprints in the snow, and within those giant prints were the tiny imprints of bow shoes. They did not know what kind of phantom <gloss>meaning something illusory or strange</gloss> this was. The host <note>likely the monk from the temple</note> merely watched and smiled, saying nothing. The crowd followed the tracks with excitement, tracing them all the way up onto the city wall; it was the same everywhere. Furthermore, the tracks were extraordinary: they all passed along the very edge of the wall, imprinted over empty space <gloss>the word "edge" is pronounced in the departing tone</gloss>. How could a mere mortal walk so close to the precipice? Thus, they knew these were divine beings.

Scholar Ni then gave a full account of what he had seen the previous night: their whispered words and humming songs, and the manner of their boat journey. Everyone was shocked and amazed. For their sake, the scholar prepared offerings of incense, silk, and wine, prostrating himself beneath the statue before departing. This occurred during the early years of the Wanli era <note>c. 1573 onwards</note>.

<summary>Scholar Ni witnesses a spectral encounter between the legendary figures Fan Li and Xi Shi on a snowy night, which is confirmed the next morning by the discovery of miraculous, impossible footprints atop the city wall.</summary>

# Adding Radiance to the Twofold Virtues

The wife died for her integrity, a white jade disk of priceless value <note>original: "liancheng" (worth many cities), a classical idiom referring to the legendary He Shi Bi jade, implying a value so great it could be traded for several cities.</note> now hidden beneath the dust.

<image-desc>An interior woodblock illustration depicting a scene with two figures. On the left, a man wearing a high official cap and flowing robes is seated on a raised platform or chair. On the right, a woman in traditional dress stands facing him. The scene is partitioned by draped curtains, suggesting a formal household or courtly setting.</image-desc>

<column-break/>

The husband perished for his loyalty, a single spark of his sincere heart <note>original: "danxin" (cinnabar heart), a term for a heart of pure, fiery loyalty that never fades.</note> hangs high with the sun and moon.

<image-desc>A woodblock illustration showing a landscape with a defensive fortification wall, similar to the Great Wall, zigzagging across the middle ground. The sky above contains a large celestial disk (representing the sun or moon) and a constellation of seven stars forming the Big Dipper. In the lower right foreground, two figures stand before a large, arched city gate reinforced with studs.</image-desc>

### Key Terms
*   **Sincere heart** (*danxin*): A heart of pure loyalty, often used for those who sacrifice themselves for the state.
*   **Priceless value** (*liancheng*): Literally "worth a string of cities."
*   **Died for loyalty** (*zhongwang*): A death occurring in the service of one's lord or country.
*   **Died for integrity** (*yisi*): A death chosen to preserve one's honor or righteousness.
*   **Twofold virtues** (*liangjie*): Refers to the "two integrities"—the loyalty of a subject/husband and the chastity or moral resolve of a wife.
*   **Adding radiance** (*zenghui*): To bring further glory or honor to a reputation.
*   **Dust** (*chen'ai*): Represents the world of the living or the physical remains of the deceased.

<summary>This page concludes the tale of the mysterious couple with a poetic tribute, using the metaphors of "white jade" and the "sun and moon" to immortalize their loyalty and virtue. It features woodblock illustrations that contrast the couple's former courtly life with the symbolic landscape of their final sacrifice.</summary>

<keywords>loyalty, integrity, cinnabar heart, white jade, didactic poetry, woodblock illustration, Suzhou history</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general moral eulogies of the previous section to a specific historical narrative concerning General Wu Yuan and his wife, Lady Lu, during the fall of the Southern Song Dynasty. It establishes the "scholar-general" archetype for the protagonist.</meta>

# Wang Kan’s Selection of Screen Paintings, Collected Volume One

### The Loyal Soul of General Wu

Wu Yuan, the Regional Commander of Jingmen, was a man of great loyalty and filial piety. Though he was a warrior by profession, he was exceptionally fond of the ancient classics. He would flip through his books until the middle of the night, and indeed, day or night, he was never seen without a volume in hand. He often read the *Book of Han*, specifically the passage where General Ma Yuan speaks of "wrapping one’s corpse in horsehide" <gloss>the story of Ma Yuan</gloss> <note>original: "Ma ge guo shi." Ma Yuan (14 BCE – 49 CE) was a famous Han general who believed a soldier should die on the battlefield rather than in a bed; this phrase became a classic idiom for military sacrifice.</note>. Upon reading this, Wu would close the book and sigh, saying, "This is my true ambition!"

Thus, even while living amidst helmets and armor, he remained composed through poetry and ritual, as if he were a son receiving instructions in the family courtyard <note>A reference to "Li Dui," the story of Confucius instructing his son, Bo Yu (Li), on the importance of the Odes and Rituals.</note>. His loyal heart was as bright and fierce as a sunflower tilting toward the sun. His wife, Lady Lu, was also skilled in poetry and prose. For this reason, the husband and wife respected one another like honored guests. The world spoke of them as "birds sharing a wing" or "intertwined branches," and these descriptions were not exaggerations.

In the seventh year of the Xianchun era of the Song Dynasty <note>Approximately 1271 CE, during the final decade of the Southern Song Dynasty's struggle against the Mongol invasion.</note>, the Yuan Mongol troops besieged the city of Xiangyang with great urgency. The imperial court ordered Wu Yuan to its rescue. Wu did not depart immediately, and Lady Lu, fearing a delay, exhorted him: "To serve one’s Lord with loyalty is a universal principle of both past and present. If one does not encounter gnarled roots and tangled joints, how can the sharpness of a blade be seen? <note>This metaphor suggests that true talent and character are only revealed during times of extreme difficulty or crisis.</note> My Lord, do not linger and thus fail the King’s affairs!" <gloss>She urges his departure</gloss>. Wu thanked her, saying, "I shall treat your words with the utmost care."

<margin>Wu Yuan led his troops into the thick of battle and died; his life ended in service to the Emperor, truly having no cause for shame regarding the words of the ancient paragons.</margin>

<column-break/>

<margin>
None of the
troops returned
alive, and so
Lady Lu did not
know of 
his death.
</margin>

He set out that very day. Lady Lu composed a poem in the *lüshi* style <note>A classic eight-line regulated verse form.</note> to present to him. The poem read:

"I admire your family's long lineage of official service,
In a hundred battles, your heart always holds the debt to our Lord.
You have the talent to draft proclamations like a master scribe, <gloss>Dispatching proclamations and building the altar</gloss>
But no path to follow the Great General of Huaiyin. <gloss>The red-hearted general</gloss> <note>Huaiyin refers to Han Xin, one of the most brilliant military strategists in Chinese history; she is comparing her husband's potential to the greats of old.</note>
You hunt the eagle at the purple border while autumn clouds darken;
You gallop your horse by the Yellow River through deep night snows.
I know your white head and cinnabar heart <note>“Cinnabar heart” (danxin) signifies a heart of pure, unchanging loyalty.</note> will never change;
When you return, may gold seals hang from both your elbows."

Wu departed, wiping away his tears. He personally led his troops to the walls of Xiangyang and encountered the Yuan forces. He won five successive battles, and the fame of his army shook the region. However, the Yuan commanders signaled for their external armies to gather, surrounding and striking from all sides. Our army was isolated and weak, unable to withstand the pressure. Wu Yuan threw his arms wide and gave a great shout, increasing the morale of his soldiers a hundredfold. The sound of the slaughter moved heaven and earth, and the battle flags obscured the sun and moon. However, the roles of "guest" and "host" <note>In military strategy, the "guest" is the invader and the "host" is the defender. Here, it suggests the tactical positions were already compromised.</note> were already divided, and the strength of the opposing sides was unequal. <gloss>The enemy troops were powerful and numerous.</gloss> Wu Yuan was struck by several spears, yet he fought with all his might until he died. Of the retreating soldiers, not a single one returned alive. At this time, Xiangyang was nearly lost to the Yuan <gloss>The battle ended in silence</gloss>. When the roads finally became passable, a scholar from Jingmen named Liu Chun passed through Xiangyang and stayed at a government hostel. Going out at night, he heard...

<summary>General Wu Yuan, a scholar-warrior, is encouraged by his poet-wife Lady Lu to break the siege of Xiangyang; he dies heroically in battle after five victories when his unit is overwhelmed.</summary>
<keywords>Wu Yuan, Lady Lu, Xiangyang, Song Dynasty, Mongol Invasion, loyalty, military poetry, Ma Yuan</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of General Wu Yuan. Following his death in battle during the defense of Xiangcheng, his spirit appears to a traveler named Chun to send a final message to his wife, Lady Lu, in Jingmen.</meta>

# Selection of Elegant Conversations, Volume One

<margin>Clad in armor, moving with somber restraint, yet his vital spirit remains sharp.</margin>
<margin>Reed flutes and drums echo in the west wind; those who hear it feel a stinging grief.</margin>

...walking along. At that time, flutes played from the painted tower, the tune of "Plum Blossoms Falling." A waning moon was veiled by clouds, and dust buried the makeup mirror. After walking barely three or five steps, a sorrowful wind blew sharply, and a murderous aura spread wide. A warrior appeared in the lead, wearing armor and clutching a sword, followed by hundreds of attendants. His presence was terrifying. Chun stepped aside to avoid him. The warrior called out: "Sir, do you not recognize me? I am your fellow townsman, Wu Yuan. I died in service to the King while defending Xiangcheng, and thus I was unable to return home. The Heavenly Bureau, acknowledging my loyalty and righteousness, has appointed me the local Earth God <note>A tutelary deity of a specific district or village in Chinese folk religion.</note>. When you return, please inform my family of this. My wife <gloss>my wife, Jing</gloss> might not believe you, so I give you this iron token <term>iron token: a "tiejuan," usually a metal plate issued by emperors as a pledge of favor, used here as an undeniable proof of identity</term> as proof." He then leaned on his sword and chanted a poem:

> A lone horse returns south, gazing at the ancient city,
> Through half a forest of lingering rain, the evening sun shines bright.
> By the clouds, the peaks meet the colors of the mountains of Qin,
> Among the trees, the river flows with the sound of the Han River.
> There is a monument for shedding tears, its mossy color ancient,
> No songs play on the streets, where wine banners hang horizontal.
> How can I bear to look back on things turned to ruins?
> Reed flutes and drums in the west wind—the traveler’s sorrow.

The poem finished, the gloom suddenly vanished, and the warrior vanished in a solemn flash. Chun was greatly amazed but did not fully believe it. Returning to Jingmen, he sought out the family.

<column-break/>

<margin>She calmly took her own life; thus the loyal official and the chaste wife both achieved their ends. For those who live shamelessly, can they see this without embarrassment?</margin>

He went to her residence and explained the matter. Lady Lu was skeptical and hesitant <gloss>she did not believe it at first</gloss>. Chun then produced the iron token he had been shown. Only then did Lu believe. She held the token and wailed in great grief, fainting and then reviving. She said: "My husband died for the King's service—that is loyalty. I, his wife, die for my husband's hardship—that is righteousness. Since my husband has fulfilled his loyalty, how dare I be unrighteous? I wish only to follow him to the underworld; that shall be enough." She then spent all her resources to perform Buddhist merit rituals <term>yin guo: offerings and prayers intended to improve the deceased's status in the afterlife</term> for her husband for seven days and nights without end. As the saying goes: "Paper ashes are like white butterflies, blood-tears are like red cuckoos" <note>A classic poetic image of mourning: the burnt paper offerings look like white butterflies, and the grief is as deep as the cuckoo, which in legend coughed blood.</note>. She settled all household affairs, leaving nothing behind. After bathing and burning incense, she bowed twice and wept, saying:

> The husband died for loyalty and filial piety, <gloss>The word "for" is read here in the departing tone</gloss>
> The wife perishes for chastity and righteousness.
> Husband and wife die one after another,
> Loyalty and righteousness are both preserved.

Then she calmly hanged herself and died. The family opened the door and saw her; her breath had been gone for a long time. The local people honored her righteousness, collectively providing a fine burial and establishing a shrine called "The Double Virtue Shrine," which remains to this day.

<summary>This page concludes the tale of General Wu Yuan, whose spirit appears as a local deity to prove his death to his wife. Upon receiving proof of his loyalty to the state, Lady Lu commits ritual suicide to maintain her own "righteousness," leading the community to deify them both at a shared shrine.</summary>
